TO THE PUPIL 

Most people like to travel. They like to see beautiful scenery and strange places, to see the different ways in which people live and work and play. What would you want to see if you were to plan a trip? Would you want to see high snow-capped mountains, low level plains, rivers and lakes? Would you want to see fields of cotton, groves of oranges, and forests of giant trees? 

Would you like to go down into mines where coal, iron, gold, or other minerals are dug from the ground? Would you like to see some of the factories that make cereals, flour, clothing, and many other things that you use every day?

Perhaps you have already traveled and seen many interesting things. If so, you have noticed that people make a living in many different ways. Even if you have not traveled, you know that this is true. You have seen pictures of other parts of the country and of the world, and have read or heard about the different ways in which people live, work, and play. You know, for instance, that the people in the southern part of the country need not dress as warmly as the people in the northern part. You know that the work of people in the country is different from that in the city. Even the work of farming varies from place to place. 

People everywhere must have food to eat, clothing to wear, and houses in which to live. If they live in cold regions, they must also have fuel to help keep them warm. The work which people do to secure food, clothing, shelter, and fuel is often called making a living. The farmer gets part of his food and perhaps some of his fuel and shelter from his farm, but sells part of his products to get clothing and other things he needs. The man who works in a factory earns a wage or salary which he uses to buy the things he needs. The man who works in a mine likewise earns a wage. Thus everywhere you will find people doing various kinds of work in order that they may have food, clothing, shelter, and fuel. 

Have you ever wondered just why people have so many different ways of making a living? 

Have you ever wondered why some of them work on farms, some in factories, some in mines, and in such a variety of places? You know, of course, that people cannot be farmers if the land is not good for farming. They cannot work in factories if there are no materials on which to work, and they cannot be miners if there are no minerals to dig from the earth. In short, people must make a living from what they find around them to use. Their success depends on how well they use what they have.

Doubtless you have wanted to be able to answer many questions. Why, for instance, are some lands suited for growing corn and others not? Why do you find large cities in some places and no cities at all in other places? Why do oranges and cotton grow where it is hot and wheat and oats grow where it is rather cool? Why do cowboys live in the great open country and business men nearly always live in the cities? Why do some places have a very heavy rainfall and other places have almost no rain at all? Why do some places have cold weather throughout the year, others have hot weather, and still others have winters and summers? 

Your study of geography will help you answer all such questions. Geography is the story of how people live, how they work, how they play. It tells about the land, the water, and even the air. Best of all, it tells how people make use of the land, water, and air and why they live, work, and play as they do. It helps you answer questions of what, where, and why about lands and people in various parts of the world.

TO THE TEACHER 

Living in the Americas is built around the general ideas of human-use and the distribution of population. Throughout the book, the materials have been selected and organized to help the child understand and appreciate the relation of human activities to the natural environment. 

Fundamentally, then, Living in the Americas is the story of human-natural adjustments, showing how man has adapted his living to the conditions of his environment and how he has sometimes modified his environment to suit his conditions of living. 

This is a child's book. The language is addressed to him in a simple, thought-provoking style. 

The content is based on his experiences and is adjusted to his level of understanding. A special attempt has been made to make every new concept perfectly clear. The problem approach is used throughout, not only by means of questions, but by means of challenging statements and activities as well. In short, the book is built around the child's own ways of thinking and doing. 

Through it all, however, as a teacher you will be able to discover certain implied suggestions as to method.

The units in the book are definite in nature and purpose. The preview to each unit stimulates interest in the subject matter of the unit and guides the thought during study. It seeks to orient the child or to give him an advance picture of the region he is to study. Here special emphasis is placed upon the distinctive natural features of the region and the activities of the people. The 

Study Guides suggest generalizations which are to be developed in the unit. A class discussion of the Study Guides will be found very effective in paving the way for a study of the unit as a whole.

The studies accompanying the maps do not include place-geography questions of the traditional type. On the contrary, they are intended to help the child develop an understanding of environmental conditions and of human-natural relationships. Furthermore, they are so organized that they aid in building up gradually the ability to use maps as tools of learning. Notice the care with which the child is led from the simple to the more complex. 

After each major topic within a unit, you will find a short summary exercise. The purpose of this exercise is to help the child organize his knowledge around certain important facts or generalizations developed in the topic. Exercises at the end of each unit serve the same purpose for the unit as a whole. Here the pupil is given an opportunity to summarize and make over as his own the content of the entire unit. Many of the exercises provide excellent material for class discussions and for oral reviews. Although they are not intended as achievement tests, you will find them very helpful in checking individual progress. Moreover, they should be of great value in helping you discover whether further study on the part of the pupils is needed, or whether further explanation on your part is desirable. 

You will observe that the pictures and the maps of this book are integral parts of the content. 

Pupils, therefore, should be encouraged and directed to make the fullest possible use of these visual aids. The maps are mechanically and pedagogically correct. Moreover, they are so simple in nature as to be easy to understand and use. The pictures have been carefully chosen to portray vividly the concepts developed on the printed page. Notice how they are tied into the running text. A study of Living in the Americas, then, means more than reading the text. 

It means the careful use and interpretation of maps and pictures as well. vi

LIVING IN THE AMERICAS

Figure 1. 

Forest Indians of early days. Once Indians lived in nearly all parts of what is now the United States.

Figure 2. 

Plains Indians of early days. These Indians made a living largely by hunting buffaloes. The mother, at the front of the picture, is preparing a buffalo skin for use. The boy just back of her is spinning a small top. 

UNIT ONE  INTRODUCING THE UNITED STATES 

The United States is a great country, one of the largest and richest in the world. You already know much about it, because it is your home. Most of all, you know the community where you live. You know some of the people and how they live, work, and play. The following pages will help you to learn about other communities as well. 

Of course you know that there hasn't always been a United States. A few hundred years ago only Indians lived in this part of the world. Then white people came across the Atlantic Ocean from Europe and began to settle here. 3 Gradually they took over the land, laying it out into farms and cities. 

How the Indians lived. Many people think that the Indians everywhere lived alike. If we study them carefully, however, we find that they lived in many different ways. Those in one place lived in one way and those in another place, in another.

Once much of the land in what is now the eastern half of the United States was covered with trees. The Indians who lived here, known as forest Indians, made a living by hunting deer, bears, and other animals, fishing in the streams, and farming. Their farming was very simple, being limited to the raising of

plants here and there among the trees. 

For homes they built log houses or made houses of poles covered with bark. 

West of the forest was a fairly dry area of land covered with grass. The 

Indians who lived here, known as plains Indians, made a living largely by hunting buffaloes that lived upon the grasslands. They moved frequently in order to follow the buffaloes from place to place. For homes they made tepees, consisting of a circle of poles tied at the top and covered with buffalo skins. 

Much of the land in the Southwest was extremely dry, too dry for the growth of trees or even grass. The Indians who lived here, known as pueblo Indians, dug ditches from the streams to water the land and made a living by farming. 

They built their houses of bricks which they dried in the sun.

The lands of the West consisted of high mountains, wooded slopes, and fertile valleys, often close together. The 

Indians here lived in different ways because of the many kinds of land. Some, known as coast or fish Indians, lived along streams or the ocean and made a living largely by fishing. Others lived like the plains Indians and still others like the pueblo Indians. 

Thus you see that the Indians lived in many different ways. They lived in these ways because they depended greatly upon the conditions around them. In other words, they were greatly affected by their environment. 

How the white people changed conditions. When the white people came, they first settled along the Atlantic Ocean. Here they cut down trees, built log cabins, and cleared fields for farming. 

They had no means of traveling except on foot, on horseback, or in canoes, for there were no roads. Neither could they buy ready-made things, because there were no factories.

As time passed, more and more people came here to live and some of them began to move westward. Men, women, and children set out on long, dangerous trips through the woods. Usually the men walked and the women and children rode horseback or in large covered wagons. 

Sometimes they rode in crude boats on the larger streams. Thus they kept going until they came to places that looked good for homes. Here they settled and built up communities in new parts of the country. The people kept moving in this manner until they reached all parts of the country. 

At first most of the people were farmers, but gradually they took up other kinds of work. Some became storekeepers, blacksmiths, and shoemakers, and others ran flour mills. Finally some began to build factories, making such things as clothing, furniture, and tools. 

When this happened, many turned to lumbering and mining to help supply the factories with materials. 

Changes also came about in transportation, or in traveling and shipping goods. 

People began to build roads, canals, and railroads, and to use steamboats. They began more and more to carry on trade, that is, to exchange or to buy and sell different kinds of goods. 

Thus slowly the white people took over large areas of land and learned to make a living in a variety of ways. In this manner, they helped to build the 

United States of America.

Figure 3. A colonial woman pounding grain into flour. The branch helped to lift the weight. 

Study guides. Near the beginning of each unit you will find a list of questions called "Study guides." Read these questions and talk about them with your classmates. You won't be able to answer them in full, but you will learn from them what the unit is about. When you have finished the unit, turn back and answer the questions again. 

1. How did the early settlers depend upon their environment for a living? 

2. Why did the settlers move westward toward the Pacific Ocean? 

3. What are natural resources, and how did they affect the settlers? 

4. What effect did rainfall have upon the settlers? 

5. What is meant by growing season, and how did it affect the settlers? 

6. What is meant by altitude, and how did it affect the settlers? 

7. What is meant by latitude?

Figure 4. A colonial farmer at work. Oxen were commonly used for plowing and other similar work. 

MAKING THE MAPS TELL A STORY I. What colors show on a map 

Look at the map on pages 6-7. This is called a relief map. It shows the location of mountains, lowlands, plains, lakes, and rivers. Find the Rocky Mountains and the Appalachian 

Mountains. Find the Great Plains, the Central Lowland, and two coastal plains. Find several rivers and lakes.

Turn to the map on pages 8-9. This is called a physical-political map. The word physical means that this map uses colors to show the location of mountains, lowlands, plains, lakes, and rivers. It also shows how high various places are above the level of the sea. In the lower left-hand corner is an explanation of what the colors mean. A colored map usually has an explanation of this kind. The word political means that the map shows cities, states, countries, and other things that have come about through the work of man.

Now practice using the colors on the map to find out what kinds of stories they tell.

1. Find the dark-green line marked "O to 

500 feet" in the explanation of colors. Dark green, as you will see, is used to show regions that are from 0 to 500 feet above the level of the sea. Such regions are called lowlands.

If they are along the coast, they are called coastal plains. If they are along rivers, they are called river valleys or flood plains. Find several lowlands on the map. 

2. Just above the dark-green line in the explanation, find a line that is lighter in color. 

This color is used to show places that are more than 500 feet but not more than 1,000 feet above the level of the sea. Look at the map and find where some of these places are. 

3. Above the dark-green line in the explanation, find the yellow line. How many feet above sea level are places marked in yellow? Find regions on the map that are marked in this color. Such regions are usually known as plateaus and are commonly more hilly than lowlands. 

4. Find the next colors, tan, brown, and red.

The tan color shows higher regions, some of which are known as plateaus and others as mountains. The brown color shows mountains from 5,000 to 10,000 feet above the sea.

The red color shows the high peaks of the mountains. Find places marked in tan, brown, and red on the map. 

II. Measuring to scale

Look at the map of the United States on pages 8-9. In the lower left-hand corner find a line marked "scale." This line stands for a certain number of miles, and therefore is a ruler of measure by which you can tell distance on the map. Make a long ruler of your own similar to the short one in the book. Cut a piece of paper into a strip the shape of an ordinary ruler. Then place the edge along the scale in the book and mark it off by making short lines the same distance apart as divisions on the scale. When you have finished, your ruler should look something like this:

Each space on your ruler stands for a certain number of miles, as marked on the scale. 

Now use the ruler for measuring the distances on the map between large cities. 

1. On the map find Chicago and St. Louis.

Place the ruler on the map so that the left end just touches the dot that stands for St. Louis and the edge of the ruler touches Chicago.

Notice that the mark on the ruler is just a little beyond Chicago. Since this mark measures 

300 miles, you may say that it is almost 300 miles from Chicago to St. Louis. 

2. Now measure the distance from Chicago to New Orleans; from Chicago to New York.

III. Telling directions on a map 

1. Look at the map and notice the fine black lines that run across it in both directions. Those that run up and down are northsouth lines and those from left to right are east-west lines. With a pencil trace one of the north-south lines from the bottom of the map toward the top. In other words, move the pencil toward the north. Trace the line toward the bottom of the map, or move the pencil south. Trace one of the east-west lines toward the right, or move the pencil east. 

Trace the line toward the left, or move the pencil west. To find a place that is directly north of another, you must follow the direction of the nearest north-south line. To find a place that is directly east of another, you must follow the direction of the nearest east-west line. 

2. Find New Orleans, Louisiana and Memphis, Tennessee. In what direction is Memphis from New Orleans? Find Atlanta, Georgia and Detroit, Michigan. In what direction is Atlanta from Detroit? 

3. Find Los Angeles, California. From the following list choose the city that is most nearly straight north of Los Angeles: 

Seattle, Washington 

San Francisco, California 

Spokane, Washington 

4. Find Philadelphia, Pennsylvania. Name two cities you would pass through if you should travel directly west from Philadelphia.

THE COMING OF COLONISTS 

11 Figure 7. Early forest scene. This picture shows what the country was like when the white people first came to 

America. Much of the land in the eastern part was covered with trees. Notice the path made by the Indians. 

THE COMING OF COLONISTS 

America, as you know, was discovered by Christopher Columbus, who crossed the Atlantic Ocean from Europe. When he returned home, he told interesting stories about the land that he had found. 

Within a few years other explorers came to see the land too. Then people began to make plans to come here to live. The early settlers in this country are commonly known as colonists.

The first settlements failures. The first colonists in America had hard times trying to make a living. When they landed along the Atlantic coast, they found much of the land covered with trees and nobody living here but Indians. 

Figure 7 shows what much of the land was like. After they had landed, the colonists began to cut down trees, build houses, and lay out fields. This work was so slow and difficult, however, that they usually ran out of food before they could raise any crops. When their food gave out, they became discouraged and returned to Europe. Many died from sickness and exposure or were killed by neighboring Indians. As a result, one settlement after another failed, the people returning to Europe or losing their lives.

In some cases they completely disappeared, and nobody knows to this day what happened to them. 

As the 

How the colonists succeeded. years passed, the colonists began to plan more carefully how they could succeed in the new world. Finally they made three important settlements along the Atlantic coast. The first of these settlements was at St. Augustine in what is

now the state of Florida. The second was at Jamestown in what is now the state of Virginia. The third was at 

Plymouth in what is now the state of Massachusetts. Find all these states on the map and notice that they are many miles apart.

When the colonists came to these places, they brought extensive supplies. 

Besides large quantities of food, they brought such farm animals as horses, cattle, hogs, and sheep. Also, they brought various kinds of seeds, especially grains to plant in their fields. The animals and plants were strange to the Indians, for they had never grown in 

America before.

The colonists planned their work carefully, striving to raise everything that they needed. While they waited for their crops to grow, they made their food last as long as possible by securing other food from the surrounding country. They hunted wild animals in the forests, fished in the streams, and gathered wild fruits from bushes in the more open places. 

Thus they were able to get along and finally succeeded in making new homes. 

Even so, they had very difficult times and many years passed before they actually became prosperous. 

Farming the leading occupation. Most of the colonists, as the foregoing shows, made a living by farming. They tried to raise the same kinds of crops here as they had raised in Europe. In some cases they succeeded in raising the crops well, but in other cases they failed. 

Much depended upon whether or not the climate was suited to their growth. 

Climate, as you know, means the amount of rainfall, the temperature from day to day, and the length of summer and winter. All these have great effect upon the growing of crops.

Besides their own crops the settlers obtained others from the Indians. The most valuable crop they obtained was corn, which the Indians called maize. 

They had never seen corn before, for it had grown only in America. When they began to raise corn, they found it very valuable. It could be ground into meal and used for making bread, mush, and other kinds of food. Moreover, it provided excellent feed for the farm animals, especially the hogs.

Things to do 

1. Write a story about the early settlers telling exactly how they lived. Perhaps you have read an interesting story in a book which you would like to write in words of your own. 

2. Prepare a story to dramatize, telling how the Indians trained the colonists to raise corn. 

Imagine what took place and make the acting as real as you can. 

3. Make a list of things that you have today which the colonists never saw, such as automobiles and radios. Compare your list with those prepared by the other pupils. 

4. Make a drawing of a log cabin, an Indian home, or other early scene. Explain the drawing to the other pupils of the class. 

SETTLING NEW ENGLAND 

Finding New England on the map. 

Look again at the map on pages 8-9 and find the states of Maine, New Hampshire, Vermont, Massachusetts, Rhode Island, and Connecticut in the northeastern part of the country. These states together are commonly referred to as New England. The red lines that separate them are known as boundary lines. Such lines are put on maps to show the boundaries

SETTLING NEW ENGLAND 

133 

Figure 8. A New England cabin. The settlers in New England built comfortable cabins as a protection during the cold winters. They made the cabins of logs with large fireplaces where they could burn wood to keep warm. They also made most of their own furniture. 

The woman in the picture is cooking over a large fireplace. The other members of the family are seated at a crude table. 

Notice the broad collars which they wore on their clothes. This shows that they were Pilgrims, the same people who gave thanks for their abundant harvest and celebrated the first Thanksgiving. or places where one state or country leaves off and another begins. Usually there is nothing on the land to show boundaries, except certain markers put up by man. Sometimes, however, a boundary follows a river, lake, or mountain. Such a boundary is commonly known as a natural boundary. 

Notice the various colors on the map and what each tells about the land. 

Some of the states contain mountains, and the Atlantic Coastal Plain, in most places, is very narrow. If you were to visit the region, you would find much of it hilly and rocky. 

Farming in early New England. When the first settlers came to New England, they found the country much colder than where they had lived in Europe. Therefore they built snug cabins, similar to that shown in Figure 8. They cleared the land, planted crops, and began to make a living by farming. They soon found farming rather difficult, however, because the hilly land was hard to cultivate, and the summers were too cold to ripen their crops. Therefore, after they had tilled the soil a few years, many turned to other ways of making a living.

They found other natural resources which they could use to better advantage. A natural resource is anything useful which has been provided by nature, such as soil, lumber, water power, or minerals. 

Fishing in early New England. As the colonists looked about for other things to do, they found many fish along the coast in the Atlantic Ocean. Therefore many began to fish to secure extra food. At first, they tried to supply only themselves and their neighbors. Then some began to catch fish to sell and the work became more and more important. 

Finally they began to salt and dry some of the fish and to ship them to Europe. 

Thus they built up a great trade that proved very helpful. It was helpful because they could exchange their fish for other things that they needed, such as clothing, furniture, and tools. 

Manufacturing in New England. As the colonists began to give up farming,

Figure 9. Building a ship in early New England. This picture shows men at work on a ship along the New England coast. The people here built ships because they needed them for fishing and for carrying on trade. some of them turned to manufacturing.

As a result the people of New England began to carry on manufacturing before other parts of the country were settled. 

One reason why the people turned to manufacturing was that they could easily get power for running their factories. 

Many of the streams in New England contain rapids and falls. The factories were built beside the falls and run by water wheels. In these days there were no steam engines or electric motors such as we have today. 

Another reason why the people turned to manufacturing was to use certain products from the farms, such as wool, and certain natural resources, such as lumber. Therefore many people began to make a living by lumbering. They cut trees in the forests, hauled logs, and cut them into lumber. 

One of the most interesting forms of manufacturing in New England in early days was shipbuilding. The ships were made entirely of lumber and were very clumsy in comparison with the ships that we know today. They were sailboats because the steamboat had not yet been invented. Figure 9 shows one of the ships as it was being built.

2. How can you tell on the map where one state leaves off and another begins? 

3. What do the colors on the map tell about the land in New England? 

4. Why did many of the people give up farming and turn to other ways of making a living, such as fishing, lumbering, and manufacturing? 

5. What is meant by a natural resource, and what examples can you give?

SETTLING THE MIDDLE ATLANTIC STATES 

Finding the Middle Atlantic States on the map. Turn again to the map on pages 8-9 and find the states of New 

York, Pennsylvania, New Jersey, Delaware, Maryland, Virginia, and West

Virginia. These states together are commonly known as the Middle Atlantic 

States. Which of them is the largest, and which the smallest? Which have the longest boundary lines?

What do the colors on the map tell about the land? Find the Atlantic

Coastal Plain and notice that it is fairly wide, especially in the southern part. Find the Appalachian Mountains in the western part. The land between the mountains and the plains is known as the Piedmont Plateau. A plateau is an area of fairly level land higher than lowlands but not so high as mountains. On the map it is usually shown in yellow or orange. West of the mountains is another plateau known as the 

Allegheny Plateau.

Notice that several large rivers flow across the Middle Atlantic States. Most of these rivers, as you can see, empty into the Atlantic Ocean. 

Farming on the Atlantic Coastal Plain.

The colonists in this part of the country first settled along the coast. They cleared the land, as shown in Figure 10, built log cabins, and laid out fields just as the farmers in New England did. Moreover, since the land was very level, they found it easy to cultivate. For the most part, they did mixed or general farming, that is, they raised a variety of crops, such as corn, wheat, oats, hay, fruits, and vegetables. Later some of 

Figure 10. Clearing the land. Before the settlers could use the land for farming, they had to cut the trees and clear away or burn the logs and brush. They did much of this work with axes, as shown above. them found certain crops more profitable than others and began to do specialized. farming. In specialized farming, a farmer uses his land for growing one or two crops or for raising certain farm animals. 

One reason why some of the settlers turned to specialized farming was that they began to grow tobacco. This crop, like corn, had been grown by the Indians but had never been raised in Europe. 

The settlers in the southern part of the 

Middle Atlantic States cured a few plants and soon began to grow it on a large scale. Most of them laid out great farms, known as plantations, and kept many workers. Usually the plantations were along rivers where the 2-5G

Figure 11. Rolling tobacco from a plantation. The settlers put their tobacco in huge barrels, such as the one shown above. They hitched oxen or horses to the barrels and thus rolled the tobacco across the fields to the banks of rivers. tobacco could be easily loaded on ships. 

Figure 11 shows a worker on a plantation taking tobacco to a ship. 

Farming on the Piedmont. As more and more people settled on the Atlantic Coastal Plain, some of them began to move westward to the Piedmont Plateau.

These settlers found their way through the wilderness, taking little along except a kettle, an ax, and a gun. They were cut off from the rest of the world and had to depend entirely upon their surroundings for a living. They cut trees and used the lumber for making houses, furniture, and tools. They grew grains, vegetables, and fruits in the fertile soil. They hunted wild animals in the woods and used the skins or furs for making clothing. In this manner they slowly opened up the country until they could begin to carry on trade. 

Finally roads were built, and the Piedmont region became very prosperous and has remained so ever since.

As 

Later ways of making a living. in New England, some of the people in the Middle Atlantic States soon began to turn from farming to other kinds of work. Some began to cut lumber in the forests, for lumber was needed in the rapidly growing cities and towns. Others began to work in factories, such as flour mills, woodworking plants, and textile mills. In general, factories grew more slowly here than in New England because there was less water power. 

As the people moved westward, they found that certain parts of the Allegheny Plateau were richly underlaid with coal. 

Therefore coal mining became an important occupation in the region, especially after factories began to use steam engines for power. Later many large steel mills and other factories were

built near the coal mines and the area became noted for manufacture and trade.

The work of the southern pioneers. Frequently hunters from the Atlantic Coastal 

Plain and the Piedmont crossed the mountains and explored the lands to the west. Here they found rich lands covered with forests. 

When these hunters reported what the land was like, people began to move into the country to live. These people were known as pioneers because they were opening up a new part of the country for settlement.

Figure 13. A pioneer cabin. When the pioneers settled the country west of the Appalachian Mountains, they made part of their living by hunting. 

This picture shows a pioneer father and son returning from a hunt. 

The Southern States are cut into two parts by the Mississippi River. Along this river and other rivers in the South are very fertile valleys. To the west, in western Texas and Oklahoma, lies part of a large area known as the Great Plains. Find this area on the map and notice what the colors tell about its height above the sea.

In 

Settling along the Atlantic coast. general, the colonists came later to the southern part of the Atlantic Coastal

Plain than to the parts farther north. 

Gradually, however, they moved into this area and settled it in much the same way. In North and South Carolina to the north they laid out plantations for the raising of tobacco, but in Georgia to the south they used smaller farms for mixed or general farming. 

After the Atlantic Coastal Plain was settled, people began to push farther west. Some of them moved to the 

Piedmont Plateau and others crossed

The pioneers led hard and dangerous lives in the strange country. They left their friends behind and traveled far into the forest with their families. Figure 13 shows a pioneer and his son coming in from a hunt. Usually several families settled together so that they could help one another. Moreover, they felt safer in this way because the Indians were opposed to their coming. 

At first, travel in the South was very slow, for there were no roads. Before long, however, the people began to make use of the rivers. They built boats known as flatboats which looked much like boxes traveling downstream. 

Downstream, you remember, means the direction in which the water or current flows. Fortunately for the settlers, most of the rivers in the Southern States flow south or in a southerly direction.

Each flatboat carried several families with their belongings. The men steered the boat and pushed it along with poles. 

They kept traveling in this manner until they chose a place for a home. 

The use of cotton in the South. When the settlers moved into the South, they raised the same crops as settlers in other parts of the country. In other words, they did general farming, raising grains, vegetables, and fruits. Before long they began to raise cotton and found it very profitable. In many places they set up plantations and began to carry on specialized farming as shown in Figure 14. 

Finally the entire South, with the exception of lands along the coast and in the northern and western parts, became a cotton country. 

Most of the cotton in the South was shipped by boats to other parts of the country or to Europe. It became much more profitable after the invention of the steamboat.

Figure 15 shows an early scene along the wharf in New Orleans. 

As you can readily see from the picture, many steamboats were in use in earlier days. Figure 14. An old-time cotton plantation. Once much of the cotton was raised on large plantations, as the one above. Notice the house in the rear.

Average Annual Precipitation 

20 -40 40 60 60 60 40 30 60 20 40 Figure 16. The rainfall of the United States. The word "rainfall" in geography means precipitation or the moisture that falls as rain, snow, sleet, and hail. All places along each line on the map have the same rainfall. For example, places along the line marked 30 have thirty inches of rainfall and along the line marked 40, forty inches of rainfall. All places between the lines have from 30 to 40 inches. The rainfall is greatest where the dots are thickest. 

Rainfall favorable to the growth of cotton. There were several reasons why the southern farmers began to grow cotton. One was the good soils, which were well suited to the purpose. Another was the climate, which, as you know, includes the amount of rainfall. This is very important, for all crops must have moisture. Some, of course, must have much more than others.

The rainfall has been carefully measured in all parts of the country, making it possible to compare one region with another. The amount of rainfall in various places is given in inches. Thus we may say that a region has 30 inches of rainfall. This means that if we were to catch in an open vessel the rain that falls for a year, the water in the vessel would be 30 inches deep.

The rainfall varies greatly in different parts of the country, as you will see by looking at Figure 16. It ranges in the country as a whole from less than 10 inches per year to 120 inches per year.

In most of the South, it ranges from 20 to 60 inches per year. Cotton grows best where the rainfall is between these amounts. In fact, most of it grows where the rainfall is between 30 and 50 inches per year. Therefore you can see that the favorable amount of rainfall in the region was one of the reasons why southern farmers began to grow cotton.

Figure 17. The growing season in the United States. In the extreme southern part of the country the growing season lasts throughout the year. As you go north, it becomes shorter and shorter, being as low as 3 or 4 months at the northern boundary. It is greatly affected in places by large bodies of water, such as oceans and the Great Lakes. It is also affected by the height above the level of the sea. In general it is longest where the dots are the thickest. 

Growing season favorable to the growth of cotton. Another feature of the climate which led the southern farmers to grow cotton was the length of summers and winters. The summers in the South are long, providing a long growing season. A growing season is the part of the year during which plants can grow. Usually it includes the time from the last hard frost in the spring to the first hard frost in the fall. Figure 17 is a map showing the length of the growing season in various parts of the country. 

Notice that, in general, the season becomes shorter and shorter from south to north. Only in the South is it long enough for cotton to grow and ripen.

How the pioneers moved westward.

As in the South, the people who explored and settled the North Central States are known as pioneers. They had trouble moving westward because of the Appalachian Mountains. Usually they followed streams and crossed at certain lower places. One of these places was the Mohawk Valley across the state of 

New York. Another was Cumberland

Gap, a break in the mountains between 

Kentucky and Tennessee. Figure 18 shows a family moving in early days. 

When the pioneers reached the west side of the mountains, many of them built flatboats like that shown in Figure 19. On these flatboats they traveled

SETTLING  

23 down the Ohio River with their families and their belongings. They floated along peacefully until they came to places that looked good for homes. Thus because it flowed gently westward, the Ohio became a favorite route for settlers. 

When other settlers reached the west side of the mountains, they traveled over the Great Lakes. The lakes were used less than the river because they were more difficult to travel. Some of the settlers traveled entirely by land.

Later the settlers were helped greatly in their travel by certain inventions and improvements. One, of course, was the building of roads and another the digging of the Erie Canal. A canal is a large ditch or stream dug by man for the use of boats. The Erie Canal followed the

Mohawk Valley and helped the settlers reach the Great Lakes. Here they could either continue their travel by land or ride boats on the Great Lakes. Figure 

20 shows people traveling on one of the boats on the canal. These boats had no motors or sails but were pulled by horses or mules that walked along a path on one of the banks.

The settlers were also helped by the building of steamboats and railroads. 

The steamboats made it possible for settlers to make greater use of the Great Lakes and also the Ohio River. The railroads, too, helped to open up the country rapidly. Several lines were built westward through the mountains to the lands beyond. The first railroads, of course, were different from those that Figure 20. A boat on the Erie Canal. This picture shows how people once traveled on the Erie Canal. During the day they rode on top of the boat, and during the night slept in quarters below. The boat was pulled by mules or horses that walked along one of the banks. Of course the boat moved very slowly, only a few miles an hour.

Figure 21. A stockade at Marietta,

Ohio. This picture shows one of the noted stockades built by the pioneers in the lands west of the Appalachian Mountains. In case of attack by the 

Indians, the settlers could go inside the stockade for protection. At the same time, with their rifles, they could carry on an effective battle.

The settlers lived in cabins close by the stockades. They led very simple lives for a time, raising and making nearly everything that they needed for food, shelter, and clothing. you know today. Both locomotives and cars were small and traveled very slowly, only a few miles an hour. Even so, they were faster than other means of travel, and made it possible for people to settle where there were no waterways. 

How the pioneers lived. The pioneers in the North Central States lived much like those in the South. In many places they built stockades as protection from the Indians. A stockade is a pen inclosed by a high log fence, the logs being set side by side on end. Such a fence in former days enabled persons to defend themselves in case of attack. Figure 21 shows a stockade along the Ohio River.

As in other parts of the country, the pioneers cleared the land and built log cabins for homes. Often they split logs and built rail fences around their fields. 

They did general farming, raising grains, vegetables, and fruits. The land was especially good for growing such grains as corn, wheat, and oats. Therefore it gradually became one of the best farming regions in the country. 

Why the pioneers raised grains. There are several reasons why the farmers of the North Central States began to raise grains. First, the land was fairly level and easy to cultivate. Second, the soils were fertile and well suited to the purpose. Third, the rainfall was favorable and came at the right time of the year to help the young plants. Fourth, the growing season was about the right length for the crops to grow and ripen. 

Turn to Figure 16 and compare the rainfall in the North Central States with that in the Southern States. Then turn to Figure 17 and compare the growing season with that in the Southern States.

The growing season is shorter here because the North Central States are farther from the equator. Distance from the equator is called latitude and is measured in degrees. Each degree is about seventy miles long and there are ninety degrees between the equator and the North Pole. Also, as you can readily see, there are ninety degrees between the equator and the South Pole.

The eastern part of the Western States lies on the Great Plains. West of the

Great Plains, extending north and south, are the Rocky Mountains. The land west of the Rockies is broken into fertile valleys, high plateaus, and high and low mountains. Notice the variety of colors on the map, showing that the land is of different heights above sea level. 

Settling the Great Plains. As the pioneers moved westward from the Mississippi River, they came to a region where the forests left off and grasslands began. The only trees to be found grew along the streams. Just beyond the forests the grass was very thick, but farther west it grew in scattered clumps.

This great region of grasslands came to be known as the Great Plains. As you have already learned, it was the home of buffaloes and also the plains Indians. 

Perhaps you wonder why grass grew on the Great Plains rather than trees. 

The answer is that the region was too dry for trees. Turn again to Figure 16 and notice that the rainfall is only from 

10 to 20 inches, and that the area is much drier than any to the east. Therefore few crops grow except grass. Figure 23. Indians hunting buffaloes on the Great Plains. Before the white people came to America, the Indians hunted the buffaloes on foot, for they had no horses. For weapons they used bows and arrows, for they had no guns. 

This picture shows how they hunted after they had obtained both horses and guns from the white people.

Figure 24. The meaning of altitude. This picture shows how altitude is measured from the level of the sea. Most mountains, however, are far from the sea, surrounded by large areas of land. Even so, their altitude is figured as if the sea were near by. What is the altitude of the mountains in the picture, and what is the altitude of the plateau? 

The settlers found the Great Plains too dry for farming and kept pushing on to better lands farther west. As a result the region was one of the last in the country to be settled. Finally men began to use the region for raising cattle and sheep. They rode about on horses, driving their herds from place to place. 

The country was laid out in large farms known as ranches, and the workers became known as cowboys.

Over the Rocky Mountains. The set tlers found it hard to reach the land west of the Rocky Mountains. First they had trouble crossing the dry lands on the 

Great Plains. Next they had difficulty crossing the Rocky Mountains, which in most places are very high and rough. 

If you look at the map you will find that most of the mountains are 5,000 or more feet high. Five thousand feet, as you know, is almost a mile. This does not mean that the mountains reach upward for a mile. They extend much less than a mile above the surrounding land because it also is high above the sea. 

Height above sea level is commonly known as altitude. Thus when a mountain is 5,000 feet high, we may say that it has an altitude of 5,000 feet. Figure 

24 shows how altitude is measured. 

High mountains are very cold, some of them being covered with snow and ice throughout the year. The higher you climb on a mountain, the colder the weather becomes. The higher you climb, too, the shorter the growing season becomes. In other words, climbing a mountain has the same general effect as traveling toward the North or South Pole.

Figure 25. An early mining town in the West. Many small towns sprang up where important metals were found, especially gold and silver. Often these towns sprang up within a few months, or even a few weeks. 

From what you have just read, you can readily see why the settlers had hard times traveling to the West. At first, they traveled in covered wagons drawn by oxen. The men walked, and the women and children rode with the belongings. Often as they crossed the 

Great Plains they died from hunger, thirst, or sickness. Now and then they were attacked by Indians or hungry wolves. When they reached the mountains they had to work their way cautiously along streams. Often their wagons were wrecked going over the rough lands.

Occasionally settlers stopped in the 

Rockies to make their homes. Most of these settlers hoped to make a living by mining. They had learned from the 

Indians that the mountains contained gold and silver. Finally they discovered gold, silver, and other minerals and 

Figure 26. Washing gold along a stream. Most of the gold in early days was found in the sand along streams. 

Miners usually washed the sand in small pans or in long especially-made troughs to separate the gold. certain parts of the mountains became very prosperous. Figure 25 shows an early mining town and Figure 26, some miners washing gold.

Settling along the Pacific coast. The first settlers along the Pacific coast were Spaniards who came from Mexico, a country to the south. These settlers made a living by fishing, farming, and raising cattle and sheep. They settled largely in the southern half of the state of California. While they were taking over the land, other settlers came to the Pacific coast from the eastern part of the United States. Some came to California and others to the part known as the Northwest Territory, which included lands now found in Oregon and Washington. Most of these people were attracted by the rich farming lands. 

A little later settlers came to the west coast looking for gold and silver.

SETTLING  

29 Finally they located gold in a stream not far from the settlement of San 

Francisco. News of the discovery spread rapidly, and people came to California from all parts of the world. Most of them, of course, came from the eastern part of the country. Figure 27 shows a scene in San Francisco a few years later, when stagecoaches furnished the chief means of transportation between the West and the eastern part of the country. 

Within a few years, thousands and thousands of people came to the west coast. Some were successful in mining, but others turned to farming. The farmers settled along streams and found the land very productive. As more and more people came some turned to lumbering, for many slopes of the mountains were covered with trees. Others turned to fishing, especially in the northern part, where there were many salmon. Thus the entire region was laid out into farms and cities and the people made a living in many different ways.

UNIT TWO  INTRODUCING THE NEW ENGLAND STATES 

More than three hundred years ago certain people called Pilgrims and Puritans came from Europe and settled along what is now our New England coast. 

Scattered tribes of Indians were the only people who lived in New England at that time. The Indians depended mostly on hunting and fishing for food, but a few planted corn in the open places in the woods. As soon as the Pilgrims and 

Puritans landed, they began to build homes for their families. They were helped by the fact that much of the near-by land was covered with forests. 

From these forests they obtained logs and built cabins. They also secured wood from the forests for fuel. 

Next they began to think of ways in which they could make a living. Naturally they first thought of farming and set to work to cut down trees and clear the land. In the new fields they planted various grains. These grains supplied them with bread, hominy, and mush. 

They also kept a few cattle, pigs, and sheep which they pastured in the woods. 32

INTRODUCING  

33 In the dense forests they found certain wild game, such as deer, bears, and turkeys. They also found cranberries in the lowlands and berries on the hillsides. Moreover, from the Indians they learned to raise a new crop in their fields. Corn, as you may know, was first found growing in America and had never been raised in Europe. This explains why corn is sometimes called Indian corn. It is one of the few crops the 

Indians were raising when the settlers first came. 

Farming with difficulty. From the first unit you learned that settlers everywhere had hard times in the new world. 

This was especially true for a while of the settlers in New England. In the first place, the winters in New England were longer than those where the settlers had lived in Europe. Some of the crops they had raised in Europe would not ripen in the shorter growing season here. 

Then, too, much of New England is not well suited to farming. The soil in many places is thin and the land is rough and hilly. Much of the ground is covered with rocks and boulders. 

Even some of the land along the coast, as you will see in Figure 30, is rough and hilly. Notice the large boulders that rise out of the water. 

New ways of making a living. You may think, then, of New England as a land that, for the most part, is not well suited to farming. Gradually the settlers had to learn what they could raise here. 

Some moved westward to other regions where the land was better. Others clustered about the more fertile spots where certain crops would grow very well. 

Instead of raising many crops, therefore, Courtesy Maine Development Commission 

Figure 30. A rocky coast in Maine. Rough coasts such as shown in this picture may be found along much of the New England shore line. There is no coastal plain such as you find farther south along the Atlantic Ocean. most farmers of New England came to raise only a few. Some people gave up farming for other occupations. Since the sea had an abundance of fish, a number took up fishing as a means of making a living. The great forests led others to take up lumbering as an industry. Later the discovery of large quantities of marble, granite, and slate in some parts of New England gave rise to quarrying, which is much like mining. 

Rise of manufacturing and trade. If you turn to the population map, Figure 31, you will see that many people live in New England. In fact, Figure 28

Figure 31. A population map of New England. Refer to this map as you study the New England section. Ask yourself how each new fact you learn helps to explain the distribution of the people. For one thing, you will want to know why there are so many more people in the southern part than there are in the northern part. 

Figure 32. An old mill in New England. This type of water wheel is called an overshot wheel. The water flows from above into the troughs called buckets on the wheel. 

The weight of the water causes the wheel to turn. Modern water wheels are not so simple, and they obtain much more power from the same quantity of water. 

Courtesy American Woolen Co., Boston 

Figure 33. Machinery in a New England factory.

This machinery is used for making woolen cloth. shows that it is one of the most thickly settled regions of the country. From what you know about farming, fishing, lumbering, and quarrying, would you think that such work could support so many people? Figures 29 and 33 suggest a way in which numerous people now make a living in New England. They work in factories. Have you ever seen a water wheel? Figure 32 shows a water wheel beside an old mill. It was largely because the rivers and streams of New England furnished such excellent water power that many mills and factories were built here. It is little wonder, then, that New England came to be the first great manufacturing region of the United 

States. Even today, as you will soon learn, it is still one of the greatest. 

Do you wonder what is made in all the mills in New England? Strange to say, most of the raw materials used in the mills are brought in from other places. 

Raw materials, you remember, are such things as cotton and iron from which manufactured articles are made. At first most of the raw materials used in the

INTRODUCING  

35 factories were obtained in New England itself. The manufactured articles consisted of shoes, clothing, furniture, and tools which the people needed. Later, however, many New Englanders began to make a living on the sea and became good seamen. More and more they sent out ships to other parts of the United States and even to other countries. 

These ships brought in raw materials and carried away manufactured articles. this way a great trade with other regions arose, and thus manufacturing became a great industry in New England.

In From one occupation to another. From what you have just read, it is clear that the people of New England have gradually changed their ways of making a living. What they have done has been determined by the nature of the soil, the climate, the nearness of the ocean, and many other things. All such conditions taken together are usually spoken of as environment. In other words, environment means the nature of the soil, the kind of climate, and anything else in their surroundings that affects the way people live. As you study further you will see how the people of New England have fitted their ways of working and living to their environment, the conditions round about them. Since they have done this so well, a great many people now live here and New England is a very important part of our country.

McLaughlin Aerial Surveys

Figure 36. The Connecticut Valley from the air. In the low land of the valley you see many well-kept farms, with houses, barns, and other farm buildings. Notice the higher land bordering the valley. 

If you were going to buy a farm, where would you look for it? In what part of the United States would you want your farm? Your answer would, of course, depend greatly on what you would want to raise on your farm. If you wanted to grow cotton, for instance, you would buy a farm in the Southern States. If, on the other hand, you wanted to grow potatoes, you might buy your farm in

New England. In general, there are two matters that you would have to consider in picking out your farm: first, the quality of the soil, and second, the climate. By quality of soil is meant its fitness for growing crops. Some crops grow best in river valleys and lowlands, and other crops grow best on hillsides and higher lands. When you think of climate, you think of the amount of rainfall, the length of summer and winter, how hot it is in summer, how cold it is in winter, the length of the growing season, and the direction of winds. 

A great ice sheet and what it did. 

You have learned that there is snow all the year round on the tops of high mountains. As the snow grows deeper year by year, the lower part freezes into solid ice. Finally some of it begins to slide slowly down the mountain. It may move only a few feet a year, but it moves on steadily, like a river of ice, until it reaches a region where the climate is warm enough to melt it. Such a river of ice is called a glacier. If it is wide, it is usually called an ice sheet.

Thousands of years ago a great ice sheet covered almost all of northern North 

America. Figure 37 shows you how far this ice sheet extended. Notice that it covered all of New England. The climate in all the region that was covered by the great ice sheet was very, very cold. 

Gradually, however, the climate became warmer and the ice melted. As it did so, it left in some places great ridges of stones and soil which today appear as hills. 

In other places it left great holes in the ground that became lakes and river valleys. Look at the map on page 37 and notice how many lakes there are in New England. The beds of many lakes were dug by the ice sheet. In some parts of the United States the ice sheet left a thick layer of fertile soil, but in parts of New England it scraped off the soil and left a rough, stony surface. Rocks and boulders were carried from lands farther to the north. Some of these rocks were scratched deeply by other rocks as they were carried along. In fact, the scratches on the rocks help to show which way the glacier moved.

Special crops raised. From what has just been said, then, you may think of much of New England as a land that is rough and rugged with a soil that is thin and stony. Many of the rocks have been removed from the land. The freezing in winter, however, brings more to the surface each year. Indeed, from the way the stones come through the soil in some places you might think that they grow like toadstools or mushrooms. Of course, these rocks must be picked up before the land can be easily plowed again. 

You must not think, however, that there is no good farming land in New 

Figure 37. How the ice sheet covered North America.

The shaded part shows the area once covered by ice. 

The arrows show the directions in which the ice moved. 

England, for there is. Some regions, such as river valleys like the one shown in 

Figure 36, are very fertile. Then, too, farming is carried on to some extent all over New England, even on the hilly and rocky lands. The farmer, however, tries to raise only the kinds of crops that will grow on his land. Thus, if he lives in 

Maine he may try to grow potatoes or sweet corn, and if he lives in Connecticut he may try to raise tobacco. As you have already learned, the raising of certain crops in this way is known as specialized farming.

Dairy farming. The soil and the cool, moist climate of New England are not well suited to raising corn, wheat, or other grains. Most of the corn is cut green for canning or as feed for farm animals.

Figure 38. Dairy cows in New England. The map shows that dairying is an important industry in every state in New England. This is partly because much of the land is too poor or too rough for other kinds of farming. 

Grass can be grown on the hills for pasturing cattle, and hay is produced as part of the winter feed. For these reasons many farmers have turned to dairying. 

Milk from their farms is hauled each day to towns where it is loaded on fast trains that carry it to the cities. Thus, because the cities use large quantities of milk and because the farms in the dairy regions are suited to raising cattle, many people make a living by this kind of farming. 

The dots in Figure 38 show where dairying is an important industry. 

Raising corn. Since the growing season in New England is not long enough for corn to ripen well, it must be cut while it is still green. Much of it is used as feed for dairy cows. Often the green stalks and ears are chopped up by a machine and put into a tall, round building called a silo. The chopped corn is usually spoken of as silage or ensilage and makes good winter feed. Figure 39 is a picture of a silo being filled. 

Maine and a few other states raise large quantities of sweet corn. This corn, too, is cut while green. Then it is canned and shipped to various parts of the country. 

Raising vegetables for city markets.

When the farmers of New England found that they could not make a good living by raising corn and other grains, some of them began to grow vegetables, such as tomatoes, sweet corn, onions, spinach, cabbage, lettuce, and potatoes. These products are needed by the people of towns and cities. Some vegetables are Figure 39. Filling a silo. The tall, round building is the silo. The machine at the foot of the silo is cutting the cornstalks into small pieces. These pieces are blown up through the long pipe into the top of the silo.

sold fresh just as they are gathered from the farms. Others are taken to canning factories and canned before they are sold. Raising vegetables for the city markets is a form of specialized farming. 

Such work is usually very profitable and provides work for many people. Lands used for the special purpose of raising vegetables are called truck farms. 

Potatoes in Maine. In Aroostook 

County, in the northeastern part of Maine, more potatoes are raised than in any other region of the same size in the country. Here sandy soils and the cool, moist climate are favorable for growing potatoes. The potatoes here are usually planted in May and dug in September. 

This growing season, while too short for corn, is long enough for potatoes. Furthermore, the land is fairly level, so that machinery can be used for planting and digging. Look at the map at the right to see where most of the potatoes are grown. At the right in Figure 41 you will see a potato digger at work. 

Most of the potatoes raised in Maine are shipped to the cities of New England, where they are sold for food. Some, however, are shipped as far as Florida and other Southern States, where they are used as seed potatoes. There they are planted in the fall and are ready to be harvested and shipped to northern markets in late winter or early spring. 

Growing apples. You have learned that New England farmers can raise only certain crops because of a short growing season. Hay and corn, for instance, are 

Figure 40. A vegetable map of New England. The dots in northern Maine show where potatoes are raised. Figure 41. Harvesting the potato crop in Aroostook County, Maine. 

The machine at the right is a potato digger. It digs the potatoes and leaves them on top of the ground to be picked up by the men. grown for dairy feed. Another important crop is apples. Since it is not necessary to cultivate apple trees with machinery, they may be grown on hilly land such as that shown in Figure 42.

Figure 42. Apple blossom time in New Hampshire. If you were to travel through New England in the spring, you would see thousands of trees like the one shown here. Everywhere the hills would be dotted with pink and white. 

The cool, moist climate of New England is well suited to the growth of these trees. Then, too, an early frost does not injure apples as it does many other fruits or plants. By raising apples, therefore, the farmers make good use of the hilly land and produce a valuable crop. Look at Figure 43 and read the legend to see where apples grow in New England. 

Cranberries in Massachusetts. When the Pilgrims first came to America, they settled in a region in the southeastern part of Massachusetts near Cape Cod. 

Find Cape Cod on the map. Since these early settlers had to depend on the land for food, shelter, and clothing, it was but natural that they should use the fruits and other products they found there. Cranberries grew in the marshes near by and ripened in early September. 

Perhaps they were a part of the first 

Thanksgiving dinner. 

Cranberries are very popular. More than half of all the cranberries produced in the United States are raised near the region where the Pilgrims first found them growing. New Jersey and Wisconsin are the only other states that produce large quantities.

Century Photos 

Figure 44. Cranberry pickers. These pickers are gathering the berries by hand. Notice the wide stretch of flat country on which the cranberries grow. 

Figure 43. A fruit map of New England. Apples are the leading fruit, but in the southern part of the section large quantities of peaches and pears are raised. 

Visiting a cranberry bog. Figure 44 is a picture of a cranberry bog. If you were to visit this section, you would learn some interesting things about cranberries. You would find them growing in wet, swampy lands that are called bogs. The soil on which the berries are grown is very sandy. At certain seasons of the year you would find the farmers putting a fresh layer of sand over the bogs. They do this in order to kill the insects, to help keep down the weeds, and to keep the soil damp and cool. 

Cranberries thrive best in cool, sandy, marshy soil. 

The cranberry harvest. Cranberries ripen in September. Before the picking season the farmers drain the marshes by means of ditches so that the land will be

Figure 46. A tobacco map of New England. The river that flows through the tobacco region shown by the group of dots is the Connecticut. crop. Figure 46 just above shows two states in which tobacco is grown. In these states the soils and the climate are favorable for growing tobacco with large, thin leaves. Figure 47 is a picture taken in a tobacco region in the Connecticut Valley. Notice the cloth that is used to shade the growing tobacco. The leaves of tobacco grown under cloth are lighter in color than those of tobacco grown in the open. 

Raising tobacco is hard work. The farmers of the Connecticut Valley are able to get good prices for their tobacco, but it is hard to raise. First the tobacco seeds are planted in beds in the spring. 

After the plants have made a little growth, they are taken up and set out in 

Courtesy Franklin County, Massachusetts,

Extension Service 

Figure 47. A tobacco tent in the Connecticut Valley.

Fifty acres of tobacco are growing under the cloth. the fields. Since three or four thousand plants are needed for each acre of ground, you can imagine that it takes much work to get the tobacco started. Tobacco plants must be carefully cultivated. All during the summer, workers must hoe or plow in the fields. Then, too, they must go through the fields and pull off all the small leaves that begin to grow on the lower part of the stalks. They must also go through and pick off tobacco worms that would eat the leaves.

Tobacco is cut in the fall. The plants, with their large, green leaves, are cut off near the ground and hauled to long wooden buildings called tobacco barns. 

Here the tobacco is hung upside down to cure. Later in the year it is shipped to large warehouses and from there to factories in various cities and towns. 

From general to specialized farming. 

You see now that, in spite of the cold climate and the rough, stony surface of most of New England, many people are able to make a living by farming. This is possible because the people have changed from general farming to specialized farming. In most places the farmers cannot raise on their own land all the things they need. They can,

THE FISHING INDUSTRY IN NEW ENGLAND 

45 however, raise hay for dairy cattle and thus produce milk and butter to sell in near-by cities. Or they can raise potatoes or other special crops for which their particular farms are well fitted. 

In other words, the New England farmers have adjusted themselves to the conditions of the region in which they live. 

They have made the best use of the climate, the soil, the hills, and other things in their environment as they have found them. It is very important for people to be able to make such adjustments in order to make a living. A matching exercise

THE FISHING INDUSTRY IN NEW ENGLAND 

As the early settlers of New England looked for better ways of making a living, some of them turned to fishing. They did this because in the near-by sea there were many fish, in the forests there was timber for boats, and along the shore there were many sheltered places for protection from storms. The settlers therefore built boats and went fishing. They 

H. Armstrong Roberts

Figure 48. A fisherman removing a fish from his net. 

As you can see, the net has been stretched near the shore. 

Notice the cork floats at the top of the net. caught cod, halibut, mackerel, and herring. They found lobsters, clams, and oysters in the waters near the shore. 

Fishing along the shore. Fishing is still carried on in New England waters. 

From nearly every village along the coast fishermen go out in small rowboats or motorboats to catch fish a few miles from the shore. This kind of work is called inshore fishing or simply shore fishing. 

Mackerel are caught in this way. They swim on the surface of the ocean in schools, which means that large numbers of them swim together. Fishermen catch the mackerel in nets, or seines, which they drop into the ocean and draw around the schools. Figure 48 is a picture of a fisherman at work. 

Small fish called herring swim in great schools in the cool waters along the

Publishers Photo Service 

Figure 49. A lobster fisherman. This fisherman has pulled up his trap and is taking out a fine large lobster. 

Like mackerel, they shores of Maine. are caught in nets. Most of them are dried and smoked, or canned in oil. 

Large quantities of herring are prepared for market at Eastport, Maine. Find 

Eastport on the map. 

A trip on a lobster boat. As you no doubt know, fishermen on the sea catch a number of animals besides fish which are used as food. Clams, oysters, and lobsters are found in many places in the shallow waters near the coast. If you were to make a trip with a lobster fisherman, you would start about two or three o'clock in the morning. There might be a dense fog when you started out in the little motor boat. The lantern on the boat would give so little light that you would wonder how the lobster fisherman could know where he was going. Soon you would leave the quiet water in the little cove or harbor and meet the roll of the ocean. Doubtless you would think that the little boat would turn over, but the fisherman would tell you that there was no danger. Occasionally you would hear the sound of other boats that you could not see through the fog.

After a time the fisherman would stop to raise a lobster trap, as shown in Figure 

49. Such a trap is made of wood. Iron or stones are fastened to the trap so that it will sink to the bottom of the shallow water, and a piece of fish is put inside. 

When lobsters crawl into the trap to get the fish, they are unable to get out. 

After the lobster fisherman had emptied the lobsters from a trap into his boat, he would put in new bait and go on to another trap. When the bait was gone and the front of the boat was full of squirming lobsters, the fisherman would start toward the shore. Through the fog you would dimly see a cliff rise from the mainland, and would be surprised to find how near the shore you had been all the time. 

If you asked the fisherman how many lobsters he had caught, he might say about 150 pounds, which is the usual catch for a day. When you reached the shore, you would see the fisherman cook and clean some of his lobsters. 

He might keep some of the best ones alive in a tank of fresh sea water, for some people prefer to buy live lobsters.

Large quantities, however, are packed in cans for markets all over the country. 

Fishing on the banks. The shore fishermen supply only a small part of the fish necessary for our present-day needs. 

Other fishermen go many miles out from shore to places called banks where fish are more abundant. From Newfoundland southward there are places where the water is very shallow. Since there are many schools of small fish here, larger fish, such as cod, halibut, and

How the fishing is done. Usually a crew of about fifteen men goes on one schooner, or fishing boat. The schooner must be large enough to carry sufficient food and supplies for the long trip and to carry back a large cargo of fish. 

When the schooner reaches the fishing grounds, small boats, called dories, which are carried on the deck, are let down to the water. In these small boats the fishermen fish with trawls. A trawl is a long line, hundreds of feet long. To this are fastened many shorter lines about three or four feet apart. On each of the

WORK IN NEW ENGLAND FORESTS 

New England was once thickly wooded. 

Most of the early forests, however, have been cut down, especially where the land could be made into farms. Some areas are covered with forests of second-growth timber. By second-growth is meant trees that have grown again where the land was once cleared. In other places certain companies that use lumber in manufacturing have planted large areas of trees. 

Some forested lands in New England are owned by the United States and are known as national forests. The government takes good care of its forests by planting trees, by clearing out dead timber, and by guarding against fire. Can you suggest why the government is so much interested in forests? 

Lumbering in New England. Figure 

53 is a lumbering scene in New England. 

Lumbering does not require a large number of men. Usually only a few people live in regions where lumbering is carried on. Probably the lumbermen you see in the picture are farmers who have gone to work in the woods during the winter.

Winter is the best time for logging in northern New England. Deep snows cover the rocky lands and swamps. 

Most lumbermen therefore use large sleds for hauling the logs over roads cut through the woods, as shown in Figures 

53 and 54. The logs are hauled to streams and left in piles along the banks. 

When the ice melts in the spring, they are rolled into the water and floated down to sawmills.

Figure 55 shows where forests are found in the United States. The New 

England States, as you will see, are in the Northern Forest region. Most of the trees in this region are hemlock, poplar, pine, fir, and spruce. Figure 56 shows one of the pine forests. More trees are cut in Maine than in any other New England state, although much lumbering is also done in New Hampshire and 

Vermont. 

Using wood for making paper. The first paper was made by the Egyptians thousands of years ago. This paper was made by splitting the stalks of a grasslike plant that grew along the Nile, a great river in Africa. Later, for a long time rags were the only material used for making paper. Today some of the highest grades of writing paper are still made from rags. Practically all of the paper used for books, newspapers, and magazines, however, is made from wood. 

The making of paper is one of the leading industries in New England. 

Making maple sugar and sirup. The

New England States produce a great deal of maple sirup and maple sugar. In the spring the juice, or sap as it is called, begins to move up through the trunks of the sugar maple trees. This sap looks

WORK IN NEW ENGLAND QUARRIES 

51 like water but contains much sugar. 

The men tap the trees by drilling small holes in the trunks and putting small tubes in the holes. The sap flows slowly out of these holes through the tubes into buckets hung on the sides of the trees. 

Figure 57 shows you how the trees look after they have been tapped. The buckets of sap are carried to a building called the sugar house, where the sap is boiled a long time. Gradually the water steams off, leaving a thick sticky liquid which is the maple sirup that is so popular all over the country. If the sirup is boiled still longer and allowed to cool, it hardens and turns into maple sugar. 

In regions where the sugar maple grows, the making of sugar products is a valuable industry. A good tree will produce about enough sap each spring for a gallon of sirup. Vermont produces more maple sirup and sugar than any other state in the country. Possibly your grocer sells sirup that was made from the sap of maple trees in Vermont. 

Can you explain 1. Why the population in lumbering regions is usually sparse? 

2. Why lumbering is carried on in the winter? 

3. How sap is taken from trees?

In fact, some of them, such as marble, granite, and slate, are very valuable.
