MANUFACTURING IN NEW ENGLAND 

In the early part of your study of New England you learned that it is not only the smallest section in the United States, but also one of the most thickly settled. 

Moreover, you learned that the rocky, thin soil, hilly land, and short growing season kept many people from farming and led them to turn to various other kinds of work. Today you will find most of the people living in towns and cities, and more than half of them working in factories. Look at Figure 59 to see where most of the factories in New 

England are found. You

Raw materials from other places. readily see that the products of New England, such as stone from the quarries, lumber from the forests, and fish from the sea, do not supply all the raw materials needed by the factories. New 

England, for instance, makes more than half the jewelry in the United States, but it has no gold or silver mines. 

Its factories produce large quantities of machinery, tools, hardware, and many metal articles, but it has no iron mines. 

It is especially known for its textile industries, but not a pound of cotton is raised within the section. It manufactures more than half of all the woolen goods in the country, but produces only a small quantity of wool. 

Conditions that favor manufacturing.

Doubtless you wonder why it is possible

MANUFACTURING IN NEW ENGLAND 

53 for so many people in New England to make a living by manufacturing, when the raw materials must be brought in from other places. In order to answer this question, you must know that there are other conditions besides a supply of raw materials that favor manufacturing. 

New England is fortunate in having many of these conditions. There are falls in the rivers that furnish water power for turning the wheels of factories. There are also good harbors and deep rivers.

These make it possible for ships to bring in raw materials and to carry manufactured articles away. Then, too, the development of railroads has also helped the factories to grow.

How manufacturing began. The early settlers in New England had to make nearly all of their own supplies. Trade with Europe was almost impossible, for it took nearly three months for a good ship to cross the ocean. Thus the people here began to make their own clothing, furniture, and many other things. Figure 60 will help you to see some of the ways in which the early New Englanders supplied their needs. Furthermore, some of the early settlers were already trained in certain trades, such as making clothes, shoes, and metal goods. These people began to make goods, not only for themselves, but for other people as well. In this way manufacturing as an industry was started. Since manufacturing became more profitable in some places than farming, other people gradually turned to this means of making their living. 

Factory workers from England began to come to America when they learned that they could find plenty of work here. 

Thus the factories grew very rapidly, especially near waterfalls and harbors. 

Perhaps it will be interesting to make a list of reasons why manufacturing got such an early start in New England. 

Before long the factories of New England were making more than enough for the colonists. Manufactured goods, such as shoes, clothing, hardware, clocks, and other things, were being sold to other parts of the world. Thus the cities on good harbors became important shipping centers. You can see, then, just how the conditions which favored manufacturing and shipping caused many large cities to develop. Later, of course, railroads also helped the cities to grow. Figure 60. How people worked in the early days in New England. The woman at the left is spinning yarn from wool. The man next to her is a shoemaker. The woman at the right is weaving cloth.

THE MANUFACTURE OF TEXTILES 

Making cotton goods. New England mills make large quantities of cotton and woolen cloth. Using raw cotton shipped from the South, some mills weave such finished goods as calico, muslin, and gingham. Other mills make sheets and cotton blankets, and still others, yarn and thread. Figure 61 is a picture taken inside a cotton mill. 

Lowell and Lawrence, Massachusetts, and Manchester and Concord, New

Hampshire, have many cotton mills. 

Look at the map to find the river on which each of these cities is built. 

There are waterfalls in the river at each of the cities. Does this fact suggest any reason why the cities have grown up as manufacturing centers? At Lawrence the river falls from a ledge forty feet high. 

A waterfall of this height has great power. Why, then, is Lawrence an especially good location for factories? On the same map find Pawtucket, Rhode Island, another important cotton-manufacturing center. This city has grown up at a waterfall on the Blackstone River. Other cotton-manufacturing cities that have grown up at waterfalls are Nashua in New Hampshire and Holyoke in Massachusetts. Figure 62 shows a mill where water is used for power.

A city near waterfalls usually has cheap power for its factories. Sometimes, however, the water power is not regular throughout the year. Do you see, for example, what might happen in the winter when a stream freezes, or in the summer when it becomes smaller?

In Massachusetts find Fall River and New Bedford on the map. These two cities are the most important cotton manufacturing centers of New England.

THE MAKING OF SHOES 

On 

Perhaps you have taken your shoes to a shoemaker or cobbler for repairs. You would think it strange to ask the cobbler to make you an entire pair of shoes. In the early days, however, the cobbler did not even have a shop, but went from house to house repairing and making shoes. The leather he used was made from the hides of animals raised on the 

New England farms. 

Preparing the leather. In order to make the leather, it was necessary first to tan the hides. To do this the hair was removed and the hides were soaked in a liquid containing tannic acid. This acid is found in the bark of certain trees, as the hemlock and oak. Since these trees grew in New England, it naturally became a good place for tanning hides. 

At first most of the tanneries were built near the forests. Later, however, Worcester, Massachusetts, and the area around Boston became the most important centers for tanning hides. Tanneries no longer depend on the bark of the hemlock and oak to supply the tannic acid. Large quantities of this acid are now obtained from South America.

Neither do the tanneries depend on the hides of animals raised on New England farms. Most hides now come from other parts of the United States and even from other countries. 

Shoes made in factories. Most shoes today are made in factories. The work is so divided that a hundred people may have worked on the shoes you are wearing. By the use of machinery to cut

Century Photos 

Figure 64. Work in a shoe factory. Each man works at a machine that does just one thing to the shoes. They are then passed on to the next man. and sew the leather quickly, thousands of pairs of shoes are made in a single day. 

In Figure 64 you will see a view inside. a shoe factory. 

Shoe factories are found in many New

England towns, but the most important ones are at Brockton, Lynn, Haverhill, and Boston, Massachusetts. Find all these cities on the map. What does the map tell you about their location? 

In the early days it was necessary to build tanneries and shoe factories near hemlock and oak forests, which supplied tannic acid, and at waterfalls for power.

Today, since tannic acid can be shipped from other places, and coal can easily be obtained to supply steam power, the great shoe cities are usually situated where there are good harbors. Here they can use both ships and railroads in getting raw materials and in sending finished shoes to market.

THE MAKING OF PAPER 

You have already learned that certain kinds of wood are used in making paper. 

Doubtless you wonder how a hard substance like wood can be made into thin sheets of paper. The process is easy to understand, for wood is made of threadlike fibers. In making paper the fibers of the wood must be torn apart and then pressed back together again. If you will examine the torn edge of a sheet of paper with a magnifying glass, you may be able to see some of the fibers.

In the pulp mill. The first step in making paper is to remove the bark from the logs and to cut them into a number of pieces, as shown in Figure 65. The wood is next ground up into a mass of loosened fibers called pulp or it is treated with chemicals that loosen the fibers and form pulp. The pulp is mixed with water, as shown in Figure 66, then poured on wire screens through which the water drains off, leaving layers of fibers. Finally, the layers are run through hot rollers to dry and press them into thin sheets of paper, as shown in Figure 67. 

The location of pulp mills. Paper and pulp mills are a common sight near the forested lands of New England. These very mills may have made the paper that is in this book or in the newspapers and magazines that you read. Most of the pulp mills are in Maine, New Hampshire, and Vermont, where spruce trees are most plentiful. There are also many paper mills in Massachusetts, Connecticut, and Rhode Island.

Paper mills need power for running the machinery and great quantities of water to wash the wood and soften the pulp. 

Find the cities of Millinocket, Rumford, Photo by J. F. Clark, Courtesy U. S. Forest Service

Hampshire. On what river is each city found? Why would you expect to find the leading centers for the manufacture of paper on large streams? Massachusetts, too, has some of the largest paper mills in the United States. On the map find Holyoke, an important paper city. 

On what river is it?

Three things, then, affect the location. of pulp and paper mills. They must be near a good supply of lumber, they must be able to get power, and they must have a good supply of water.

SEAPORTS OF NEW ENGLAND 

As you studied about manufacturing in New England, you found the names of many cities mentioned. In cities a large number of people live close together. 

Manufacturing, then, explains one great reason why the population is dense in southern New England. Not all the people in manufacturing cities, however, work in factories. Many make a living by doing other kinds of work. A city must have stores and storekeepers. It must have waterworks and electric light plants to supply water and light for homes. It must have people to put up houses and other buildings. It also needs teachers, doctors, lawyers, and ministers. In One of the chief ways in which the people of a manufacturing city make a living is by shipping and carrying on trade. Raw materials must be brought to the factories and manufactured articles must be sent away. Some of the people, therefore, work on railroads. cities along the ocean or on large rivers and lakes, many people work on ships. 

Cities on the ocean that use ships to carry on trade are known as seaports. 

Cities on lakes that use ships to carry on trade are known as lake ports. What, then, would you call cities on rivers that use ships to carry on trade? 

There are two very good reasons why

New England carries on a large trade. 

First, since it has so many factories, large quantities of raw materials must be brought in and manufactured articles must be carried away. The second reason is that New England lies along the ocean where it may be easily visited by ships. As you study further you will find it has a large number of seaports. 

THE STORY OF BOSTON 

If you look at the map on page 37, you will see that Boston is well situated for carrying on trade with all of the New England States and with many other states west and south. It is also well situated for carrying on trade with Europe and other parts of the world.

A ride into the harbor. Have you ever been aboard a large ship that sails on the ocean? Suppose you board one just before it enters Boston Harbor. As you know, a harbor is a protected place where ships may come up to the land. 

The ship is loaded with raw cotton from the southern part of our country. The captain explains that the ship is engaged in coastwise trade. This means that the vessel does not go to foreign ports, but trades with cities up and down the coast. 

As the vessel enters Boston harbor, you notice that Boston Bay is dotted with many islands, as shown in Figure 

69. These islands break the high waves

BAY Figure 69. A relief map of the region around Boston. The little squares on the map show where blocks of buildings are found. The lines with short lines drawn across them are railroads. The broken line is the boundary of the city. of the ocean, and the water in the bay is smooth and quiet. In the harbor you see several other ships. Just ahead is a large ship which is arriving from Europe. 

The water in the harbor is deep enough even for the largest ships. Then, too, the harbor is large and ocean liners find plenty of room in which to turn around. A large vessel must travel more than a quarter of a mile to turn. 

Landing at the pier. As you come close to shore you notice many piers pointing out into the water somewhat like the teeth of a comb. Piers are large platforms along which ships may be tied. Often these platforms are called wharves. The waterway between two piers is called a dock. Your ship comes up to one of the piers, and the sailors throw out long, heavy ropes which are used to hold the vessel closely against the pier. A long, narrow bridge, called a gangplank, is extended from the ship to the pier. You walk down the gang-

SEAPORTS OF NEW ENGLAND 

61 plank to the pier, which is covered with boxes and barrels ready to be shipped. 

After landing you visit other piers along the water front and watch the men loading and unloading cargoes. Some of the cargoes are carried by coastwise steamers like your own. 

Others are carried by large ocean vessels. All the goods that come into the United States from other countries are called imports. 

All the goods that are carried away from the United States to other countries are called exports. An especially interesting pier is the fish pier, a picture of which is shown in Figure 70. 

Why Boston is called a hub city. 

Along the wharves you find many great warehouses or storehouses. Railroad tracks also run out onto the wharves. 

Thus products may be unloaded directly from freight cars into the ships or may be stored ready to load into the ships. In the same way goods coming in on the ships may be unloaded directly into the freight cars or stored in the warehouses. 

In Figure 69 notice that railroads extend from Boston like the spokes of a wheel. 

For this reason Boston, like a few other cities, is often called a hub city. 

A world market. Because Boston is a great shipping center it has become a world market for certain products. Millions of dollars worth of wool are often stored in the warehouses along the wharves. Near by are offices of dozens of business houses that are engaged in buying and selling wool. Buyers come here from all over the United States and from many foreign countries to buy raw wool to be used in their factories. In fact, Boston is the largest wool market in the United States. It is also the leading market place for leather. Many people who are engaged in buying and selling raw hides, tanned leather, and leather goods of every description live in or near Boston.

In the central business district, therefore, are many large banks, fine hotels, and retail stores. There are also many wholesale houses that sell goods in large quantities to stores in Boston and throughout New England.

A manufacturing center. Boston has numerous factories of her own near the wharves or along railroad terminals.

The end of a railroad line, with all its tracks and buildings, is called a terminal. 

Cloth woven in the textile mills in surrounding towns is shipped back to Boston and made into men's and women's clothing. This is an important industry. 

There are also many other factories, some of which make boots, shoes, and tanned leather in large quantities. Others prepare for market cocoa, coffee, sugar, and various other foodstuffs that have been brought in by ship.

The state capital. Boston is the capital of Massachusetts. Here you find the state capitol, or state house, where the people who make laws and help with the government have their offices. Generally the capital of a state is centrally located where it can be reached easily from all parts of the state. In some cases, however, the capital is found in the most thickly settled part. In the lower center part of the map on page 37 find the sign that stands for state capitals and notice how it is used for the city of Boston. Look at the map again and find the capital city of each of the other 

New England States.

Neighbors of Boston. Near every large city there are a number of smaller cities and towns that help the city carry on its work. Such neighboring cities and towns are often called suburbs. 

Look again at Figure 69 and notice the number of cities that have grown up around Boston. Some of the people who live in these cities work in Boston.

They go in each morning on trains, street cars, or buses, or ride in automobiles. 

Most of the suburbs, however, have important industries of their own. Many of them have factories that make hardware and other metal goods. Several of them make clothing, including shoes. 

Several have chemical factories and some have factories that use wood as raw material, such as furniture and box factories. Among Boston's more important neighbors are Cambridge, Somerville, Chelsea, and Woburn. Waltham and

Lynn, about which you have already learned, are also near by. All of these suburbs help to make Boston the leading industrial center in New England. Look at Figure 69 to see where the various suburbs of Boston are situated.

OTHER NEW ENGLAND SEAPORTS The largest city of Maine. Find Port land on the map. You have already learned that Portland is an important fishing port, but it is also the largest city in Maine. Like Boston, it is situated on a bay and has a good harbor. If you were to visit the harbor, you would find it a very busy place, for Portland has a large trade. Ships sail from Portland to other ports in the United States and even to other countries. These ships carry large quantities of materials that are manufactured in Portland and near-by cities. Among these manufactured products are paper, tents and awnings, locomotives, and chemicals. Sometimes in winter when some of the ports to the north in Canada are frozen, grain is brought to Portland by railroad and loaded on ships. 

Portland, too, is a favorite city for tourists. In summer its hotels are filled with people who have come to enjoy the cool breezes from over the Atlantic 

Ocean. Some people spend all their vacations here, but others stop only on their way to other places in Maine. 

Courtesy Boston Chamber of Commerce 

Figure 70. The fish pier in Boston Harbor. About a tenth of the fish caught by United States fishermen are brought to Boston. They are prepared for market in buildings such as shown in this picture. In fact, caring for tourists is becoming a leading industry, not only in Portland, but in all of Maine as well.

A large variety of ports and harbors. You have just found that the harbors of 

Boston and Portland are bays. Other ports on bays are Fall River in Massachusetts and Providence in

Rhode Island. New Haven and Stamford in Connecticut are on small bays off Long

PLAY AND TRAVEL IN NEW ENGLAND 

63 Island Sound. Look at the map and find these cities and bays.

Sometimes rivers are deep enough near their mouths for large vessels to enter, and such river mouths make good harbors. There are several such harbors in New England. Among them are New 

Bedford in Massachusetts, Portsmouth in New Hampshire, and Bridgeport in 

Connecticut. Find all these cities and rivers on the map. Now find Bangor in 

Maine. Bangor is a new kind of port.

It is many miles from the ocean, but steamships can go up the river as far as 

Bangor. They can go no farther, however, and so Bangor is said to be at the head of navigation.

Besides the ports that have been mentioned, there are many smaller harbors along the coast. Most of them are the home ports for fishing vessels. Small ships, however, that make regular trips along the coast stop at several of the more important ones.

PLAY AND TRAVEL IN NEW ENGLAND 

You have learned many ways in which the people of New England make use of the land and water in farming, fishing, manufacturing, and other occupations. 

Several places in New England are of interest, however, not because of any industry, but for quite different reasons. 

If you were to travel here, you would enjoy the views of mountains, valleys, streams, and the ocean. Many people from other parts of the country like to spend their vacations here. In the summer they enjoy the mountains where they may climb the wooded slopes or fish for trout in the streams and lakes. 

Others go to the seashore for boating and bathing. Newport, Rhode Island, and a number of places along the coast of Massachusetts and Maine are especially famous as seashore resorts. During the winter, too, people skate on the frozen lakes and rivers or coast down the snowcovered hills on skis or sleds.

Many people also visit New England each year because of its places of interest. 

It is, as you know, one of the oldest parts of the country. It was in New England, not far from Boston, that the Pilgrims landed. Likewise a number of other people came over from Europe to settle in New England. Here they built homes and towns and found ways of making a living. Later several people from New 

England were leaders in helping to set up the government of our country. 

If you were to visit New England today, you would find old houses which show how people lived long ago. You would also find old flour mills and factories with their old-fashioned water wheels for turning machinery. Then, too, you would see the home of Longfellow and other famous writers. In 

Cambridge, a city near Boston, you would see Harvard University, one of the oldest schools of the country. In 

New Haven you would find Yale University, which is almost as old.

4. You have now made a product chart which tells you quickly what each state produces or in which state each product is important. What does the chart tell you about the kinds of work the people of each state do for a living?

A trip in an airplane. How would you like to take a trip in an airplane? This, as you know, will make it possible to travel far and see many things you could not see if you were to travel by train or automobile. Suppose as you leave New England you board a plane at Boston for Buffalo, New York. 66

INTRODUCING  

67 

Entering New York State. As you leave Boston your plane flies southwest toward Springfield, Massachusetts. After a short stop at Springfield you proceed westward and soon pass over the boundary between Massachusetts and New 

York. Within a few minutes the Hudson

River comes into view. Railroads run along the bank on each side of the river. 

These railroads and the large boats on the river tell you that you are crossing an important trade route. Your plane drops down at Albany, the capital of 

New York. The great factory buildings all about show that many people here make a living by manufacturing.

Through the Mohawk Valley. Soon after leaving Albany you discover that you are following a river. This river is the Mohawk, from which the famous Mohawk Valley gets its name. Level farm land stretches out on either side of the river. To the north and south you see mountains, rugged and covered with trees. You see many cows pasturing in the fields. On almost every farm stand huge dairy barns. Along the river is a canal or waterway that has been dug so that small boats may go up and down the valley. Railroads and highways also use the valley as a route from east to west. 

On to Buffalo. After a time your plane passes over Utica and Syracuse, cities of central New York. A little west of Syracuse you notice that you are flying over a great lowland plain. This is a part of the plain or level land that borders Lake Ontario, one of the Great Lakes. Far to the north you catch a gleam of the blue waters of this lake. 

Below you are orchards, vineyards, and truck farms. Trees, grapevines, and vegetable gardens divide the land into patches of varying shades of green. Soon you come to Rochester. The number of factories here tells you that this is an important industrial city. 

Leaving Rochester, your plane heads southwestward over a region of truck farms and dairy farms. As you approach 

Buffalo you see tall elevators for storing wheat, flour mills, stockyards, and meatpacking plants. Here, too, you catch your first sight of lake freighters on Lake Erie. 

Erie. This view of Buffalo from the sky shows you that it is a very busy place. 

Another interesting trip. So far you have traveled in only one of the Middle Atlantic States, but you will now visit others. Suppose on your next trip that you take a plane at La Guardia Field, New York, and travel to Washington,

D. C., the capital of the country. This airport is very large, and you see many planes when you arrive for the take-off. 

Finally, however, your plane pulls up before the gate, and you climb on board. 

A region of truck farms. A few miles out of Newark the plane begins to pass over open country. Look at the map on pages 70-71 and notice the low strip of land that extends all the way along the coast from New York City southward. 

This strip of land is known as the Atlantic 

Coastal Plain. Much of the land on the northern part of the plain is laid out in small truck farms. The owners of these farms make a living by raising vegetables, such as tomatoes and lettuce.

From Trenton to Baltimore. The first large city over which you pass after leaving New York is Trenton, New Jersey. 

As you approach the city you see clusters of jug-shaped ovens or kilns in which

pottery is made. Near the center of the city you see the dome of the state capitol building. 

Before long you pass over Camden, another city in New Jersey. Looking westward across the Delaware River, you see Philadelphia, which contains many square miles of buildings and factories. You also see the outlines of a large and busy harbor.

Your plane now follows the Delaware 

River for a short distance to Wilmington, a large city in the northern part of the state of Delaware. Continuing southwestward, you come to Baltimore, Maryland, a large and beautiful city on 

Chesapeake Bay. 

Everywhere the Atlantic Coastal Plain over which you have been flying is low and level. Everywhere, too, there are well-cultivated truck farms. 

Landing in Washington, the capital city. A few minutes after you leave

Baltimore, a tall, white structure comes into view. This structure is the Washington Monument, a white marble shaft that reaches 550 feet into the sky. Next the dome of the Capitol comes into view. 

This is the building in which the laws of the United States are made. As you can see, Washington, like Philadelphia, is built on a river. This river, the Potomac, is broad and beautiful, winding its way to Chesapeake Bay. Before you land, you notice that there are few factories in Washington, and that it is a city of beautiful buildings and homes. Some of the buildings are used for carrying on the work of the government. 

Across the Piedmont. Find the city of Pittsburgh on the map. If you were to travel by plane from Washington to 

Pittsburgh, you would pass over new lands that are far different from those you have just seen. It will be interesting, therefore, to travel on to Pittsburgh before ending your journey. 

Soon you leave the level stretches of the Atlantic Coastal Plain and travel over gently rolling land. The large farmhouses, the numerous well-built barns, and the larger fields and pastures tell you that the farming here is different from that found on the lower lands along the coast. The region that you are passing over now is commonly known as the 

Piedmont. Notice this region between the Atlantic coast and the Appalachian 

Mountains on the map.

Over the Appalachian Mountains. As you continue your journey the land becomes rougher and rougher. You are now passing over some of the Appalachian Mountains. The plane climbs to greater heights, for the land is made up of high ridges and valleys between the ridges. The plane rises into the clouds, and everything below looks like a great fleecy carpet. Later, after you pass out of the clouds, you are able to see, far below, ridges of mountain tops. In some of the valleys you see a number of houses, but in others there are only a few scattered dwellings, as in Figure 74.

After crossing the mountains you come to the Allegheny Plateau. A plateau is a highland region that usually is fairly level. The Allegheny Plateau, however, is rough and hilly. In the heart of this plateau your plane circles over a great city that lies at the place where two rivers meet to form a great Y. This city is Pittsburgh, the greatest manufacturing city on the Allegheny Plateau.

I. Locating the states

Atlantic Ocean? Which one borders Lake Ontario? Which two border Lake Erie? Which two have no seacoast? Which state borders New England?

York and Canada? between West Virginia and Ohio? between 

New Jersey and Pennsylvania?

2. Find the Appalachian Mountains on the map on pages 6-7. 

Now find these mountains on the map at the left and notice that they extend across the Middle Atlantic States from northeast to southwest. The higher parts of the mountains are given definite names, such as the Adirondack Mountains.

States is on this plateau?

Mohawk and Hudson rivers flow through narrow valleys in a highland region? 

2. Find the New York State Barge Canal. Give the name of the lake and the river which this canal joins.

Chesapeake Bay was formed in this manner. Name three large rivers that flow into the bay. In what two states is the bay? 

4. Find Delaware Bay. What two states does this bay separate? What river empties into it?

V. Using other maps 

1. Turn to Figure 17 and notice that the growing season is longer in some parts of the Middle Atlantic States than it is in others. 

There are two reasons why this is true: first, because of differences in latitude; second, because of differences in altitude.

THE SOFT-COAL REGION 

Look at the maps on pages 6-7 and 

70-71 and find the land just west of the

Appalachians. This particular region is known as the Allegheny Plateau. What does the map tell you about the altitude of this region? Now look at the population map, Figure 76. From this map you can see that there is a dense population in some parts of the plateau. 

Just as in the case of southern New 

England, you may wonder how so many people can make a living. This is the problem as you continue to study. 

A storehouse of fuels. Some regions, as you have already learned, are favored by water power for running machinery. 

This makes possible many kinds of factories and mills. There are some factories, however, that cannot be run by water power because they require a great quantity of heat. In the making of steel and glass, for example, very hot furnaces are needed. These furnaces

Plateau contains large deposits of soft coal as well as regions of oil and gas.

The products of the Allegheny Plateau are very valuable because they furnish fuel not only for factories on the plateau, but for those in near-by regions. 

Coal barges and coal trains. If you were to go upstream on the winding 

Monongahela, you would see along the banks many mills, factories, and coal tipples, or places where coal is loaded. 

You would also see the paddle wheels of river steamers churning the water as they push huge barges, or flat-bottomed boats, loaded with coal.

In Figure 80 find the Appalachian coal fields. These fields contain the richest deposits of bituminous, or soft, coal in the whole world. What three states share these fields? The Monongahela River is navigable for river steamers as far south as West Virginia. 

Would you expect, then, to see coal tipples all along the banks?

Some coal mines are not conveniently located near rivers. Suppose you take

Figure 79. A coal mine. The picture shows you how a coal mine would look if the earth were cut away so that you I could see it from the side. The dark bands are layers or seams of coal. A deep hole, called a shaft, is cut down from the top of the ground. Elevators in the shaft carry workmen to and from the mine. Here, too, is machinery for lifting small cars of coal to the building at the top. When the coal reaches this building, it slides down chutes into railroad cars. Then it is hauled away to the places where it will be used. 

BITUMINOUS EGION ANTHRACITE REGION 

Figure 80. A coal map of the Middle Atlantic States. 

The bituminous coal region is shown by shading. Crossshading shows where the coal is being mined. The anthracite region is shown in black. Practically all the anthracite fields are being worked. a side trip inland. Here you find many coal tipples along railroads. 

Visiting a coal mine. Would you like to visit a coal mine to see just how coal is mined? You go down to the mine on an elevator that runs up and down through a shaft, or opening, into the ground, as shown in Figure 79. 

When you leave the elevator, you find yourself in a long passageway. As you walk along the passageway, you notice other tunnels leading off. You follow one of these tunnels and come to a large place that looks like a room or cave.

The walls and ceiling are of coal and rock, for miners have formed this room by digging out the coal. The ceiling everywhere is held up by wooden posts and heavy timbers. 

At last you find some miners at work. 

They all wear small lights fastened to their caps, for the mine is very dark. 

These miners are digging coal with picks and loading it on little cars. Sometimes the coal is loosened by means of an explosive material, such as dynamite. The miners then break up the large lumps and shovel the coal into the little cars. 

These cars are then pulled on tracks to the elevator. Often they are drawn. by mules, although electric engines are sometimes used instead.

THE SOFT-COAL REGION 

75 Not all bituminous coal is mined in shaft mines. In places tunnels are dug directly into the hillsides. The coal is then hauled out through the tunnels on cars, which run directly to the tipples. 

Then it is dumped into railroad cars or, if the mine is beside a river, into barges. 

The making of coke. Most bituminous coal is shipped directly to the places where it is used for fuel. Some, however, is made into coke. For this purpose coal is put into large ovens. The coal is then heated for a long time to drive out the gases and other substances. Coal that has been baked in this way is known as coke. Manufacturers prefer coke for their furnaces because it is lighter than coal and produces great heat without much smoke.

Oil and gas wells. In various parts of the Allegheny Plateau natural gas and petroleum, or oil, are found. The oil is found in the rocks deep beneath the surface of the ground. To reach the oil, wells are drilled down into the rocks. 

The oil is then pumped from the ground much as water is pumped. Usually gas is found with oil. Often the gas forces the oil upward so that it flows out of a well over the surface of the ground. This kind of well is called a flowing well. If the oil spouts out with great force, the well is called a gusher.

Oil, or petroleum, is carried in pipes. from the wells to refineries, where it is made into gasoline, kerosene, lubricating oil, vaseline, benzine, and various other products. The first three of these products are by far the most important. 

Gas found in the earth is called natural gas. Sometimes only gas is found in a well. In such a case the well is called a gas well. The gas is carried in pipes to places where it is used. 

Pennsylvania the mother of the oil industry. The first oil well in America was drilled in western Pennsylvania in 1859. Later more wells were drilled in 

Pennsylvania, West Virginia, southern

New York, and other sections of the 

Allegheny Plateau. Today you will find many derricks in these states. Sometimes you will find them in pasture lands, near grain fields, or even in towns.

PITTSBURGH-AN IRON AND STEEL CENTER 

The first people who settled in Pittsburgh came from the east across the 

Appalachian Mountains. They were seeking new lands where they could have good farms. These early settlers could not easily cross the high mountains. By following river valleys wherever possible, however, they could either go around or pass between the mountains. Thus a few routes or trails became well known as the best ways of reaching the lands to the west. Three of the trails across the mountains met at Pittsburgh. Since so many people came over the trails and passed through Pittsburgh, it gradually became known as the gate city. You will learn later that other cities have been given the same name for similar reasons. 

Pittsburgh still a gateway city. In 

Figure 78 you are looking at a spot in the city of Pittsburgh that is called the

Point. This point, as you can see, is formed by the union of two rivers. The river that comes from the south is the Monongahela and the one that comes from the north is the Allegheny. The river formed by the union of these rivers

Ewing Galloway

Figure 81. A steel mill in operation. If you were to visit Pittsburgh or vicinity, you would find many steel mills such as this. Notice the river beside the mill. Steel mills are nearly always built on the shores of rivers or lakes. is the Ohio. Find the Point on the map on pages 70-71 and observe the direction in which each river flows. Also, while you are looking at the map, notice that many railroads meet at Pittsburgh. All these rivers and railroads show that 

Pittsburgh is still a gateway city. 

A great steel center. If you were to follow any of the three rivers, you would find mills and factories along the banks. 

Figure 81 shows a steel mill. From the numerous pipes or stacks you see in the picture, volumes of flame and black smoke pour forth day and night. Pittsburgh is the largest iron and steel center in the United States. In its mills, steel rails are made for railroads, steel beams for buildings and bridges, huge pipes for mills, steel plates for engine boilers, and so many other iron and steel products that there is not space to name them all. 

Why the center started. Iron ore, limestone, and coal in the form of coke are needed in the making of steel. On the Allegheny Plateau much limestone is found, and in some of the neighboring towns coke is made from coal. In the early days iron ore, too, was found.

Figure 82 shows where iron is found in this region. You can see, then, that 

Pittsburgh was well situated for the making of steel. Most of the iron now, however, comes from northern Minnesota and Wisconsin. From these regions it is shipped in ore boats on the Great Lakes to ports on Lake Erie, and then by rail to Pittsburgh. Trace on the map on pages 8-9 the route over which iron ore travels to Pittsburgh. 

How iron and steel are made. Miners do not take pure iron from the ground. 

They mine iron ore, which is found mixed with dust and various rock materials. 

To separate the iron from the dust and rock materials, the iron ore is melted in huge furnaces called blast furnaces.

THE SOFT-COAL REGION 

77 A blast furnace is a tall structure that looks like a huge tank or barrel. Iron ore, limestone, and coke are put into the furnace. The coke is lighted and a blast of hot air is blown into the furnace.

This makes the coke burn with so much heat that the iron and limestone are melted. The iron sinks to the bottom, where it is drained off into the ladles and is ready to be made into steel. The ladles are huge buckets. Sometimes the melted iron is run into molds instead of ladles and allowed to cool. It is then known as pig iron and is in a convenient form to be used for manufacturing purposes.

The process of separating iron from ore is called smelting.

The making of glass. Although Pittsburgh is known as an important iron and steel center, it is also noted for making glass. This is because the raw materials for making glass are found so near. 

Glass, as you may know, is made of sand, and there is much good sand in the nearby valleys. This sand must be melted in very hot furnaces, somewhat as iron ore is smelted. Plenty of coal, as you know, comes from coal fields near by.

THE ANTHRACITE REGION 

Anthracite is a kind of coal. It is very hard and burns with less smoke than bituminous coal. Since anthracite is so hard, it is commonly called hard coal, whereas bituminous coal is called soft coal. Anthracite is in great demand for use in certain kinds of factories and for heating houses. The small anthracite region shown in Figure 80 produces more hard coal than any other region in the world. 

How hard coal is mined. Anthracite, or hard coal, is mined in somewhat the same way as bituminous coal. Would you expect most of it to be dug by picks or blasted out by explosives? Why?

Because of its hardness this coal is brought from the mines in large chunks and is taken to a machine called a coal breaker. In the breaker it is broken up and sorted into various sizes.

Scranton and Wilkes-Barre. At Scranton, the largest anthracite center, you find many factories. While this city has iron works and machine shops, it is best known for its silk mills. You might think that the need for anthracite to use in manufacturing is the chief reason why silk mills are located here. This is not the case, however. There are silk mills in Scranton largely because it is easy to secure workers for the mills. 

Most of the work in the silk mills is done by women. While the men are working in the mines, their wives and daughters, sisters and mothers work in the silk mills. The two industries are shown in 

Figures 83 and 84. Moreover, Scranton is favorably located for marketing its products in New York, Philadelphia, and other cities to the east. 

Wilkes-Barre, like Scranton, is located in the heart of the anthracite region. In fact, it was here that the first hard coal was found. In Wilkes-Barre there is a variety of factories, such as machine shops and mills for making lace, wire, and handles for various kinds of tools.

Figure 84. A silk mill in Scranton. Here silk thread is being wound on spools. The raw material for the manufacture of silk thread is imported to the United States from countries across the Pacific.

FALLS AND CITIES 

South and west of New York City, along the Atlantic Ocean, find on the map the region that is shown in dark green. 

What does the color tell you about the altitude of this region? What is the region called? Notice that several rivers flow across this region from higher lands to the west. If you were to go upstream in a boat on one of these, you would travel for some distance through level country. Then you would reach falls and rapids in the river, beyond which your boat could not pass. This is because you would have reached the higher region known as the Piedmont Plateau. It is at the eastern edge of this plateau that the falls in the rivers occur. The line that marks this edge is called the fall line. 

Why do you think it has been given this name? 

Fall-line cities. Refer to the map and locate the city of Philadelphia. On what river is it? Find the following cities and the rivers on which they are situated: Trenton, New Jersey; Wilmington, Delaware; Baltimore, Maryland; 

Washington, D. C.; and Richmond, Virginia. Each of these cities is built near falls in a river. Make an outline map of the Middle Atlantic States and show each of these cities by a round dot. 

Now draw a line through the dots. The line on your map marks the eastern edge of the Piedmont Plateau and also the line of cities located on falls. Why are all of these cities called fall-line cities? 

Why cities grew up on the fall line.

When the first settlers came to America, there were, of course, no roads on which they could travel inland. Much of the land along the coast was covered with

FALLS AND CITIES 

81 

OVERPRICES 

Figure 86. An air view of Philadelphia. The river beyond the city is the Delaware. The middle of the Delaware is the boundary between Pennsylvania and New Jersey. On the other side of the Delaware is Camden, New Jersey. forests, and the lands farther back were hilly and mountainous. Because of these conditions the settlers used boats as a means of travel. They sailed up the quiet, deep waters of the larger streams until they reached falls or rapids. Here they were forced to stop. 

If the settlers wanted to go farther inland, they had to travel on foot or use horses and wagons. At the place where river navigation stopped, that is, at the break in transportation, they had to unload their boats. While they were doing this, they needed shelter for themselves and their families. Inns, livery stables, blacksmith shops, and stores of various kinds, therefore, grew up near the falls to serve the settlers while they were making ready to continue their journey. As more people came, the little settlements grew into towns. Trade between the Coastal Plain and the Piedmont developed, and many of the towns became important manufacturing centers. 

Later the falls and rapids on the streams were used to furnish power for the mills and factories. Thus each fall-line city grew up at the place where there was a break in transportation.

At the head of navigation. This story is somewhat different from that about the location of cities in New England. 

There, you recall, the cities were built at falls so that the water power could be used. Few of the streams in New England, however, are large enough for navigation, that is, for the passage of boats. Many of the streams in the

Middle Atlantic States, on the other hand, are navigable for ocean-going vessels as far as the falls. For this reason the cities here are said to be at the head of ocean navigation.

OUR THIRD LARGEST CITY PHILADELPHIA 

Figure 86 shows the downtown section of Philadelphia. The stream in the picture is the Delaware River. Find 

Philadelphia on the map and notice that across the Delaware on the New Jersey side is Camden. The bridge you see in the picture connects Philadelphia and 

Camden. Since these two cities are so close together, much of the work carried on in one is carried on in the other also. 

Courtesy Philadelphia Chamber of Commerce 

Figure 87. A sugar refinery in Philadelphia. Wherever you find a manufacturing plant that must use a large quantity of heat, you will find large chimneys such as this. 

A busy water front. If you were to visit the docks along the Delaware 

River, you would see tank steamers filled with oil from the fields of Texas and 

Mexico. You would find cotton being unloaded from boats that had come from 

New Orleans, Houston, and other southern ports. At some of the docks you would see sugar from Cuba, and at others wool from Argentina and Australia.

There are numerous factories and railroads near the water front. The railroads help to carry goods to and from the city. On the map notice the number of railroads that meet here. Philadelphia, then, is not only an important seaport but a great railroad center as well.

Thousands of manufacturing plants. 

Only two other cities in our country, New York and Chicago, are larger than 

Philadelphia. Like all great cities, it has thousands of manufacturing plants, such as mills, factories, and refineries. 

Some of the people make a living by working in cotton, woolen, and silk mills. Thousands of others are busy making shoes, hats, and clothing. Hundreds of factories make iron and steel products of various kinds. Oil refineries make gasoline, kerosene, benzine, and other oil products, and sugar refineries prepare sugar for market. Figure 87 is a picture of a Philadelphia sugar refinery. 

On the outskirts of the city more locomotives are made than at any other place in the world. Philadelphia is also a great printing and publishing center, and many books and magazines are printed here. 

Indeed, so many different products are made in this great city that a list of them would cover several pages.

TWO OTHER CITIES ON THE DELAWARE 

Trenton. If you were to go upstream along the Delaware River, you would come to Trenton, the capital city of New 

Jersey. Find this city on the map. On which side of the river is it? What other city on the Delaware in New Jersey have you studied? On which side of the river is it? 

Trenton, as you have already learned, has many pottery plants. Here such articles as jugs, jars, porcelain, and fine chinaware are made. Ever since the city was started, this work has been going on. The industry has been so successful that today Trenton is known largely as a pottery center. Much of the clay used in making the pottery and chinaware is shipped from other states and even from Europe.

Trenton has another industry that is just as important as the making of pottery. This industry is the manufacture of rubber goods. Much of the raw material used in the rubber factories is obtained from southeastern Asia. 

Wilmington. Downstream from 

Philadelphia where the Delaware River empties into Delaware Bay is Wilmington, another thriving city. In what state is it? This busy seaport is Delaware's largest and most beautiful city. It is also an important manufacturing city, making such products as tobacco, machinery, railroad cars, dyes, ammunition, and gunpowder. Wilmington is also an important railroad center. South of Wilmington, in the center of the state, is 

Dover, the capital.

A CITY WITH TWO HARBORS BALTIMORE 

Look at the map and find Baltimore. 

Notice that it lies between the Piedmont and the Atlantic Coastal Plain. A small river tumbles from the hilly land to the lower land at this point. Unlike the Delaware at Philadelphia, this river is not large enough to permit ocean-going vessels to reach the city. In the mouth of this river only small boats are found. 

Ocean-going vessels, however, can reach the southeastern part of the city by coming up Chesapeake Bay. You may think of Baltimore, then, as having two docking places, an inner harbor for the small boats and an outer one for ocean vessels. In which harbor do you suppose 

Figure 88 was taken?

Many manufactured products. Besides being engaged in coastwise and foreign trade, Baltimore is a land trading center as well. On the map notice the number of railroads that meet here. It is also an important manufacturing center. If you were to visit the water front of the inner harbor, you would find tobacco warehouses, factories, and canneries for fruits and vegetables. Indeed, Baltimore is the chief vegetable canning city in the 

United States. Each year it turns out millions of cans of tomatoes, corn, and peas. Near the outer harbor you would find fertilizer factories and steel mills.

THE NATION'S CAPITAL WASHINGTON 

As you find Washington on the map on pages 70-71, you will notice that it is on the Potomac River and not far from the Appalachian coal fields. You might expect, then, to find it a busy manufacturing city. On the contrary, however, it has few factories or mills. Thousands of the people in the city work for the government.

Washington is situated in a small area called a district rather than a state. The district is known as the District of Columbia, and the city is commonly referred to as Washington, D. C.

A view of the city. Figure 89 shows the Capitol Building in which Congress meets. Near by is a beautiful structure that contains the largest library in the country, the Library of Congress. Ex tending northwest from the Capitol is Pennsylvania Avenue, which leads to the White House, home of the President.

At the intersection of some of the treelined streets are small parks. There are also many larger parks, as well as numerous boulevards and beautiful homes.

The broad Potomac River. Washington, as you recall, is built just below the falls on the Potomac River. If you were to climb to the top of Washington 

Monument, as many visitors do, you could see the river for many miles. Near the city the broad river flows gently toward Chesapeake Bay, into which it empties. George Washington, the father of our country, chose a spot beside the river for his home. This historic spot, Mount Vernon, is visited by thousands of people every year. 

It is here that Washington is buried.

you would find an abundance of coal, iron, hides, and various other products being unloaded from boats. 

Then, too, you would see tobacco, lumber, cotton, and peanuts being loaded. In the numerous factories you would find locomotives, railroad cars, and other iron and steel products being made. Large quantities of cigars and cigarettes are manufactured, and many thousands of workers find employment in leather factories, lumber yards, and paper mills. Thus Richmond is a busy manufacturing city with many large factories such as you will see in Figure 91.

THE HUDSON-MOHAWK REGION 

Find the famous Mohawk River in the central part of New York. What does the map tell you about the height of land along the Mohawk? Notice that the river flows through a narrow valley between the Adirondack Mountains on the north and the Allegheny Plateau on the south. It empties into the Hudson 

River. What capital city lies a little to the south? If you were to board a steamer here, what large city would you find at the mouth of the Hudson?

Waterways made by man-canals. 

From your study of the map you can see that the Hudson and Mohawk river valleys offer an easy route for travel. In early days they provided the only natural route from the Atlantic coast through the Appalachian Mountains to the lands farther west. The Indians first used the route. Next the early settlers used it as a means of moving westward. Since the Mohawk River itself is not suitable for navigation, our forefathers dug a canal all the way from the Hudson River to Lake Erie. This canal, known as the 

Erie Canal, was opened in 1825. Only

Century Photos 

Figure 93. The Mohawk Valley. This valley is the only lowland route across the Appalachian Mountains. Notice the canal at the left of the river. A railroad and a highway also follow the river and carry much of the traffic. small boats, of course, could use the canal, but later a much deeper waterway was dug. This new waterway was called the New York State Barge Canal. Large, flat boats, known as barges, now came into use. What relation is there between the name of these boats and the name of the canal? The barges carried a variety of heavy products, such as coal, lumber, and wheat. Today much of the traffic through the Mohawk Valley is by railroad or highway, both of which follow the route of the canal. 

A thickly settled region. You can see from the map that the Hudson- Mohawk region is very thickly settled. 

Look at Figure 93 and notice what it shows. As in the case of every thickly settled region, two important questions arise: (1) Why have so many people settled here? (2) How do so many people make a living? Keep both these questions in mind as you study the cities discussed on the following pages. 

BUFFALO AND NIAGARA FALLS 

On the map you can see how the location of Buffalo has helped it to become a large and busy city. Notice that it has two water fronts, one on Lake 

Erie and the other on the Niagara River.

Figure 94 shows one of the harbors. 

What two lakes does the Niagara River connect? The numbers on the map will show how high each lake is above sea level. Which one is the higher? As the water from Lake Erie flows through the 

Niagara River to Lake Ontario, it must fall 326 feet. Therefore the river contains many rapids and falls, including Niagara Falls, shown in Figure 95, where the water falls 160 feet. Because of the rapids and falls, boats, of course, cannot use the Niagara River in going from one lake to the other. 

A way around the falls. Niagara Falls may therefore be called a break in transportation. Why is the location of 

Buffalo like that of the fall-line cities?

Courtesy Buffalo Convention and Tourist Bureau 

Figure 94. Part of the harbor at Buffalo. Most of the boats you see here are passenger boats. Compare the boats and the docks in this picture with those you have seen in pictures of ocean ports. To get around the break in transportation a canal, known as the Welland Canal, was dug by the people of Canada on their side of the falls. This canal, however, was not deep enough to be used by large lake boats. Lake steamers, therefore, carrying products from the West and the Middle Western States, had to stop before they reached the falls. At Buffalo, then, freight was unloaded and shipped farther east by railroads or by smaller boats on the Erie

Canal or south by rail. Thus it was that a great city grew up at the break in transportation. The Canadian government has deepened the Welland Canal, but much lake freight is still unloaded at 

Buffalo to be shipped to other places. 

Manufacturing and shipping. Some of the lake boats bring wheat to Buffalo from lands farther west. The wheat is stored in great elevators. Much of it is made into flour and breakfast foods, for 

Buffalo is one of the world's great flourmilling centers. It does not, however, use all the wheat it receives. Some of the wheat is shipped by rail or canal to other places. Buffalo also receives large quantities of iron ore over the lakes from

Figure 95. Niagara Falls. The left side of the picture shows the United States shore. The right side is in Canada. the mines near Duluth and Superior. 

Part of this ore is used in mills to manufacture iron and steel products, but part is sent on to other cities east and south.

Besides flour and iron and steel mills, Buffalo has many meat-packing plants and machine and car-repairing plants.

WILLIAMS HOYT & D Courtesy Rochester Chamber of Commerce 

Figure 96. The Genesee Falls in Rochester. Doubtless these falls help to explain why a great manufacturing city was built at this point. Most of the large buildings shown in the picture are factories. 

Turning water power into electricity.

The falls of Niagara supply abundant hydroelectric power for running factories. 

Electricity made by the use of water power is called hydroelectric power. Along the banks of the Niagara are many power plants. Electricity from these plants is carried by wires to various cities where it is used to run factories and to light homes and city streets.

Much of this electricity is also used to run the many factories in the city of Niagara Falls itself. Among these factories are flour mills, paper mills, machine shops, and aluminum works. The falls at Niagara, too, have helped Buffalo to become a great manufacturing city.

CITIES OF CENTRAL NEW YORK 

Rochester. Northeast of Buffalo on the Ontario Lake Plain is the busy city of Rochester. Find this city on the map. On what river is it? Rochester grew up at a place where the river tumbles over a high cliff, making the Genesee Falls. A view of these falls is shown in Figure 96. In early days the region around Rochester was an important wheat-growing area, and power from the waterfall was used for grinding grain. For a long time Rochester was known as the "Flour City." Today, however, it does not grind so much grain, but has become an important manufacturing center otherwise. Its many

factories make cameras, scientific instruments, clothing, and shoes. 

Syracuse. On the Barge Canal east ward from Rochester is Syracuse. This city is located at the western end of the Mohawk Valley. The factories here make iron and steel products, automobiles, and dishes.

Utica. Large areas of the land in the 

Mohawk Valley around Utica are used for dairy farming. Naturally, then, Utica has become an important market for cheese and other dairy products. It also manufactures textiles.

Schenectady. At the eastern gateway to the Mohawk Valley is Schenectady, an important trading and manufacturing center. Here there are factories for making locomotives and electrical machinery. How do you suppose this city gets all its coal and iron?

ON THE HUDSON 

Troy. On the map, near where the 

Mohawk River empties into the Hudson, you will find Troy. This city is famous for the manufacture of shirts. This industry is centered here today largely because it got an early start and has continued to prosper. 

Albany, the capital of New York. 

Albany may be said to be at a break in transportation. In other words, it is a transfer point. Large ocean steamers come up the Hudson from New York

City loaded with various manufactured articles. These must be transferred to railroads or to the smaller boats on the 

Barge Canal. Then, too, railroads from the east, west, north, and south all meet at Albany. These railroads bring in raw materials from New England on the east, through the Mohawk Valley on the west, and from the region of the Adirondack Mountains on the north. Many of these goods must be transferred to other railroads or to boats to be sent on to New York City. Some of the products, however, are used in the factories at Albany, especially in the large railroad shops here. 

Albany is the capital of New York.

It is noted for its beautiful capitol and other government buildings. New York has a greater population than any other state, and large numbers of people are needed to work for the government. 

On the way to New York City. If you were to travel down the Hudson from Albany, your trip would take you through one of the most beautiful parts of the country. Just south of Albany and to the west of the river you would catch a glimpse of the famous Catskill 

Mountains. All about, you would see beautifully wooded cliffs and hills. All along the river, too, you would see signs of manufacturing and trade. Various products are made in the factories, the best-known, perhaps, being brick, tile, and cement. Both railroads and boats are used to bring in raw materials and to carry away the manufactured products.

ATLANTIC OCEAN 

begins at the Battery, a small park at the southern end of Manhattan. This park is called the Battery because in early days a fort was built here to guard the city. From the Battery, Broadway extends northward through the heart of Manhattan. For mile after mile it is lined with office buildings, shops, stores, hotels, and theaters. Great numbers of people move up and down the street all day long and far into the night. 

Almost constantly, then, there is noise, hubbub, and rush.

The world's greatest seaport. The 

United States has a great foreign trade. 

Goods are bought from other countries and sold to other countries. Half of all that is sold passes out of the country through the port of New York, and more than half of all that is brought in enters through this port. Every day in the year the wharves are piled high with boxes, bales, barrels, and packages of various kinds. 

Then, too, New York carries on a great trade with other places in our own country. That is, it has a great domestic trade. Boats come bringing cotton from the South, coal from Norfolk and Philadelphia, manufactured products from

Figure 98. Manhattan Island as it is today. The highest building you can see is the Empire State Building, the tallest building in the world. As the picture shows, Manhattan Island is a region of high buildings. 

New England, fruit from Florida and the Pacific coast. The total sea trade of New York is greater than that of any other port not only in the United 

States but in the world. 

Thousands of factories. New York ranks first in manufactures among all the cities in America. Its factories make nearly everything. Ships bring raw materials for the factories from other seaports in the United States and from every part of the world. Trains bring coal from Pennsylvania mines and raw materials from all parts of the country. Ships and trains that bring raw materials and fuel to New York carry away manufactured products to all parts of the United States and to most other countries of the world.

The making of clothing is New York's leading industry. In thousands of factories all kinds of garments are made for men, women, and children. Many people are engaged in printing and publishing books and magazines. This industry ranks next to the manufacture of clothing in importance. 

Other factories in the city supply enormous quantities of foodstuffs, such as bakery products, cereals, meats, coffee, and sugar. Still other factories in New 

York make fur garments, jewelry, perfumes, candies, drugs, and other articles too numerous to mention.

Hudson River are in Weehawken, New Jersey.

NEIGHBORS ON LONG ISLAND 

Find Long Island on the map on pages 70-71. This island is a part of 

New York State and is a little larger than Rhode Island. A part of the city of New York is situated on the island. 

In Figure 97 find Brooklyn and Queens.

How are they connected with Manhattan 

Island? Look at Figure 98 for help in answering this question.

Truck farming is the chief industry on Long Island, especially in the western part where the soil is sandy. Grains, hay, and fruits are also grown in the more open country. Much fishing is done along various parts of the coast. 

Because of its location near New York, there are a number of summer resorts on 

Long Island. Coney Island is the most noted of these public playgrounds. Excellent roads make travel all over the island easy and pleasant.

OVER ON THE NEW JERSEY SHORE 

In Figure 97 and on the map on pages 

70-71 notice the number of cities west of the Hudson River and Upper New York 

Bay. These cities are all in New Jersey and all are suburbs of New York. 

Paterson, Jersey City, Elizabeth, and 

Newark have more than 100,000 inhabitants each. Other important neighboring cities are East Orange, Irvington, Union City, Clifton, Weehawken, Hoboken, Passaic, and Bayonne. Figure 97 will show you where these various suburbs are. Figure 99 shows part of the 

New Jersey shore. As you might expect, the work carried on in these cities is like that carried on in New York City. 

How the people make a living. In

Newark there are shoe factories and cotton mills. There are other factories that make leather goods and jewelry, and still others that roast coffee. This city is the largest in New Jersey. 

Jersey City is second in size. Here you will find meat-packing plants and factories for making paints, soap, and metal goods.

Bayonne is an oil-refining center. 

Here petroleum is made into gasoline, kerosene, and other products. The petroleum is brought to Bayonne through pipes from distant oil fields. 

Elizabeth manufactures vegetable oil products and sewing machines, as well as a variety of metal goods.

Paterson, Passaic, Union City, and

Clifton have many textile mills. Paterson, Union City, and Clifton are noted for their silk mills. Passaic, too, has silk mills, but more than half of its manufactured products consist of cotton and woolen goods.

East Orange and Irvington are largely residential cities where people live who work in New York and other larger places in the surrounding region.

FARMING IN  

From the map you have just made and from what you have studied, you have learned that a great many people in the Middle Atlantic States make a living by mining, manufacturing, and trading. The problem now is to find out how so many people obtain their food. Is farming important in the 

Middle Atlantic States? If so, where is it done and what kinds of products come from the farms? Is the growing season favorable for raising many kinds of crops? Does farming differ in various parts of the section? These are some of the questions you should answer as you continue your study.

FARMING ON THE ALLEGHENY PLATEAU 

Find the Allegheny Plateau again on the map on pages 70-71. Notice that it extends nearly across the Middle Atlantic States in a northeast-southwest direction. Some of the land on the plateau is too rough and hilly for crops, but that in the river valleys and on the more gentle slopes is used for farming. 

Scattered here and there are orchards that furnish apples, peaches, pears, plums, and cherries. In Figure 104 you will see one of these hillside orchards. Corn, wheat, and certain other grains are raised in the more level places, and sheep, hogs, dairy cattle, and poultry are kept on most of the farms. 

A great dairy region in the north. The 

Allegheny Plateau in New York State and northern Pennsylvania is not so rough and hilly as it is farther south. 

Since this is the case, the region here is especially good for pasturing cattle and

Figure 100.

Where dairy cows are kept in the Middle 

Atlantic States. 

raising hay. In fact, this part of the country is one of the most important dairy regions in the whole United States. 

As you can see in Figure 100, dairying is also an important kind of farming along the Mohawk and Hudson rivers, on the 

Lake Plain, and along the St. Lawrence

River. The farmers here find dairying profitable because it is so easy to get milk and cream to market. Then, too, most of the land is well suited to raising hay and pasturing cattle. 

Every day fresh milk and cream from all this region are shipped to New York, Pittsburgh, Buffalo, Rochester, and other large city markets. Since milk sours easily, great care must be taken so that it may reach market in a good condition.

When it is sent a hundred miles or more, therefore, it is usually shipped in glasslined tanks mounted on railway cars. 

When sent shorter distances, it is commonly delivered in glass-lined tanks mounted on automobile trucks. 

Part of the milk and cream sold to towns in the dairying region is used in the manufacture of butter and cheese. 

What city have you learned is an important cheese and dairy market? Only 

Wisconsin ranks ahead of New York in the value of its dairy products. 

FARMING ON THE LAKE PLAIN 

A great fruit region. All along the shores of Lake Erie and Lake Ontario fruit is grown in abundance. The lakes have helped to make this a good fruit region. Winds blowing from over the lakes tend to keep the region from becoming suddenly warm or cold, because water does not change its temperature as quickly as land. During the winter the water in the Great Lakes becomes freezing cold. The cold winds blowing over the lake keep the region from becoming suddenly warm in the spring. 

Thus the fruit trees do not come into Figure 101. A fruit map of the Middle Atlantic States. 

Apples are the leading fruit, but peaches and pears are raised mostly on the Lake Plain and in New Jersey. 

Grapes are most abundant along Lake Erie.

blossom until all danger of frost is past. 

In the fall the winds blowing from over the lakes keep the region warm enough for fruit to ripen before frosts come. 

Figure 101 will show you how important orchards are along the lakes.

FARMING IN THE APPALACHIAN MOUNTAINS 

The early settlers found most of the 

Appalachian Mountains too rough and hilly for cultivation. For this reason they moved on westward to lands that could be more easily cultivated. From then until now most of the mountains have never been used for farming. In some parts, however, particularly in the larger valleys, farming is very important. 

In the Great Valley. The Appalachian 

Mountains, as you can see from the map on pages 70-71, are not made up of one continuous mountain ridge, but of many ridges and valleys. Find the Adirondack Mountains, the Catskill Mountains,

Figure 102. Where corn is harvested in the Middle 

Atlantic States. The dots refer only to corn harvested in the usual manner. They do not include corn that is cut green and used for silage in dairy regions. 

Figure 103. Where wheat is raised in the Middle

Atlantic States. 

the Blue Ridge, and the Great Valley. 

It is in the Great Valley that most of the farming in the Appalachian Mountains is done.

A number of the fields here are used for growing wheat and corn, while others are used for hay and pasture. Figures 102 and 103 are maps that show where corn and wheat are grown. As you would expect, some of the farmers keep dairy cows, and all of them keep a few horses, hogs, and chickens. Look at the map of the Middle Atlantic States and suggest large cities to which they might send some of their products. "Apple and Peach Pie" Ridge. Many of the hills and slopes in the Great Valley are used for growing apples. Orchards are found on nearly every farm. From 

Winchester northeast across West Virginia and Maryland, there is one orchard after another. Find this region on the map. Trees planted on the slopes and ridges are more likely to escape frosts

Perhaps you have seen frosts in a valley when there was none on higher land. In Figure 104 you will see how orchards grow on hilly land. 

Apples are not the only fruit raised in the Great Valley. Someone has called the ridge that extends northeast from 

Winchester "Apple Pie Ridge" because so many apple trees grow on it. If you were to visit the region, however, you would say that it might also be called "Peach Pie Ridge" from the number of peach trees that grow here.

FARMING IN THE PIEDMONT 

In Pennsylvania. If you were to follow the Susquehanna River southeast from Harrisburg, you would see much tobacco grown on the rolling lands. 

Figure 105 shows the regions in which tobacco is grown. In other places you would find the land used for pastures and hay. Find this region on the map of the Middle Atlantic States. 

Next find the cities of Lancaster and 

York. Around these cities lies one of the richest farming regions in the United States. Here there are many hundreds of carefully tilled farms like those shown in Figure 106. Most of the early settlers in this region came from Germany many years ago. Some of the houses here are made of stone and were built when the settlers first came. The houses are well kept, as are also the many large barns. 

The thrifty farmers of this part of the Piedmont sell most of their products to 

Philadelphia and other near-by cities.

Dairy products are the most important. 

Tobacco is also raised. Figure 107 is a picture of a tobacco-drying barn. 

Lancaster and York are important markets and trading centers. Both manufacture cigars and other tobacco products. Other important farming centers on the Piedmont in Pennsylvania, as you have already learned, are Allentown, Lebanon, Reading, and Easton.

FARMING ON THE COASTAL PLAIN 

You 

Cranberries and sweet potatoes. have already learned that the Atlantic Coastal Plain borders the ocean and is very low. In some places along the

Figure 108. A vegetable map of the Middle Atlantic 

States. Notice the dots along the Atlantic coast. shore there are frequent swamps and marshes. What kind of berries did you find in New England which grow well on land that is low and marshy? In southern New Jersey there is just such land. 

You should not be surprised, then, to find cranberries growing here. New Jersey is the second largest producer of cranberries in the whole country. Which of the New England States is the largest producer? You will find large crops of sweet potatoes in Virginia, New Jersey, and other states on the Coastal Plain. 

Peanuts in Virginia. Another crop especially suited to warm sandy soils is peanuts. Most of the peanuts found in the Middle Atlantic States are grown in southern Virginia near Norfolk and 

Richmond. Here the peanuts are usually harvested in September or October. The vines with the pods attached to them are dug from the ground and placed in little stacks to dry. Later the pods are pulled or threshed from the vines and sent to market. Peanuts are roasted before they are sold to eat, since most people do not like them raw. Perhaps you have seen a peanut roaster at a store or park where peanuts were being sold. 

Large quantities of peanuts are used in making peanut candy, peanut butter, and peanut oil. 

Truck farming and vegetables. If you were to travel outside any of the large cities along the Coastal Plain, you would soon find yourself in the midst of a truckfarming district. Figure 108 is a map that shows where vegetables are grown in the Middle Atlantic States.

Sandy soils when fertilized are very good for raising beans, peas, lettuce, radishes, tomatoes, and various other vegetables. The soils along the Coastal

Plain are easily worked. Moreover, they dry quickly and therefore warm up earlier in the spring than do heavy, wet clay soils. The ocean, too, is an aid to truck-farming sections, for there is not so much danger of late spring frosts or early autumn frosts. From what you learned about the winds blowing from over Lake Erie and Lake Ontario and protecting the orchards near by, you should be able to explain what happens here.

The nearness to large cities creates a great demand for truck-gardening products. The numerous railroads and highways make it possible to carry products rapidly to city markets. Why is rapid transportation so necessary for fresh garden products?
