Choosing the best reason 

Following are partly completed sentences about farming in the Middle Atlantic States. 

Below each one you will find three endings which might be used. Complete each sentence by choosing the ending which you think is the best. 

1. Fruit grows well on the Lake Plain because a. the land is hilly. b. the lakes prevent early frosts. c. the soil is easily cultivated. 

2. Fruit growing is an important industry in the Great Valley because a. the land is level and easily cultivated. b. there is a good supply of water. c. the mountain slopes are protected from frost because the cold air settles in the valleys. 

3. Fruit growing is an important industry on the peninsula between Chesapeake Bay and the Atlantic Ocean because a. there are good seaports for shipping fruit. b. the large bodies of water prevent early frosts. c. the soil is fertile and there is plenty of rainfall. 

4. The Piedmont Plateau in Pennsylvania is well suited for farming because a. there are many cities here. b. the soil is fertile, the rainfall is plentiful, and the growing season is long enough for many different kinds of crops to grow and ripen. c. there are many waterfalls near by. 

5. Dairy farming can be carried on in New 

York and northern Pennsylvania because a. the land provides good pasture and hay for winter feed. b. there is a long growing season. c. the region is free from early frosts. 

6. Many farmers who live near cities make a good living by truck farming because a. they can cultivate the soil easily. b. their farms are large. c. the near-by cities are good markets for fresh vegetables. 

LUMBERING IN THE MIDDLE ATLANTIC STATES 

Turn back to Figure 55 and notice that there is considerable timber in the 

Appalachian Mountains. How do the growing season and the hilly land in this region help to explain why lumbering is a good occupation here? Years ago most of the highland region from New York to Virginia and West Virginia was covered with forests. Much of the timber, of course, has been cut, and some has been destroyed by forest fires. There still remains, however, considerable timber that is valuable for certain industries. 

In northern New York State and in 

Pennsylvania and West Virginia, there are several mills that use lumber for making wood pulp. Hemlock and oak in 

Pennsylvania and West Virginia furnish bark for a number of factories that tan leather. Part of the lumber from the 

Appalachian forests is also made into furniture, boxes, crates, and barrels.

Aubrey Bodine Figure 111. An oyster schooner in Chesapeake Bay. The dredge has just been pulled up from the bottom of the bay, loaded with oysters. These oysters are about to be emptied into the boat. 

FISHING ALONG THE ATLANTIC COAST 

Chesapeake Bay and oysters. Chesapeake Bay is especially good for the growth of oysters. Oysters grow better in a mixture of fresh and salty water than in the salty water of the ocean alone. Many streams empty into the bay, bringing fresh water that mixes with the salty water from the bay. Therefore the mixture is especially favorable for the growth of oysters.

Oysters also grow best in water that is fairly shallow. In general, the water of the bay is shallow. Most of the oysters grow along the shore where the water is less than fifty feet deep. Then, too, oysters must have water that is fairly warm. Notice on the map that the bay extends many miles southward. 

What does this tell about the temperature of the water? 

How oysters are caught and marketed.

When young oysters are only a few weeks 

Ewing Galloway

The oyster 

Figure 112. Shucking oysters. schooners bring their catch of oysters to shore. 

The shells are opened and the oysters taken out. old, they fasten themselves to pebbles or to hard shells beneath the shallow water. After about three years, they are large enough for marketing. Most oystermen remove the oysters from the beds by using tongs. In deeper waters, dredges are sometimes used to rake the oysters from the bottom. This method, however, destroys so many oysters that it is forbidden part of the time. Figure 

111 is a picture of a dredging schooner.

After the oysters are gathered, they are taken to little fish houses along the shore. 

Here they are cleaned and prepared for market. In Figure 112 you will see men doing this work. Some of the oysters are shipped in the shell, some packed in ice, and others canned. Although Norfolk, at the southern end of the bay, ships many oysters, Baltimore is known as the real oyster center.

Other fishing along the coast. Regular fishing also provides a living for a number of people. Flounder, mackerel,

squeteague or sea trout, menhaden, and other fish are caught in large quantities. 

Sea bass, an important deep-sea fish, is caught farther from the shore. Fishermen also catch smelt, shad, and sturgeon which swim up the rivers from the ocean. 

Although the menhaden is not desirable as food, it is one of the most valuable fish caught, because it can be used to make fertilizer. You may know that the Indians used to put a piece of fish in each hill of corn they planted. After the menhaden are caught, they are taken to factories where the oil is pressed out. The dry part that remains makes a good fertilizer for all kinds of farming. 

Some questions to answer 

1. Why are the waters of Chesapeake Bay especially favorable for oysters? 

2. How are oysters gathered? 

3. How are oysters prepared for market? 

4. What kind of fish is used in making fertilizer?

PLAYGROUNDS IN THE MIDDLE ATLANTIC STATES The Adirondack and Catskill Moun tains. The Middle Atlantic States have numerous playgrounds. The state of 

New York maintains thousands of acres of wild mountain forests as parks. Find again on the map the Adirondack and Catskill mountains. Many people who live in the crowded cities have summer homes or cottages in these mountains. 

The cool mountain air, the beautiful drives along the wooded slopes, and the attractive scenery make an ideal place in which to escape the summer heat of the lowlands. Then, too, there are many interesting winter sports in the mountains, such as skating, coasting, and skiing.

The Blue Ridge in Virginia. Now find the Blue Ridge on the map. Every spring thousands of tourists plan trips to the Shenandoah Valley in these mountains. At this season of the year the valley is one continuous flower garden of apple and peach blossoms. During other seasons of the year, also, the Blue Ridge attracts large numbers of people. If you were to visit these mountains, you would find that they appear to be green or purple or brown, depending somewhat upon the kind of trees on the slopes, the time of day, or the season of the year.

Also there always seems to be a sort of blue haze hanging over the ridges. This explains why the mountains are called the Blue Ridge. 

If you were to follow the James River eastward from the Blue Ridge, you would come to another place that is frequently visited by tourists. This place is the Natural Bridge National Forest. 

Here you would find a region of many caves and a natural bridge of rock that is very famous.

Lakes and rivers. On the map notice the long finger-like lakes in western New

York State. These Finger Lakes are the work of the great ice sheet, about which you learned as you studied the New England States. At the southern end of 

Seneca Lake is Watkins Glen. Here a deep, narrow gorge cuts through solid rock and causes many waterfalls. Only about a half-day's drive northwest by automobile is Niagara Falls, not far from the city of Buffalo. Many tourists travel across the Mohawk Valley and down the beautiful Hudson River to New York. 

Vacation cities by the sea. All along the shore of the Middle Atlantic States

LOOKING BACK AT  

105 from New York City to Virginia, there are numerous vacation cities. Here nature seems to have collected everything needed for a pleasant vacation. Cool breezes blow from over the ocean, and miles and miles of white sandy beaches stretch along the shore. Such places attract thousands and thousands of visitors every summer. 

One of the best known of the vacation cities is Atlantic City, New Jersey. If you were to visit this city, you would most certainly wish to walk along the beach on the famous board walk, swim in the ocean, or jump the big waves as they roll inshore. 

Planning a trip. Imagine you are to take a trip through the Middle Atlantic States. 

Make a list of the places you would like to visit. Then plan your trip in detail, telling exactly where you will go, various ways in which you will travel, and interesting things you expect to see along the way.

II. Choosing the best answer

Three answers are given for each question in the following exercise. One answer is always much better than the others. Choose the best answer for each question. 

1. What use is made of much of the Allegheny Plateau in New York? a. It is used for hay and pasturage. b. It is used for growing grain. c. It is used for mining coal. 

2. Why is Pittsburgh a leading iron and steel manufacturing center? a. It is on the Ohio River. b. It is within easy reach of large supplies of coke and limestone. c. It is on the Allegheny Plateau. 

3. Why is the Great Valley a fine farming region? a. It is underlaid with limestone rock. b. It is in Virginia. c. It is protected by the mountains. 

4. Why are there many orchards in western 

New York? a. This region is free from early frosts. b. The land is low and level. c. Apples can be shipped to market by water. 

5. Why is Baltimore a leading city for canning and shipping oysters? a. It is situated on the fall line. b. It has many railroads. c. It is situated near Chesapeake Bay where the oysters are found. 

6. What helps to explain why peanuts are grown on the Atlantic Coastal Plain in Virginia? a. The Coastal Plain has a warm climate and a loose, sandy soil. b. Peanuts are used for winter feed. c. It is the only crop that will grow.

UNIT FOUR  INTRODUCING THE SOUTHERN STATES 

How would you like to take a trip along the coast of some of the Southern 

States? The winter, as you know, is a pleasant time to travel in the South. 

Suppose you take your trip on a steamer engaged in coastwise trade and board the boat at Norfolk, Virginia. Find this city on the map on pages 112-113. 

Before you start, however, color a part of your outline map of the United States to show just where the Southern States are in the country as a whole. 

Cape Hatteras and three busy southern ports. A few hours after leaving 

Norfolk, your ship passes Cape Hatteras.

Find this cape on the map. A cape, as you know, is a point of land extending into the ocean. A lighthouse on the cape flashes and warns ships of the danger of coming too near the low, sandy shore. After rounding the cape you proceed southward and drop anchor at 

Wilmington, North Carolina; Charleston, South Carolina; and Savannah, Georgia.

Although all these cities are situated on rivers and although they are all seaports 108

INTRODUCING  

109 at the head of ocean navigation, they are not large seaports as are Boston, New York, Philadelphia, and Baltimore. 

Find Wilmington, Charleston, and Savannah on the map. 

On the wharves of the busy Atlantic ports. Along the wharves at each of the ports, you see thousands of bales of cotton being loaded on ships bound for manufacturing cities in New England and various countries of Europe. At 

Wilmington and Charleston large quantities of tobacco are leaving for some of our northern ports and many foreign countries. At Charleston numerous carloads of coal from the near-by Appalachian coal fields are being loaded. At 

Savannah you see hundreds of barrels of turpentine, rosin, and tar ready to be shipped. At piers in all these cities, boats are unloading nitrate and potash to be used as fertilizers by southern farmers. 

The potash comes from Europe and the nitrate from South America. 

Jacksonville, Florida. As you continue southward the weather becomes warmer and warmer and you put on your summer clothes. Your next stop is at Jacksonville, the largest seaport of Florida. Here again you find much more lumber, turpentine, and rosin being shipped. Instead of cotton, however, you see at the wharves thousands of crates of oranges, lemons, and grapefruit. 

Although it is midwinter, you notice many crates of strawberries and vegetables in freight yards being loaded in cars to be shipped by fast freight to northern cities. You did not see these products being shipped from Wilmington, Charleston, and Savannah because they were not ripe. Since Florida is farther south, these crops ripen a month earlier than they do in the Carolinas and Georgia. 

Miami, a winter playground. After leaving Jacksonville, along the coast of 

Florida you pass several seaside towns, the largest of which is Miami. The numerous hotels and the wide sandy beach here remind you of Atlantic City, but Miami is a winter resort instead of a summer resort. The palm trees and 

Spanish moss hanging from the branches of other trees are far different from anything you have seen in the North.

The warm winters, the sunshine, the sandy beaches, the colorful flowers, and the beautiful trees make Florida an ideal winter home. 

Fishing off the Florida coast. So far on your journey you have seen few fishing vessels, although fishing is carried on to some extent all along the coast. Many of the people who fish along the Florida coast, however, do so for sport and not for money. As you leave Miami you see, far out from shore in a small boat, several sportsmen who have just caught a huge tarpon more than six feet long. This is one of the largest fish that can be caught with a hook and line. Along the Florida coast you find a few fishermen catching shad, mackerel, mullet, shrimp, oysters, and even turtles for market. 

A string of islands. After your ship has passed the southern tip of the mainland of Florida, it goes southwest for some distance along a chain of very small islands. Look at the map and locate these islands. Notice that they are called the Florida Keys. On the southernmost island you will find Key West, our southernmost city. To the southeast is a group of large islands known as the West Indies.

ALKI ESMINE HOLE SAIN 

UN 

Publishers Photo Service 

Figure 116. Sponge fishermen. The sponge boats are tied beside the wharf. The fishermen have spread out their catch to dry in the sun. Later the sponges will be packed for shipment to various parts of the world. 

Along the southern and western coasts of Florida and not far from the Florida 

Keys you notice some small sailing boats.

The men on these boats are gathering sponges, which grow in the warm, shallow waters of this region. These men wear diving suits because they must take the sponges from the bottom of the ocean. 

Figure 116 shows sponges that have been brought to shore and unloaded.

Tampa and Pensacola. Upon depart ing from Key West you leave the Atlantic and enter the Gulf of Mexico. Your first stops along the west coast of Florida are at Tampa and Pensacola. At Tampa you see tobacco being handled at the wharves, as well as hundreds of tons of a rock or mineral called phosphate, which is used for fertilizer. At both of these ports, lumber, turpentine, and rosin are being sent to the West Indies, Europe, and South and Central America. 

Mobile Bay. At Mobile, Alabama, you enter a fine harbor at the mouth of the Tombigbee River. Here the ships are unloading potash from Europe, nitrate from South America, and various other products. Cotton is being loaded for shipment to Europe. Iron and steel products which have been brought from 

Birmingham, a great steel city not far to the north, are being shipped to the West 

Indies and South America. Turpentine, rosin, and lumber are also being loaded for shipment. 

A great river with many mouths. Not long after you leave Mobile you pass 

Gulfport, another lumber port. Soon you see streaks of muddy water mingled with the blue waters of the Gulf. Farther

INTRODUCING  

111 on all the water takes on a muddier color. 

This is because the great Mississippi River empties its waters here. This river carries thousands of tons of mud into the Gulf of Mexico every year.

As the mud settles, it gradually builds up the land. The river has cut a number of channels through the land. The channels and the land between form a region known as a delta. 

Houston and Galveston. After rounding the delta of the Mississippi and continuing westward, your ship next pulls up in Galveston Bay. Fifty miles inland from this bay is Houston, Texas-one of the largest cities in the South. A large canal connects the city with Galveston 

Bay. Ocean vessels go up this canal and take on loads of cotton, fertilizers, and oil. You may think of Houston, then, as a great inland seaport. 

On an island at the mouth of Galveston 

Bay is the city of Galveston. This city, too, is an important port and ships cotton, wheat, and meats. 

Corpus Christi and Brownsville. The steamer continues to one more town, Corpus Christi. Like Galveston, this town attracts many tourists. The balmy weather attracts you, too, and you wish you could tarry for many days. Your ship, however, soon picks up a small load of farm and oil products and is ready to turn back northward. If you had continued a little farther south, you would have come to the river that marks a part of the boundary between the United States and Mexico. This river is the 

Rio Grande. Near the mouth of the Rio 

Grande is Brownsville, a city situated in a region that grows large quantities of citrus fruits and vegetables.

Making a map. Turn to the map on page 357 and make an outline map of the Southern States. Show each state by printing its name or the abbreviation of its name. Locate the capital of each state by making a small cross.

Refer to the map on pages 112-113 and find all the cities you visited from 

Norfolk, Virginia, to Brownsville, Texas.

Make a small round dot for each of the cities and draw a line from city to city to show the route you traveled. 

Study guides. Before you study further, talk about the following questions in class and add any others that you may think are important. Keep the questions in mind as you read about the Southern States, and at the end of the unit be sure you can answer them all. 

1. Why is there a Cotton Belt in the South? 

2. Why have many people turned to mixed farming in addition to raising cotton? 

3. Why are parts of the Gulf Coastal Plain in Louisiana and Texas well adapted to growing sugar cane and rice? 

4. What conditions in the Piedmont Plateau and in other regions of the South favor the growing of tobacco? 

5. Why is the growing of fruits and vegetables such an important industry in the South? 

6. Why are conditions especially favorable in certain regions for growing citrus fruits? 

7. Why do fruits and vegetables grow well in the dry region around El Paso? 

8. Why is the raising of cattle, sheep, and goats the chief occupation in the western part of the Southern States? 

9. What various kinds of lumber come from the South? 

10. What important industries have developed in the South because of mineral deposits? 

11. Why are there so many cotton mills on the Piedmont Plateau? 

12. Why is fishing a rapidly growing industry along the Gulf coast?

I. Locating the Southern States 

1. Which of the Southern States border the 

Atlantic Ocean? Which ones border the Gulf of Mexico? Name three states that do not have a seacoast. 

2. What river forms a part of the boundary between Texas and Mexico? between Oklahoma and Texas? between Mississippi and Louisiana? between Tennessee and Arkansas? 

3. Which states are east of the Mississippi 

River? Which ones are west?

Middle Atlantic States continues along the 

Atlantic coast of the Southern States? Find a similar region along the coast of the Gulf of Mexico.

Middle Atlantic States extend into the Southern States? In which states do these mountains appear?

Appalachian Mountains in the Middle Atlantic 

States? Does this plateau extend into the 

Southern States? If so, in what states does it appear?

Appalachian Mountains in the Middle Atlantic 

States? You will find a different plateau west of the Appalachians in the Southern States. 

Find this plateau on the map on pages 6-7. 

5. What highland regions do you find in western Arkansas and eastern Oklahoma? 

6. How do the western parts of Oklahoma and Texas compare in elevation with the eastern parts of these states? The western parts belong to a region known as the Great Plains, which extends north and south across the 

United States. 

III. A great river basin

Notice the broad lowland in the central part of the Southern States. What great river flows through the center of the lowland from north to south? Notice that several rivers empty into the Mississippi. All the land drained by a river and its tributaries is called a river basin. The Mississippi River Basin is one of the largest in the world.

States. Which has the more broken coast line?

THE FATHER OF WATERS-THE MISSISSIPPI RIVER

THE FATHER OF WATERS-THE MISSISSIPPI RIVER 

As you made your trip along the coast of the Southern States, you noticed the waters of the Mississippi River emptying into the Gulf of Mexico. This river is the natural gateway from the south to the interior of the country. If your boat had steamed up the river, you would have found low, flat land on either side. 

You would have noticed banks called levees built along the river to prevent floods. In the heart of this low country you would have come to the city of New 

Orleans. Find this city on the map.

NEW ORLEANS, THE CRESCENT CITY 

New Orleans is the first large city which ships reach as they steam up the river. 

It was first built in a great curve of the river. Because of the shape of this curve it is often called the "Crescent City."

The word crescent means shaped like the new moon. To the north of the city is a lake known as Lake Pontchartrain. 

Although New Orleans is a hundred miles from the mouth of the river it is an important seaport. Even the largest ocean vessels come to its docks to trade. 

The land on which the city is built is low and level. All along the river, levees are built to protect the city from floods. 

Were it not for these levees great damage might be done when the river rises in times of high water. 

A beautiful southern city. New Orleans is noted as one of our most beautiful cities. There are modern office buildings, hotels, and department stores, as well as many attractive boulevards and homes. 

Most of the streets are broad and bordered with trees. The lawns are large and decorated with flowers and shrubbery. 

The balmy weather and the beautiful surroundings make the city a favorite spot for tourists. 

If you were to go to New Orleans you also would want to see a part of the city known as the French Quarter. This is the old part of the city which has been

Courtesy New Orleans Association of Commerce 

Figure 119. The Huey P. Long Bridge across the Mississippi at New Orleans. This bridge, with the raised part leading up to it, is almost four and one-half miles long. On it may be found two railroad tracks, two roadways, and two sidewalks. Notice that it is built very high above the river. Can you suggest why it is built so high? kept much as it was when the city was founded many years ago. Here the crooked streets, fountains, statues, and even the people might make you think you were visiting a foreign land. 

Then, too, you would want to see the wonderful bridge across the river. This bridge, a picture of which is shown in Figure 119, is one of the longest bridges in the world. Also, you would find large boats called ferryboats hauling railroad cars, automobiles, trucks, horses, and wagons, back and forth across the river.

A great trading center. Very different from the rest of the city is the scene at the wharves. Here vessels from many foreign countries, especially from Central and South America, unload coffee, sugar, bananas, hemp, and various other products. They carry away great quantities of cotton and other products that grow near by, such as sugar, meat, and lumber. 

Tramp steamers carry on a big trade with various cities along the coast of New England and the Middle Atlantic states. 

Next to New York, New Orleans has a greater trade than any other seaport in the country. There is also much trade on the river itself. Grains are brought down the Missouri and coal is brought down the Ohio. Lumber, cotton, and oil are sent back in exchange. 

New Orleans is also an important railroad center. Numerous railroads connect it with large inland cities, such as St. 

Louis and Chicago. Goods brought in by ships are loaded on railroad cars, and goods from the railroads are loaded on ships. Thus railroads and ships work together to make the city a great trading center. Figure 120 shows a part of the harbor and Figure 121, a near-by warehouse.

A manufacturing center. Many of the raw materials that come to New 

Orleans go to factories in the city. Some of these factories blend and roast coffee which is brought from South America.

Others refine sugar, part of which is obtained from near-by sugar plantations. 

The rest is brought in by ships from various countries. Other factories are engaged in cleaning and polishing rice, making cottonseed oil and cottonseed meal, manufacturing cigars and other tobacco products, planing lumber, and making shingles and furniture.

A TRIP ON A RIVER BOAT 

Suppose you now take a steamer at 

New Orleans for a short trip up the 

Mississippi River. This steamer is much like the ones you saw on the rivers at 

Pittsburgh except that its paddle wheels are on the side instead of at the back. 

It may be pushing a group of barges, or its decks may be stacked high with freight. Usually it does not carry passengers, but perhaps the captain will take you along to see the country. You cannot see much of the land, however, because of the levees along the river. 

Figure 121. A warehouse in New Orleans.

Goods brought by boat and railroad are stored in warehouses such as this until they are shipped. 

A slow, muddy river. As you travel upstream, you notice that the boat does not meet a strong current of water. The land is so low and level that the water flows very slowly. In some places the river is almost as high as the bordering plains. This is because it receives large quantities of dirt, or silt, from the streams that empty into it. Some of these streams carry dirt from the mountains in which they have their source, or beginning. Others pick up soil as they flow along their way. For years and years the Mississippi has been receiving the silt from its tributaries. Since the water moves very slowly some of the silt settles on the bottom. Thus today the river is almost as high as the land. 

A flood plain. When there is a heavy rainfall or when some of the tributaries bring melting snows, the Mississippi sometimes overflows its banks and floods the surrounding land for many miles. 

The soil carried by the river is left on the flooded land. This process of building land along the Mississippi has been going on for thousands of years. As a result a plain has been built up from river-laid soils. Such a plain is called a

Figure 122. A levee along the Mississippi. The levee is the high grassy bank at the right. The river is just beyond it. The tree is a live oak, and the trailing streamers on the tree are Spanish moss. flood plain. Today the river floods only in certain places, as its banks for many miles are lined with levees. Sometimes, however, the levees break when the water in the river is high, and great damage is done in the surrounding country. Figure 122 is a picture of the land side of a levee. 

A wet region. As your boat moves up the broad stream, you pass many swamps on the flood plain. These swamps are filled with cypress trees. The pale bark of the trees and the masses of finger-like roots hanging from their trunks make a strange sight. Now and then you see a forest of oaks hung with streamers of gray Spanish moss. Sometimes trees and vines are so thickly matted that they form a green wall on either side of the river. 

Although the region is wet, the land here is not all covered with swamps and trees. Every now and then from the boat you see great stretches of level land that are used for farming. The crops, however, for the most part seem different from any you have yet seen.

Making comparisons. Compare the Missis sippi River and the Delaware River by telling: 

1. In what ways they are alike. 

2. In what ways they are different.

Also compare a steamboat on the Mississippi with a ship on the ocean.

As you stopped at the various ports along the Atlantic Ocean and the Gulf of Mexico, you noticed a number of products from the farms that you did not find in New England and the Middle Atlantic States. Among these products were cotton, sugar, rice, and citrus fruits. 

Can you suggest any reason why these crops grow in the Southern States? Look at Figure 17. What does it tell you about the length of the growing season? 

Most of the crops just mentioned require a long growing season.

RAISING SUGAR CANE 

In Figure 123 notice that sugar cane is raised in a number of the Southern States. 

The cane in Louisiana is used for making sugar, but in most of the other states it is used for making sirup.

A hot, wet region. Sugar cane needs a long, hot, wet summer. It must have eight months of frost-free weather. As you looked at Figure 17 how long a growing season did you find in southern 

Louisiana? Sugar cane also needs plenty of rain during the growing season. 

Figure 16 tell you that the region here has a heavy rainfall? Then, too, sugar cane requires a fertile soil. All the land Did

along the lower end of the Mississippi is covered with a deep, rich soil. This deep, rich soil was left by the river before it was held between its banks by levees. 

The sugar region of southern Louisiana is the only place in the United States where a growing season of at least eight months, a heavy rainfall of nearly 60 inches annually, and a deep, fertile soil are all found together. 

Planting stalks instead of seeds. To raise sugar cane, farmers do not plant seeds as they do for most other crops.

After the fields have been made ready for planting, stalks of sugar cane are laid in little ditches, or furrows, and carefully covered with soil. Soon small sprouts or shoots start growing at the joints of the stalks. During the growing season the plants must be carefully cultivated to keep out the weeds. Harvesting usually begins in early October before the first autumn frost. 

Sirup and sugar. It is for the stalks that sugar cane is raised, since they contain the sweet juice. After the stalks are cut, they are loaded on small cars or, if the plantation is small, on wagons drawn by mules. The loaded stalks are then taken to mills, where they are crushed between heavy rollers that squeeze out the juice. The juice is then strained and boiled in tanks until it forms a thick yellow sirup and a coarse brown sugar, known as raw sugar. 

Preparing sugar for your table. Raw sugar is not the white sugar you buy at the store. It has a dark brown color and must be sent to factories known as refineries before it is ready for use. The sugar prepared for your sugar bowl, then, is known as refined sugar. 

Most of the raw sugar used by refineries comes from the West Indies and other parts of the world. The refineries, therefore, are usually found near seaports where the sugar may be handled with the least expense. Some of the sugar which is used in the United States, of course, is refined in other countries.

RAISING RICE 

It was more than three hundred years ago that rice was first brought to America.

At first small quantities were raised in the Carolinas on the Atlantic Coastal

Plain. Later it was found that rice would grow best on the delta lands of the 

Mississippi River and on the neighboring lands of the Gulf Coastal Plain. Today 

Louisiana and Texas together produce more than three-fifths of all the rice grown in the country. Find this riceproducing region in Figure 124. Now turn to Figure 123 and notice that a part of the rice-producing region is in the sugar-cane country. Both rice and sugar cane are grown in the same region. 

A thirsty plant. Rice has been called a thirsty plant because it must grow for many weeks in soil covered with water. 

Look at Figure 16 again and notice the heavy rainfall around the mouth of the 

Mississippi River. Although the rainfall here is heavy, it is not heavy enough for rice. The rice farmers, therefore, must pump in more water from wells and near-by streams.

When the rice plants are from six to eight inches high, the fields are flooded with about two inches of water. They then look like lakes in which the tender green sprouts appear like grass. As the plants grow, more water is added until it is six to eight inches deep. This water not only helps the rice grow, but it helps to kill the weeds as well. Thus rice needs very little cultivation and grows in water all summer long. When the plants begin to change from a green to a golden tint, the water is drained off. The field is then left to dry for the harvest. 

Harvesting the rice. Usually harvesting begins about the first of October.

The rice stalks are tied in bundles and

RICE RICE

Mississippi. Notice, too, in what scattered regions tobacco is raised.

placed in shocks. Then they are left in the fields about two weeks in order that the kernels may harden. Rice is threshed in much the same manner as wheat is threshed. After the threshing the rice is shipped in sacks to mills, where it is cleaned and polished before it is ready for use.

1. Why is rice grown only on lowlands? 

2. Why is rice not grown in the Middle

Atlantic States or in New England? 

THE LAND OF COTTON 

As you made your trip on the steamer along the coast of the Southern States, you found cotton being shipped from nearly every port except in Florida.

This told you that the South must be a land of cotton. Figure 125 is a map of the Cotton Belt. As you look at the map, you will see that some of the Southern States produce more cotton than others. You will notice, too, that there are three large cotton-producing regions, one in the eastern part of the Southern States, one in the north-central part, and one in the western part. What states are included in each of these great producing regions? 

Why there is a Cotton Belt. Cotton grows best where the summers are long and sunny, yet moist. Look at Figure 

17 and find how many months there are in the growing season in most of the 

Cotton Belt. Now look at Figure 16 and find the annual rainfall in the Cotton 

Belt. What does this map tell you about the land west of the Cotton Belt? about the land around the mouth of the 

Mississippi River? about Florida?

The Cotton Belt is marked out by nature. Cotton needs a growing season of about eight months and an additional two months for harvest. Farther north, then, you see, the growing season is too short, so little or no cotton is grown. 

Cotton requires at least 20 inches of rainfall annually, and does not thrive Figure 125. Where cotton grows in the South. You can see from the map that cotton is an important crop in every state except Florida. Texas leads in the growing of cotton.

well in lands that have more than 60 inches of rainfall. The lands west of the 

Cotton Belt have too little rainfall for cotton to grow, and the lands near the mouth of the Mississippi have too much.

Cotton does not thrive in sandy soils or in soils that are poorly drained. This explains why little cotton is raised in the 

South and East along the Coastal Plain.

Thus you can understand that natural conditions, such as the growing season, rainfall, and kind of soil, fix the location of the Cotton Belt just where it is. 

THE CENTRAL COTTON-PRODUCING REGION 

If you were to travel up the Mississippi

River through the rice and sugar-cane region, you would come to a place where rice and cane fields give way to cotton plantations. On the map find Jackson in the state of Mississippi and Shreveport in the state of Louisiana. All along both sides of the river, from Jackson westward beyond Shreveport, and northward to northeastern Arkansas and northwestern Tennessee, there is a great cottonproducing region. Some of this region is a part of the flood plain of the Mississippi and contains very fertile soils. Here you will find numerous cotton plantations. Have you ever visited a plantation? 

Suppose you do so now.

A cotton plantation. A cotton plantation is a large farm, often including a thousand or more acres of land. Only a part of the land, however, is planted to cotton. The rest is used for other kinds of farming and for pasture, and some of it may be woodland.

The owner of the plantation must have several tenants to help cultivate his fields. He supplies them with mules, tools, seed, and fertilizer. He also provides them with houses or cabins for their homes. In payment for these services, he receives part of the cotton raised on his land.

In Some of the plantations are small. such cases the owner takes care of the farm himself and hires men to help him raise the cotton and do the other necessary work. These men use the owner's equipment and provide their own homes. Find Memphis, a gateway to cotton. 

Memphis on the map. This city is built on bluffs along the Mississippi. Two large steel bridges span the river at this point. How do the lands along the river here differ from those farther south? which side of the river is Memphis? In which state is it? What state is on the other side of the river?

On Memphis may be considered a gateway to the great cotton-growing regions of the South. At the docks along the river cotton is loaded on river boats to be carried downstream to New Orleans.

Memphis is also a very important railroad center. Trains going east, west, north, and south meet here. There are also numerous factories in the city. 

Some of them make cottonseed oil, meal, and cake. Others make furniture and lumber products, for, you will see later, Memphis is near a region of hardwood forests. Figure 126 shows a scene along the wharves. 

Three other cotton cities. Another important cotton center of this region is 

Little Rock, Arkansas. Look at the map and find the river on which it is situated. 

Notice that it is a railroad center as well. 

Why would you say that shipping is important here? Little Rock also manu-

Figure 126. The water front at Memphis. The city is at the left, at the top of a bluff. The boats nearest shore are barges, to be towed by steamboats. Several steamboats are tied up just beyond them. Notice the bridge over the river. factures furniture and lumber products, for, like Memphis, it is near a region of hardwood forests.

Still another important cotton city of the central-producing region is Shreveport. Besides being the center of a rich farming region, it has excellent transportation. It is situated on the Red 

River and has numerous railroads and improved highways. Therefore it has become noted for the buying, selling, and shipping of large quantities of cotton. 

In addition it has large lumber mills, oil refineries, and various other factories. 

Of the four important cotton centers of this region-Memphis, Little Rock, Shreveport, and Jackson-two are state capitals. Which two are they? Why are they good sites for capital cities? If necessary, look at the map again to answer these questions.

THE WESTERN COTTON-PRODUCING REGION 

Look again at Figure 125 and notice the parts of Texas and Oklahoma that make up the western cotton-producing region. The farms here are somewhat smaller than the plantations along the 

Mississippi. Usually a farm contains about 160 acres. Only three-fourths of the cultivated land is planted to cotton. 

The other fourth is used for raising corn, oats, and sorghum, a kind of tall grass that is used chiefly for feeding stock. 

The owner of a farm needs five or six workers to help him take care of his farm throughout the year. He may hire additional workers, however, during the busy cotton-picking season.

Cotton lands very level and fertile. 

The Black Waxy Prairie is a name given to some of the cotton land of Texas 8-5G

because it is a level region of black, waxy soils. The Black Waxy Prairie is one of the greatest cotton-producing areas in the world. The surrounding lands are also fertile, so that both Texas and Oklahoma rank especially high in the growing of cotton. In fact, Texas produces more cotton than any other state. 

The cotton lands of Texas and Okla homa are easily cultivated. The rich soil, a favorable amount of rainfall, and a sufficiently long growing season all help to explain why cotton grows so well in these states. On the map find San 

Antonio, Austin, Waco, Fort Worth, and 

Dallas. These cities are important cotton centers which you will study later. 

THE EASTERN COTTON-PRODUCING REGION 

The third chief cotton-producing region is in North Carolina, South Carolina, Georgia, and Alabama. Find this region again in Figure 125 and notice that it is largely on the Piedmont Plateau. 

Most cotton farms small. On the 

Piedmont most of the cotton farms are small. Occasionally, however, you will find a white-pillared, stately looking southern home in the midst of a plantation. On such a plantation the work is carried on in much the same way as on the large plantations in the central region. The smaller farms are cultivated largely by the owners and a few hired men, as in the Black Waxy Prairie of

Texas. The cotton crop in the Piedmont makes up from one-third to one-half of all the crops raised. How does this compare with the other two regions?

Land kept fertile. The Piedmont region of Alabama, Georgia, and the Carolinas is the oldest cotton-producing region in the country. The continued planting of cotton over a period of years has used up some of the fertility of the soil. To make the soil more fertile, the farmers plow certain crops under the ground. These crops then decay and fertilize the land. 

Spanish beans, which look somewhat like peas or beans, are often grown for this purpose, as are peanuts also. Other fertilizers, such as potash and nitrates, which you found being brought to many harbors, are also used in the South.

Important cotton cities. The Pied mont Plateau you are now studying is merely the southern end of the same Piedmont Plateau you studied in the 

Middle Atlantic States. Throughout the southern part there are numerous important cotton centers, such as Montgomery, Atlanta, Augusta, Greenville, and Columbia. Locate these cities on the map. Can you now see why much cotton was exported from Wilmington, Charleston, and Savannah, and even from 

Norfolk near the north edge of the cotton region?

THROUGH THE YEAR WITH THE COTTON GROWER 

Cotton fields are plowed and made ready for planting in the late winter or early spring. The seeds are planted as soon as the ground is warm enough for the seeds to sprout. In southern Texas planting begins about the first of March, but in North Carolina it does not begin until the latter part of April. How does

Figure 17 help explain the difference in the time of planting? 

Blossom time. Eight or ten weeks after the plants are up, they begin to

Ewing Galloway

Figure 127. Cotton pickers in the South. The pickers have long bags carried by a strap over the shoulder. Into these bags they put the cotton as they pick it. By the time a bag is filled, it drags far behind the picker. It will be necessary to pick over this field several times because not all the bolls ripen at once. blossom. Each field takes on the appearance of a great flower garden. On the first morning the blossoms are white or yellow, but by afternoon they change to pink. In two or three days they begin to fall, leaving tiny pods, called bolls, in which seeds and cotton fibers grow. Not all the blossoms appear at the same time, however, on a plant. Instead, new blossoms keep forming on the higher branches as the plant continues to grow. For this reason the blossoms and bolls on a single plant may show various stages of growth.

When the bolls ripen and burst open, they are filled with fluffy white cotton and numerous small seeds. Figure 127 shows what a cotton field looks like when many of the bolls have opened. In general, it looks somewhat like a great garden of snowball bushes. If you look at Figure 128 closely, you may be able to see bolls in various stages of development. 

Those near the top of the plants are still green, those a little lower have opened, while those on the lowest branches have been open so long that they have already turned brown.

Cotton-picking time. The cotton-picking season is a very busy one, when all the family-men, women, and childrenwork in the fields. Each picker carries a long bag, known as a pick-sack, which he drags along on the ground by means of a strap fastened over his shoulder. 

When full, it contains about one hundred pounds of cotton. A fast picker fills his pick-sack several times a day. 

Since the cotton bolls ripen at different times, a field must be picked again and again. In the southern part of the Cotton Belt the picking season usually begins

M. Hassell 

Ewing Galloway 

Figure 128. 

Cotton ready for picking. Now you can see why a cotton field is so white. 

Wouldn't you like to pick some of this fluffy cotton?

There are several bolls on each stem. 

Figure 129.

A line of wagons waiting at a cotton gin. Notice that mules are used instead of horses. Mules are used on most cotton plantations of the South. Cotton gins are seldom situated in large cities, but are scattered about the country. in July or August and lasts several months. In the northern part the season does not begin until September. 

How cotton goes to market. Cotton, of course, cannot be used until the seeds have been removed from the soft fluffy fibers, or lint. For a long time this work had to be done by hand. Finally in 

1793 Eli Whitney invented a machine for removing the seeds. This machine, called the cotton gin, made the raising of cotton profitable. Immediately after cotton is picked, it is hauled on wagons or trucks to a cotton gin. Figure 129 shows what you might see at any one of the many cotton gins scattered throughout the South.

After the seeds are removed, the fibrous part, or lint, is pressed into bales, or big bundles, each weighing about five hundred pounds. The bales are covered with coarse cloth, usually burlap, made from hemp, and bound with wire or iron. bands, as shown in Figure 115. The cotton is now ready to be shipped to mills. Most of the cotton that is to be shipped to foreign countries is sent to a compress. This is a large warehouse where the bales are put into a powerful press and made only about one-third as large as they were before. The smaller bales take up less space on trains and ships. 

Using the cotton seed. For a long time cotton seeds were thrown away after being removed from the fiber. Now, however, the seeds are a very valuable part of the plant. They contain an oil that can be used in cooking, for salad oil, for making soap and candles, and as a substitute for lard. In a cotton-oil

mill you may even find some of the workers using cottonseed oil on bread as you would use butter. After the oil has been pressed from the seeds, they are ground into meal and used as feed for cattle and other stock, and often as fertilizer. 

COTTON MILLS IN THE SOUTH 

For many years the Southern States shipped all their raw cotton to New England and to various foreign countries to be manufactured into cloth. Later a few factories were built on the Piedmont Plateau in the heart of the eastern cotton-producing region. These factories were successful, and today hundreds of mills like the one shown in Figure 130 are found in the Carolinas, Georgia, Alabama, and Tennessee. These mills use about one-half of the total cotton crop of the South.

Cotton-mill cities. Among the more important cities in the cotton-mill district are Augusta, Macon, and Columbus, Georgia; Columbia, Greenville, and Spartanburg, South Carolina; Gastonia, Charlotte, Concord, and Greensboro, North 

Carolina. Find all of these cities on the map. Notice that some of them have been built on the fall line between the 

Piedmont and the Atlantic Coastal Plain. 

What reason can you give for these locations? Some of the mills are run directly by water power, but others are run by hydroelectric power-that is, by electricity made by water power. Great power plants have been built in the streams that flow across the Piedmont to the Coastal Plain. In addition, the government has built several dams in the 

Tennessee River for electric power and as a protection from floods. The first of

MIXED FARMING IN THE COTTON BELT 

From what you have just learned about cotton you might think that it is the only crop raised in the South. This, however, is not the case. In every southern state, even in the rice and sugar-cane regions, much mixed farming is done. Corn and oats are grown in every state, and large quantities of wheat are raised in Tennessee, Oklahoma, and northern Texas. Dairying, too, is an important industry in every state, as you will find by looking at Figure 131. A few dairy cows are kept on nearly every

farm. On many farms in the South cotton is planted for a few years, then other crops for a few years, and finally cotton again. This kind of planting is called rotation of crops, and is much better for the soil than is planting the same crop year after year. 

Mixed farming is new in the South.

For a great many years the farmers raised only cotton. Just why they began to raise other crops makes a highly in teresting story. 

The destructive boll weevil. The cot ton plant has many insect enemies, including the leaf worm, the cotton flea, the cotton louse, and the boll weevil. 

The warm climate of the region favors the growth of these pests. They live through the mild winters and increase in numbers from year to year. 

The most harmful of all the pests is the cotton boll weevil, which lays its eggs in the green bolls and causes them to fall from the plants. It came from 

Mexico in 1892 and gradually spread northward and eastward across the Cotton Belt, destroying crops as it went.

The cotton growers were helpless, and for a time it seemed that the cotton industry would come to an end. Finally, after a period of several years, ways were found of checking it by means of poison. Therefore farmers all through the 

South now dust their fields with poison. 

A monument to an insect. Did you ever hear of a monument to an insect? 

Strange to say, one was erected to the boll weevil at Enterprise in southeastern Alabama. On the monument you will find this inscription: "In profound appreciation of the boll weevil and what it has done as the herald of prosperity, this monument is erected." Doesn't it seem strange that the people of the South should erect a monument to a pest that almost put an end to the growing of cotton? But there is a reason, as you will soon see.

What the boll weevil taught. From the time of the invention of the cotton Figure 131. Where dairy cattle are raised in the South. Notice that they are raised in most parts of the South except in some of the southern and western parts.

gin to the coming of the boll weevil, the farmers of the South used most of their lands for the growing of cotton. As they planted the same crop year after year, the food in the soil was gradually used up and the land became less and less fertile. Each year the crop became smaller. 

Moreover, the farmers had nothing but cotton to sell because it was the only crop they tried to raise. If there was a crop failure or if the price of cotton happened to be low, as it sometimes is, they had little with which to buy food and clothing.

When so much cotton was destroyed by the boll weevil, the farmers began to plant other crops or to raise livestock in order to make a living. In some of the fields they raised corn. In others they raised such crops as peanuts, cowpeas, and velvet beans, all of which make good forage, or feed for cattle. Unlike cotton, all these crops improve the soil. 

Thus, because of the coming of the boll weevil, the farmers of the South learned that they could raise many other crops. 

Can you tell why 1. The Cotton Belt does not extend farther north? Why it does not extend farther west? 

2. Virginia produces only a little cotton? 

3. Florida produces less cotton than any of the other Southern States? 

4. Little or no cotton is raised in the southernmost part of Alabama, Mississippi, and Louisiana? 

5. There was a great increase in the raising of cotton after the invention of the cotton gin? 

6. The southern part of the Piedmont has become an important district for cotton mills? 

7. The southern farmer has turned to mixed farming? 

8. The cotton farmer rotates his crops?

FARMING ON THE COASTAL PLAINS 

Although mixed farming is carried on all over the South, there are some regions in which certain crops grow much better than in others. In other words, there are places where the farmers use most of their land for raising special crops. For example, you found near

New Orleans a region where the farms produce large quantities of sugar cane and rice.

IN THE CAROLINAS AND GEORGIA 

Along the coast. Truck farms in great numbers dot the land all the way from 

New Jersey to Florida. These truck farms supply large quantities of vegetables for northern markets during the winter and early spring. Vegetables in the South, as you know, can be grown much earlier than vegetables in the 

North. Savannah, Georgia, and Charleston, South Carolina, are important centers for shipping vegetables to northern markets during the early spring.

Fruit is another important crop in the Carolinas and Georgia, as you will find by looking at Figure 133. The most important crop in the Carolinas is apples, but the most important in Georgia is peaches. 

Peaches are raised in every southern state, but Georgia ranks first in the size of the crop. Figure 132 is a scene in a 

Georgia peach orchard.

Augusta, Macon, and Columbus, Georgia.

Raleigh is a center for general agricultural products. The other cities, Columbia, Augusta, Macon, and Columbus, depend largely upon cotton. All have extensive cotton mills and are important cotton centers. Augusta and

IN THE STATE OF FLORIDA 

Look at the map and notice that the entire state of Florida is a part of the Coastal Plain. Notice, too, that it is a peninsula almost surrounded by the waters of the Atlantic Ocean and the Gulf of 

Mexico. How would you expect these waters to affect the growing of fruits and vegetables? Think once more about the ways in which the ocean affects the growth of fruit east of Chesapeake Bay.

A subtropical climate. As you look at the map you will see that Florida and southern Texas are nearer the equator than other parts of the South. As a result, these areas have a longer growing season than other parts of the South.

People take advantage of this long growing season by fertilizing the land and raising two, and sometimes three, crops a year. The winters are mild and there is seldom any frost. Regions like Florida and southern Texas that have long hot summers and short mild winters are said to have a subtropical climate. Now look at Figure 17 to see how long the growing season in these regions is.

Sandy soils. Since Florida is a part of the Coastal Plain, it has no mountains or deep river valleys. The soils, as on most of the Coastal Plain, are largely made up of sand. Such soils, you have learned, drain easily and warm rapidly. 

They are therefore especially good for the raising of fruits and vegetables. 

The southern part of Florida is low and swampy. The largest area of swamps is known as the Everglades. This area is covered with a very dense growth of trees, bushes, and a tough kind of grass known as saw-grass. For a long time. little farming could be carried on in the Everglades, but now some of the land is being drained.

An abundant rainfall. In Figure 16 you will observe that Florida has an abundant rainfall. The heavier rains fall in summer and the lighter rains come during the winter. This is largely because the winds shift with the seasons.

In the summer most of the winds blow from the south over the ocean, bringing with them an abundance of moisture.

The heat of the sun, however, soon dries the sandy soils, and even the heaviest showers do very little damage to growing vegetables. 

Growing vegetables in midwinter.

Truck farming is important in Florida and near-by states largely because these states can supply northern markets with green vegetables during the winter and early spring. In southern Florida certain vegetables are ready for market by the fifteenth of December. Of course the vegetables that are first on the market bring the highest prices. Florida has the advantage of being able to ship vegetables earlier than any of the other states. Tourist trains going to Florida in winter are sometimes sidetracked to permit trainloads of vegetables to pass.

While fields in the North are still white with snow, Florida truck farmers dig new potatoes and pick tomatoes, green beans, and peas. 

Potatoes and strawberries. Find St. 

Augustine on the map. Near this city in the northeastern part of the state is an important potato region. Most of the potatoes in this region are planted in the late fall or early winter and are ready for market in the early spring.

Now find Tampa on the map. Just east of this city hundreds of acres are planted to strawberries. This fruit is ready for market in December, January, and 

February. Thus potatoes and strawberries in Florida ripen at a time when the people of the northern states are having their coldest weather. It is easy to understand, then, why the farmers of Florida find these crops especially profitable to raise. 

Citrus fruits. Florida is not alone noted for its truck farms. Another equally important industry is the growing of citrus fruits-oranges, lemons, grapefruit, and limes. Larger quantities of these fruits are raised in Florida than in any other state except California.

Most of the fruits are raised in the central part of the state. Here you will

ALONG THE GULF COAST 

On the Gulf Coastal Plain all the way from Florida to Texas, truck farming is carried on. Figure 135 shows a truck farm such as you might see on the Gulf coast. Near New Orleans truck farming is especially important.

In north-central Florida find Tallahassee. What does the sign on the map tell you about the city? Just north of this city is a region where the pecan, a famous nut of the South, is raised. Pecans are also found in nearly every state westward through Texas.

FARMING ON THE PIEDMONT 

You have already learned that one of the great cotton-producing regions of the 

South lies on the Piedmont Plateau.

This plateau is also a good region for mixed farming. Nearly every farmer raises a variety of crops and keeps a few cattle and hogs. 

Figure 124 will show you that tobacco is an important crop in the Southern States. It is, in fact, one of the most important. The fertile and well-drained soils on the Piedmont in the Carolinas are especially well suited to growing tobacco. Figure 136 shows a tobacco field on the Piedmont. Some tobacco is also grown on the Atlantic Coastal Plain, particularly in South Carolina and Georgia. 

Planting and cultivating tobacco.

Growing tobacco, as you have already learned, requires much hard work. The seeds are very small and the tiny plants

must have more care than they can get in a field. The seeds, therefore, are usually planted in special beds. 

After the plants begin to grow well they are taken up and set out in the field. Here they must be carefully cultivated, and every now and then all the shoots must be pulled from the stems. Also the plants must be sprayed to kill harmful insects. Figure 135. A truck farm on the Gulf Coastal Plain. The people here are getting carrots ready to be shipped to northern markets. 

Curing tobacco. When the tobacco is ready for cutting, the leaves turn lighter in color. The stalks are then cut and hung upside down in curing barns. These barns usually have many openings in the sides to admit air. Another method of curing, however, is used for most of the tobacco in North Carolina.

This method is called flue-curing because the barns have flues, or pipes, running through them. Heat in these flues warms the air of the barns until it is very hot. In the hot air the tobacco dries quickly.

It does not turn dark, as it does when cured by ordinary drying, but remains a bright brownish yellow.

About half the tobacco crop raised in the United States is now cured by the flue-curing method. 

Courtesy Asheboro Chamber of Commerce 

Figure 136. A North Carolina tobacco field. If you look carefully, you will see several men among the tobacco plants. They will give you an idea of the size of the plants and their leaves. 

Three tobacco cities. On the map find 

Winston-Salem, Durham, and Wilson in 

North Carolina. All of these cities are important tobacco cities in the heart of the great tobacco country. Winston 

Salem and Durham manufacture large quantities of cigars and cigarettes. Wilson is noted as a tobacco market. Since cotton as well as tobacco is raised in the surrounding region, each of the cities has a number of cotton mills. Winston- 

Salem has one of the largest chemical plants in the South, and Durham manufactures large quantities of fertilizer.

FARMING IN THE HIGHLANDS 

In the Great Valley. You will remember that in the Middle Atlantic States you found a region in the Appalachian 

Mountains known as the Great Valley.

This region is not a single valley, but consists of a series of ridges and valleys. 

If you look at the map on pages 6-7, you will find that the Great Valley extends southward into the Southern States. Here it lies between the Great 

Smoky Mountains and the Cumberland

Mountains, and is known as the Valley of East Tennessee. Find these mountains on the map and notice that the Tennessee River flows southward between them. The southern part of the Great 

Valley, like the Shenandoah Valley in 

Virginia, has very fertile soils. Corn is grown on nearly every farm. Dairying, too, is an important industry. 

Tobacco thrives on the well-drained land in the northeastern part of Tennessee.

Farther south in Alabama, where the climate is warmer and the growing season longer, cotton is a leading crop.

Much of the land in the Great Valley outside the Valley of East Tennessee cannot be used for raising crops because it is very hilly. Most of it, however, can be used for pasture. Forests grow on many of the ridges and steeper 'slopes.

On the Cumberland Plateau. On the map find the Cumberland Plateau in eastern Tennessee. Notice that this region is really an extension of the Allegheny Plateau which you studied in the Middle Atlantic States. What river do you find just east of the plateau?

A large part of the Cumberland Plateau is a land of hills and valleys. There are few roads because it costs too much to build roads up steep hills and across deep valleys. People in the more hilly parts of the plateau are almost shut off from the outside world. From the near-by forests they obtain logs with which to build their houses and wood to use as fuel. Often they make their own furniture. They cultivate small patches of ground in the valleys and on the slopes. 

Corn, their chief crop, is used both as food for themselves and as feed for farm animals.

On the Ozark Plateau. Find the 

Ozark Plateau on the map. Notice that it lies in parts of three states. What are the states? Which ones belong to the section you now are studying? The 

Ozark Plateau is a region of hills and valleys somewhat like those of the Cumberland Plateau in eastern Tennessee.

In the wider valleys, especially in Arkansas, there is good land where large crops of cotton are raised. Corn is raised in most parts of the plateau. As on the 

Cumberland Plateau, the corn is used as food for people and as feed for farm animals. 

What does Figure 133 show you about another use made of the land in the 

Ozark Plateau? You can see that the plateau is a good region for fruit. In which state do you find the greater number of dots, Oklahoma or Arkansas? In a number of places you have found fruit trees growing on hillsides. The Ozark 

Plateau is another region in which the farmers have found it profitable to use the slopes for raising fruit. The leading crop is apples.

In the Nashville Basin. Find on the map the part of Tennessee that lies

between the Cumberland Plateau and the Tennessee River on the west. This is the Nashville Basin, so named because the lowland, with a rim of higher lands surrounding it, resembles a large basin. 

Like parts of the Great Valley, it is a very fertile region. It is too far north, however, for many southern crops to be raised. Most farmers raise wheat, corn, cattle, and hogs. A large part of the farming resembles that in the states farther north. The Nashville Basin, too, as you will learn later, is in the region of hardwood forests.

Nashville, a busy city. On the map find Nashville, the capital of Tennessee. 

Notice that it lies in the center of the Nashville Basin. Notice also that several railroads meet in the city. How, then, might you expect to find the people making a living? Nashville is not only an important trading center, but it is a busy manufacturing city as well. The railroads bring in farm products and raw materials and carry away manufactured articles. Among the manufactured products are textiles, furniture, and food stuffs. 

FARMING IN TEXAS AND OKLAHOMA 

Find the state of Texas on the map.

Notice that Oklahoma bounds it on the north. How does it compare in size with the other states in the South? with other states in the United States? Indeed, Texas is so large that you could place all the New England States and all the Middle Atlantic States in it and still have room for more. 

Texas, as you have already learned, leads all the states in the production of cotton. Throughout the state, also, except in the western part, much mixed farming is carried on. Many farmers at the western edge of the Cotton Belt in Texas and Oklahoma raise wheat and sorghum. Peanuts and vegetables are raised in the eastern part of the state not far from Dallas.

The Great Plains. Turn to the map on pages 6-7 and find the Great Plains. 

Now find the same region on the map on pages 8-9. Across which two of the 

Southern States does the region extend? 

What is its altitude? Notice that the western part of Oklahoma and Texas is about 4,000 feet above sea level. 

Much of the land on the Great Plains is very level, but some of it is very rough. 

What does Figure 16 show you about the rainfall on the Great Plains? You will find that the region is very dry. In fact, some places are so dry that little grows except scattered patches of grass known as bunch grass. As a result much of the land cannot well be used for raising crops, and grazing is the leading occupation. 

Raising sheep and goats. In Figure 

137 find where most of the sheep in Texas and Oklahoma are raised. Texas leads all the states in the country in the raising of sheep. It also raises many goats. 

Most of the sheep and goats are raised on the Edwards Plateau, which is really a part of the Great Plains. Indeed, nearly half the goats in the country are raised on the plateau. In some parts of the plateau the land is gently rolling, but in other parts it is hilly and rough. In many places the grass is too short for cattle but is long enough for sheep and goats. These animals can graze on grass that is very close to the ground, because

they have sharp noses and divided upper lips. Moreover, sheep do not need so much water as cattle.

Raising beef cattle. In parts of the 

Great Plains bunch grass grows in abundance. This makes an ideal food for cattle and contains a great deal of nourishment even when it has dried out. 

This fact helps explain why grazing can be carried on in such dry regions as western Texas and Oklahoma. Figure 

139 shows part of one of the many large herds of beef cattle you will find in Texas.

Texas cattle farms or ranches are very Figure 137. Where sheep are raised in the South. In many parts of the Southern States sheep are not raised because of the warm, moist climate. Figure 139. Beef cattle on the plains of western Texas. These cattle have been brought in from the range and fattened for market. They are now awaiting shipment to one of the meat-packing cities. large. A single ranch may contain from 

8,000 to 300,000 acres. Where grass is scarce, one cow needs 25 to 40 acres on which to graze. How much land, then, is needed for three hundred cattle, which is a small herd for a ranch? Now tell in your own words why ranches are so large as compared with other farms. 

Most of the cattle raised on ranches are grown for meat rather than milk.

Such cattle, therefore, are larger than most dairy cows and are known as beef cattle. Beef cattle must be fattened before they are ready for market. Although the bunch grass of the Great Plains contains plenty of food to make cattle grow, it does not contain enough to make them fat. Thus before the cattle are sent to market they are often taken to a region where there is enough corn for fattening. From the fattening pens they are shipped on to large meatpacking centers. Every year thousands of cattle are shipped to Fort Worth, which ranks high among the larger stockyard cities or meat-packing centers in the country. 

By studying Figure 138 you will find that beef cattle are raised to some extent in every state throughout the South. In a recent year, however, Texas ranked first, not only in the South, but in the entire country.

El Paso and southwestern Texas. In southwestern Texas along the Rio Grande is a very rich farming region. The land is fertile and water is available from the 

Rio Grande for irrigation. Irrigation is a method of supplying water to the land, usually by means of little ditches. The water here comes from a large lake in the river where the water is held back

El Paso, it is far away from the other large cities of Texas. Amarillo is in the heart of a great cattle and wheat country. 

Its chief factories are railroad shops, meat-packing plants, and a plant where most of the helium in the United States is prepared. Helium is a gas which is used in dirigibles and balloons. Near by are oil and gas wells.

TWO IMPORTANT SEAPORTS IN TEXAS 

Houston-the world's greatest cotton port. On the map find the city of 

Houston. Notice that it is situated on a small river that flows into Galveston Bay. This river has been deepened and

made into a canal so that large ocean vessels can come up to trade. Houston is near the Black Waxy Prairie cotton lands. What, then, would you expect to be the leading export? Houston's excellent site near the cotton lands has helped it become the greatest cotton-shipping port in the world. All along the water front there are great sheds where cotton may be stored until it is shipped away.

Galveston, like Houston, is an important cotton-shipping port. It does not, however, ship so much cotton as Houston.

LUMBERING IN THE SOUTHERN STATES 

In the early days of the South most of the land was covered with forests. Here, as in New England, it was necessary for the early settlers to cut down trees in order to clear fields for farming. Although much of the timber has been cut, there still are large forests in certain sections of the South. Most of the forests form a belt from fifty to a hundred miles wide along the coast from 

North Carolina to Texas. Altogether the Southern States supply more than a third of the annual output of lumber in the entire country and also much wood pulp for southern paper mills.

Lumbering in the southern pine forests. 

Logging in the southern pine forests differs from logging in New England. 

In the North the logs are carried on sleds in the winter and floated downstream to sawmills in the spring. On the level land of the Atlantic and Gulf Coastal

Plains, however, railroads can easily be built for hauling the logs. First a railroad is built into the heart of the forest. 

Then the trees on each side of the railroad are cut. The logs from these trees are hauled to the track by a skidding machine, where they are loaded on flat cars and taken to a sawmill. Figure 142 shows a skidding machine at work. The strong ropes or cables you see in the picture are used to pull the logs to the railway. Sometimes these ropes are as much as a thousand feet long.

LUMBERING IN  

141 

Making naval stores. Tar and turpentine are known as naval stores because tar products were once used to paint the bottom of wooden ships so that they would not leak.

If a cut is made in the bark of southern pine, or a few other varieties of pine, a thick oily substance called resin will flow from the tree. 

It is from this resin that naval stores, such as turpentine, rosin, and tar, are made.

Figure 143 shows how most of the trees are tapped.

Small cuts are made and the resin runs from little gutters to cups fastened to the trees. 

The resin is collected from the cups and poured into barrels. It is then heated in a kettle or pan. The vapor which passes off is collected and cooled to form liquid turpentine. The substance left in the kettle after the turpentine has been removed is called rosin. 

Photo by A. Gaskill, courtesy U. 8. Forest Service 

Figure 142. Logging in the South. Near the center of the picture is the trunk of a tree with cables extending into the woods. The steam engine pulls one of the cables and drags a log to the car on which it is to be loaded. steam or The states that lead in the production of naval stores are Georgia, Florida, and 

Alabama. Can you now understand why you saw so much turpentine and rosin being exported from Savannah, Jacksonville, and other southern ports? 

Southern hardwoods. The southern hardwood forests are the most important in the country. They contain oak, maple, chestnut, hickory, elm, beech, magnolia, gum, and other trees. The hardwoods grow largely in the Appalachian Mountains and surrounding regions, but a belt of hardwood trees also extends into Arkansas, Oklahoma, and Texas. Now look 

Figure 143. Trees tapped for turpentine. When the sap ceases to flow from one group of cuts, another set is made a little higher. The cup to catch the sap is moved up also.

Figure 144. A cypress swamp. The stumplike projections in the water are called cypress knees. They grow up from the roots of cypress trees. Lumbering for cypress is very difficult, for men must paddle out to the trees in boats. After the trees have been cut, the logs must be floated to places where they can be loaded. Often they are pulled out with long cables. at Figure 55 and notice the number of dots in the hardwood region. 

Just east of the Appalachian Moun tains on the Piedmont is a mixed hardwood and softwood region. Here both hardwoods and pines grow together in the same forests. This belt of mixed trees extends all the way from Pennsylvania in the North to Mississippi in the 

South. Figure 145 will show you the regions in the South where the various kinds of trees grow. 

Lumbering in the hardwood forests. 

In parts of the hardwood forest region, such as the Ozark and the Cumberland plateaus, hardwood timber is used only to help supply the needs of the people. You have learned how these people cut logs for their houses, their furniture, and their fires. In less shut-off parts of the highlands lumber companies have taken over large tracts of land. The logs are usually hauled to streams and floated downstream to sawmills. From the saw-

Cypress and palms. Cypress trees grow in swamps and other lowlands such as you will see in Figure 144. They grow chiefly in Florida and on the Mississippi flood plain. Cypress lumber is one of the most popular softwoods. It is used for making the framework and siding of houses and for making shingles and fence posts. One of the reasons why it is so popular is that it decays or rots much more slowly than most other kinds of lumber.

Palm trees are found in many parts of the South, especially in the lands that are farthest south. These trees have no value as lumber but are grown only for their great scenic beauty.

MINING IN THE SOUTHERN STATES 

Mining is an important occupation in most of the Southern States. Some mining or quarrying is done in nearly every state. The leading product is petroleum, which is found in the states west of the Mississippi River. In fact, these states contain the greatest oilproducing regions in the country. 

THE LIQUID MINERAL-PETROLEUM 

There are two general regions or fields where oil is produced in the South, the 

Mid-Continent Field and the Gulf Field. 

Find each of these important fields in

Figure 146. The Mid-Continent Field produces more oil than any other field in the country, yielding more than half of all the oil produced. The leading states in this field are Texas and Oklahoma.
