A group of derricks close together always marks a strong producing region. Can you suggest what becomes of much of the oil from the Mid-Continent Field? What becomes of the oil from the Gulf Field? How is oil taken to market? 

known as the East Texas Field. Find this field on the map. 

Drilling and pumping. You have already learned that oil is found in bedrock deep down in the earth. To get the oil, wells must be dug into the rock. 

These wells are dug by heavy steel drills that slowly hammer or bore their way into the earth. The tall derricks that you see in Figure 147 are used to support the drills. The little sheds or houses near by contain the engines or motors that provide the power. 

Petroleum is pumped much as water is pumped from wells. Usually several pumps are fastened together by cables so that they may be operated by one engine or motor. More oil is usually taken from the wells than can be shipped away at one time. Consequently, large tanks are needed for storage. The oil is left in the tanks until it is sent to refineries. 

Shipping to refineries. Petroleum as it comes from the wells is known as crude oil. Before it is ready for use, it must be refined and made into gasoline, kerosene, and various other products. 

Crude oil from the tanks in the oil fields is sometimes hauled to refineries in tank cars on railroads, or in tank trucks if the distance is not too great. From most of the larger fields, however, the oil flows to refineries through pipes laid beneath the surface of the ground. These pipes are from six to ten inches thick and extend across hills and plains, over mountains, and across rivers. Great power is needed to force the oil through the pipes.

Pumping stations, therefore, are located about twenty miles apart all along the lines. By means of the pumps the oil is forced along for hundreds of miles.

Figure 146 shows the pipe lines through which the oil is pumped.

MINING IN  

145 

OIL CITIES IN THE MID-CONTINENT FIELD 

When oil is discovered in a region, it becomes a very busy place. Near-by towns and cities prosper because of the oil. Sometimes these towns and cities grow very rapidly. This has been especially true of some of the cities in the 

Mid-Continent Field. 

Three prosperous cities. On the map find Oklahoma City and Tulsa in Oklahoma and Wichita Falls in Texas.

Notice that Oklahoma City is the capital of Oklahoma and an important railroad center. Most of the factories here are refineries and other plants that depend on the productive oil fields that surround the city. Then, too, the city has a number of flour mills and meat-packing plants. Most of the people in Tulsa are also interested in oil. Here you will find oil refineries that make a number of products from oil. Near Tulsa, too, there are mines from which large quantities of coal, lead, and zinc are obtained.

Both Oklahoma City and Tulsa have grown very rapidly since the discovery of oil in the Mid-Continent Field. Today when people think of oil in Oklahoma, they usually think of one of these two cities. 

Wichita Falls is in the center of the oil fields of north-central Texas. Like 

Oklahoma City and Tulsa, it has a number of refineries and most of the people are interested in oil. It also has a number of other factories that depend on the surrounding region, especially flour mills.

SEAPORTS THAT PROFIT FROM OIL 

Port Arthur and Beaumont. You have already learned that there are large oil refineries at Houston, one of the seaports near the Gulf Field. Two other ports are also important for their refineries and for the shipping of oil. These two ports are Port Arthur and Beaumont. 

Find these cities on the map. Port

Arthur has large oil refineries that refine petroleum from most of the oil fields. of the South.

Beaumont is situated on a river at the head of ocean navigation. Oil is brought to the city in pipe lines. Like 

Port Arthur, its neighboring city, it has large oil refineries and ships large quantities of oil products to many parts of the world. Beaumont and Port Arthur together make one of the greatest oil centers in the country. 

REGIONS AND CITIES HELPED BY COAL AND IRON 

Regions of coal. The South, like the 

Middle Atlantic States, is favored with coal, but mining is not such an important occupation as it is on the Allegheny Figure 148. Where coal beds are found in the South. The solid black spots show where coal is being mined at present.

Plateau. The coal in the South is mined largely in eastern Tennessee and northern 

Alabama. Look at Figure 148 and find each of these regions. Now look at the map to see whether the regions are low or mountainous. How do they compare with the region where coal is found on the Allegheny Plateau?

In order to mine the coal in the South, it usually is not necessary to dig deep shafts into the ground. This is because the seams, or layers of coal, lie near the surface of the ground. Most of the coal is mined by digging tunnels into the hillsides. In some places the seams are so near the surface that the coal may be reached merely by removing the covering of dirt. Getting minerals in this way is known as open-pit mining.

Regions of iron ore. The iron ore in the South, like the coal, is found in eastern Tennessee and northern Alabama.

The most important mines are near Birmingham in northern Alabama. In most places limestone is found near the iron.

MINING SULPHUR AND SALT 

Taking sulphur from the ground. 

Nearly all the sulphur produced in the 

United States comes from the Gulf

Coastal Plain of Texas and Louisiana. 

The sulphur beds here are hundreds of feet below the surface of the ground. 

Wells are drilled to these beds, and hot water is forced down through pipes to the bottoms of the wells. The hot water melts the sulphur so that it can be run into tanks where it soon cools and hardens into huge blocks of pure sulphur. These blocks are crushed or broken up into smaller pieces and loaded on freight cars for shipment.

Taking salt from the ground. Not far from the sulphur beds in Louisiana and Texas are found large deposits of rock salt. The salt is obtained from mines three or four hundred feet below the surface of the ground. A visit to a salt mine would remind you of your trip to a coal mine. You would enter the mine by a shaft and would find tunnels leading to large rooms or chambers where the rock salt is blasted.

Salt has a great many uses and is a very valuable product. Perhaps some of the salt on your table at home came from mines in either Texas or Louisiana. 

MINING PHOSPHATE 

Fertilizers, as you know, are added to soils to keep them fertile. Most fertilizers are made out of waste products and minerals. One of the most valuable of all minerals is phosphate. Other minerals used are nitrate and potash. The 

United States has very few deposits of nitrate and potash, but it possesses the world's largest supply of phosphate. 

Most of the phosphate at present is mined in the Gulf coastal lands near 

Tampa. Find Tampa again on the map. 

Phosphate is also mined in Tennessee.

The rocks are always fairly close to the surface. They are dug from the earth with steam shovels. Before they can be shipped, they must be crushed and washed. The product is sent to market by rail, and also by boat from Tampa. 

MARBLE, POTTERY, CLAY, AND BAUXITE 

In Alabama, Tennessee, and especially in Georgia an abundance of marble is found. If you were to visit any of these marble-producing regions, you would find quarrying being carried on in very much. the same way as in New England. 

Clay used in making chinaware and pottery is found in North Carolina and 

Georgia. Near the deposits in these states you would find factories similar to those in New Jersey.

In Tennessee, Alabama, and central Arkansas there is a special kind of clay that contains the ore of aluminum. This clay is known as bauxite. Since it is found near the surface of the ground, bauxite is dug by steam shovels and loaded in railroad cars. After it has reached the factories, the aluminum is removed by electricity.

FISHING IN SOUTHERN WATERS 

In the Atlantic Ocean. On your trip along the Atlantic coast you found much fishing being done, although the industry here is not so important as it is farther north. Among the fish that are caught are the shad, mackerel, and mullet. A large fish known as the tarpon is often caught for sport. 

In the Gulf of Mexico. Fishing is more important in the waters along the Gulf coast than along the Atlantic coast of the Southern States. For a long time it was impossible to ship fish from this warm southern region because they would spoil before they reached the northern markets. Now, however, the fish are packed in ice soon after they are caught and may be sent long distances safely. 

Red snapper, mullet, and squeteague, or sea trout, are some of the leading fish caught. The most important products of the fishing industry, however, are oysters and shrimps. These are found along the Gulf coast. Most of the canned oysters and shrimps used in the United

States come from this region. The oyster industry will probably become greater, too, because oyster beds are given better care than formerly, and many new beds are found each year. In addition to the products just mentioned, clams and large turtles are also found. The men shown in Figure 151 are hauling in shrimps. 

Fishing ports on the Gulf. Look at the map and find Pensacola, Florida; 

Biloxi, Mississippi; and Galveston, Texas.

These are leading ports for the various fishing boats, and are the shipping centers for the fish products after they are prepared for market. Many oysters and shrimps are canned at Biloxi.

Courtesy Biloxi Chamber of Commerce 

Figure 151. A shrimp trawler near Biloxi. The fishermen are hauling in the net loaded with shrimps. 

THE SOUTHERN STATES AS A PLAYGROUND 

The South attracts many visitors and tourists from the North because of its subtropical climate. In the winter when much of the North is covered with snow and ice, the South has bright sunshine and balmy weather. The ground is covered with green grass and the trees with green foliage. Vegetables and other crops grow in the gardens and fruits in the orchards. It is easy to understand, then, why thousands of people spend their winters in Florida, Georgia, Texas, and other Southern States. They like the moderate climate of these states rather than the colder climate farther north. Some people own homes both in the North and in the South. They spend the winters in the South and the summers in the North and thus enjoy delightful weather throughout the year. 

Favorite spots in the highlands.

Throughout the mountains and bordering highlands in the Southern States there

THE Courtesy Asheville Chamber of Commerce 

Figure 152. Asheville and its basin. You can see from the picture that the valley around Asheville may be called a basin. Perhaps you can see, too, why Asheville is noted as a popular tourist resort. If you were to drive out from the city in almost any direction, you would find winding roads, rushing mountain streams, and clear mountain lakes. are beautiful wooded slopes. Parts of the mountains are so steep and rocky that nothing but trees will grow. In many places the government has taken over the woodlands and made great forest reserves. Here only the largest trees are cut, and new trees are always planted to take their places. The planting of new trees to take the place of old ones and the saving of forests in various ways is known as forest conservation.

In the midst of the wooded regions of the South there are a number of health and summer resorts where people may escape the summer heat. 

On the map find Asheville, North 

Carolina. Near this city a small river has cut a gap through the Blue Ridge. 

This pass has helped to make Asheville an important highway and railroad junction. The city is best known, however, as a mountain playground, and is often mentioned as a city in the "Land of the Sky." Figure 152 is a picture showing Asheville with the Blue Ridge in the distance. 

Find Hot Springs, Arkansas, on the map. Here the government has set aside a large tract of land as a national park. 

The location of the city on the edge of the highlands gives it an ideal climate in both summer and winter. In addition to the natural forest setting, it has, as its name suggests, many hot springs. 

Its climate and springs have helped it become a noted health resort.

UNIT FIVE  INTRODUCING THE NORTH CENTRAL STATES The section of the United States you are now about to study is often called the "bread basket" of the country. In order that you may understand just why it is given such a name, suppose you try to get a picture of some of its excellent farms. You might enjoy an automobile trip through the North Central States in early summer when all the land is covered with growing crops. You will enjoy, too, riding over some of the excellent highways for which this part of the country is especially noted.

Winter wheat and mixed farming. A good place to begin your trip is Wichita, Kansas, in the heart of a wheat region.

Find Wichita on the map on pages 156157. In Kansas, wheat is planted in the fall. Since it lives through the winter, it is known as winter wheat. 

From Kansas eastward, the southern part of the North Central States is often called the Winter-Wheat Belt because so much winter wheat is grown. As you travel eastward into Missouri, you find that many other crops are grown along with wheat. In other words, you are coming into a region where less winter wheat is grown and where more of the land is used for mixed farming.

A greater variety of crops is raised in the eastern part of the North Central States than in the western part. There are two reasons why this is true. First, 152

INTRODUCING  

153 as you can see in Figure 16, there is more rain in the eastern part than in the western part. Second, the western part is almost a level plain, while the eastern part is a mixture of hilly lands and lowlands. Where there are differences in the surface of the land, as there are in the eastern part, you will always find a greater variety of crops. 

As you travel eastward, then, through 

Missouri, southern Illinois, Indiana, and 

Ohio, you find fields of oats and other grains, especially corn. You see much land in pasture and many fields planted to such crops as clover and timothy, which are grown for hay. You see also many hogs, cattle, and sheep. There are orchards and truck farms, and in 

Kentucky you find field after field of tobacco. 

Mixed farming and the Corn Belt. 

Suppose you now turn northward from

Kentucky to Columbus in central Ohio. 

Find Columbus on the map. This city lies in the heart of a mixed-farming region where the farmers raise corn, wheat, and oats. You also see hogs on every farm and many cattle and sheep grazing in the pastures. At Columbus you turn westward and travel across western Ohio, Indiana, Illinois, and Iowa. 

The farther west you go, the more land you find planted to corn, for you are now driving through the great Corn Belt.

This vast region stretches east and west nearly all the way from one side of the North Central States to the other.

Although you find much mixed farming throughout the Corn Belt, everywhere you look you see corn and hogs. You also see many beef cattle being fattened for market. In fact, more beef cattle are found in the Corn Belt than in any other part of the United States. 

A spring-wheat region. When you have traveled through the fertile Corn 

Belt as far as Omaha, Nebraska, you turn northward into the Dakotas. Find

Omaha on the map. Upon reaching 

South Dakota, you can see that you have left the Corn Belt. You are now in the spring-wheat region where, as far as you can see, the land seems to be all one field of waving wheat. The wheat here is called spring wheat because it is planted in the spring. If you went on north to the boundary of the United States and even farther, you would still be surrounded by endless fields of wheat. 

Certain other crops are raised, as you will learn later, but they are so much less important than the wheat that you scarcely notice them.

A dairy and fruit region. In the 

Dakotas you turn eastward toward Minneapolis and St. Paul in Minnesota. Find these cities on cities on the map. the map. Soon you begin to see corn again, but the many silos tell you that much of the corn is used as silage. All the country eastward to Lake Michigan is a mixedfarming region. Dairying, however, is the most important industry. On nearly every farm across Minnesota and Wisconsin you see wide pastures dotted with trees and many fields of hay. This hay may be clover, timothy, alfalfa, or soybeans. In the pastures and barnyards are large numbers of dairy cows. Here, too, you see many fields of vegetables, especially potatoes, and a great many orchards. 

When you reach Lake Michigan, you find you must travel southward around

the lake. In your trip through the North

Central States you have seen many large cities. Near the southern end of the lake you come upon the largest city in the section. This city is Chicago. With its suburbs it stretches for miles along the southwestern shore of the lake. Near

Chicago you find many more truck and dairy farms than you found in the less thickly settled parts of the country. Find 

Chicago on the map and notice where it is situated on the lake. 

After leaving Chicago you travel northward on the eastern shore of Lake 

Michigan. Here you pass through one of the greatest fruit regions in the United

States. The road is lined with orchards and vineyards. Soon you turn eastward toward Detroit, Michigan. Find this city on the map. On the way across 

Michigan you see fields of wheat and oats and a few fields of corn. Also you see a great many dairy farms, chicken farms, fruit farms, and truck farms. 

At Detroit you swing southward and follow the shore of Lake Erie through 

Toledo, Ohio, to Cleveland, Ohio. Find both these cities on the map. At first you find many truck farms, but along the southern shore of the lake you pass through another great fruit region. The drive here is very beautiful. On the one side you look out on the clear, blue waters of Lake Erie, and on the other side you see orchards and vineyards as well as a variety of crops stretching away on the Lake Plain.

ORCHARD 

Figure 156. A farm in the mixedfarming region. This is a farm of about 

80 acres. The farmer can do nearly all the work himself, with the help of his family. The farm provides him with nearly all his food. 

The farmer leaves the field marked pasture in grass from year to year. In this pasture there are trees for shade, and a stream where the animals can drink. The horses are turned into the pasture when they are not working. 

Corn and hay are fed to the animals in winter. Some of the corn is cut green for ensilage, but most of it is allowed to ripen. Perhaps some of the corn will be sold. A little of the wheat will be kept for the chickens, but most of it will be sold. The clover and timothy will be cut and stored in the barn as hay. 

Next year the farmer may plant corn where he now has timothy, and wheat where he now has corn. He may plant timothy where he now has clover, and clover in this year's wheat field. He may even change his hay crops and raise alfalfa, soybeans, or some other crop that can be used for hay. He may also plant oats instead of part of the wheat next year. The changing of crops from year to year on a piece of soil is, as you know, called rotation of crops.

IN THE REGION OF MIXED FARMING 

As you traveled through the North 

Central States you learned that mixed farming is carried on in nearly every state. In Kansas and the Dakotas you found great fields of wheat, but even here mixed farming is carried on to some extent. While Iowa and parts of Nebraska, Missouri, South Dakota, Minnesota, Illinois, Indiana, and Ohio belong to a region called the Corn Belt, much other farming is carried on along with the raising of corn. Although Michigan, Wisconsin, and Minnesota are important dairying states, the farms in these states produce a variety of products. In parts of the North Central States, as in Ohio, Indiana, and Illinois, several crops are raised and no one crop is of much more value than the others. One farmer may raise more wheat than his neighbor, or he may keep more hogs, but no one product is much more important than another. 

If you could look down from an airplane on some of the farms of this region, you might see a number of different crops.

No one of the crops, however, would cover much more land than the others. 

Here, then, is a region of real mixed farming. You would see many farms like those shown in Figure 153. The drawing in Figure 156 shows how a farmer in the mixed-farming region often uses his land. Notice the several fields.

Make a list of the products that the owner of this farm may have to sell.

IN THE REGION OF MIXED FARMING 

159 In various places in the mixed-farming region there are small areas where conditions favor the growing of certain crops or the raising of certain animals. These conditions may be a favorable soil, a favorable climate, good transportation, or nearness to market.

The 

Truck farming. Near every large city, and in certain fertile regions, truck farming is an important industry. In a number of places in Ohio, Indiana,

Illinois, and Michigan there are fertile lowlands and areas of swampy land or small lakes that have been drained. loose, black soil in these places is especially well suited to growing vegetables, such as onions, beans, cabbage, and tomatoes. In Figure 157 you will see a truck farm in southern Michigan planted to celery.

Truck farms, of course, must either be near cities or be favored with good means of transportation. Figure 158 shows where most of the vegetables are grown in the North Central States. Are most of the vegetables grown in the eastern or western part of the section? 

Give several reasons why more vegetables are grown in the eastern part. Are some of the vegetable regions located around large cities? Name some of the cities. 

Raising potatoes. Potatoes grow well in regions that have cool, moist summers. 

Figure 159 shows where potatoes are grown in the North Central States.

Notice how many dots there are in eastcentral Minnesota, in central Wisconsin, in north-central and eastern Michigan, and in northeastern Ohio. In all these regions you will find large fields of potatoes on nearly every farm. Altogether the regions produce about one-fourth of Figure 157. A truck farm in Michigan. The plants growing in long rows are celery. Notice how dark the soil is. Celery must have a moist, black soil to grow well, and can be raised successfully in only a few parts of the country. Michigan is one of the leading states in the raising of celery and various other vegetables. all the potatoes grown in the country. 

Look at the map to find where the potatoes go to market. Do near-by large cities suggest a reason why the potatoes are grown? Does the color on the map tell you that they can be easily shipped from the fields?

Special products. In various parts of the North Central States there are regions known for special crops or products. 

For instance, you may have wondered where the grocer gets the dry beans he sells in his store. More of these beans are raised in central Michigan than in any other state. A great many watermelons are raised in southern Indiana.

Peppermint, too, grows in Indiana and in the neighboring states. Cranberries 10-SG

LARGE REGIONS OF SPECIALIZED FARMING Within the North Central States there are several large regions of specialized farming. The farmers in these regions carry on mixed farming, but depend on one crop or product as the chief means of making a living.

IN THE SUGAR-BEET COUNTRY 

You have learned that sugar is made from a plant grown in the South. Do you know, though, that sugar is also made from a plant grown in the North?

This plant is the sugar beet, which is somewhat larger and not so red as the beet you eat as a vegetable.

The sugar beet grows best in a cool climate where the summers are not too warm and where there is a moderate amount of rainfall. It must have a growing season nearly five months long. 

Look at Figure 161 and find regions in

Figure 163. A field of sugar beets. These beets are nearly grown. Earlier in the season you would have found a number of workers in the field. 

Figure 164. A sugar-beet loader. The loader has a shovel in front to scoop up the beets. The wheels are made like those of a caterpillar tractor so that the loader will not sink in soft ground. 

Michigan, Ohio, and western Nebraska where the sugar beet grows. In western 

Nebraska the beet grows in lands that are irrigated. 

How the beets are raised. Sugar beets are raised in large fields such as you see in Figure 163. The work of growing beets, however, is very difficult because they must be thinned and weeded by hand. They must also be cultivated several times while they are growing. 

When the harvest season comes, they must be plowed out of the ground and the tops must be cut off with a knife. Then the beets are piled up in the fields and covered with straw for protection from the hot sun. In Figure 164 you will see piles of sugar beets ready to be taken to the sugar mill. 

How the sugar is made. If you were to go to a sugar mill, you would see that making sugar from beets is different from making sugar from cane. First the beets are washed and cut into slices. Next they are soaked in hot water to draw out the sweet juice. Then the juice is heated in pans so that the water will steam off and leave the raw sugar in the bottom. 

This sugar, like raw cane sugar, must be refined before it can be used.

IN THE TOBACCO COUNTRY 

Look at Figure 161 to see where tobacco is grown in the North Central States. 

Which state produces the largest crop?

Notice that the tobacco region of Kentucky extends over into southern Ohio and Indiana. Tobacco is also grown to some extent in southern Wisconsin. 

Kentucky tobacco. In what other parts of the country have you found tobacco. an important crop? The kind of tobacco grown in a region usually depends upon the soil and climate. Kentucky raises both a dark variety of tobacco and a famous light-colored tobacco known as 

Burley. This kind of tobacco is not generally used for cigars, but is made into chewing tobacco, smoking tobacco, and cigarettes. 

If you were to travel through the tobacco region of Kentucky, you would see hundreds of fields of tobacco like that shown in Figure 165. You would notice

hundreds of barns where tobacco is hung for drying. 

Each of these barns would have many openings along the side. You would also see great numbers of workers in the fields, for the raising of tobacco, as you have already learned, requires much work with the hands. In some ways the growing of tobacco is like the growing of sugar beets. Both are expensive crops to raise. 

Century Photos 

Figure 165. A Kentucky tobacco field. This tobacco is younger than that shown in Figure 136. The leaves will become much larger. 

Two cities of the blue grass region. Find Lexing ton on the map. This city is situated in the heart of the bluegrass region, famous for its excellent pastures. 

On these pastures are kept some of the finest race horses in the world, as well as many herds of cattle. Lexington also is noted as a tobacco market and has many tobacco warehouses and factories. You may think of Lexington, then, as a thriving city surrounded by a rich agri cultural region. 

Find Frankfort on the map. What does the sign tell you about the city? 

What advantages would you say Frankfort has as a capital city? Like Lexington, it is in the tobacco country and in the heart of the bluegrass region.

IN THE FRUIT COUNTRY 

Along the lakes. On nearly every farm in the North Central States are found a few fruit trees and berry bushes. There are certain regions, however, where farmers make a living largely by growing fruit. 

Look at Figure 166 to find where most fruit is grown. Notice that the chief fruit regions are along Lake Michigan and Lake Erie. Why would you expect to find the lands bordering these lakes good regions for the growing of fruit? 

In what other regions have you found fruit growing along the lakes?

The lake regions are especially good for growing apples, cherries, peaches, pears, plums, and grapes. In some places, especially in areas along Lake Erie, grapes are the leading crop. On the eastern shore of Lake Michigan is a region called the "Cherry Country." 

On the west, the peninsula between

Green Bay and Lake Michigan, too, is famous for cherries.

Notice that much fruit is also grown in southern Ohio, in southern Illinois, and in southwestern Missouri. What does the map tell you about the surface of the land in most of these regions? You have already learned that fruit grows well in hilly regions where it is protected from early frosts. The leading crop in most of the hilly regions is apples.

IN THE DAIRY COUNTRY 

While dairy cows are kept in most of the North Central States, there is one large region where dairying is a very important industry. Find this region in 

Figure 167. What three states lie largely in the dairy region? Which state keeps the most cows? Wisconsin sells millions of gallons of milk and cream, makes large quantities of butter, and produces more cheese than all the other states together. Minnesota also sells large quantities of milk and cream, but makes more butter than any other state. Michigan makes less cheese and butter, but sells milk and cream to near-by cities. 

Why there is a dairy country. Dairy cattle, as you have already learned, thrive in a region where there is plenty of hay and pasture. Moreover, they thrive where the climate is moderately cool. Look at Figure 17 and notice the length of the growing season in Minnesota, Wisconsin, and Michigan. While this season is too short for growing certain crops, it is about right for growing hay, grass for pasture, and corn for silage. 

Much of the corn that is raised in the dairy country is cut green and put into silos for winter feed. Figure 170 shows a Wisconsin pasture.

Look at the map and notice the number of large cities in or near the dairy region. 

Name some of these cities. Would you say that they provide a good market for milk and other dairy products? Look again at the map. What does it tell you about the surface of the land? Why would you expect to find numerous highways and railroads over which milk and a number of other products may be sent to market?

IN THE CORN BELT 

Although corn is raised to a certain extent in all the North Central States, there is one great region where so much corn is grown that it is known as the 

Corn Belt. Look at Figure 168 to see where this region is. What states lie partly in the Corn Belt? Now turn to page 358 and make an outline map of the North Central States. Draw a line around the part of the map that shows the region included in the Corn Belt. 

Mixed farming in the Corn Belt. You will remember that in the Cotton Belt the farmer depends largely on his cotton for

Century Photos 

Figure 171. Corn and hogs. You might find a scene such as this on almost any farm in the Corn Belt. The hogs are eating corn from the ear. Beyond them are cows grazing in a pasture, and beyond the cows is a field of corn. The picture shows one way in which corn goes to market. Would you say that the hogs are about ready for shipping? a living, but usually raises several other crops. In the Corn Belt the farmer depends largely on corn, but raises considerable wheat, oats, barley, and rye.

He also raises hay and keeps a few cows. 

In addition he has a garden and an orchard that supply most of his vegetables and fruit. Thus much mixed farming is carried on even in the Corn Belt. 

If you 

Many hogs and cattle. Perhaps you have wondered what the farmers of the 

Corn Belt do with their corn. were to travel through this part of the 

North Central States, you would soon understand, for on every farm you would see hogs and cattle. As the corn is gathered from the fields it is stored in cribs or granaries. Later it is used for fattening hogs and cattle. The farmers have found that they can make more money by feeding corn to hogs and cattle than they can by selling the corn. Most of the corn from the Corn Belt, then, goes to market in the form of fattened cattle and hogs. In Figure 171 you will see a bunch of hogs on a farm in Iowa. 

West of the Corn Belt, as you will soon learn, is a great grazing region, known as the Great Plains, where many beef cattle are raised. The cattle grow well on the grass of the Great Plains, but do not become very fat. Many cattle from

the Great Plains are sold to the farmers in the western part of the Corn Belt.

These cattle are fattened on corn. In some parts of the Corn Belt there are also many dairy cows. Figure 172 will show you how much of the corn is fed to hogs and cattle.

How corn is raised. Corn is planted in the spring just as soon as there is no danger of frost. Most corn is planted in 

May, although it may be planted as late as early June. The machine used in planting is known as a corn planter. As soon as the corn is a few inches tall it must be cultivated to prevent the growth of weeds. All through June and July the farmer must keep on cultivating.

When the corn is about three feet high, it begins to shade the ground so greatly that few weeds can grow. From then on no more work need be done on the corn until harvest.

How corn is harvested. In July little ears begin to form about halfway up the stalks, and tassels appear at the top of the stalks. The corn ripens during the fall and the farmers harvest it in various ways. Often they cut it with a machine, known as a binder, or by hand, and put it into shocks like those shown in Figure 174. Here they leave it until they can find time to husk it-that is, remove the husks from the ears. The husking of corn, like the cutting, may be done either by machine or by hand. On large farms the farmers commonly use a machine, known as a picker, that jerks the ears from the stalks and husks them in one operation. 

Sometimes they jerk the ears by hand or allow hogs to get them. hogs eat 40 bushels horses and mules eat 20 bushels cattle eat 

15 bushels people eat 

10 bushels poultry eats

Figure 172. How most of every 100 bushels of corn raised in the North Central States is used. Part of the 8 bushels left is sold and shipped away. 

Figure 173. Where hogs are raised in the United States.

This tells you that each of the cities lies in a rich farming country. 

Ohio's capital city. Figure 

175 shows a downtown view of Columbus along the Scioto 

River. The large buildings that you see suggest that

Columbus is a busy city. If you look at the map, you will see that it is also a very important railroad center. 

Because of its central location in the state and its many railroads and good highways, it serves as a shipping center for a large farming region. It also has several large meat-packing plants. To the southeast lie the rich coal deposits of the Allegheny Plateau. 

By way of the Great Lakes iron ore is within easy reach. As a result, Columbus has blast furnaces and factories that make machinery, farm implements, and other iron and steel products.

Figure 174. Corn in shocks, a common autumn scene in many parts of the country. The shocks are built when the farmer cuts his corn with a machine or by hand. If he uses a picker or picks the corn from the stalks by hand, of course, he leaves the stalks standing in the field. 

In dairy regions, they often cut the corn green and put it into silos. 

Why there is a Corn Belt. Corn grows best on black fertile soil. If you look at the map, you will see that the land in the Corn Belt is low and fairly level. In fact, all the region is known as the Central Lowland. Besides a fertile soil, corn needs sunshine and rain. The frequent thundershowers in the Corn Belt and the long, hot days make good growing. weather for corn. Figure 16 will show you that all the Corn Belt has plenty of rain. In addition to sunshine and rain, corn must also have a growing season of at least 140 days.

FIVE STATE CAPITALS 

Look at the map and find the capitals of Ohio, Indiana, Illinois, Iowa, and Nebraska. If you were to draw a line through these cities, it would pass almost through the middle of the Corn Belt. 

Indiana's capital city. Indianapolis is the largest city in the United States that is not on a navigable river or other body of water. Like Columbus, it is an important railroad center. Its railroads and excellent highways have helped it become the leading shipping center for much of the farming region in central Indiana. Indianapolis is also a manufacturing city.

Meat-packing plants, automobile factories, and machine shops furnish employment for many workers. How does the location of Indianapolis explain one great advantage the city has for manufacturing? 

The capital of Illinois. Springfield, which is near the center of Illinois, is the

Figure 175. Columbus, Ohio. The picture was taken from under the arch of a bridge over the Scioto River. meeting place for a number of railroads.

It is on a direct line between Chicago and St. Louis. How does this help to explain the fact that Springfield is a good shipping point? Most of the products shipped from Springfield come from farms in the surrounding region. It has flour mills, machine shops, and a variety of industries. Then, too, it is not far from productive coal fields.

The capital of Iowa. Des Moines, like all the other capitals so far mentioned, is near the center of the state. Like the others, it is also an important railroad center. Being in the heart of the Corn 

Belt, it trades largely in farm products. 

Among its factories are meat-packing plants, steel and iron works, and factories for making agricultural implements. 

The capital of Nebraska. Of all important cities in the Corn Belt, Lincoln is the farthest west. Unlike the other capitals, it is not in the center of the state, but it is the leading shipping center for a fertile farming region. It has meatpacking plants and canning factories. It also is the home of one of the leading airplane factories in the country. 

OTHER CORN BELT CITIES 

Manufacturing centers in western Ohio.

Find Dayton on the map. This city has many manufacturing plants. Its leading industry is the making of cash registers. 

In fact, you may think of Dayton and cash registers just as you think of Pittsburgh and steel. Dayton, too, has some of the greatest airplane factories and flying fields in the world. It also manufactures electric refrigerators and has machine shops and foundries.

Find Lima north of Dayton, Spring field northeast of Dayton, and Hamilton south of Dayton. All these cities are busy manufacturing centers. Lima has locomotive works and oil refineries. 

Springfield makes gas and gasoline engines and farm machinery and prints many magazines. Hamilton has large paper mills and steel mills. 

Manufacturing centers in Indiana. 

Find South Bend, Fort Wayne, and

Muncie on the map. South Bend was once a center for the manufacture of wagons and buggies. Today it manufactures automobiles instead. In addition it makes sewing machines and many kinds of farm machinery. Fort Wayne has railroad shops and makes railroad cars and locomotives. It also has a wide variety of other industries. Muncie has iron and steel mills, glass factories, machine shops, and pulp and paper mills. 

Trade centers and manufacturing centers of Illinois. Find Rockford, Peoria, and Decatur on the map. Outside of 

Chicago, Rockford is the chief trading center in northern Illinois. It manufactures farm machinery, furniture, and tools of various kinds. Peoria is in the heart of a rich agricultural region and near productive coal fields. It builds much farm machinery and has meatpacking plants and paper mills. It is also an important center for trade in grains, such as corn and wheat. Decatur has railroad shops and is on the edge of coal fields. It manufactures iron and steel goods and soybean products. 

Agricultural centers in Iowa. Find 

Cedar Rapids and Waterloo on the map.

Both of these cities are in the heart of a rich agricultural region and both get water power from the Cedar River. 

Cedar Rapids has meat-packing plants, machine shops, and factories for making cereals. Waterloo has machine shops and factories for making farm machinery. 

Corn Belt cities much alike. As you have read about the Corn Belt cities, you doubtless have discovered that they are alike in many ways. First, they are all shipping centers for farm products. Second, in most of the cities there are meatpacking plants, flour mills, and other factories that use products from the farms. Third, in most cities there are factories that make farm machinery and tools for use on the farms. Fourth, they are all busy manufacturing centers.

IN THE SPRING-WHEAT COUNTRY 

In the north-central part of our country and in southern Canada is a region that grows so much spring wheat that it is known as the Spring-Wheat Belt.

One of the most productive areas is the Red River Valley. The soils here are especially fertile because the land was once covered by a lake. Turn to page 

358 and make an outline map of the 

North Central States. Next look at

Figure 169 and color a part of your map to show where the Spring-Wheat Belt is. 

Now find the Spring-Wheat Belt on the map. Does most of the land seem to be level or hilly? What does Figure 154

tell you about the population of the region? If you were to travel through the Spring-Wheat Belt, you would find thousands of acres of black, level land which would stretch out for miles and miles in all directions. There would often be several miles between farmhouses because the farms are all very large. 

Every now and then you would come to a small town, but you would find no large cities. 

Why wheat is sown in the spring. 

The wheat grown in the extreme northern part of our country and in southern Canada is called spring wheat because it is sown in the spring. Can you suggest why the wheat in this region is not sown in the fall? Look at Figure 17 and notice the length of the growing season in the Spring-Wheat Belt. Would you say that the winters here are longer or shorter than they are in regions farther south? Your answer gives one reason why the wheat is sown in the spring. 

The winters are so long and cold that the wheat would freeze if it were sown in the fall. The temperature often falls as low as 20° below zero and in places even as low as 50° below zero. 

Another reason why wheat is sown in the spring in the northern states is the fact that, even though the growing season is short, there is plenty of time for wheat to grow and ripen. This is partly because the summer days are long and plants grow more during the day than they do during the night. Summer days, as you will learn later, are longer and longer the farther and farther you go from the equator. Thus the days in the Spring- 

Wheat Belt are long when compared with the days farther south. 

Light but favorable rainfall. Spring wheat does not require heavy rainfall. 

In fact, it grows best where the rainfall is light. Figure 16 will tell you the amount of rain that falls annually in the 

Spring-Wheat Belt. Moreover, moisture does not evaporate in the cooler temperatures of the North as rapidly as it does in the warmer temperatures of the South. 

Then, too, most of the rain in the Spring- 

Wheat Belt falls in the early summer.

This is just the time when it will do the most good for growing wheat. 

Methods of harvesting wheat. There are three ways of harvesting wheat. It may be harvested with a binder, a header, or a combine. The binder cuts the wheat and ties it into bundles, or sheaves. A number of men follow the binder and set the sheaves together in shocks. After standing awhile in the shocks the wheat is hauled to a threshing machine.

The header is a machine that cuts off the heads of the wheat and drops them on a wide, moving canvas. This canvas carries the heads up into a wagon. When the wagon is loaded with heads, it is driven to a part of the field where the heads are piled up in stacks to await the threshing machine.

The combine is a machine that cuts and threshes the wheat all at once. By threshing is meant the work of separating the grains of wheat from the stalks or straw.

The grains from the combine are carried through a pipe to a wagon or truck. The straw is forced out through another pipe at the back of the combine and scattered upon the field. Later the straw is plowed under to help fertilize the soil. Since a combine is a very expensive machine, it is used only on the larger and more

prosperous farms. Figure 176 shows a combine at work in the wheat country. 

Working with a threshing machine. 

Have you ever seen a threshing machine or separator at work? Suppose you join a threshing crew and help thresh some of the wheat on the Dakota farms. Your crew is made up of men who travel from farm to farm with the machine. You eat and sleep in a little house on wheels that is taken with the machine wherever it goes. Sometimes, however, you sleep in the farmer's house, and his wife cooks your meals.

The entire outfit reminds you of a traveling factory or mill. The separa- 

Courtesy J. I. Case Co. 

Figure 176. A combine. The wheat is cut in front at the right and carried into the machine. The grain falls into a container at the left and the straw drops from the Notice that the combine is pulled by a tractor. rear. tor, or machine itself, gets its power from a tractor by means of a long belt. It is also pulled from farm to farm by means of the tractor. Sometimes a steam engine is used instead of a tractor. Figure 

177 shows a threshing machine at work. 

The newly threshed grain is either stored in the farmer's granary or sent to a near-by grain elevator. Here it is weighed and stored. Later it is shipped to near-by mills or to grain markets in larger cities. At every town and village in the wheat country you will see two or three elevators towering above the surrounding buildings. The elevators are always built along railroads over which grain may be shipped to market. Though most of the towns are small, they are busy centers during the harvesting season. 

Other crops in the Spring-Wheat Belt. 

Wheat farmers usually do not depend on wheat alone, but raise a variety of other crops, such as oats, rye, and barley.

Then, too, more flax is raised in the 

Spring-Wheat Belt than in any other part of the country. The flax is grown largely for its seeds, which are made into linseed oil. This oil is used for mixing paints of various kinds. Flax is threshed with a threshing machine in much the same way as wheat is threshed. 

Courtesy International Harvester Co. 

Figure 177. A threshing machine. Notice the farmer pitching bundles into the machine. The grain comes out the side of the machine, and the straw is blown out the rear. A tractor is used for power. 

Besides grain, much hay is also raised in the Spring- 

Wheat Belt. Nearly every farmer keeps a few hogs and cattle, and some keep sheep.

Each farmer also has a garden and an orchard. Thus you see that even the Spring Wheat Belt is a region of mixed farming.

IN THE WINTER-WHEAT COUNTRY 

Turn to the map on page 358 and make an outline map of the North Central 

States. Next find the Winter-Wheat

Belt in Figure 169 and show its location by coloring a part of your map. Notice that the Winter-Wheat Belt covers part of Nebraska, Kansas, Oklahoma, and

Texas and extends eastward, including parts of Missouri, Illinois, Indiana, Kentucky, and Ohio. Kansas, in the heart of the Winter-Wheat Belt, usually produces more wheat than any other state. 

Raising the crop. Winter wheat is usually sown in the autumn in late September or early October. Within a few weeks the fields take on a beautiful green color. The little wheat plants look much like blades of grass except that they are slightly darker in color. Before snow and frosts occur, they may have grown several inches high. The cold weather does not kill the plants, but merely stops their growth until warm weather comes in the spring. 

The farmer in the Winter-Wheat Belt hopes for many spring showers. If rain occurs, he may harvest a crop of 20 to 

40 bushels per acre. If the season is dry, however, he may harvest only a few bushels per acre. In the far-western regions the wheat crop may be a complete failure because of dry weather. 

Mixed farming in the Winter-Wheat

Belt. Some of the lands in the western part of the Winter-Wheat Belt resemble a single field of waving wheat. This is another way of saying that wheat is the most important crop. It does not mean, however, that wheat is the only crop raised. Just as in all other parts of the 

North Central States, much mixed farming is carried on. The farmers in Kansas and western Nebraska usually plant crops that require very little rain, such as sorghums, including Kafir corn. They also raise large crops of alfalfa for winter feed. 

Alfalfa grows somewhat as does clover, but sends its roots deep into the ground where there is moisture. This explains why alfalfa can be grown in regions of very light rainfall. Usually two or three crops of alfalfa are cut during a single year. It is harvested in much the same. way as are other kinds of hay. 

Trading centers in Kansas. On the map find Wichita. This city is on the 

Arkansas River in the heart of the winter-wheat region. What industry, then, would you expect to be very important here? Wichita has great grain elevators, flour mills, and factories for making breakfast foods. It also has stockyards and meat-packing plants.

Find Topeka on the map. This city, as you have already learned, is in both a wheat and a corn country. Like 

Wichita, it has grain elevators, flour mills, and meat-packing plants. It also has machine shops and a number of industries that supply manufactured products to the surrounding region.

If you were standing in western Kansas or Nebraska, you would see few trees.

The land all around you would be a level plain. The earth and sky would seem to meet twelve to fifteen miles away. If the sky were clear, you would find yourself in brilliant sunlight. The Great 

Plains are truly a land of almost dazzling sunshine, of wide views of treeless prairies, and of great open spaces. 

What the Great Plains are like. Turn to the map and find the Great Plains. 

Notice that this region of open prairies extends north and south across the country and that it lies partly in the Southern 

States, partly in the North Central

States, and partly in the states to the west. Figure 178 is a picture taken on the Great Plains. Not all of the land is as flat as that in the picture. Many of the plains are rolling, and some parts are even rough and hilly. Find the Black

Hills in South Dakota on the map. 

These hills are really mountains that rise to a height of several thousand feet above the surrounding plains.

Raising cattle. What does 

Figure 16 tell you about the rainfall in the western part of the North Central States? 

Would you say there is enough rainfall for the growing of crops? What kind of work, then, would you expect to find in this region of little rainfall? Figure 179 shows you what most people do for a living. You will remember that a kind of bunch grass grows on the prairies in western Texas and Oklahoma. Bunch grass grows also in western North and 

South Dakota, Kansas, and Nebraska.

This grass makes good pasture and hay. 

Often the land, houses, or trees. 

Sending the cattle to market. The cattle from the Great Plains are usually sent to market in the fall. Because of the great distances they must go, they are commonly shipped by railroad. Several men drive the cattle to the nearest railroad station, where they are loaded on cars. Later these cars are attached to long stock trains headed for such cities as Kansas City, Omaha, St. Louis, Minneapolis, St. Paul, and Chicago. A few men often ride in the stock train to see that the cattle are supplied with feed and water. As you have already learned, not all the cattle from the Great Plains go directly to market. Some of them are shipped to the Corn Belt to be fattened. 

This fact will help to explain why so many beef cattle are found in the Corn 

Belt, as shown in Figure 179.

Raising sheep on the Great Plains. In some of the drier and more hilly parts of the plains many sheep are raised. Look at Figure 160 and find where most of the sheep are raised. Sheep do well on the drier lands because, as you will remember, they can eat very short grass.

They also do well on hilly land because they can readily climb steep slopes. In the western part of the Great

Plains they are often driven up into the mountains to pasture during the summer. 

Figure 179.

Then when winter comes they are driven back down on the Plains. Large quantities of hay and alfalfa are fed to the sheep in winter. Usually they are shut up in small fields or pens during the winter feeding period. 

The sheep on the Great Plains are raised partly for wool and partly for meat. In the fall of the year many of the sheep are sold. They are taken by railroad to the various large cities where there are stockyards and slaughterhouses.

Possibly some of the mutton on your table has come from the western plains. The wool is usually shipped to cities in the eastern part of the country, especially 

Philadelphia, New York, and Boston.

Raising crops by dry farming. In some parts of the Great Plains crops are raised by a method known as dry farming. 

The ground is plowed deeply so that as much rain as possible will soak into the ground. After it rains, the land is harrowed or dragged to loosen the soil at the top. The loose soil helps to keep the 

Where beef cattle are raised in the North Central States. water from escaping into the air, or evaporating. Sometimes straw is spread on the ground to keep the water from evaporating. Finally seed is planted and the plants get a good start because of the moisture in the ground. Dry farming, though, has never been very successful. 

The crops are often failures. Then, too, the cost of producing crops by dry farming is high because so much work must be done and because there is not enough moisture to raise crops every year. 

Raising crops by irrigation. Certain lands in the dry western part of the North Central States are irrigated. 

Water from wells, reservoirs, or streams is carried over the country in ditches. 

Gates in these ditches, as shown in Figure 210, are opened to let the water into smaller ditches that spread the water over fields. In this way moisture is provided much as it is by rain. Moreover, it can be provided just when the crops need it most. Figure 211 shows how the water commonly spreads over the land.

The Arkansas River furnishes much water for irrigation. Find this river on the map and notice that it rises in the mountains to the west. The rain and melting snow on these mountains give the river much of its water. Along the 

North Platte River in western Nebraska the government has built a large irrigation dam. Find this river on the map. 

The government has also provided irrigation for certain parts of the Dakotas. 

Wheat, oats, corn, rye, and especially sugar beets and alfalfa are raised on irrigated land. Look at Figure 161 to see where sugar beets are raised on the Great Plains.

LIGNITE IRON, DEPOSIT BITUMINOUS LIGNITE MINING IN THE NORTH CENTRAL STATES 

You have found that farming is an important way of making a living in the 

North Central States. By this time, you should understand why these states are often called the "bread basket" of the country. Strange to say, however, only about a fourth of the people of the North Central States are farmers. The other three-fourths make their living in a variety of ways. Some work in mines and dig minerals from the earth.

Mining iron ore. The richest deposits of iron ore in the world are found near the shore of Lake Superior in Minnesota, Michigan, and Wisconsin. Find this iron-producing region in Figure 180. Most of the iron mined in the United States comes from this district. About half of it comes from Minnesota. Michigan ranks second in production.

The iron ores found in the Lake Superior district are of a very high quality. 

Some of them are more than half pure iron. Moreover, the deposits here are found near the surface of the ground. 

Mining is carried on at less cost than would be possible if the ores were buried deep

COAL Figure 180. Where coal and iron are found in the North Central States. below the surface. In northern Minnesota most of the iron is mined in open pits. 

The reddish-black iron ore is scooped up with steam shovels that handle more than two tons at a time. 

Most of the ore is then taken by railroad to two lake ports, Duluth and Superior, about seventy-five miles

MINING IN  

177 away. Find these two ports on the map. In what state is each situated? 

Mining copper in Michigan. In northern Michigan along Lake Superior there are valuable deposits of copper, the only important ones east of the Mississippi River. 

These deposits are found on the small peninsula of Keweenaw. Millions of tons of copper have been taken from mines on this peninsula and sent to manufacturing cities in the eastern and central parts of the country. The copper mines here are very deep. One mine is 6,254 feet deep, and another 5,690 feet below the surface of the earth. Copper, then, you may readily see, is expensive to mine.

Mining lead and zinc. Figure 182 shows where lead and zinc are mined in the 

North Central States. Missouri mines more lead than any other state, much of the mining being done in the southeastern part. It also ranks very high in the mining of zinc. Joplin, in southwestern 

Missouri, is one of the leading lead and zinc centers in the United States, although much of the mining is done in Oklahoma.

Near by also is Springfield which has packing plants, flour mills, machine shops, and factories for making clothing. The city is also a popular place for tourists in the summer because of the cool climate on the Ozark Plateau.

The Black Hills were named by the Indians because they are covered with forests of evergreens. From a distance the dark trees make the hills appear almost black.

In the center of the Indiana coal region is the city of Terre Haute. an important trading center for a large part of western Indiana and eastern Illinois. It has steel mills, foundries, railroad shops, and glass factories. 

In western North Dakota is a soft, coal-like substance called lignite which is mined for fuel. Since this substance is brown in color, it is often called "brown coal." In a few places materials known as peat are dug out of the swamps and bottoms of old lakes and used as fuel. 

Oil and gas wells. Look at Figure 146 and notice where petroleum is found in the North Central States. In what regions do you find the greatest production? There are many wells in Ohio, Indiana, and Kentucky, but only in 

Kansas and Illinois is oil produced in abundance. The Kansas region is part of the famous Mid-Continent Field. 

What other regions have you found that belong to this field? Look again at 

Figure 146 to answer this question. To what fields do the wells in Ohio, Indiana, Illinois, and Kentucky belong?

In most of the places where there is oil there is also gas. In fact, in some places there are more gas wells than oil wells.

Since gas is usually found nearer the surface than oil, gas wells are not so deep as oil wells. The gas is piped to cities where it is used for cooking and heating, and to some extent in industry. 

Quarrying and clay products. Indiana is noted for its fine limestone. Many quarries are found in the southern part of the state near Bedford. Eastern Ohio is one of the leading pottery districts of the country. East Liverpool is the center of the industry. Here you will find many low kilns like those you saw at 

Trenton, New Jersey. Brick and tile mills are found in many parts of Ohio and some of the other states. Cement, too, is made in large quantities.

LUMBERING IN

Figure 183. A scene in the northern woods.

Here some of the largest trees have been cut.

LUMBERING IN THE NORTH CENTRAL STATES Century Photos 

Figure 184. A sawmill in the hardwood region. In the hardwood region there are many small sawmills such as this, and also a few large ones. For the most part these mills make boards and other lumber for the farmers who bring in logs from their wood lots. 

When the early settlers came to the 

North Central States, their first problem, like that of the Puritans and Pilgrims in New England, was to clear the land for farming. They cut down trees, piled them up, and burned them in great bonfires. Later, lumbermen came into the forests and cut large numbers of trees. 

Forest fires also swept large areas from time to time. In all of these ways, then, a large part of the timber in the North Central States has been cut or burned. Some very valuable timber, however, still remains, especially in the northern parts. 

In the northern woods. The northern forests have always attracted lumbermen, largely because of white pine. Lumber from this tree is especially good for general use. It is soft and consequently doesn't split readily when fastened with nails. Also, it is extremely durable and doesn't warp or shrink when exposed to water. Most of the white pine is found in Michigan, Wisconsin, and Minnesota.

Many years ago these states led the country in the production of lumber. 

Today so many of the trees have been cut that none of the states ranks especially high in the lumbering industry. Many trees, however, are still being cut in certain regions. Figure 183 shows a 

Minnesota pine forest. Notice how tall and straight the trees are.

In the northern woods winter is the best time to carry on lumbering. Heavy snows cover the ground and it is easy to build roads through the woods. Then, too, the swamps are frozen and men can go out on the ice to cut down trees. 

The lumbermen usually live in camps in the woods.

Among the hardwoods. Small quantities of lumber are cut throughout Ohio, Indiana, Illinois, and Kentucky. Although sawmills are found in a number

of towns, lumbering as an industry is fast disappearing. No large hardwood forests are left. Nearly every farm has a wood lot with a few valuable trees. 

Most of the timber cut in these wood lots is used as fuel and as material for building fences and buildings. There is very little lumber to sell. 

Grand Rapids and furniture. No city in the North Central States depends entirely on lumber. Grand Rapids, however, started as a sawmill town beside a waterfall and for the most part owes its growth to lumber. Find Grand Rapids on the map. In the early days Grand 

Rapids was on the border of two forest belts, the northern evergreen forests and the hardwood forests. In the northern belt such trees as cedar, cherry, hard maple, and white pine grew. In the central hardwood belt oak, walnut, hickory, and chestnut grew. It was natural, therefore, that furniture factories should spring up. Even though most of the near-by trees have been cut, Grand Rapids is still one of the leading furniture centers in the country. This is largely because it got an early start.

Lumber in the North Central States. Below is a list of states with the number of feet of lumber each produced in a recent year. Arrange the states in order of importance, beginning with the state that produced the most. 

Some of the states, you will see, do not produce enough lumber to be included in the list.

Do you remember what you learned about the great glacier or ice sheet when you studied the New England states? 

This same ice sheet also spread over most of the North Central States. In some places, as in northern Michigan and Wisconsin, it scraped off the soil and left it too thin for farming. In other places it left the land strewn with rocks or covered with coarse sand and gravel. In still other places the ice sheet scooped up the earth and left great basins that are now filled with water. This explains the thousands of lakes and swamps found particularly in Michigan, Wisconsin, Minnesota, and northern Indiana. Then, too, the glacier explains the five great bodies of water known as the Great Lakes. 

Figure 186 is a map of the Great Lakes region. Which lake is farthest west? 

Which one is farthest east? 

A great waterway. Ever since white men first reached the North Central States, the Great Lakes have served as an important route for travel. As settlers from the east moved to lands farther west, they found the Great Lakes a great help in travel. As the population increased, people used the lakes more and more for travel and for carrying goods. 

Today the Great Lakes make up one of the greatest waterways in the world. 

Canals between lakes. Look at the map on pages 8-9 and notice that the water in the Great Lakes drains eastward through the St. Lawrence River to the Atlantic Ocean. Lake Superior, being farthest west, is higher than any other of the Great Lakes. In fact, it is about twenty-one feet higher than Lake Huron. There are falls and rapids in

Century Photos 

Figure 185. The locks at the Soo. Here you are looking directly at the gates at one end of a lock. These gates must be very strong to hold back the water. They will be opened when the boats are to be lowered. Beyond the boats you can see the gates at the other end of the lock. When will the gates at the other end of the lock be opened? the St. Marys River, which leads from 

Lake Superior to Lake Huron. Canals have been dug on either side of the river, one on the Canadian side to the north and one on the United States side to the south. These canals are known as the 

Soo Canals. Find them on the map.

Notice the cities called Sault Ste. Marie. 

They are connected by a bridge across the St. Marys River. 

Perhaps you wonder, however, just how the canals help ships get from lower to higher water or from higher to lower water. This is all done by a number of sections of the canal known as locks. 

Figure 185 is a picture of a ship entering a lock in the Soo Canals. At each end of the lock is a gate that holds back the water. When a boat is going upstream and must be lifted, it slowly steams into the lock. The gate back of the ship is closed and the one in front of it is opened. Water from the higher part of the canal then flows into the lock and raises the boat. When a boat is going downstream and must be lowered, it slowly steams into the lock just as before, except that it is going in the opposite direction. This time, when the gate back of the boat is closed and the one in front is opened, the water runs out into the lower part of the canal and lets the boat down. Water to fill the locks comes from the upper part of the canal. 

Now look at Figure 185 and try to explain just what would happen if the gates in front of the boats were opened. 

Lake cities at breaks in transportation. 

If you look at the map, you will see that many large cities have been built

on the Great Lakes. There are more cities here than on any other large inland body of water in the world. Make a list of the large cities on the lakes, including both those in the North Central States and those in the Middle Atlantic States. Notice that several are situated at the mouths of rivers that help to provide good harbors. All these lake cities, like the fall-line cities of the Middle Atlantic States, are at a break in transportation between land and water. 

THE CHICAGO-GARY INDUSTRIAL REGION 

Find Chicago again on the map and notice that it is situated near the southern end of Lake Michigan. Notice, too, that it is near the center of the North Central States. Its favorable location will help you to understand, as you study, why it has become one of the greatest industrial regions in the world. 

THE SECOND LARGEST CITY IN AMERICA 

Perhaps even before you read about 

Chicago you can give several reasons why it has become a great city. Today it is not only the second largest city in 

America, but also the fourth largest city in the world.

A great port and railroad center. The little river on which Chicago started, the 

Chicago River, makes an excellent harbor for boats. All along this river as it winds through the city are factories and piers, where boats may load and unload their cargoes. Although the river is small and must be continually dredged, it gives the city one of the best harbors Duluth

Figure 186. A relief map of the Great 

Lakes region. The black line through the middle of the lakes is the northern boundary of the United States. Trace the route of iron ore on the lakes from 

Duluth and Superior to Milwaukee, Chicago, Detroit, Toledo, Cleveland, and Buffalo.

The thin black lines with little lines across them are railroads. Notice how the railroads are forced to go around the lakes. This fact helps to explain why so many railroads pass through Chicago, the greatest railroad center in the world. 

Although the map shows that there are few highlands about the lakes, very little of the land is really flat. Much of it is gently rolling, and in some places there are steep hills, but most of them are not very high. Most of the region was once covered by the great ice sheet which helped to level the land and give much of it a deep, rich soil. This soil makes some of the finest farming land in the world.

Century Photos 

Figure 187. The Chicago River. The bridges have opened to allow a large freight boat to go through. The white boat beyond the freighter is a passenger vessel. Most of Chicago's largest buildings are near the river. Chicago has another busy river, the Calumet, in the southern part of the city. Many ore boats enter the Calumet. on the Great Lakes. Figure 187 shows a scene not far from the harbor entrance.

Notice on the map just how the railroads coming from over the land join like the spokes of a wheel. Chicago is a great railroad center-the chief railroad center of the world, in fact. 

The world's leading grain market.

From what you have already learned about the growing of wheat, corn, and other cereals in the North Central States, you can readily understand why Chicago is a great grain market. The enormous yields in the Corn Belt, the Winter- 

Wheat Belt, and the Spring-Wheat Belt all help to make the city a great shipping and trading center. Moreover, the many railroads and steamers on the Great

Lakes provide excellent means of carrying the grains into Chicago and on to eastern markets. Thus a location in a fine farming region and good transportation help to make Chicago the world's leading grain market. 

The world's leading meat-packing center. Chicago packs more meat than any other city in the world. Nearly every visitor to Chicago plans to see its famous

An important steel center. 

Chicago is an important iron and steel manufacturing center largely because iron ore, coal, and limestone can be obtained easily. Places where these materials can be readily brought together are best for making iron and steel. The greatest iron-ore mines in the world, as you have learned, are near the shores of Lake Superior in 

Minnesota and Michigan. 

This ore can be brought cheaply by boat from the mines to Chicago. Coal may be had either from mines on the Allegheny Plateau or from mines in southern 

Indiana and Illinois. Limestone may be obtained from quarries near Chicago in Indiana and Illinois or may be brought by boat from quarries on the western shores of Lake Huron.

Figure 188. A section of the Chicago stockyards. Here you see a few of the pens in which cattle are kept until they can be used. Hogs and sheep are kept in other parts of the stockyards. The buildings are parts of the great packing houses that surround the yards. All the stockyards and meat-packing plants are in one section of the city. In this part of the city more meat is prepared for use than anywhere else in the world. stockyards and meat-packing plants. It leads in the meat-packing industry chiefly because it is situated on the edge of the 

Corn Belt, where many hogs and cattle are raised. Large numbers of these animals are shipped to the Chicago stockyards. Here they are inspected, sold to packing houses, and made into meat. 

You will see some of the cattle pens in the Chicago stockyards in Figure 188. 

The meat-packing plants produce many other things besides meats. These things are known as by-products because they are made out of what is left over from the main product. The hides of the animals, for example, are saved for leather, and the bristles for brushes. The hoofs and some of the bones are used in making glue. Other bones are made into buttons and knife handles, and the horns into knife handles and combs.

First in the manufacture of farm machinery. It is only natural that Chicago should lead in the making of farm machinery, since so much of the city's trade depends on products from the farm. There are two very good reasons, however, why Chicago makes more farm machinery than any other city. In the first place, the rich farming region near by needs all kinds of machines for planting, cultivating, and harvesting crops.

In the second place, iron, lumber, and other raw materials for making farm machinery are within easy reach by water or rail.

"GREATER CHICAGO"-THE OUTLYING REGIONS OF A GREAT CITY 

A number of cities have grown up near 

Chicago. Some of these cities are only a few miles away and are merely residential cities with many beautiful homes in which workers in Chicago live. This is especially true of the cities north and west of Chicago. Among the more important residential suburbs are Evanston on the north; Berwyn, Cicero, Aurora, Joliet, and Oak Park on the west. Some of these cities, such as Aurora and Joliet, are far enough from Chicago to depend partly on their own industries. 

The factory suburbs of Chicago have the same advantages for manufacturing as Chicago itself has. These cities, for the most part, are situated on or near the lake shore southeast of Chicago. 

Among the factory cities Gary, East

Chicago, and Hammond, in the state of 

Indiana, are the largest. Find Gary on

the map. This city is noted for its large iron and steel mills. Iron ore and limestone are brought by boat to the harbor at Gary and unloaded at blast furnaces built near the edge of the water. Coal for making coke is brought chiefly from mines on the Allegheny Plateau. Gary is favorably located for distributing iron and steel. It can ship these products east or west by boat or train, and has rail connections with all parts of the country. Then, too, Chicago is an excellent market for some of the products. 

East Chicago and Hammond have a variety of industries. Many lake boats are loaded and unloaded at East Chicago.

It also has several steel mills. Figure 190 shows a scene in one of the mills. In some of the suburbs of Chicago there are huge oil refineries.

INDUSTRIAL REGION IN EASTERN MICHIGAN 

Figure 189 will help you locate another important manufacturing region in eastern Michigan. This region extends from 

Figure 191. An ore boat in the harbor at Gary. Most of the ore is brought from the Lake Superior mines in boats like this. The machinery and living quarters of the crew are in the ends of the boat. All the rest of the boat is a great open bin to be filled with ore.

Since large boats cannot be handled safely in a small space, they are usually pulled and pushed into their docks by tugs. You will find a number of tugs at every important port. 

Bay City on the north to Detroit on the south, and includes many of the great automobile factories of the country. Not all the cities in this region are on the Great Lakes, but all are helped by their nearness to the Lakes.

THE AUTOMOBILE CAPITAL 

Find Detroit on the map. In which direction is it from Lake St. Clair? 

Notice that Lake St. Clair lies between 

Lake Huron and Lake Erie. A broad, deep river, the Detroit River, joins Lake 

St. Clair and Lake Erie. This river forms a natural boundary between part of Michigan and Canada. Detroit is situated on the Detroit River. 

Automobiles to all the world. During the past thirty years Detroit has grown very rapidly. In size it ranks fourth among the cities of the United States.

In the value of its manufactured products, however, it ranks third. It ranks high in manufacture largely because it makes so many automobiles. While most of these automobiles are sold in the

United States, many are shipped to all parts of the world. Thus you can see why Detroit is sometimes called the capital of the automobile world. 

Why Detroit makes automobiles. In the early days there was a great need for wagons, buggies, and other vehicles in the farming lands of the North Central States. Then, too, there was always a great demand for boats around the 

Great Lakes. Detroit was was favorably situated for making some of the vehicles and boats. In addition the near-by forests provided plenty of lumber for manufacturing. When the automobile was invented, Detroit had many factories which could be changed from wagon, buggy, and boat factories to automobile factories like the one shown in Figure 192. Thus the city got a very early start in making automobiles. Moreover, it is well situated for getting raw materials and for shipping automobiles by water, railroad, and highway. 

Suburbs of Detroit. Like every large city, Detroit has a number of suburbs.

In fact, it is difficult to tell where one city leaves off and another begins. Dearborn and Highland Park have large automobile factories. Hamtramck has steel mills and makes many automobile parts. 

For a period of years it grew more rapidly than any other city in the country. 

All of the suburbs of Detroit have the same advantages for manufacturing as 

Detroit itself.

OTHER CITIES IN EASTERN MICHIGAN 

Two inland cities in eastern Michigan are Flint and Pontiac. Find these cities on the map. Although they are not on the Great Lakes, they both receive raw

Find Saginaw and Bay City on the map. Although these cities manufacture many products, they depend for trade largely on the near-by regions. Both are near bituminous coal fields and both are in the sugar-beet country of Michigan.

Bay City is also in the dry-bean country. 

Both cities have an extensive trade in lumber and both manufacture furniture.
