THE LARGEST CITY OF OHIO 

Figure 193 is a picture taken in Cleveland. This city is built at the mouth of the Cuyahoga River. The river has been deepened to form a part of the harbor. 

A wall, or breakwater, has been built out in the lake to form a large and safe outer harbor as well.

Meeting place for coal and iron. By studying the map you will see that Cleveland is a very good place for coal and iron to meet. Boats bring iron ore from Lake Superior mines, and trains bring coal from mines on the Allegheny 

Plateau in Ohio, Pennsylvania, West

Virginia, and eastern Kentucky. Many of the huge boats that bring iron ore to

Cleveland return to Lake Superior ports loaded with coal. Figure 180 will tell you one reason why the Lake Superior country is so glad to get coal.

Manufactures iron and steel products. 

Cleveland is within easy reach of limestone. By this time, perhaps, you are tired of being told that the iron and steel industry must have iron ore, coal, and limestone. You learned that all these materials were provided at Pittsburgh, Birmingham, Chicago, Gary, and a number of other iron and steel centers. 

Cleveland is especially well situated for bringing together the three materials which are necessary, and, as you may expect, makes iron and steel. Cleveland, however, does more than make iron and steel. It uses iron and steel in manufacturing many different products. A list of these products would include automobiles, steel rails, bridges, engine boilers,

wire, hardware, nails and bolts by the millions, and hundreds of other articles. 

Cleveland has various other factories besides those that make iron and steel products. Meat-packing is an important industry. Large quantities of clothing are also made. Many of the factories lie along the Cuyahoga River. Beside the factories are great railroad yards and warehouses where raw materials are brought in and manufactured products are loaded for shipment.

Suburbs of Cleveland. 

Cleveland has a number of beautiful suburbs, made up largely of the homes of people who work in Cleveland. The largest of these suburbs are Lakewood and Cleveland Heights. A few miles west of Cleveland is Lorain, another thriving port on Lake Erie.

OTHER IMPORTANT CITIES IN NORTHEASTERN OHIO The rubber city-Akron. Find Akron on the map. Notice that it is not far from Cleveland and Lake Erie. More rubber goods are made in Akron than in any other city in the country. Indeed, more rubber goods are made here than in any other city in the world. All the rubber used in Akron must be shipped in from foreign countries. Some of this rubber comes from South America, but most of it comes from southeastern 

Asia. Tires for automobiles are the most valuable of all the rubber goods made. 

Figure 194 shows the inside of a tire factory. Tires are made at Akron largely because the city got an early start in the work. Millions of tires are now made at 

Akron every year.

Two steel cities. Find Youngstown on the map. This city is on a direct route between Cleveland and Pittsburgh. 

You can see, then, that it is favorably located for getting iron ore that comes to Cleveland and other lake ports by boat. Then, too, an abundant supply of coal is at hand on the Allegheny Plateau. 

Youngstown, therefore, is an important iron and steel city. More than half the workers here are employed in iron and steel mills. Warren, a little to the northwest of Youngstown, is also a busy manufacturing center.

Find Canton on the map. Although this city does not make much iron and steel itself, it uses iron and steel from 

Youngstown and other places in making iron and steel products, such as farm machinery, bridges, bank vaults, and electric floor sweepers. Canton also manufactures brick and tile. 

Figure 194. A tire factory in Akron. If you have ever seen an old tire cut across, you know that it is built up in layers. The man in the picture is adding another layer to a partly finished tire.

OTHER IMPORTANT LAKE PORTS 

Toledo on Lake Erie. Find Toledo on the map. Like Cleveland, this city is situated at the mouth of a river and has an excellent harbor. If you were to visit the water front, you would see big boats unloading iron ore brought down from the Lake Superior mines. Part of this ore would go to iron and steel mills in Toledo itself, and part would be loaded on cars to be shipped by rail to certain iron and steel centers not on the lake. 

Then, too, you would see some of the big boats being loaded with coal bound for ports in Michigan, Wisconsin, Illinois, and Minnesota. Thus coal would come by railroad from coal mines in Pennsylvania, Ohio, West Virginia, and eastern 

Kentucky. Several railroads from the coal fields lead to Toledo.

In addition to iron and steel mills, Toledo has several large flour mills as well as factories for making automobiles and machinery of various kinds. 

Wisconsin's largest city. On the map find Milwaukee. This is the most important city on Lake Michigan except Chicago, and is the largest city of 

Wisconsin. Like Chicago, Cleveland, and Toledo, it grew up at a place where a small river flows into the lake. Boats come to Milwaukee bringing large quantities of coal chiefly from ports on Lake 

Erie. Boats leaving Milwaukee carry large shipments of grain, some of which go to such flour-milling cities as Toledo and Buffalo. From what region does 

Milwaukee get its grain? 

Tanning leather and packing meat are 

Milwaukee's leading industries, but the city has many different kinds of factories. Machinery, clothing, furniture, and leather goods are among the most valuable products. 

Two other cities on Lake Michigan.

Find Racine and Kenosha on the map. 

In what state are they? Racine has one of the best harbors on Lake Michigan. It manufactures farm machinery, leather, and iron and steel products. Kenosha manufactures flour, leather, and furniture. It also is a noted health resort. 

Cities at the western end of the Great 

Lakes.

The two largest ports on Lake 

Superior are Duluth and Superior. Find these two cities on the map. In what state is Duluth? In what state is Superior? These cities grew up on opposite sides of a small bay that makes an excellent harbor. They are situated near great iron mines, extensive forests, and fertile farm lands that produce large quantities of wheat. Thus they have large quantities of iron, lumber, and grain to ship to other parts of the country. 

Many of the boats that carry these raw products eastward on the Great 

Lakes bring back coal and manufactured articles. Enough coal, for instance, is brought to Duluth to make it an important iron and steel manufacturing center. 

Its iron ore, of course, is obtained near by. 

INLAND CITIES OF THE LAKE STATES 

The lake states have several inland cities that depend largely on the surrounding country. These cities ship farm products and use farm products in their factories. They also manufacture many things the farmers use. 

In southern Michigan find Kalamazoo, Battle Creek, and Jackson on the map. 

Kalamazoo is in the heart of a celery and truck-farming region. Battle Creek is in

In Wisconsin find Madison on the map. 

Notice that it is the state capital. Because of the many near-by lakes and its cool climate Madison is a popular summer resort. It is also a manufacturing city.

Among its products are farm machinery, flour, and shoes.

RIVERS IN THE NORTH CENTRAL STATES 

Before railroads and highways were built, rivers offered the best means of travel. Many towns grew up as trading centers along the rivers. Goods from the country were traded for goods brought in by boats. Other towns grew up where falls caused a break in transportation, and still others developed at the head of navigation. Today river transportation is not so important as it once was. More goods now are being shipped by railroad and truck. Some products, however, especially heavy, bulky materials, are still shipped on rivers. Where have you found coal and cotton being shipped on rivers? In most cases cities that began as river ports and have favorable sites have continued to thrive.

Kaufmann & Fabry Co.

THE TWIN CITIES-MINNEAPOLIS AND ST. PAUL 

Find Minneapolis and St. Paul on the map. Notice how close together they are. It is because of their nearness to each other that they are often called the twin cities. What does the sign on the map tell you about St. Paul? Of what state is it the capital? 

Both Minneapolis and St. Paul are healthful and attractive places. There are numerous lakes in and near the cities.

These lakes, together with the streams 11-5G

000 

Century Photos 

Figure 196. Part of the river front at St. Louis. The many tall buildings suggest that St. Louis is a large and prosperous city. The river front is crowded with factories and warehouses. Several railroads follow the river to the city. and waterfalls, invite people to spend part of their time out of doors. 

Minneapolis a flourishing city. Minneapolis grew at the Falls of St. Anthony in the Mississippi River. When the city first started, the surrounding region was covered with forests. Water power from the falls was used to run sawmills. 

Later, when the lands near Minneapolis were used for growing wheat, the waterfalls were used for running flour mills. 

Thus Minneapolis began to make flour, and today ranks with Buffalo as one of the leading flour-milling centers of the world. You can see from Figure 195 that it is a prosperous city.

Minneapolis is still an important lumber center and has various kinds of factories, but flour is the leading product. 

This is because the city is situated at the edge of a great wheat country and because it has water power to help run its mills. 

Since the falls on the river, however, cannot provide power for all the mills, many mills use large quantities of coal. 

St. Paul at the head of navigation. 

St. Paul is at the head of navigation on the Mississippi River. For this reason, as you can readily understand, St. Paul, with its many factories, is an important trading center. Like Minneapolis, it has flour mills, but it also makes dairy products, boots, and shoes. South St. Paul is one of the leading meat-packing centers in the United States. The twin cities, together with South St. Paul, make up a great business district.

THE LARGEST CITY ON THE MISSISSIPPI- ST. LOUIS Find St. Louis on the map. What river flows into the Mississippi just north of the city? What river flows into the

Mississippi about a hundred miles south of the city? From St. Louis products can be shipped by boat to nearly all the river towns in the North Central States. 

Thus you can see that, in the days before railroads, St. Louis had an excellent location. Later, its location as a

RIVERS IN  

193 river port helped it to become a great railroad center. Moreover, it was easy to build railroads from all directions. 

How does the map explain one reason why this is true? Figure 196 is a scene along the river front. 

Greatest fur market. 

Largely because it got an early start as a fur-trading center, St. Louis is today the greatest fur market in the world. In early days hunters and trappers from St. Louis went up the Missouri River to the Rocky Mountains and up the Mississippi to the northern woods. Here they found and killed many fur-bearing animals. They loaded the furs into boats and brought them back to St. Louis to sell. 

A variety of industries. St. Louis lies in the midst of rich farming lands. 

This helps to explain its importance as a meat-packing center and grain market. 

The railroads bring in live stock and grains for hundreds and hundreds of miles. Part of the wheat is ground into flour in St. Louis mills and part is shipped on to other cities farther east.

There are vast coal deposits just east of St. Louis and it is within easy reach of iron. Thus it makes many iron and steel goods, such as automobiles, trucks, and railroad cars. It also has great shoe factories. 

Just across the Mississippi from 

St. Louis is a smaller city known as 

East St. Louis. The industries here are very similar to those in St. Louis itself. 

In what state is East St. Louis?

OTHER CITIES ON THE MISSISSIPPI 

Look at the map and find Davenport, Rock Island, and Moline. In which state is Davenport? In which state are 

Rock Island and Moline? While these cities have three separate names, they really make up one large city. They are near productive bituminous coal fields. Davenport is a great grain center and manufactures a number of iron and steel products. Rock Island and

Moline have machine shops and manufacture farm machinery.

Now find Dubuque. This city is the center of a lead and zinc region in Iowa, northwestern Illinois, and southwestern 

Wisconsin. It is also a market for fur products and is the leading manufacturing city of northeastern Iowa. Among its products are meat, farm machinery, flour, and shoes.

CITIES ON THE OHIO RIVER 

A market center in Ohio - Cincinnati.

Find Cincinnati on the map. The business section of this city is built upon a level plain along the river. Back of the plain is a circle of hills that add to the beauty of the surroundings. Cincinnati has hundreds of mills and factories that make soap, clothing, shoes, and machinery of various kinds. Moreover, it is a great railroad center, as you will find by looking at the map. Then, too, it is one of the leading trading centers of the country. Cincinnati's growth may be explained in several ways: 

1. It is within easy reach of the corn, hog, and cattle lands of the country to the north. This has helped it become an important meat-packing center. 

2. It lies opposite the bluegrass country of Kentucky, which has long been famous for its crops of tobacco, corn, wheat, and livestock. Cincinnati is the market for many of these products.

Figure 197. Cincinnati on the Ohio River. Notice the bridge at the left. As you know, Cincinnati is in Ohio, but this picture was taken from across the river in Kentucky. In what direction, then, are you looking in the picture? Figure 198. The canal at Louisville. The boat in the background is pushing barges loaded with coal and other products. Steamers usually push a barge tow in a river or canal instead of pulling it, because it is easier to guide. 

3. It is within easy reach of the coal mines in West Virginia, Kentucky, and 

Ohio. Much coal is brought to Cincinnati both by railroad and in great flatboats or barges on the Ohio. 

Just across the river from Cincinnati, in the state of Kentucky, are several suburban cities, the largest of which is 

Covington. A view of Cincinnati is shown in Figure 197. The bridge at the left leads to Covington. 

Louisville-Kentucky's largest city.

Find Louisville on the map. Like Cin cinnati, this city is an outlet for the rich bluegrass region in north-central Kentucky. It was located at or near falls in the Ohio River. These falls are really rapids where the river flows over a rocky ledge. In early days boats could not pass through the rapids, so cargoes were unloaded, carried around the rapids, and then loaded again. Thus Louisville, like many other cities, grew up at a break in transportation. Today, however, there is no such break, for a canal has been built around the rapids. Figure 198

Evansville, Indiana's largest river port.

CITIES ON THE MISSOURI 

Kansas City-two cities with the same name. On the map find Kansas City in 

Missouri and Kansas City in Kansas. 

You can see that these two cities are located at a place where the Kansas 

River flows into the Missouri. These cities have different governments because they are situated in different states, but otherwise they are really one city. 

Kansas City in Missouri is the larger. 

Several advantages have helped Kansas City to grow. In the first place, it is a great railroad center, partly because railroads may be easily built on the gently rolling land. In the second place, it is situated between the Corn Belt to the east, where millions of cattle and hogs are fattened, and the grazing lands to the south and west, where millions of sheep and cattle are raised. In the third place, it is situated in the leading winterwheat region. As a result of these factors, it has become a very important manufacturing and trading center. center 

Nebraska's meat-packing 

Omaha. Omaha is a busy commercial and manufacturing city on the Missouri 

River. Like Kansas City, it is an important grain and livestock market. Large numbers of hogs fattened in the Corn 

Belt, together with cattle and sheep raised on the dry pasture lands west of the Corn Belt, supply meat for packing plants. Winter-wheat fields on all sides of the city supply wheat for many flour mills. Omaha has good railroad connections and can readily carry on trade with the people who live in the Corn Belt to the east, the winter-wheat region, and the grazing lands to the west.

Just across the Missouri River from 

Omaha is Council Bluffs, a city in Iowa.

Naturally the people here make a living in much the same way as the people of Omaha. The two cities together serve as a trading center for a large area in two states.

St. Joseph-a trading center in Missouri. Find St. Joseph on the map. 

This city, like Kansas City and Omaha, is so situated that it can trade with the 

Corn Belt, the Winter-Wheat Belt, and the grazing lands to the west. What, then, might you expect to find the people here doing for a living? You will find that St. Joseph has great stockyards and meat-packing plants. It also has grain elevators and flour mills. 

Two shipping centers for neighboring states. North of Omaha in the state of 

Iowa is Sioux City. Find this city on the map. It is the shipping center for a large area in three states. Name these states. Among its factories are meatpacking plants, flour mills, machine shops, and factories for making clothing.

Falls the largest city of South Dakota.

It is situated in a thriving farming region and is a shipping center for three states.

Three state capitals. Trace the Missouri River as it flows across the western part of the North Central States. Find three state capitals on the river. Bismarck, which is the farthest north, is in the wheat country of North Dakota. 

To the east are rich farming lands, but to the west are the prairies. Pierre in 

South Dakota, like Bismarck, has rich farming lands to the east, but grazing lands to the west.

Jefferson City in Missouri is the trading center for a fertile farming region. 

It manufactures flour, farm machinery, shoes, and clothing. Of the three capitals on the Missouri it is the only one that can be reached by boats.

FISHING IN THE NORTH CENTRAL STATES 

Although the North Central States do not border the ocean, they have a small fishing industry. Fishing is carried on in the Great Lakes and in rivers and smaller lakes. Most of the fishing in the 

Great Lakes is done in Lake Michigan and Lake Erie. The principal kinds of fish caught are whitefish, herring, trout, and perch. The lake cities are ports for fishing boats as well as for trading vessels. 

The fish are brought to these ports, then

AS A PLAYGROUND 

197 Figure 200. A favorite vacation spot in the North Central States. This picture was taken along the Wisconsin River. 

The river has cut a valley through soft rocks, leaving some of them standing in curious shapes. packed in ice and shipped to cities many miles away. Thus you can see that, even though fishing is not so important as various other industries of the North Central States, a number of people depend upon fishing as a means of making a living.

The Mississippi is the principal river for fish. Black bass, carp, and a variety of other fresh-water fish are caught and sold to near-by markets. More important than fish, however, are clams or mussels, which are found in the beds of a number of streams. The shells of these clams are used extensively in making pearl buttons.

You have already learned that there are a great many small lakes in Michigan, Wisconsin, Minnesota, and northern Indiana. All of these lakes are popular places for fishermen who fish for sport. 

THE NORTH CENTRAL STATES AS A PLAYGROUND 

Like every other section of the country, the North Central States have many lovely spots that attract tourists and travelers. The numerous railroads and the fine highways throughout the section make travel easy and enjoyable.

Among the lakes. The Great Lakes, of course, interest many visitors. All along the shores of most of these lakes there are cottages, hotels, and tourist camps. Cedar Point is a popular island beach in northern Ohio. Along the Indiana shore of Lake Michigan there is a region of sandy ridges known as the Sand 

Dunes. Northern Michigan has more summer resorts than any other state along the lakes.

In addition to the Great Lakes, Minnesota, Wisconsin, Michigan, and northern

Indiana have thousands of small lakes that offer fishing, boating, and swimming. 

Other favorite spots. Besides these great vacation states, there are many other places of interest in the North 

Central States. Mammoth Cave, in central Kentucky, is one of the large caves in the United States.

In Wisconsin and Minnesota there are rapids and falls in some of the rivers where the water has cut through the hard rocks. All along these rivers shaded cliffs and waterfalls attract thousands of people during the summer. Figure 200 shows a scene near Madison in a region called the Dells of the Wisconsin. 

The Ozark Mountains in Missouri and the Black Hills of South Dakota are also favorite spots. Here the people enjoy the cool breezes of higher lands. 

The Dakotas have many Indians. 

These Indians do not live in wigwams or wear the same kinds of clothes as Indians of early days. Even so, they enjoy showing trinkets and telling tourists what they can about conditions in the past.

UNIT SIX  INTRODUCING THE WESTERN STATES 

The Western States are noted for their great variety and beauty of scenery.

In nearly every state in the West there is something new and different. In order that you may see what the country is like, suppose you take a trip by railroad over some of its famous lands. 

Before you start, however, color the part of your outline map of the United States that shows just where the Western 

States are. 

A trip by railroad. As you look out the car windows you will doubtless see many things you have never seen before. 

It will be more interesting if you follow your trip on on a map. On page 358 you will find an outline map of the Western States. From this map make an outline map of your own. your own. Put in the abbreviation of the name of each state and a cross for each state capital. Use your map as you travel. Make a small round dot for each city you visit and draw a line from one dot to another to stand for the railroad over which you ride. Turn to the map on pages 206-207 for any help you may need. 

From New Mexico into Arizona. Suppose you board your train at Clovis in eastern New Mexico and travel west- 200

West of the Colorado River for many miles the land is exceedingly dry and barren. Later, however, you come to a region of orchards, gardens, and flowers. So great is the change that you can hardly believe your eyes. 

On every hand you see groves of citrus fruits, avocados, and olives. This is the famous subtropical region of California. Do you remember what "subtropical" means?

California. Find these two cities on the map. At some of the stations along the way you see Indians offering blankets, pottery, or silver trinkets for sale. As the train moves farther westward, you pass a region where flocks of sheep and herds of cattle are pastured. Cowboys watch these animals grazing in the open country. The land is generally level, except for scattered flat-top mountains called mesas that look as if they might have been sliced off with a knife. In western Arizona you come to a region that is more rugged and mountainous. 

Along the boundary you cross the Colorado River. Find this river on the map. 

Across southern California. What state extends southward almost to the place where you cross? You do not enter

Nevada, but you learn that it is much like the country through which you have All the country now is thickly settled. 

You pass through one town after another.

Finally you come to Los Angeles, the great business center of southern California. The many factories tell you that it is also a great manufacturing city. 

A few miles away are vast oil fields.

Los Angeles has a fine harbor where many ocean vessels come to load and unload their cargoes. You will see a picture of 

Los Angeles in Figure 203.

Through the Valley of California. As you leave Los Angeles, you board a train that runs northward through the Valley of California. This great valley lies along the San Joaquin and Sacramento rivers. Find these rivers on the map. 

The Valley of California is largely a region of fruits and vegetables. The ditches in the fields tell you that much of the land is irrigated. In the southern end of the valley you find citrus fruits, cotton, and vineyards of grapes. In the heart of the region lies Fresno. Find

Fresno on the map. Near the center of the Valley of California the citrus fruits give way to apricots, plums, apples, and pears. Between the rows of trees in some of the orchards alfalfa and various other crops are planted. In the Sierra 

Nevada to the east grow the great sequoia trees for which California is famous. 

These trees are the oldest living things in the world.

When about halfway up the Valley of 

California, your train turns westward toward San Francisco. Find this city on the map. All the land in this part of the valley is very thickly settled. Shortly you reach the city of Oakland on the eastern side of a body of water known as San Francisco Bay. Here you leave the train and ride to San Francisco over the famous San Francisco-Oakland Bay 

Bridge. Ferryboats also cross the bay.

San Francisco is a large and busy city and an important seaport. It serves central California just as Los Angeles serves the southern part of the state. 

Some of the ships in the harbor have come from China and Japan. They have brought such products as spices, silks, and tea in exchange for American goods. 

From San Francisco northward. After a short visit in San Francisco you return by ferry to Oakland and board a train to continue your journey. First you pass through Berkeley, then come to Sacramento, the capital of the state. Find these cities on the map. You continue through a wide, level valley and come to a country that is hilly. The farther you go, the higher are the hills, for you are entering the southern part of the Cascade Range. The rich farm land has given way to great forests of evergreens. 

Into Oregon and the Willamette Valley.

After traveling several hours in the mountains, you suddenly come into the fertile valley of the Willamette River.

On either side stretch towns, villages, fertile farm lands, and orchards. In the heart of the valley you pass through 

Salem, the capital of the state. Find this city on the map. To the north of 

Salem find Portland. This city is on the Willamette River, which flows into the Columbia, the largest river in the Northwest. The Columbia is noted for its salmon fishing. Perhaps the salmon that you have bought from your grocer have come from this river. Later you will learn how these salmon were caught. 

At Portland you see large ocean liners which tell you that it is a great seaport.

These vessels come up the Columbia and Willamette rivers just as large ocean liners come up the Delaware to Philadelphia and up the Mississippi to New 

Orleans. Along the wharves you see cargoes of lumber, grain, fruit, and wool ready to be shipped. What does this tell you about the surrounding region? 

Through Washington, the northwestern state. Rapidly now your train takes

Building railroads in such places is very expensive. to the Yakima Valley, noted for its apples. Here you find, as far as you can see, long rows of trees bearing red or yellow apples. Between the rows of trees are little ditches of water. What do these ditches tell you about this part of the country? Next you cross a large river you have seen before. Look at the map and find its name. On eastward through Washington you travel through a great wheat country. Your next stop is at Spokane, a large city near the eastern boundary of the state. Find 

Spokane on the map. Here you find large flour mills, meat-packing plants, and great stacks of lumber. you through fertile valleys that need not be watered by irrigation, for these are regions of abundant rainfall. Your next important stops are at Tacoma and 

Seattle. Find these cities on the map. 

Tacoma and Seattle, like Portland, are seaports. Ocean vessels reach the cities by coming through a baylike body of water known as Puget Sound. Find this body of water on the map. On the wharves at Tacoma and Seattle you find the same kinds of products being shipped as you saw at Portland. What are these products? 

From Seattle you start eastward across 

Washington and northern Idaho toward 

Butte, Montana. Find Butte on the map. Soon after leaving Seattle you cross the Cascade Range. The train does not climb to the highest ridges, but runs through some of the mountains in tunnels. 

At the foot of the mountains you come 

Crossing Idaho and Montana. As you travel eastward across northern Idaho, you find a region that is noted for the mining of lead. Lumber, too, is an important product. The land becomes more and more rugged, for you are now

entering the Rocky Mountains. The train winds around through the mountains, gradually working its way higher and higher, as you will see in Figure 204.

It continues around and over mountains all the way to Butte. Every now and then it passes through a long tunnel. At Butte you find a great copper-mining region. 

From Butte you travel southward to 

Ogden, Utah. Find Ogden on the map. 

Notice that your route takes you into 

Idaho a second time. Which part of the state is it? Here you find some of the big potatoes for which Idaho is noted. You also see sugar beets growing in the irrigated valley of the Snake River. 

Flocks of sheep graze on hillsides and on small plateaus among the mountains. 

Utah and Great Salt Lake. Soon after entering the state of Utah you catch glimpses of a great body of water glistening in the sun. Look at the map and find 

Great Salt Lake. Do you wonder why this body of water has been given such a name? It is because the water in the lake is very salty. The water in most lakes contains little salt. Great Salt

Lake, however, contains so much salt that a person bathing need have no fear of drowning. The water is so heavy with salt that he will not sink.

Much of the land east of the lake is covered with orchards and gardens. 

Then, too, there are many fields of sugar beets. Just as in other places, little ditches extend across the fields to carry water to the growing crops. Once all this region was barren, but with irrigation it has become a very rich country. 

Two important cities in Utah are

Ogden and Salt Lake City. What does the sign on the map tell you about Salt 

Lake City? Ogden is an important flour-milling city and livestock market.

Into Wyoming and Colorado. At Ogden you turn eastward toward Cheyenne, the capital of Wyoming. Find Cheyenne on the map. Now you pass through a region that is rugged and dry. Very little grows except sagebrush and bunch grass. Now and then you see a flock of sheep in the mountain pastures, but almost no farming of any kind. In southcentral Wyoming you find a region that produces oil. All along the tracks are derricks like those you found in Texas,

Oklahoma, and a few other states. 

Your journey takes you through one more state. This state is Colorado. Find

Denver, its capital, on the map. Your ride from Cheyenne to Denver over the 

Great Plains is one of the most thrilling rides you have had. All the way southward you can see at the west the towering snow-capped peaks of the Rocky Mountains. Some of these peaks even extend into the clouds. All too soon your train begins to enter the suburbs of a great city. Here, in a level region at the foot of the Rockies, lies the beautiful city of Denver. The cool mountain climate is delightful, and you can see that this must be a good place for tourists. 

Looking back at your trip. How much do you remember about your trip? Did you make a map as you traveled? If so, you may use it to help you remember what you saw. Along the railroads that mark your route print in the names of some of the leading products you found.

For instance, in Arizona and New Mexico you found sheep and cattle, so you will print these words on your map.

10. Why mining is so important in the 

Western States.

12. Why salmon, which live most of the time in the ocean, are caught far up some of the rivers. 

13. Why the West is especially noted for its beautiful scenery.

Where do you find lowlands? 

Where do you find mountains? Where do you find plateaus, or lands that are higher than lowlands but not so high as mountains? By looking at the colors on the map, then, you can understand one very good reason why there are so many kinds of regions in the western part of the country. 

What do the many 

Differences in growing season. The map may also be used to help you understand another good reason why there are so many different kinds of regions in the Western States. Notice that there is a great difference in latitude between the southern boundary of the 

Western States and the northern boundary. Would you expect this difference in latitude to make a difference in the length of the growing season? Look at

Figure 17 and see just what the difference in growing season is.

Figure 206. A population map of the Western States. If you study carefully the rainfall and irrigation maps for this region, you will find that they help to explain most of the areas of dense population. 

As you traveled through the Western

States by railroad you saw many different kinds of regions. You found wet regions and dry regions, hot regions and cold regions, high rocky mountains and fertile lowlands. In fact, it would be necessary to travel in many parts of the world before you would find such a variety of regions. Naturally, you wonder why the

West has so many different kinds of regions. Keep this question in mind as you read the following paragraphs. 

Differences in altitude. For one thing, the land is of many different altitudes, that is, many different heights above sea 

Earlier you learned that altitude also makes a difference in the length of the growing season. As you go up the side of a high mountain the growing season becomes shorter and shorter. Since the 

West has land of many different altitudes, you can see that the length of the growing season in different regions must vary greatly. Look at Figure 17 and notice how the length of the growing season varies from place to place.

Differences in rainfall. If you look at

Figure 16, you will find that there is also a great difference in rainfall in various parts of the Western States. Notice that the heaviest rainfall in the country is in the northwestern part of the Western

States along the Pacific coast. The map on pages 206-207 will show you that there are mountains in the region of heavy rainfall. These mountains are the 

Coast Ranges and the Cascade Range. 

Now look again at Figure 16 and notice that east of these mountains the rainfall is very light.

In order to understand why it rains so much on the mountains along the Pacific coast and why there is so little rain farther east, it is necessary to study winds. 

The winds along the Pacific coast come from over the ocean. As they blow over the water they pick up moisture. When these winds rise to cross over the mountains, they become cooler and lose much of their moisture as rain or snow. The rain and the snow fall on the western side of the mountains. It rains very little on the eastern side or on the lands farther east because the winds have already lost much of their moisture. 

Figure 207 will show you how the winds blow in various parts of the earth. The United States, as you can see, is in the belt of the westerly winds. 

In other words, most of the steady winds that blow across the country come from a westerly direction.

Farther south, in southern California, during the summer the winds blow from the east over the land and bring little rain. in the Great Plains? Which of the

Plains? What does the color on the map tell you about the altitude of the 

Great Plains? Notice that the Rocky

Mountains lie to the west and that several rivers from the mountains flow eastward across the Great Plains. These rivers get most of their water from the rain and snow that fall in the mountains. 

Farming by irrigation. From your study of the North Central States you have learned that the Great Plains are very, very dry. This is because the winds that come from the west over the mountains carry little moisture. Look at Figure 16 and find just what the rainfall is. Farming in such a dry region,

Figure 208. An irrigation dam. Be fore the dam was built, a river flowed at the bottom of the deep valley. Now the water from heavy rains does not run away down the valley. It is held back behind the dam and forms a reservoir or lake. The water in this river came from far-away mountains. By means of dams such as this, bodies of water may be formed even in a very dry country. 

The water from this reservoir is carried over the land in ditches such as are shown on the following page. Thus a barren land is made very fertile. of course, is carried on with difficulty. 

In most places, the land itself is very fertile, but it needs more moisture. 

Plenty of water for irrigation can be obtained from the rivers. These rivers, as you have just learned, come from the mountains to the west. The rains, together with the melting snow and ice in the mountains, give them a steady flow.

In order to help the farmers of the Great Plains, the government has built a number of dams in the rivers. Sometimes these dams form lakes or reservoirs in the rivers by holding back the water. 

Figure 209 shows where land is irrigated in the Western States. When farmers use water from government dams, they must pay a certain fee, according to the quantity of water they use. It costs great sums of money to build dams like the one in Figure 208 above. You can readily see, then, why farmers must pay a fee for the use of the water. 

The water from reservoirs or rivers is carried over the land in large ditches. 

Large gates are opened to let the water into the ditches. Figure 210 shows what the water gates are like. The water is carried across the fields by small ditches that branch from the larger ditches. 

Part of the water in the ditches soaks into the ground. In this way the soil gets moisture much as it would from rain. 

Figure 211 shows how the ditches are used to irrigate an orchard in one of the 

Western States. 

Certain crops grow well on irrigated land. Alfalfa is one of these crops. It

Figure 211. An irrigated orchard. The water flows along the small ditches until the soil is well soaked.

Dry farming. As you studied the 

North Central States you learned that crops on the Great Plains are sometimes raised by dry farming. You will remember that the soil is kept loose so that the moisture from rains will not evaporate, Figure 213. Where sheep are raised in the West. or escape into the air. As in the North 

Central States, the chief crops are wheat and sorghums, including Kafir corn. 

The Great Plains as grazing lands. 

By this time you should have come to think of the Great Plains largely as a grazing region. In Figure 212 find where most of the cattle are raised in the Western States, and in Figure 213 where most of the sheep are raised. If you were to travel in certain parts of the Great 12-5G

Plains, you might go for miles and miles and see nothing but ranches or great pasture fields separated by wire fences. 

This great region, all the way from Texas and New Mexico in the south to 

Montana and North Dakota in the north, is noted for its cattle and sheep. 

The work of the ranches differs from south to north. In New Mexico, since the climate is warm, the rancher can keep his cattle and sheep out of doors the year round. In the North, however, the winters are long and cold and there are many blizzards. Often the ground is covered with many feet of snow and ice. 

Here the rancher must furnish some kind of shelter for his cattle and sheep. Then, too, he must raise crops during the summer to feed the cattle and sheep during the cold winters.

In the drier parts of the Great Plains more sheep than cattle are raised. The sheep are raised partly because they can eat short grass whereas cattle cannot. 

More sheep than cattle are also raised in the hilly regions because sheep can climb much better than cattle.

Sending stock to market. Cattle from the Great Plains, as you already know, are sold in the fall. Most of the cattle are shipped by railroad. The rancher must drive his cattle to the nearest station. From here they are sent eastward directly to stock markets and packing houses or to various places in the Corn 

Belt. Why are some of them sent to the Corn Belt? Most sheep are sold in the fall. Like cattle, they go either directly to stock markets and packing houses or to places in the Corn Belt. 

Not all sheep are sent to market, for sheep are raised for wool as well as for meat. Each spring their fleecy coats must be shorn. The shearing is usually done by a machine that removes the coats of several sheep an hour. 

Can you answer these questions? 

1. Why are not the same crops raised on the 

Great Plains as are grown in the Corn Belt? 

2. How is farming carried on by irrigation? 

3. What crops can be grown by dry farming? 

4. Why is cattle raising the chief occupation on the Great Plains?

FARMING IN THE ROCKY MOUNTAINS 

The eastern front of the Rocky Mountains rises bold and sharp along the western edge of the Great Plains. The lower slopes are covered with forests, but trees do not grow on the higher slopes. 

What have you learned about altitude and climate which helps to explain why trees do not grow on the higher slopes? 

The highest place on mountain sides where trees grow is called the timber line. 

Find the timber line in Figure 214.

Above the timber line only small bushes and grasses grow. On the highest slopes it is so very cold that no plants are found. 

In fact, the highest slopes are covered with snow and ice throughout the year. 

Mountain pastures. Where there is enough grass, both cattle and sheep feed in the mountains. In the spring thousands and thousands of sheep are driven from the plains up the mountain sides. 

Figure 215 shows a flock of sheep on their way to high pastures. First the sheep are pastured on the lower slopes. 

Then, as the snow melts, they are driven higher and higher. Finally, they are driven above the timber line to the highest pastures that grow. In September

or October when the weather gets cold, they are driven back down to lower land on the plains. They are kept on the plains throughout the winter.

A flock usually contains more than a thousand sheep. One man known as a shepherd, with the help of a faithful dog, watches the sheep. The shepherd always has a camp near a place where he can get water for his sheep. Each morning, with the help of his dog, he drives the sheep out to pastures and each evening he brings them back. He leads a very lonely life. 

Usually his dog is his only companion.

He must be on the lookout all the time for bears, coyotes, and mountain lions that may attack his sheep. He must take care of any sheep that are sick and go after those that wander away.

Mountain farms. In some of the valleys in the Rocky Mountains the land is very fertile and is level enough for raising crops. Head lettuce is an important crop in high mountain lands. Potatoes, too, grow well in the higher valleys. 

This is probably because potatoes are so much at home in the mountains. Potatoes, as you may know, were first found in the mountains of South America.

Some of these potatoes were taken to 

Europe by early explorers. When they were planted in Ireland, an island near Europe, they grew so well that they became known as Irish potatoes. The potato regions in the Rocky Mountains are much like those in South America where potatoes first were found. Look at Figure 216 and find where potatoes are raised in the Western States. Now look at the map and see how high these lands Most of the lands are irrigated with water from near-by mountain streams. are. 

Figure 214. Timber line in the Rockies. This picture was taken in the winter, but you can see where the trees cease to grow on the higher slopes. Find the timber line in other mountain pictures in the book. Figure 215. Sheep on their way to summer pastures in the mountains. The slopes are still partly covered with snow, but before long they will be covered with grass. 

Among the more important crops in the lower mountain lands are apples, wheat, and alfalfa. Like potatoes, these crops are usually irrigated with water from streams that come tumbling down from the near-by peaks and slopes.

Figure 216. Where potatoes are grown in the West. 

Some problems 1. Why is sheep raising the leading occupation on the eastern slopes of the Rocky Mountains? 

2. Why can potatoes and head lettuce be grown on Rocky Mountain farms? 

3. Why is there a place on very high mountains that is known as the timber line? 

4. Why does a shepherd need a dog?

FARMING ON THE PLATEAUS 

Look at the map on pages 6-7 and notice the lands westward from the Rocky Mountains to the Cascade Range and the Sierra Nevada. Sierra is the 

Spanish word for "mountain range." 

The great area westward, as you can see on the map, is broken up into a number of plateaus. Among these plateaus are the Wyoming Basin, the Colorado Plateau, the Columbia Plateau, and the 

Great Basin. Locate all these regions, and then turn to the map on pages 206207 to find the altitude of each.

IN THE WYOMING BASIN 

The Wyoming Basin lies largely in the state of Wyoming. Would you like to know why it is called a basin? If you look at the map, you will see that it is nearly surrounded by mountains. In other words, the basin is lower than the surrounding lands. 

As you looked at Figure 16 you found that the Wyoming Basin has very little rainfall. Moreover, very little of the land is irrigated. Not much grows in the basin except grass, shrubs, and bushes.

Only along the streams is farming carried on. Sheep raising is the leading industry. 

Most of the towns in the region have grown up as sheep or wool shipping centers on railroads.

ON THE COLORADO PLATEAU 

Find the Colorado Plateau on the map.

This region lies largely in Utah, Colorado, Arizona, and New Mexico. The rainfall is very light. In some places the land is covered with beautiful colored rocks. 

These rocks are so numerous and highly colored in a certain region in Arizona that it is called the Painted Desert. In another part of Arizona there is a region known as the Petrified Forest. Here the ground is covered with logs that have turned to stone. turned to stone. These logs are from an old forest that grew here many, many years ago.

In the driest places on the Colorado Plateau nothing grows but small bushes and shrubs. Grasses grow in places where it is not quite so dry, and in places where there is still more moisture trees are found. Sheep graze on parts of the grasslands, and even in the desert they feed on bushes and shrubs. In

Figure 217. Looking down into the Grand Canyon. Notice that the land at the top of the canyon is very level. Most of the walls are bare rock. Here and there a little soil has gathered and a few trees and shrubs have found a foothold. other parts of the plateau farming is carried on by irrigation. Figure 209 will show where the government has furnished irrigation on the plateau.

The Grand Canyon of the Colorado. 

The Colorado River, which flows across the Colorado Plateau, is one of the natural wonders of the world. Find this river on the map and trace it all the way from where it rises in the state of Colorado to where it empties into the Gulf of 

California. Along part of its route the river has worn away the rocks and formed a deep gorge known as the Grand 

Canyon of the Colorado River. In some places the gorge, which is one of the wonders of nature, is 6,000 feet deep. 

You will notice in Figure 217 that the canyon is really a deep valley with steep, rocky slopes. It is more than two hundred miles long and five to twelve miles wide. The lands bordering the deepest and most beautiful part of the canyon have been set aside by the government for a national park. Find this park on the map. It is in this park that the 

Painted Desert is found. 

If you were to look down from the rim of the canyon to the river far below, it would have the appearance of a tiny stream. Along the sides of the canyon, you would see great masses of rock piled up like cathedrals and temples. The rocks would be of many colors-yellow, orange, pink, red, and purple. They would be especially brilliant in the bright

Figure 218. Desert lands near the mouth of the Colorado. The tall plants are cactuses. Wherever you see them, you may know the climate is very dry, or arid. sunshine. It is impossible to describe the Grand Canyon because words fail to tell just how beautiful it is.

The great Boulder Dam. On the map find Las Vegas in southern Nevada.

Southeast of this city the government has constructed a great dam across the canyon of the Colorado River. This dam, known as Boulder Dam, is 729 feet high and one of the largest in the world. 

It holds back the water of the Colorado, forming a large lake known as Lake 

Mead over a hundred miles long. At the foot of the dam are power plants that use water power for the making of electricity. The electricity is carried to cities and towns in various directions for hundreds of miles. Some of the water from Lake Mead is carried across the mountains to the city of Los Angeles in a huge pipe known as an aqueduct.

Other water from the lake is used for irrigation of near-by lands. 

Farming along the lower Colorado. If you were to follow the Colorado River southward from Boulder Dam, you would find that the canyon gradually disappears. Then the river flows through vast stretches of desert lowland much like that shown in Figure 218. Some of these stretches receive less than five inches of rainfall a year. The water held back by Boulder Dam, however, provides excellent irrigation over a great many acres. Other wide areas are also irrigated especially in southwestern 

Arizona and southeastern California. 

Find these irrigated regions in Figure 209.

Here the parched, dry lands have been turned into gardens, grain fields, and orchards. In the orchards are found oranges, olives, and even dates. An especially fine grade of cotton is raised on some of the irrigated lands. This cotton is an Egyptian cotton, the same kind as is raised along the Nile River in Africa. It can be grown only on dry irrigated lands because rain would ruin its silky fibers. 

To the east of the Colorado River. 

Find Phoenix, Arizona, on the map. 

On what river is this city situated?

About seventy-five miles from Phoenix, in the hilly region of Arizona, is the famous Roosevelt Dam. This dam holds back the waters of the Salt River and forms a great lake or reservoir. Water from this lake is used for irrigating thousands of acres in the Salt River 

Valley around Phoenix. Look at Figure 

209 to find some of the irrigated areas in this part of the country. 

In many other parts of Arizona, as well as in most of New Mexico, agriculture is the principal occupation. Although the rainfall is light, many crops are raised. 

Stock raising, too, is a leading industry.

In several of the rivers and streams dams have been built to secure water for irrigation. On the irrigated lands tropical fruits are raised, especially dates,

oranges, and grapefruit. Winter vegetables are an important crop. Many acres are planted to forage crops, espe cially alfalfa. 

On the map find Santa Fe and Albuquerque in central New Mexico. Santa 

Fe is the capital city. Albuquerque is the center of trade for a large area and has a variety of industries. Both Santa

Fe and Albuquerque are noted for their healthful climate.

In southern Arizona find Tucson. This city is the center of a stock-raising region and is noted as a health resort.

In the Imperial Valley. On the map find the lowland called the Imperial Valley. This region is hot and dry, as is that along the lower Colorado River.

Some parts of it are irrigated with water from the Colorado River. The long growing season and the warm sunshine enable plants to grow throughout the year. Among the leading products are oranges, olives, dates, Egyptian cotton, and other subtropical crops. The region is also noted for the growing of vegetables. Cantaloupes, lettuce, and early vegetables are grown and shipped to all parts of the country, especially in winter. 

Where else have you found early vegetables being shipped?

ON THE COLUMBIA PLATEAU 

The Columbia Plateau lies largely along the Columbia River and includes parts of the states of Washington, Oregon, and Idaho. Find this plateau on the map on pages 6-7. What mountains border it on the east? on the west?

In the wheat country. Find the region between the Snake River on the south and the Columbia and Spokane rivers 

Figure 219. Where wheat is grown in the Western

States. The farmers in Montana and Idaho raise spring wheat; those in Colorado and New Mexico winter wheat; and those in the Northwest both winter and spring wheat. on the north. This part of the plateau, together with neighboring lands in Oregon and Idaho, is one of the great wheatproducing regions of the country. The average yield of wheat in the United

States is about fourteen bushels per acre. 

In some parts of the Columbia Plateau, however, farmers grow twenty to forty bushels per acre. The yield is high largely because the soils are extremely fertile. Now you can see why you found so much wheat along the wharves at Portland, Tacoma, and Seattle. 

Both spring and winter wheat are raised. The rainfall is light, but fortunately most of it comes in the winter or early spring. Thus the young plants get an early start. Then comes the dry summer with warm, sunny days which are needed to ripen the grain.

At present the government is building a great dam in eastern Washington

Courtesy Spokane Chamber of Commerce 

Figure 220. Grand Coulee Dam under construction. 

This huge dam, which is to hold back the water of the 

Columbia River, will be 4,300 feet long and 550 feet high, the largest man-made structure in the world. across the Columbia River. This dam, known as the Grand Coulee Dam, will hold back the water of the river and form a lake more than one hundred fifty miles long. Figure 220 shows a picture of the dam under construction. At the ends are power plants to be used for making electricity. This electricity will be carried long distances to cities and towns in this part of the country. 

Some of the water from the lake will be pumped to an old river bed known as the Grand Coulee. Thus a great reservoir will be formed for purposes of irrigation. Water from the reservoir will be carried over the land in canals. Altogether it will supply moisture for 1,200,000 acres of fertile land now too dry for farming. This great tract of land will support thousands of people. Most of the people will live on farms, but others will help to build towns as trading centers. All this land is situated west of the city of Spokane, in a section of the state known as the Big Bend because of a bend in the river. 

Valleys of apples. On the Columbia

Plateau are several valleys famous for the growing of apples. Streams fed by rain and melting snows in the mountains furnish the valleys with water. This water is used for irrigation and carried to orchards in little ditches. The dry air helps to keep the fruit trees free from disease, and the bright sunshine helps them to grow. Altogether, then, the conditions are especially good, and many trees have been planted. Washington, Oregon, and Idaho rank high in the production of apples, Washington leading the country.

One of the valleys noted for the growing of apples is the Yakima Valley near the eastern edge of the Cascade Range. 

Find this valley on the map. Other valleys in the West noted for the growing of apples are the Wenatchee in Washington, the Hood in Oregon, and the Snake in Idaho.

Cities on the Columbia Plateau. The largest city on the Columbia Plateau is Spokane. Find this city on the map.

Notice that it is situated on the Spokane 

River. Spokane is an important flourmilling center. The wheat comes from great wheat lands on the plateau. Some of the mills are run by water power supplied by the river. Besides flour mills, Spokane has meat-packing plants, lumber mills, and mills that make lum-

ber products. It is also an important trading center for the surrounding area. 

Other busy trading centers on the 

Columbia Plateau are Yakima, Wenatchee, Walla Walla, Boise, and Pocatello. 

Find these cities on the map. Which ones are in Washington? Which ones are in Idaho? Which one is the capital of 

Idaho? Yakima and Wenatchee are in apple regions, and Walla Walla is in the wheat lands. Boise is surrounded by irrigated lands in a fertile farming region, some of which are shown at the right in Figure 221. Pocatello is a trading center for the rich potato and sugar-beet country to the west and north.

If you were to journey southward from the Columbia Plateau, you would come to the Great Basin. Find this region on the map. What states share the Great 

Basin? Like the Wyoming Basin, this region is bordered by mountains. From the colors on the map you will see that some of the Great Basin is only about

Although much of the Great Basin is a high plateau, there are a few lowlands in the southernmost part, and one small area is actually below the level of the sea. This area is Death

A region of little rainfall. What do

Figure 221. Boise, the capital of Idaho. The capitol is near the center of the picture. The city is situated in a beautiful valley among the mountains. rainfall in the Great Basin? Most of the winds that blow over this region come from the west. When these winds rise to cross the high mountains, they are cooled and lose much of their moisture. 

What, then, would you expect to find about the rainfall on the western slopes? 

What would you expect to find as the winds move down the eastern slopes?

These winds not only contain little moisture but tend to take up moisture as they become warmer. Thus you can understand why much of the Great Basin is so dry. In what other parts of the 

Western States did you find winds blowing from over the mountains and causing dry regions to the east? Mountains and winds help to explain why all of the plateau lands in the Western States have very little rainfall. Since the region is so dry, grazing is the only industry in many parts of the Great Basin.

Cities of the Great Basin. 

Most of the cities in Utah are situated east and south of Great Salt Lake in the region of irrigation. The largest of the cities is Salt

Lake City, the capital, which you may find on the map. 

Figure 222 is a scene in its business section. Salt Lake 

City is a trading center for parts of four states on the plateaus. It is also the manufacturing center of a large area. Among its factories are oil refineries, creameries where butter is made, and textile mills. It is especially noted for its fine buildings. Many tourists visit the city each year to see various points of interest, such as the lake and the near-by mountains. 

Courtesy Salt Lake City Chamber of Commerce 

Figure 222. A part of Salt Lake City as it appears from an airplane. In the foreground is the famous Mormon Temple Square. Back of the city are the Wasatch Mountains and near by is the Great Salt Lake. These points of interest help to make the city a popular tourist resort. 

Great Salt Lake. Great Salt Lake is by far the largest body of water in the Great Basin. Find this lake on the map. 

Notice that a few streams run into the lake but that it has no outlet. For a long, long time streams have been running down from the high mountains into the lake. These streams all contain a little salt. As the water on the lake evaporates, or passes off into the air, the salt is left in the lake. For this reason the water in the lake is very salty. If a river flowed out of the lake, would the water still be salty? Why or why not?

The water from Great Salt Lake cannot be used for irrigation, because salty water is not good for growing crops. Extensive irrigation, however, is carried on in the near-by lands. The water comes from streams which start in the Wasatch Range. Find these mountains on the map. 

Find Ogden on the map. This important city is situated where two rivers meet. The rivers contain waterfalls that provide power for making electricity. 

Ogden has numerous factories, including sugar refineries, canneries, and cereal plants. It is also a noted railroad center as well as a center for trade. Provo and 

Logan, Utah's third and fourth largest cities, are located in rich farming districts. Murray is a copper smelting center. Find all these cities on the map. 

Now find Carson City and Reno in the state of Nevada. What does the sign on the map tell about Carson City? Both these cities are situated in small river valleys and surrounded by farming and stock raising regions. Near Carson City are mined large quantities of silver.

FARMING IN THE WESTERN VALLEYS AND LOWLANDS 

As you traveled through the Western States by railroad you found a number of very fertile valleys and lowlands.

Most of these valleys and lowlands are in the western part of the section. Turn to the map and find the San Joaquin and Sacramento rivers in central California, the Willamette River in northwestern 

Oregon, and Puget Sound in northwestern

Washington. 

THE VALLEY OF CALIFORNIA 

What does the color on the map tell you about the land between the Sierra 

Nevada and the Coast Ranges? This lowland is called the Valley of California. 

It is 450 miles long, and in some places more than 50 miles wide. The southern part of the valley is really the valley of the San Joaquin River, and the northern part is the valley of the Sacramento. 

Into what bay do these rivers empty?

How the valley was settled. A long, long time ago what is now the Valley of California was a wide gulf of the ocean, bordered by mountains. Streams that flowed down from the surrounding mountains carried silt or mud and other materials into the gulf and filled up the valley. Therefore you can see that the soils here are very fertile.

It was not the good soils, however, that brought settlers to the Valley of California, but the discovery of gold. In 

1848 a few men found shining particles of gold in the sands of a stream. Then thousands of people, seeking fortunes, came from the eastern part of the country. Some of them found gold and became rich, but others searched in vain. 

A few of the people went back, but others decided to make the country their home. 

Irrigated by mountain streams. The soil in the Great Valley of California is very fertile, but the rainfall is very light. 

Moreover, most of the rain falls in the winter, and the summers are long and dry. Water, then, for growing crops must be obtained by irrigation. Many streams tumble down the western slopes of the Sierra Nevada. Water from these streams is carried to the fields in the valley by a network of canals and ditches. 

To secure additional water, the state of California has undertaken an extensive program of storing floodwater in the central valley and releasing it later for purposes of irrigation.

A noted fruit region. The Valley of 

California is one of the most productive regions in the world. Its fertile land, long growing season, and mild winters are especially favorable for the growing of fruits. If you were to visit the valley, you would find hundreds of orchards and vineyards. The orchards produce such

Notice 

Figure 223. A prune orchard in California. The picture was taken when the trees were in blossom. that the orchard is on the floor of the valley. fruits as figs, pears, prunes, plums, and olives. The picture above shows one of the many prune orchards to be found. 

Look at Figure 224 and notice the black area in central California, showing that the valley produces unusually large quantities of fruits.

Many fruits are shipped to market fresh, but far more are dried. In fact, the drying of fruits is an important industry in the valley. The dry, sunny summers are especially good for the purpose. Among the leading fruits dried are grapes and prunes. When grapes are dried, they are sold as raisins. Perhaps you didn't know that raisins are dried grapes. 

California produces most of the raisins used in the United States and also sends large quantities to countries in other parts of the world.

Late in summer the grapes are picked and put on trays to dry in the hot sun. 

Then the juice slowly turns into sugar and the grapes become raisins. While the grapes are drying, the bunches are turned over and over so that both sides will dry. Toward the end of the drying stage, the trays are shaded to allow the raisins to dry more slowly. 

Other products of the valley. Besides fruits, the valley produces large quantities of vegetables, rice, wheat, alfalfa, and cotton. Some of these products are grown in one region and others in another. 

The rice is grown on the lowlands of the 

Sacramento River, where the fields may be flooded by irrigation. Thus the rice here grows in water just as it does in 

Louisiana, Arkansas, and Texas. In

CITIES IN THE VALLEY OF CALIFORNIA Find Fresno, Stockton, and Sacra mento on the map. Fresno and Stockton are the largest cities in the San Joaquin

Valley, and Sacramento is the largest in the Sacramento Valley.

Fresno is in the center of the great raisin district. It is known largely as a fruit center and has large packing houses for fruits. It also has flour mills and machine shops. 

Stockton carries on an extensive trade in fruit, vegetables, and other products from near-by farms. It has good shipping facilities both by railroad and by ships on the San Joaquin River. Also it is an important manufacturing city, making farm machinery, paper, and a variety of products. 

Sacramento, as you have already learned, is the capital of California. It is at the head of navigation on the Sacramento River and ships a wide variety of products to Asia and Australia. It also has many factories and mills and, of course, is the chief trading center for the surrounding farming region. 

PACIFIC COAST CITIES IN CALIFORNIA 

San Francisco and the Golden Gate. 

The largest cities in central California are on San Francisco Bay, for it is the only natural gateway through the Coast 

Ranges into the Valley of California. 

Find this bay on the map. What city is on the western side of the bay? What cities are on the eastern side? 

San Francisco, as you can see on the map, is built at the end of a peninsula between the Pacific Ocean and San

Francisco Bay. The Golden Gate, a strip of water about a mile wide, connects the bay with the ocean. Figure 225 is a picture of a huge bridge built across the water at this point. Ships come in

through the Golden Gate to docks on the eastern side of the peninsula. 

In addition to being a shipping port, San Francisco is a great manufacturing center as well. It turns many of the raw products that come from farms, mines, forests, and various other places into manufactured articles. Food products rank high among its manufactured articles. It also roasts coffee which is brought in from foreign ports. Among the other manufactured articles are furniture, machinery, and clothing. 

San Francisco's neighbors. Across the bay from San Francisco, as you have already learned, are Oakland and Berkeley. Both these cities are situated near the foothills of the Coast Ranges and both are noted for their great beauty. 

They are connected with San Francisco by the famous San Francisco-Oakland

Bay Bridge, which is more than four miles long and high enough for ships to pass beneath. Ferryboats also run back and forth across the bay. Both Oakland and Berkeley have many factories and many railroads that lead to the east, north, and south. Oakland manufactures automobiles and electrical machinery and cans fruits and vegetables. 

Berkeley makes motors, breakfast foods, soap, and other products. Near by is a third important city, Richmond, that has extensive oil refineries. 

To the south of San Francisco find

San Jose. This city is situated in a small fertile valley especially noted for raising and drying prunes. Near by also are mercury mines. Mercury, as you may know, is used in making thermometers, mirrors, medicines, and paints.

COASTAL REGION OF SOUTHERN CALIFORNIA 

Along the Pacific coast, south of the Valley of California, is another region of fruits and sunshine. The most important parts of the region are the narrow strip of coastal lowland that extends southward from Los Angeles and the hills to the north and east. On the whole, the region is very thickly settled. 

About three million people make their homes in this part of California.

Figure 226. A relief map of the region around Los Angeles. The heavy line is the boundary of Los Angeles. Notice the narrow extension of the city down to the harbor on San Pedro Bay. Hollywood, a part of Los Angeles, is a little north of the center of the city. 

Both this map and the picture in Figure 203 show that there are mountains near by. You can see clearly, too, how the railroads coming into the city follow the low places, or passes, through the mountains. Usually these passes are river valleys.

The climate in this region is delightful. The days may be hot, but the nights are always cool. Moreover, all the country is very beautiful. 

The seashore, with the snowcapped mountains not far away, the warm winters, and the fertile land have all helped to attract a great many people. 

CITIES OF THE COASTAL REGION 

In a thickly settled region there are always numerous cities. Look at the map and notice the number of cities in the coastal region of southern 

California. The largest of these cities is Los Angeles.

Figure 227. A motion-picture studio in Hollywood, a part of Los Angeles. 

This picture shows a scene about to be taken in the studio. Motion pictures provide work for many people besides those who appear on the screen. 

Los Angeles, the sunshine city. Not only is Los Angeles the largest city of the coastal region, but it is also the largest city west of the Mississippi River. 

In Figure 226 you can see that the city does not lie on the ocean. The business center is more than twenty miles from the coast. A part of the city, however, has been extended along a narrow strip of land that leads to San Pedro on the coast. This strip of land and the excellent harbor at San Pedro make 

Los Angeles a great seaport.

Los Angeles has an unusual water system. Its water comes from the mountains 250 miles to the east. In some places along the way it runs in canals and in other places flows through great steel and concrete pipes. Additional water, as you have already learned, comes from Lake Mead above Boulder Dam. 

What conditions have helped Los 

Angeles to become a large city? To begin with, it has a delightful climate and attracts many visitors. The entire region in and around the city, as you will remember, has a subtropical climate. 

Then, too, not far away, as you will discover, is a large productive oil field. The oil industry has brought thousands of people to the city. Oil is used in many factories and thus has favored the city much as coal has favored Pittsburgh in the eastern part of the country. 

Figure 227 helps to explain another reason why Los Angeles has grown. 

Hollywood, a part of Los Angeles, has become the greatest motion-picture center in the world. The industry developed here partly because of the sunny conditions which are good for taking pictures. 

It also was helped by the great variety of

harbor. The high mountains to the east, however, make it difficult to send goods across the country. The mild winters at San Diego attract many visitors. Like 

Los Angeles, it has a subtropical climate.

VALLEY LANDS IN OREGON AND WASHINGTON The western part of Wash ington and Oregon has a milder climate than any other part of the United States in the same latitude. On the map on pages 8-9 notice that these states are about as far north as the Dakotas or New

England. In the Dakotas and in New England many houses have storm doors to help keep out the bitter, cold winter winds. In western 

Washington and Oregon, however, the thermometer usually stands above freezing even in midwinter. 

Figure 228. A fruit-packing plant. Here oranges are being packed for shipping. At each side and in the center empty crates are moving along on a conveyer. Bernardino in Figure 226. These neighbors of Los Angeles are in the heart of the most important orange-growing district in the West. If you were to visit any part of the surrounding region, you would find packing houses like that shown in Figure 228. The growing of citrus fruits is southern California's most important industry. 

Our southwestern city. On the map find San Diego on the coast in southern 

California. Notice that it is our most southwestern city. It has a fine natural 

You have already learned that the winds in western Oregon and Washington blow from over the ocean. These westerly winds bring much moisture and lead to a heavy rainfall as they rise to cross over the mountains. These same winds give western Oregon and Washington its mild climate. In winter the water of the ocean, as you will remember, is warmer than the land because water cools more slowly than land. Thus the water of the ocean warms the winds before they reach the land and helps to keep the coastal lands warm. 

How would you expect the warm winds that blow over this region to affect the length of the growing season?

The Willamette Valley. Find the 

Willamette River on the map. Between what two mountain ranges does it flow?

The Willamette Valley is a very fine farming region. Among the leading crops are wheat, oats, hops, and fruit.

The valley is noted, however, for its prunes and berries. The cool, moist climate is especially favorable for the growing of berries. Large quantities of loganberries, strawberries, blackberries, and raspberries are grown.

The Willamette Valley is also a good dairy region. Here you will find many large pasture fields where cattle may graze throughout the year.

South of the Willamette Valley is the small but fertile valley of the Rogue River. This valley is known for its fruits and vegetables. Find it on the map. 

The Puget Sound region. Find the lowland around Puget Sound on the map and in Figure 229. What mountains border the region on the west? on the east? Many of the same conditions are found around Puget Sound as you just found in the Willamette Valley. In fact, the Puget Sound region is really a northward extension of the Willamette Valley. 

Berry fields, dairy farms, poultry farms, and truck farms dot all the land.

Perhaps you have already noticed in your study of geography that mining is usually done in highlands rather than in lowlands. Would you expect, then, to find much or little mining in the Western 

States? Check your answer to this question as you read the following pages.

THE PRECIOUS MINERALS 

Gold and silver are frequently mined together. The rocks that contain one often contain the other. These rocks, like those that contain iron, when dug out of the earth, are called ore. 

Placer mining. Gold in the West, as you have learned, was first found in sand and gravel in the bed of a stream. The miners gathered the sand and gravel in pans and washed out the gold with water.

This kind of mining is called placer mining. However, in placer mining

Figure 232. A large dredge in a gold region of California. The piles of waste materials shown around the dredge have been dug up and washed for gold. today the sand and gravel are scooped up with a machine called a dredge. The gold is then washed out and the sand and gravel are left in piles along the streams. 

Most gold obtained in this manner is free from other materials. Figure 232 shows a powerful dredge used for placer mining in California. 

Shaft mining. Most gold and silver lie buried deep in the rocks of the mountains. To reach them, it is necessary to dig or drill shafts down into the rocks. 

Cripple Creek, Colorado, is a center of deep mines. Find this city on the map. 

The shafts leading into some of the mines at Cripple Creek are several hundred feet deep. When the rocks have been loosened in the mine, they are brought up the shafts and loaded into railroad cars. 

Then they are taken to a factory where the gold and silver are removed by heating. Such a factory for removing metals from ores is known as a smelter. 

You can see that taking ore from deep mines and smelting it is more expensive than getting gold by placer mining. 

Producing regions. Look at Figure 233 to see where gold and silver are found. 

California, where gold was first found in the West, still mines more of this product than any other state, and Utah leads in the production of silver. Other goldand silver-producing states are Colorado, Montana, Nevada, and Arizona. The

West, as you can see, is very rich in the precious minerals. 

Denver, the mile-high city. Several cities owe their early location and growth partly to gold and silver. Among these cities is Denver. Find Denver on the map. Notice that it lies between the 

Rocky Mountains and the Great Plains. 

Can you suggest why Denver is sometimes called the Queen of the Plains?

Denver has become a large and busy city largely because it lies between the mining regions to the west and the grazing and farming regions to the east. Although its factories produce mining machinery, meat packing is its leading industry. It is also a great sheep market. 

Sheep come to Denver from mountain pastures and from the Great Plains. Flour mills grind wheat from the plains.
