COPPER, LEAD, ZINC, AND IRON 

Look at Figure 233 and find where copper is mined in the United States.

The United States produces nearly as much copper as all the other countries of the world. Arizona produces more than any other state, having the richest deposits that have yet been found in the country. The mining is done in several parts of the state, but Bisbee, Miami, and Jerome are the more important centers. Find these towns on the map. 

Other important copper mines are found at Bingham Canyon, Utah, and near Ely, Nevada. In these regions shaft mining is not necessary, since the copper ores lie in huge beds near the surface. 

After the ore is loosened, it is loaded on cars by steam shovels.

A third region especially noted for the mining of copper is western Montana.

Find Butte on the map. This city is in the heart of the copper district. Now find Helena, the capital of Montana. 

Not far from Helena are gold, silver, and zinc mines. Large quantities of lead are also mined in the state. 

Montana has a number of smelters, or factories, for separating metals from their ores and making them ready for use. 

On the map find Anaconda and Great

Falls. Both of these cities are important copper-refining centers. On what river is Great Falls? How does its name suggest one of its advantages for manufacturing? It owes its importance as a refining center largely to falls in the river which are used in making electricity. 

The electricity provides heat needed to separate the copper from other materials in the ores.

One of the richest lead-mining centers in the United States is the Coeur d'Alene district in northern Idaho. Find this region on the map. It produces not only large quantities of lead, but also much silver which is mixed with lead ores. Other important lead and silver

A MINERAL FOR THE TABLE-SALT 

As you studied the Southern States you found salt being taken out of the ground. In the West it is obtained by evaporating salty water. Most streams on the earth take up a little salt as they flow over the land. They carry this salt to the sea and occasionally to lakes where it settles. Thus the water of the ocean and a few lakes has become very salty. 

In California, salt is obtained by evaporating ocean water. In Utah, water is taken from Great Salt Lake and evaporated for the salt.

FISHING IN THE WESTERN STATES 

As you may expect, since the Western

States are along the ocean, many people here make a living by fishing. Several kinds of fish are caught in waters along the coast, including halibut, herring, cod, and tuna fish. Large quantities of the 

Century Photos 

Figure 237. Salmon leaping over a waterfall. The fish near the center of the picture has just leaped from the lower water. The one at the left is entering the water head first after having made two jumps. fish are canned. Some are dried and salted, and some are packed in ice and shipped fresh.

Salmon in the Columbia River. The most important fish in the West are salmon. They are not caught in the ocean, as you might suppose, but in the streams that flow into the ocean. They live in such streams until they are partly grown. Then they travel downstream and live for several years in the ocean. 

Strangely, however, after spending most of their lives in the salt water of the ocean they return to the streams from which they came. Thus they lead very interesting lives, partly in fresh water and partly in salt water.

The Columbia River is noted for the large numbers of salmon caught in its

waters. Each spring salmon come in from the ocean and swim upstream for hundreds of miles. They even jump over waterfalls in order to reach their old homes. Figure 237 is a picture of salmon making their way over a waterfall. 

When the salmon get as far upstream as necessary, they deposit their eggs in little round nests or holes which they scoop out in the gravel bed of the river. 

Then they die. 

How salmon are caught and marketed.

The fishing is done when the salmon are going upstream. Most of the fish are caught in nets or by hook and line near the mouths of the rivers. Then many of them are cleaned and canned, but others are shipped fresh. 

The salmon industry is not carried on the year round because the fish swim upstream only during the spring and summer months. During these months, though, the industry gives work to many people and provides a popular kind of food for markets throughout the country. 

Many cities and towns are engaged in the salmon industry, but the most important centers are Seattle and Bellingham, Washington; and Astoria, Oregon.

Life as a fisherman. Imagine that you are a salmon fisherman on the Columbia River.

Tell about your work and the fish you catch. 

LUMBERING IN THE WESTERN STATES 

Rocky Mountain forests. In many parts of the Rocky Mountains trees grow abundantly. They grow best in places where the winds bring rain as they rise to cross over the mountains. There are more trees on the western slopes than on the eastern slopes because the winds commonly blow from the west. What are these winds called? Usually the trees grow rather high up the slopes because the soils are dry at the foot of the mountains. Do you remember what is meant by the timber line?

National forest reserves. Large areas of land in the mountains have been set aside by the government as national forests. If you were to visit the Rocky 

Mountains and camp in a national forest, you would probably meet some of the forest rangers. These rangers lead very lonely lives in their work. They usually live high in the mountains and go for weeks without seeing a single person.

They must be on a constant lookout for forest fires. If a fire breaks out, they must get fire fighters and check it before great damage is done. Sometimes a great opening must must be cut through the forests to stop a raging fire. Fires may be started by lightning during a storm, by the spark of a locomotive, or by a careless camper. Forest rangers are also on a constant lookout for insects that damage the trees in one way or another. 

Then, too, they often mark the trees that may be cut for lumber. 

Forest rangers are not only friends of trees, but friends of animals as well. 

They help to protect certain wild animals that live in the national forests. 

THE PACIFIC FORESTS 

The mountain slopes of western Washington and Oregon and northern California are covered with great forests of cedar, pine, spruce, fir, and redwood trees. The most valuable tree in this region is the Douglas fir. The height

People who live near by also find much work to do in making lumber products.

PLAY AND TRAVEL IN THE WESTERN STATES 

The western states are noted for their natural scenery. Chief among the attractions are the national parks which the government has set aside for playgrounds or for historical purposes. 

Beautiful canyons and caves. One of the most famous parks is Yellowstone 

National Park in northwestern Wyoming. 

This park includes the beautiful canyon and falls of the Yellowstone River, strong geysers or springs that spout hot water into the air, and many interesting animals. Another similar park is Yosemite

National Park, on the western slopes of the Sierra Nevada in the state of California. This park is noted for the beautiful falls of the Yosemite River and numerous imposing cliffs. Not far away is Sequoia National Park, noted for huge redwood trees known as sequoias. The park most noted for its canyons is Grand 

Canyon National Park along the Colorado River in Arizona. The canyon here is very deep and has attractively colored walls. Other similar parks are Bryce

Canyon National Park and Zion National 

Park in Utah. A somewhat different park is Carlsbad Caverns National Park in New Mexico, which contains the largest caves in the world.

Majestic mountains and trees. Many parks in the West are noted for their beautiful mountain scenery. The Water ton-Glacier International Peace Park, which extends across the northern boundary of Montana, contains towering peaks, glaciers, and lakes equal to those found any place in the world. Farther south in 

Colorado is Rocky Mountain National 

Park with snow-capped mountains, heavy forests, and interesting animals. Still farther south in Colorado is Pikes Peak, which may be explored by a winding road that extends all the way to the top, one of the highest roads in the country. 

Famous volcanic peaks and craters.

The West has several peaks and craters formed many years ago by volcanoes. 

Mount Rainier in Washington is noted for its snowy cap, glaciers, and mountain forests and meadows. Farther south, in 

Oregon, are a similar mountain, Mount

Hood, and a lake in the opening of an old volcano, Crater Lake. Still farther south, in California, is Mount Lassen, the only active volcano in the country.

UNIT SEVEN CANADA AND INTRODUCING  

You have just finished a study of your own country, the United States. Now you will study your neighbors to the north. How much do you know about these neighbors? How do they make a living? What crops do they raise? 

What do they make in their factories?

These are some of the questions that will be answered as you study.

NORTHERN LANDS Locating Canada and northern lands. 

In order that you may get a picture of the lands you are about to study, turn to the map on pages 284-285. This is a map of North America. Find the 

United States on the map. Notice that it covers a great area and that there are lands both to the north and to the south. 

Think first of the lands to the north. 

Find the Dominion of Canada, Alaska, Labrador, and two islands, Newfoundland and Greenland.

Something about the countries. When you studied the United States, you may have thought it was the largest country in North America. You can see from the map, however, that the Dominion of Canada is even larger. Notice that it is wider from east to west and that it extends far northward, including a tattered fringe of islands in the Arctic

Ocean. The Dominion of Canada is one of a group of countries known as the 

British Empire. The word dominion is used as the name of one of the large divisions of the British Empire that makes its own laws. Usually, however, 240

the Dominion of Canada is spoken of merely as Canada. 

Large as Canada is, you can see from the map on pages 284-285 that it does not cover all of northern North America.

Labrador, along the Atlantic coast, and the island of Newfoundland are not a part of Canada. Together they form another division of the British Empire. 

Alaska, in the northwestern part of 

North America, should be of special interest to you because it is a dependency of the United States. Our country, as you will find later, controls lands in other parts of the world. These scattered lands are known as dependencies.

Greenland, northeast of Canada, is the largest island in the world. It belongs to Denmark, a small country of Europe. 

Study guides. Talk about the follow ing problems in class and add others of your own. Use the problems as you study and make certain you can explain every one. 

1. Why the population of Canada is so much smaller than that of the United States. 

2. Why most of the people in northern North 

America live in southern Canada.

6. How the people of Alaska make a living. 

7. Why fishing is the leading occupation in 

Newfoundland. 

8. Why Labrador and Newfoundland have a colder climate than most places of the same latitude. 

9. Why the southern coastal lands of Alaska have a warmer climate than most places of the same latitude. 

10. Why Greenland has not become an important country.

IV. Using several maps 1. The boundary between the United States and Canada is about halfway from the equator to the North Pole. If you were to travel northward all the way to the North Pole, you would find that the latitude there is 90°. In other words, it is 90° from the equator to the North 

Pole. Now find the parallel marked 75° in northern Canada. How many degrees is this parallel from the equator? How many degrees from the North Pole? Would you expect the region here to have a longer or shorter growing season than the United States? Why?

THE CLIMATE OF NORTHERN NORTH AMERICA 

Visiting southern Canada. If you were to travel from the United States into 

Canada, you probably could not tell when you crossed the boundary line between the two countries. You would find most of the people of Canada speaking your own language. They would be wearing the same kind of clothes. They would be riding in the same kinds of trains and automobiles. They would be raising the same kinds of crops as the people in the northern part of the United 

States. They would be running their factories in the same way. You may think of southern Canada, then, as a region much like that of the northern part of your own country. 

3. Look at Figure 245 and see where most of the people live in Canada and the northern lands. Use other maps to help explain why the people live where they do. 

Visiting far-northern lands. If you were to travel northward toward the 

North Pole, however, you would find conditions quite different. The farther north you went, the fewer people and the fewer towns you would find. Soon you would come to a land of forests. 

There would be no railroads, and you would find it necessary to travel either on foot or in canoes on small streams such as the one shown in Figure 246. 

In winter you might use dog sleds as the

Eskimos do. If you pushed on through the forests, you would finally come to a region where only small trees and bushes grow. The few Indians who live in this region call it the "land of little sticks."

Still farther north much of the land would be covered with snow and ice. Now and then you might see a few reindeer. 

If you were to travel northward in summer, you would find more hours of daylight the farther north you went.

In fact, you would finally come to a place where during a part of the summer the sun would shine all twenty-four hours of the day. If you were to travel northward in winter, however, you would find fewer hours of daylight the farther north you went. 

Finally you would come to a place where during a part of the winter the sun would not shine at all. 

Of course a trip to farnorthern lands could be taken only during the sum- Figure 246. In the Canadian forests. Notice how thickly the trees crowd down to the edge of the water. Traveling is done chiefly by canoe. mer. Even then it would be almost impossible because there are no roads. 

During the winter all the country would be covered with snow and ice, and great blizzards would sweep across the lands.

The winters are very long. The summers, on the other hand, are very short. 

Moreover, they are never very warm and do not melt all the snow and ice. Even though the sun shines many hours of the day, it is never high in the sky and does not give much heat. If you were to point to the noonday sun, you would point to a place low in the sky even in summer. 

You may think of the far-northern lands as a region of long summer days. and short winter days and of short, cool summers and long, extremely cold winters. 

Now you can understand why so few people live in the far-northern lands. 

CANADA-A FRIENDLY NEIGHBOR 

The boundaries between countries in some parts of the world are guarded by soldiers and forts. Along the three thousand miles of boundary between the 

United States and Canada, however, there are no soldiers or forts at all.

The United States and Canada are very friendly neighbors even though the boundary between them is one of the longest in the world.

You have already learned that the people across the Canadian boundary live very much as do the people in the northern part of the United States. Canada, however, has only about one-twelfth as many people as the United States. The state of Pennsylvania alone has nearly as large a population as all the vast Dominion of Canada. If you imagine the people of Pennsylvania spread all over the United States, you may be able to understand how small the population really is. The population is small because of the cold climate. Few people can make a living in the cold far-northern lands. Most of the people of Canada, then, live in the southern part of the country. Figure 245 will help you see where the people live. Find the regions that are most thickly settled.

THE DIVISIONS OF CANADA 

The various parts of Canada are called provinces and territories instead of states as in our country. Turn to page 359 and make an outline map of Canada, showing where the various divisions are. 

THE MARITIME PROVINCES 

Three small provinces. Because they are so near the sea, Nova Scotia, New 

Brunswick, and Prince Edward Island are called the Maritime Provinces. The word maritime means "bordering the sea." 

Find the Maritime Provinces on the map. Color a part of your outline map of Canada to show where they are. 

Print the abbreviation of the name of each province.

Making a living. Large areas of the land are rough and hilly, but a little farming is carried on. Potatoes, oats, and hay are the leading crops. In some places, especially in Nova Scotia, many apples are raised. Potatoes are the leading crop on Prince Edward Island. On this island, too, there are many fox farms. 

The foxes are kept in iron-fenced pens and are cared for much as the people of the United States care for cattle and hogs.

There are many forests in Nova Scotia and New Brunswick, just as in New

England. Lumbering, therefore, provides work for many people. Swiftly flowing streams with numerous falls and rapids furnish cheap water power for running sawmills and pulp and paper mills. 

Since the Maritime Provinces are situated near the great fishing banks, many people make a living by fishing, especially those who live along the coast. 

They go out in small fishing boats just as the people do in New England. 

Coal is mined in Nova Scotia and New Brunswick. Part of it is used for power in factories and part is used for making coke. There are large iron and steel mills at Sydney on Cape Breton Island. 

Find Sydney on the map. 

Two great winter shipping ports.

Montreal, on the St. Lawrence River in the province of Quebec, as you will learn later, is Canada's greatest shipping port.

Find this city on the map. It has, however, one great disadvantage as a seaport. For four or five months of the year the St. Lawrence River is frozen over. Find Halifax in Nova Scotia and

St. John in New Brunswick on the map. 

The harbors at these places are free from ice the year round. During the winter, therefore, they take over part of Montreal's trade. Their harbors are busy shipping grain, meat, lumber, and other products from the regions farther to the west. 

Perhaps you wonder why so many products from western regions are not brought by railroad to Halifax and St.

John in summer. The answer is that in general goods may be shipped more cheaply by water than by rail. If these products are loaded on ships at Montreal, it is not necessary to carry them so far on railroads. 

Can you answer these questions? 

1. What is the name of the fishing grounds near the Maritime Provinces? 

2. From what ports in the United States do fishing boats come to share these fishing grounds? 

3. Why are the seaports of the Maritime

Provinces busier in winter than in summer? 

4. What other industries besides fishing and shipping are important in the Maritime Provinces?

EASTERN CANADA-THE PROVINCES OF QUEBEC AND ONTARIO 

On the map find the provinces of 

Quebec and Ontario. Next color a part of your outline map and print the abbreviations for Quebec and Ontario to show just where these provinces are. 

Farming in Ontario and Quebec. In the valley of the St. Lawrence the soil is rich and the climate is favorable for growing hay, oats, potatoes, and apples.

Many of the farmers keep dairy cows. 

If you should visit the region partly bounded by Lakes Huron, Erie, and Ontario, often called the Ontario Peninsula, you would find many orchards like the one shown in Figure 247. In fact, because of its fruit the peninsula is called the "fruit garden of Canada."

Another important farming region in 

Ontario and Quebec is the famous Clay

Belt. This region lies between Lake 

Huron and James Bay. Find these bodies of water on the map. Grain, hay, vegetables, and dairy foods are the leading products. 

Mining in Ontario and in

Quebec. In southern Quebec are found the richest asbestos mines in the world. These mines lie south of the St. 

Lawrence River near the boundary between Canada and the United States. When mined, asbestos looks like wood with silky fibers, but it is really a rock or mineral.

Since it will not burn, it is used chiefly for making shingles, curtains for theaters, and various other articles that need to be fireproof.

Find the Laurentian Upland on the map. 

Figure 248 will show you that the upland is a very rough country made up of ridges and valleys. It lies largely in southeastern Ontario and Quebec, and is one of the richest mining regions in the world. 

Among the many minerals found here are gold, silver, lead, iron, nickel, asbestos, zinc, and copper. Near the town of Sudbury, in the province of Ontario, ninetenths of the world's supply of nickel is found. Find Sudbury on the map. A little to the northeast, in the region of 

Cobalt, are extensive deposits of silver. 

Find Cobalt on the map. To the northwest of Cobalt is the second largest gold-producing region in the world. 

Lumbering in Quebec and Ontario.

A large part of Quebec and Ontario is covered with forests. Though the soil of the Laurentian Upland is thin and rocky, trees grow well. These trees are chiefly evergreen, such as pines and spruce.

Pine trees are used for lumber, and spruce trees for pulpwood. Lumbermen in the

Canadian forests work in much the same 

Figure 247. A peach orchard on the Ontario Peninsula. The steep, treecovered hill at the back helps to protect the orchard from cold winds. Peaches freeze more easily than apples, plums, or cherries. 14-5G

Figure 248. A scene on the Laurentian Upland. Notice the small town in the sheltered valley among the hills. The hills are largely waste land. 

Figure 249. Lumbering in Quebec. The deep snows in Quebec make it easy to haul logs to rivers on which they can be floated to sawmills. way as those in New England. They cut the trees in winter and haul the logs out of the woods on sleds to near-by streams, as you will see in Figure 249. 

Then when spring comes and the ice melts, the logs float down the streams to sawmills and paper mills. 

Find Abitibi Lake and the 

Abitibi River on the map. 

Near by are extensive forests. Moreover, the Abitibi 

River furnishes an abundance of water power to operate sawmills and paper mills. Here you will find some of the largest paper mills in North America.

The Ottawa Valley is noted for lumber mills and paper mills. Power for these mills is furnished by falls in the Ottawa River. If it were not for the evergreen forests of Canada, newspapers, books, and magazines would be much more expensive than they are. 

Perhaps you can explain why this is so. 

Great areas of northern Ontario and

Quebec are also covered with forests. 

These forests, however, have not yet been opened up for use. Lumbering in the far-northern lands is very difficult.

In all northern Quebec, for instance, there isn't a single railroad, and of course no highways. Moreover, many of the streams are frozen a large part of the year.

Hunting and fishing in Ontario and Quebec. In the great forests in the northern part of Ontario and Quebec hunting is the only occupation. Most of the hunting is done in the winter. 

Figure 250 shows one of the trading posts that may be found near the great forests. 

Some of the people in northern Ontario and Quebec depend on fish in the many streams and lakes for a part of their food. 

In Hudson Bay and James Bay fish are caught and shipped to market.

Figure 250. A trading post in northern Ontario. Notice the sign that says "Fur Dealer." From little villages such as this, called trading posts, trappers go out in the fall for a long, lonely winter in the forest. They carry traps, guns, and food. Each trapper sets a long line of traps and walks many miles every day to take animals from the traps. 

When spring comes, he brings his furs to the post and trades them for food, clothing, ammunition, and perhaps for new guns and traps. Then, when the fall comes, he starts out for another long hunt of a similar nature which may keep him away throughout winter. 

The largest city of Canada. Find the

St. Lawrence River on the map. From what lake does it flow? Into what body of water does it empty? Large ocean vessels come up the St. Lawrence as far as Montreal. This city is at the head of ocean navigation, just as are Philadelphia, New Orleans, Portland, and various other cities you have studied. From the western part of Canada, grain, meat, lumber, wood pulp, and minerals are shipped by train to Montreal and then loaded on ships to be sent to all parts of the world. 

Montreal, then, is not only Canada's largest city but also its greatest seaport.

Figure 251 shows a part of the harbor at Montreal and one of the huge grain elevators in which grain is stored until it can be shipped. As you look at the picture the main part of the city is behind you on a hill, which the early 

French settlers called "Mont Royal," or, in English, "Mount Royal." "Montreal" comes from the name which the French settlers gave this hill. 

Other cities in the St. Lawrence Valley.

River at Quebec. Find this city on the map. On what river is it? In what general direction is it from Montreal?

Quebec is an important seaport, but it is

Figure 252. Quebec from the St. Lawrence River. The hill jutting out into the river is called the Heights. 

Many not nearly so busy as Montreal. ocean vessels go past Quebec to the head of ocean navigation. What reason can you give as to why these vessels go on to Montreal?

Find Ottawa on the map. On what river is it? This city is the capital of the whole Dominion of Canada, just as Washington is the capital of the United States. It is also a manufacturing city and has many large sawmills and paper mills that use timber from near-by lands. 

Cities on the Ontario Peninsula. On the map find the part of Ontario that lies between Lake Huron on the west and 

Lake Erie and Lake Ontario on the east.

This region is often called a peninsula because it is nearly surrounded by water. 

It is a region of cities and is the chief manufacturing district of Canada. It enjoys many advantages that have helped to make it a busy region. First, raw materials can be shipped easily and cheaply here by way of the Great Lakes and the St. Lawrence River. Second, it has railroad connections with all parts 

Figure 253. An ocean liner coming into Quebec. of Canada and with neighboring regions in the United States. Most of the coal used here comes from the United States.

Find Toronto on the map. This is the second largest city in Canada. On what lake is it? Toronto is a busy manufacturing city and is an important trading center for a rich farming region. 

Find Hamilton on the map. On what lake is it? Its position on this lake makes it an important shipping port. It is also in a great fruit-growing region.

Figure 254. A Canadian wheat field. The shocks tell you that this wheat was cut by a binder, not by a combine. 

If you look closely, you can see that shocks extend far into the distance, showing that the farms here are very large. 

THE PRAIRIE PROVINCES 

On your outline map of Canada color the three provinces that make up the prairie region of Canada: Manitoba, Sas katchewan, and Alberta. Next print the abbreviations of the names of the provinces on the map. These provinces are called the Prairie Provinces because much of the land was grass covered and treeless when the first settlers came. 

Another great region of spring wheat.

The surface, soils, and climate of the Canadian prairies are much like the surface, soils, and climate of the Great 

Plains in the United States. What does

Figure 244 tell you about the rainfall on the prairies? You will remember that 

Minnesota, North Dakota, and Montana produce large quantities of spring wheat. 

The spring-wheat lands of the United States extend northward into Canada, and it is hard to tell just where Canada begins. Figure 254 is a picture taken on the Canadian prairie lands. The same kind of picture, however, might have been taken in the spring-wheat lands of the United States.

There is another grain region in the 

Prairie Provinces farther north. On the map find the Peace River. The soil in the valley of this river is very fertile. 

Moreover, the surrounding mountains protect the valley from cold winds. The climate is therefore much milder than you would expect, and large crops of wheat and oats are grown each year.

Nearly all of the wheat of Canada is shipped out of the country. Most of it is carried by railroad to the Great Lakes or to Montreal. However, in recent

Figure 255. A wheat map of Canada. 

Courtesy Natural Resources Intelligence Service 

Figure 256. Sheep on the Canadian prairies in Alberta.

Sheep usually find plenty to eat even on grasslands that are too dry for growing grain. years, farmers have been shipping some of their wheat by railroad from Saskatchewan to Hudson Bay. This body of water is frozen over part of the year, but it can be used in summer. Wheat is also shipped by railroad to Vancouver on the Pacific coast.

Grazing lands. Not all the lands in the Prairie Provinces are used for growing grain. The nearer the Rocky Mountains you go, the drier the climate becomes. 

Did you find this true also in the United 

States? In western Alberta the rainfall is so light that only grass will grow.

Large areas of the land, therefore, are used only for grazing. By looking at 

Figure 256 you will find that the land here is much like that on the Great 

Plains in the United States.

In the western part of the prairies millions of acres of land have never been used for farming or grazing. There are not enough people to use all the land. 

Mining in the Prairie Provinces. The 

Prairie Provinces have rich deposits of minerals. In northern Manitoba large quantities of copper are mined. Alberta and Saskatchewan have extensive coal fields and mine more than a third of all the coal that Canada produces. Find 

Cities of the Prairie Provinces. 

Winnipeg on the map. On what river is it? Of what province is it the capital?

Its location near the southern end of Lake Winnipeg has helped it to become an important railroad center somewhat like 

Chicago. All the east-west railroads that cross Canada must pass south of the lake. Several of them, therefore, pass through Winnipeg. Other railroads run from Winnipeg southward to Minneapolis and Chicago. As you might expect, Winnipeg is a city of grain elevators, flour mills, and meat-packing plants. 

On the map find Regina and Saskatoon in Saskatchewan, and Calgary and 

Edmonton in Alberta. Notice that each of these cities is an important railroad center. Which city is farthest north? 

All four of the cities are leading trading centers for the farming and ranching lands of the prairies. What industries, then, would you expect to find?

Can you explain 1. Why wheat raising is the chief industry in the eastern part of the Prairie Provinces? 

2. Why grazing is the chief industry in the region west of the wheat belt? 

3. Why Winnipeg is a great railroad center?

WESTERN CANADA-THE PROVINCE OF BRITISH COLUMBIA Find the province of British Columbia on the map. Next color a part of your outline map and print the abbreviation of its name, to show where the province is. As you studied about the Rocky 

Mountains in the United States you found that they were greatly noted for their beauty. Some of the most beautiful scenery in North America is also found in the Canadian Rockies. Wherever the mountains can be reached by railroads, many tourist centers have developed. Among the most important of these centers is Banff. Find Banff on the map. Part of the region around 

Banff lies in western Alberta and part in eastern British Columbia. 

Along the Pacific coast find the Coast Mountains on the map. Between the 

Rocky Mountains and the Coast Moun tains there are great plateaus and river valleys. The western slopes of the Coast 

Mountains have a very heavy rainfall. 

Find this region of heavy rainfall in 

Figure 244. The lands east of the Coast 

Mountains have a very light rainfall.

Find this dry region in Figure 244. What did you learn about the cause of the heavy rainfall along the coast in Washington and Oregon and the light rainfall to the east? How, then, can you explain the heavy rainfall along the coast and the light rainfall to the east of the mountains in British Columbia?

How the people of British Columbia make a living. In the rainy coastal region lumbering is the leading industry. 

The trees, the logging camps, and the sawmills are much like those found in 

Washington and Oregon. The Douglas fir is the most valuable tree. British 

Columbia produces more lumber than any other province in Canada and has many large pulp mills. 

Farming is much less important in British Columbia than it is in Washington and Oregon, largely because there are few lowlands like the Willamette Valley and the region along Puget Sound in the United States. Wheat, however, is raised between the Rocky Mountains and the Coast Mountains and apples are grown in some of the sheltered inland valleys.

Fishing is a very important industry along the Pacific Coast. In fact, British 

Columbia ranks first among the Canadian provinces in the value of its fisheries. 

Salmon are the most valuable fish. They are caught in large numbers as they swim up the Fraser River and its tributaries to deposit their eggs. Find the Fraser

River on the map. 

British Columbia ranks second among all the provinces in mining. In the eastern part near the province of Alberta, coal is mined. There are also rich deposits of copper, silver, gold, lead, and zinc in the southeastern part. What minerals did you find to the south in the mountains of Montana and Idaho?

The cities of British Columbia. Figure 

257 is a view of a part of the business district of the largest city in British Columbia and the third largest in Canada. Find Vancouver on the map. This

Figure 257. Vancouver, British Columbia. Vancouver has both mountains and sea to make it a beautiful city. city has a fine harbor and is a gateway to the Fraser River Valley. Here also is the western end of a great transcontinental railway. Trans means "across." 

What, then, does transcontinental mean?

Notice that this railroad runs all the way across Canada from the Pacific 

Ocean to the Atlantic. Large quantities of grain and livestock raised on the

Canadian prairie lands are brought to Vancouver to be loaded on ships bound for foreign ports.

The most important factories in Vancouver are sugar refineries, lumber mills, and salmon canneries. Raw sugar comes to Vancouver from islands in the Pacific. 

Why would you expect to find lumber mills here? Why would you expect to find numerous salmon canneries?

The capital of British Columbia is the city of Victoria. Find Victoria on the map. On what island is it? This city, which is much smaller than Vancouver, is noted for its beautiful homes. 

Far north of Vancouver find Prince

Rupert on the map. This city has an excellent harbor and, like Vancouver, is at the western end of a transcontinental railway. 

Three questions about British Columbia 

1. How do most of the people make a living? 

2. Why is farming less successful than it is in Washington and Oregon? 

3. Why is Vancouver such a large and prosperous city? 

YUKON AND THE NORTHWEST TERRITORIES 

Notice that you still have a large part of your outline map to color and name. 

All of Canada that you have not yet studied belongs to two divisions of 

Canada-the Yukon Territory and the 

Northwest Territories. Find these two regions on the map. The many islands which you find to the north in the 

Arctic Ocean belong to the Northwest Territories.

The Yukon Territory is the most important part of northern Canada. 

Find Dawson on the map. This is the capital and the leading city. 

Hunting and trapping. The climate in both Yukon and the Northwest Territories is very cold. Some of the most northern lands are covered with snow and ice throughout the year. You can see that such a region has little or no growing season. Farther south the lands

NEWFOUNDLAND AND LABRADOR 

255 are covered with forests. The climate is too cold, however, for much farming to be done. Hunting and trapping are the leading occupations. Each year hundreds of hunters and trappers go into the forests just as they go into the forests of Ontario and Quebec. After weeks and months they return bringing many furs. 

Mining. Parts of the Northwest Ter ritories have rich deposits of minerals. 

In all the land, however, there are no railroads or highways. The Mackenzie

River, which flows across the western part of the Northwest Territories, is the only means of travel, and it is frozen over many months of the year.

In spite of these conditions there is one region in which much mining is done. 

Find Great Bear Lake on the map. 

Along the shores of this lake, in the last few years, large deposits of pitchblende have been discovered. In pitchblende is found the most valuable mineral in the world-radium. For four months of the year it is dark day and night in this region, and little or no mining can be carried on. The rest of the year miners are busy digging the ore.

Much more mining is carried on in 

Yukon than in the Northwest Territories. Dawson, the capital, is in the center of an important mining region. 

Here gold and several other minerals are found. Most of the minerals from the

Yukon field are shipped out over the 

Yukon River. Some, though, are taken to railroads about two hundred miles away. 

Raising reindeer. Most of the people in the northern forests are Indians.

Beyond the forests, a few Eskimos live. During the winter these Eskimos hunt the seal and walrus along the edge of the Arctic Ocean. During the summer they hunt caribou, a kind of large deer. 

The caribou feed upon moss, lichens, and scattered grass that grows during the short summers. Where the land is covered with snow, the caribou may be seen nosing or scraping away the snow to find the few plants that grow.

The Canadian government is encouraging the Eskimos to raise reindeer. In some places, therefore, you will find large herds of reindeer. The Eskimos use some of the reindeer to haul their sleds, others are kept for their milk, and still others are killed for their hides and meat.

The hides are used for making clothing, tents, harness, and fish lines.

NEWFOUNDLAND AND LABRADOR 

Newfoundland and Labrador, as you have already learned, form a separate division of the British Empire. 

Cooled by an ocean current. Newfoundland is nearly as large as the state of Pennsylvania, but it has only about as many people as the state of Delaware. 

In fact, there are only about seven persons for every square mile of land.

Doubtless you wonder why so few people live in Newfoundland. It is largely because much of the land is too rough and hilly for farming. Then, too, the climate is too cold for growing many kinds of crops. Only hay, oats, and potatoes do well. 

Did you ever know that great streams of water, called currents, flow in the ocean? In other words, in some places the water of the ocean moves about much as water moves in a river. Newfoundland gets part of its cold climate from one

as food. 

Larger fish, such as the cod and haddock, then come to the Grand Bank to feed on the smaller fish. 

Another good reason why the Grand Bank is a good place to fish is that the water here is very shallow.

St. Johns, the capital of 

Newfoundland, is an important fishing center. Figure 

258 shows the harbor at 

St. Johns. Find this city on the map. 

Lumbering and mining.

Although fishing is the leading industry, timber is the most valuable export. Most of Newfoundland is covered with forests of spruce. 

As you know, spruce is an excellent wood for making paper. Wood pulp from Newfoundland is sent to paper mills in Ottawa and various cities in 

New England. 

Figure 258. The harbor of St. Johns, Newfoundland. The entrance to the harbor is a narrow inlet between the hills. There is so little level land around St. Johns that most of the city is built on the slopes of steep hills. of these currents. A cold stream, called the Labrador Current, comes down from the icy waters of the Arctic Ocean and flows along its shores. Winds blow over this icy current onto the land and keep it cool even in summer. Newfoundland, then, has a much colder climate than it would have if it were not in the path of the Labrador Current. 

Fishing the leading occupation. Newfoundland is largely a fishing country. 

Since the land is not well suited for farming, many people have turned to the sea for a living. Moreover, fish are abundant in the near-by waters. It may seem strange that the icy waters near Newfoundland contain fish, until you learn that certain fish are most at home in cold waters. This is true of cod, haddock, and halibut. Then, too, you will remember that the Grand Bank is not far away. On this bank both large and small fish find an abundance of food. 

The Labrador Current brings many tiny plants and animals which small fish like A third product that is exported by 

Newfoundland is iron ore. Most of this ore is found near St. Johns.

Labrador-a cold and barren land. 

Find Labrador on the map. What does the color on the map show you about the altitude of most of the land? If you were to travel in Labrador, you would find the same kind of land over much of the area. Only a few fishermen live along its barren shores, and only a few Indian trappers and hunters live in the interior. There are two reasons why this is so. In the first place, the land is too rough and the soil too thin to be used for farming. In the second place, the climate is too cold for crops to grow well. 

Labrador is made cold by the same ocean

ALASKA-ONE OF OUR DEPENDENCIES 

257 current that gives Newfoundland a cold climate. What is the name of this current? Just how does it make the climate cold?

GREENLAND-THE LARGEST ISLAND IN THE WORLD 

On the map find Greenland northeast of Canada. This is the largest island in the world, being about three times as large as the state of Texas. It has only a small population, however, mostly Eskimos. These few people make a living largely by fishing and hunting. 

In Figure 259 you will see a family of Eskimos fishing with nets from a rocky shore. Most of their products are sold to the people of Denmark, for Greenland, as you will remember, belongs to Denmark.

Greenland is best known, perhaps, for the part it has had in aiding exploring parties on their way to the North Pole.

The climate here is too cold for farming and even for trees to grow. Greenland, therefore, can never hope to become a very prosperous country. For a long time the Eskimos were cut off from the rest of the world. Today, however, the radio and airplane are helping them get acquainted with other people. 

Telling about Newfoundland, Labrador, and 

Greenland. Write a sentence about each of the following to tell the leading ways of making a living: Newfoundland, Labrador, Greenland. 

ALASKA-ONE OF OUR DEPENDENCIES 

Alaska is of special interest because it belongs to the United States. For a long time it was owned by Russia, a country in Europe and Asia. Then in 1867 the

Figure 259. Eskimos fishing in southern Greenland. 

This picture was taken in the summer when most of the snow and ice along the shore has melted. Even in southern 

Greenland the short summers are warm and pleasant. 

United States bought it for $7,200,000. 

At that time many people thought our country had paid too much for it. 

Alaska, however, has already produced metals, fish, and furs worth about two hundred times the price paid for it. 

People often think of Alaska as a cold, barren region. They believe that most of the country is buried under snow and ice throughout the year. Really, though, Alaska has warm summers, and snow and ice are found throughout the year only in the northern part and on the high mountains.

If you measure Alaska on the map, you will find that it is a large country, being about one-sixth as large as the 

United States. Only seventy-three thousand people, however, make their homes here. If these people were evenly scattered over the country, each person would have about eight square miles of land. Then, too, every other person would be either an Indian or an Eskimo.

1-34 On the map above locate the land that borders the Pacific Ocean. This is the part of Alaska where most of the people 

High mountains near the coast. As you can see from the map, all along the coastal lands not far from the ocean there are ranges of mountains. Some of these mountains are very high. Mount 

McKinley, for example, is the highest mountain in North America. Find this mountain on the map. How high is it? Several other mountains are nearly

Latitude North 

60

ALASKA-ONE OF OUR DEPENDENCIES 

259 as high. Great glaciers cover the slopes of some of the higher mountains and flow slowly downward toward the ocean. 

Heavy rainfall and mild winters. As you can see from Figure 261, the mountains along the coast have a very heavy rainfall. The rainfall comes from the westerly winds that blow from over the 

Pacific Ocean. When these winds reach the mountains along the coast, they rise, become cooler, and lose their moisture as rain and snow.

The westerly winds that bring rain and snow also help to make the winters mild. 

Water, as you remember, does not change its temperature so quickly as land. Then, too, Alaska is favored by a warm ocean current that flows along the southern shore. What kind of current did you find flowing near Newfoundland and 

Labrador? How does the current there affect the climate?

HOW THE PEOPLE MAKE A LIVING Fishing the leading industry. The 

Pacific coastal lands of Alaska do not make a good farming country, for people cannot plow the steep mountain sides.

In some of the valleys, though, there is good level land. The rainfall is too heavy for growing grains. Farmers keep a few dairy cows, but there are no large markets for fresh milk and cream. By far the most important industry along the coast is fishing. Salmon are the most valuable fish. Alaska salmon swim in great numbers from the ocean up the rivers just as they do along the coast of 

Washington and Oregon. Fishermen catch the salmon throughout the summer, when the fish swim upstream from the ocean to deposit their eggs. 20 40 10 10 20 to 4040 80 

Less than in 20inches More than 80 inches 

Figure 261. A rainfall map of Alaska.

Heavy forests, but little lumbering. 

A few people in the Pacific coastal lands work in the forests. The lower slopes of the mountains and the coastal valleys are thickly wooded with such trees as hemlock, spruce, and poplar. Have you ever seen such a thick growth of trees as that shown in Figure 262? Not much timber is cut, because there is little need for lumber. Neither is much lumber shipped to distant markets, for the trees here do not make as good lumber as the trees that grow in the Pacific coast lands of the United States. Most of the lumber from Alaskan forests is made into cases for packing canned salmon.

Part of the timber is used for making wood pulp and paper. 

Rich in minerals. Alaska has a great many minerals. The most valuable one is gold, which is mined near Juneau and 

Sitka and in the Kenai Peninsula near 

Seward. There are also many deposits of coal along the coast and in some parts of the interior, but only a small quantity is mined. Another valuable mineral is copper. In a recent year the value of

Figure 262. A wooded valley on the coast of Alaska. This picture was taken from an airplane, and the trees are much larger than they appear. Notice that they grow down to the very edge of the water and cover all the lower slopes. copper alone was almost equal to the sum that our government paid for the entire dependency. Find the Copper

River on the map. As the name suggests, this river flows through the region where important copper mines are found. 

Raising foxes and hunting seals. Rais ing foxes is an important industry in parts of the coastal lands. Hundreds of fox farms are found on the Alaska Penin sula and the Aleutian Islands. Find these regions on the map. In what other country did you find people raising foxes?

In the Bering Sea large numbers of seals are caught. These seals, of course, are caught for their skins. Do you know for what purpose most sealskins are used? Each summer large herds of seals come out of the water and live on the 

Pribilof Islands. Find the Pribilof Islands on the map. It is in this general region that most of the seals are caught.

CITIES OF THE COASTAL LANDS 

Find Juneau, Ketchikan, Anchorage,

Cordova, and Sitka on the map. All of these towns are centers for canning and shipping fish. None of them is large.

Juneau, the capital city, which has only slightly less than 6,000 inhabitants, is the largest city in Alaska.

THE CENTRAL PLATEAU OF ALASKA 

Much of the central part of Alaska is a great plateau surrounded by mountains. 

Through this plateau flows the Yukon River. Trace this river on the map. 

Light rainfall and cold winters. The central plateau has a climate quite different from that of the coastal lands. 

The rainfall is very light, and most of it comes during the summer. The winters are long and cold. Killing frosts are likely to occur as late as May and as early as September. You can see, there-

ALASKA-ONE OF OUR DEPENDENCIES 

261 fore, that the growing season is very short. 

Hot summers and long days. In parts of central 

Alaska wheat and other grains, as well as vegetables and fruits, are raised. The valley of the Tanana River is the chief grain-growing district. Here the grain grows so rapidly that it ripens in spite of the short growing season. This happens for two reasons. In the first place, the summer days are hot. In the second place, the sun shines nearly twenty-four hours a day in midsummer. Now find the

Tanana River Valley on the map. What. | depended largely on wild animals for 

Figure 263. A herd of Alaska reindeer. You can see that the reindeer is a useful animal heavy enough to pull a sled and large enough to furnish meat. 

Some of the men in the picture are Eskimos, though they are dressed much like white people. The boat at the right is a kayak, a small canoe made of skins. is its latitude? How does the latitude explain the long summer days? The most important town in the valley is Fairbanks. 

Hunting on the plateau. Alaska has many wild animals that are valuable for their fur. The central part of the country is the home of the wolf, bear, fox, muskrat, and beaver. The Indians hunt and trap these animals during the long cold winters.

THE ARCTIC PLAINS 

Most of the lands in northern and northwestern Alaska are too cold for trees to grow. Although in a few places bushes, shrubs, and grass grow, usually only reindeer moss is found. About the only people who live here are Eskimos. 

Raising reindeer. When the white people came to Alaska, they began to hunt and fish. Before long the Eskimos were in danger of starving because they food, and these were being killed rapidly. 

The government therefore began to teach the Eskimos to raise reindeer. About forty years ago, it is said, there were only sixteen reindeer in Alaska. Today there are hundreds of thousands.

Look at the map and find Seward Peninsula. This is where most of the reindeer are raised. If you were to visit 

Seward Peninsula, you would find many scenes like that shown in Figure 263. 

Large quantities of reindeer meat are shipped from Nome. Find Nome on the map.

 I. Locating Mexico and the Caribbean Lands 

1. Look at the map on pages 264-265 and notice that Mexico lies just south of the United States. What body of water borders it on the east? What ocean borders it on the west? What river forms part of the boundary between Mexico and the United States? 

2. Notice that all the other lands just south of the United States surround a body of water called the Caribbean Sea. What group of islands borders the sea on the north? What smaller group borders it on the east? What group of countries borders it on the west? 

II. What different maps tell about Mexico 

1. What mountains do you find in Mexico near the eastern coast? What mountains do you find near the western coast? The highland region between these two mountain ranges is known as the Mexican Plateau. How does the width of the lowland region along the Gulf of Mexico compare with the width of the lowland region along the Pacific Ocean? 

2. Most of Mexico is nearer the equator than any part of the United States. Because of the altitude, though, some places in Mexico have a shorter growing season than the southern part of the United States. Where would you expect the growing season in Mexico to be the shortest? Where would you expect it to be the longest? 

3. Look at Figure 207 and notice that the winds in Mexico blow from over the Gulf of 

Mexico. What are these winds called? In all the regions along the equator the winds blow from an easterly direction. How do these winds explain the heavy rainfall along the eastern coast and the light rainfall in the highlands and along the western coast, as shown in Figure 266? 

4. What does Figure 267 tell you about the population of Mexico? In general, is the country thickly or thinly settled? Where do most of the people live?

2. Which two islands of the Greater Antilles have the highest lands?

MEXICO-THE AZTEC LAND 

Find the city of Mexico on the map.

Almost directly east of the city of Mexico find the city of Veracruz on the coast. 

In 1519 a Spanish explorer named Cortés, with about three hundred Spaniards, landed where Veracruz now is and began to explore the land. He led his men westward through a hot, damp lowland. 

Then he began to climb the eastern slopes of mountains, where he found a delightfully cool climate. Finally he came to a broad plateau where the days were hot and the nights cold. Here he heard of a great city farther to the west which was ruled by the Indians. These Indians, known as the Aztecs, were much more advanced in their ways of living than most of the Indians in North America.

They built cities, irrigated the fields, and raised crops. 

Cortés pushed on. Finally he looked down on the Valley of Mexico, which you see in Figure 268. In this valley he came to a shallow lake. In its center was the beautiful city of the Aztecs.

Stone roadways led across the water to the city. On their farms the Aztecs were raising corn, beans, and other vege- Figure 267. A population map of Mexico and the 

Caribbean Lands. 

tables. They wore garments made of cotton adorned with the colored feathers of birds. From the seeds of cacao trees they made a delicious drink and gave the 

Spaniards their first drink of chocolate.

Although in the beginning the Aztecs were friendly, later they drove the Spaniards from the city. Cortés came back, however, and with the help of other Indians conquered the Aztecs. Thus the capital of Aztec land became the capital of Spanish Mexico.

The Mexican people. When the Span iards first came to settle in Mexico, many of them married Indians. Today, therefore, many of the Mexican people are a mixture of Spanish and Indian. The 

Spanish language is spoken in most of the country, but only a small part of the population is made up of pure white people. Then, too, a number of people from other countries have settled in Mexico, mostly Spaniards, Americans, English, and Germans.

Mexico is divided into states much as our country is divided into states. The government, too, is much like that of the United States. At the head of the government is a president, together with a congress to make the laws.

Figure 268. A scene in the Valley of Mexico, not far from Mexico City. This beautiful plain, surrounded by lofty mountains, was the center of the country of the Aztecs. The lake that Cortés saw on the plain has since been drained. 

You should be interested in Mexico for a number of reasons. First, it is our nearest neighbor on the south. Second, many Mexicans come to live and work in the United States. Third, the people of our country have invested many millions of dollars in Mexico. Fourth, we buy many products from Mexico and sell more manufactured goods to Mexico than does any other country.

Many kinds of lands. 

Because Mexico has such a variety of regions and products, it is often called a land of contrasts. 

Most of Mexico seems to be a southern extension of the Rocky Mountains. It consists largely of a high plateau with a range of mountains on either side. Find the name Sierra Madre Occidental on the map. Sierra is the Spanish word for mountain range," madre here means chief," and occidental means "western." 66 66 The eastern range is called Sierra Madre 

Oriental. What does oriental mean?

Notice that the two ranges of mountains come together in the southern part of the country.

On the Pacific side the Sierra Madre 

Occidental drops down to the ocean with very steep slopes. The coastal plain is very narrow. On the eastern side the 

Sierra Madre Oriental drops down much more slowly. The eastern coastal plain, then, is somewhat wider. Trace this plain on the map from Texas southward. 

Different kinds of climate. If you were to travel by train from Veracruz to 

Mexico City, you would be surprised at the changes in climate. Veracruz is on the low coastal plain where it is always hot, even in January. In fact, the 

Mexicans call all the lowlands along the coast tierra caliente, or "hot lands."

MEXICO THE AZTEC LAND 

269 

Traveling westward from 

Veracruz you would slowly climb to a height of more than 3,000 feet. By and by you would come to the 

Sierra Madre Oriental and your train would wind up the eastern slopes to still higher lands. The Mexicans call these higher lands tierra templada, or "temperate lands," because of the delightful climate. Finally you would reach the plateau itself. The height of the plateau varies, but it is somewhat higher in the south than it is in the north. The highest lands of Mexico are called tierra fria, or "cold lands." 

Where would you expect to find most of the people of Mexico, on the coastal plain or on the plateau? Why? The most thickly settled region is the Valley of Mexico in the southern part of the plateau. This valley is in the tierra templada and has a delightful climate. 

In the heart of the valley is the capital city of Mexico. 

Century Photos 

Figure 269. A herd of goats in the city of Mexico. The Mexicans often find goats better to keep than cows. Goats do not give so much milk as cows, but they eat much less. Then, too, they are easier to keep in a city than cows. 

Moreover, goats can find a living on pastures that are too poor for cows. 

MAKING A LIVING ON THE MEXICAN PLATEAU FARMING ON THE PLATEAU 

Most people on the Mexican Plateau make a living by grazing, farming, and mining. It will be interesting to compare the ways the people work here with the ways people work on the plateaus in the United States. 

Mexican ranches and cowboys. The northern and central parts of the plateau are largely used for grazing. Here thousands of cattle, sheep, and goats eat the scanty grass that grows. There are twice as many goats as sheep. Indeed, many families of Mexico, even in the cities, get most of their milk from goats like those shown in Figure 269. The animals on the plateau are herded much as they are on the Great Plains in the 

United States. 

Mexican cowboys are called vaqueros.

A vaquero spends much of his time on horseback. He wears wide leather chaps to protect his legs from the thorny plants that grow on the dry lands. His saddle, his belt, his wide-brimmed sombrero, and his spurs are decorated with silver. 

Figure 270 shows some Mexican cowboys driving a herd of cattle. 

Mexico has many fine riding horses.

The people ride horses because there are few good roads. In some places mules and burros are used to carry goods and to pull carts and wagons. 15-5G

Hugo Brehme, Mexico

Figure 270. A herd of cattle on the Mexican Plateau. From the clouds of dust, you can see that it has not rained recently. Rain falls often enough, however, for grass and small trees to grow. Notice the kind of hat the cowboys wear. 

Raising crops on Mexican farms. Al though the northern part of the plateau, as you have just learned, is used largely for grazing, crops are raised to some extent where water can be obtained for irrigation. In the southern part of the plateau the rainfall is more plentiful and crops can be grown without irrigation.

Here farming is an important occupation. 1 The large farms and ranches of Mexico are called haciendas. The owner usually lives in a large and beautiful house. The workers live in little villages and are known as peons. The peons usually farm small plots of ground that the owner allows them to use. Sometimes, however, the peons own the land they use.

The government in recent years has been furnishing irrigation. In the irrigated regions some of the large ranches have been broken up into small farms and divided among the peons.

In some parts of Mexico the peons use very simple methods of farming. Look at Figure 271. What are the men doing?

How would the picture differ if it had been taken somewhere in the United States? Much of the work is done by hand. On the large haciendas, however, modern machinery like that in the United States is often used. The chief crops on 

Mexican farms are corn, wheat, barley, beans, alfalfa, and cotton. Many vegetables, such as potatoes, peppers, carrots, and beets, are also raised. The greatest single crop is corn. From corn the 

Mexicans make tortillas, or corn pancakes, which are their favorite food.

Farming on the temperate slopes. On the slopes of the mountains at the outer edges of the plateau, there are many beautiful valleys with hundreds of small villages and towns. Here great quantities of corn and beans are grown. Other crops, too, not found on the plateau are raised. Coffee, for example, is grown on a number of the higher slopes not far from Orizaba, Córdoba, and Jalapa. The

finest coffee plantations, however, are on the Pacific slopes of southern Mexico.

Tobacco is grown in some of the smaller fields. Most of the tobacco is used by the Mexicans themselves, and very little is sent to other countries. Many fruits are raised on the slopes and in the valleys. 

Among these fruits are oranges, apples, peaches, pears, and mangoes. 

A LAND OF SILVER AND GOLD 

When the Spaniards first came to Mexico, they found the Indians wearing trinkets of gold and silver. Even some of their dishes in everyday use were made of precious metals. Thus the early Spaniards soon thought of Mexico as a very wealthy country and began to look for the places from which the minerals were obtained. 

There are rich silver deposits in the mountains and on the plateau. For over four hundred years Mexico has led the world in the production of silver. Even today it furnishes nearly one-half of all the silver that is mined.

Mexico is rich in copper also. The leading copper mines are found in the northern and western parts. Gold, lead, zinc, iron, and other metals are mined in the mountains. Near Durango there are rich deposits of iron ore.

Some of the mining is carried on in a simple way. The ore is hauled up in leather buckets and taken to smelters on the backs of mules. In the larger mines, however, modern machinery is used. 

Most of the large mines have been opened up by people from other countries. 

Not only have they opened the mines, but they have also built railroads to carry the metals to ports. 

Hugo Brehme, Mexico 

Figure 271. Mexican farm workers. Notice the twowheeled cart and the oxen. Notice also that the grain is tied in bundles by hand. Such work is very slow. 

CITIES ON THE PLATEAU 

99 

The city of Mexico. Today, just as before the coming of the Spaniards, most of the people of Mexico live in the southern part of the plateau. The city of 

Mexico, which is situated here, has more than a million inhabitants. Because of its many fine buildings it is sometimes. called the "City of Palaces.' The president of Mexico lives in a lovely castle on a high hill. Each year many people from all parts of Mexico and the United States visit the city of Mexico. The cool air, the clear sky, the near-by fertile lands, and the snow-capped mountains make it a very attractive place. It is also an important industrial center. Large manufacturing plants produce foodstuffs, leather goods, textiles, and metal goods. 

Figure 272 will show you that the city of Mexico is very beautiful. 

Other cities on the plateau. Find the city of Guadalajara on the map. This is the second largest city in Mexico. It is the trading center for a rich farming region and is famous for its potteries.

It It also has tanneries, shoe factories, flour mills, and meat-packing plants. 

Find Monterrey in the northeastern part of the country. This city, the third largest city in Mexico, is an important manufacturing and trading center. also has iron and steel works supplied with ore from the iron-ore mountain near 

Durango. Find San Luis Potosí and 

Torreón on the map. These cities are trading centers for near-by mining and farming regions. Much cotton is also raised around Torreón. 

Cities on the temperate slopes. Find

Puebla, Orizaba, and Oaxaca on the map. 

Puebla is the fourth largest city in Mexico and is noted for its cotton mills. These mills are run by hydroelectric power. 

The water power for making electricity is obtained from mountain streams. 

Puebla is also the center of a rich farming region and near by are valuable quarries. 

Orizaba, like Puebla, has cotton mills run by hydroelectric power. Oaxaca is the trading center for a mining, farming, and forest region.

MAKING A LIVING ON THE HOT LOWLANDS 

The jungles and forests. The lowlands, especially the lands bordering the 

Gulf of Mexico, are very hot. Then, too, the rainfall is very heavy, as you can see from Figure 266. Most of the hot, wet lands are covered with a jungle of trees, vines, and various other plants. In some places the jungle is so thick that it is hard to clear the land for farming. 

Among the many trees that grow here are mahogany, rosewood, ebony, cedar, palms, and dyewoods. For the most part, the region is not very healthful. 

This is partly because there are so many 

Hugo Brehme, Mexico

Figure 272. A cathedral in the city of Mexico. You are looking at the cathedral across an open square, or plaza, surrounded on all sides by buildings. In the distance beyond the cathedral you can see the tops of some of the bare, rocky mountains that are found outside the city. insects. A hot, wet jungle usually contains a great variety of insects.

In places the jungle has been cleared away. Here the hot lands produce valuable crops of sugar cane, vanilla beans, bananas, pineapples, and other fruits. 

The oil city of Mexico. 

Find Tampico on the map.

This is one of the two leading Mexican ports on the 

Gulf. Because of rich oil fields near by, the city has grown rapidly. Though the output of oil today is not so large as it was formerly, Tampico is still noted as an oil city and has several large refineries. Much oil,

however, is shipped to other countries to be refined. 

A gateway to the plateau. Veracruz is important largely because it is a gateway city to the rich lands on the eastern slopes and on the plateau around the city of Mexico. Here passengers and goods leave ocean vessels and go inland by railroad. The high, cool, healthful plateau to the west is so much better as a place to live that more people pass through Veracruz than stop to visit or trade. 

Yucatan, the land of henequen and chicle. Find the peninsula of Yucatan on the map. What bodies of water border it? One crop raised in Yucatan is of special value to farmers in the United States. This crop is henequen, or sisal. 

Look at Figure 273 to see the kind of plant from which henequen is obtained. 

The outer covering of the thick leaves is scraped off and the fibers that are left are dried and baled for market. Large quantities of henequen are shipped to the United States. Here it is made into binder twine, which is used for tying bundles of wheat, oats, barley, and corn. 

Henequen is at home in Yucatan, and can be grown only in a few other places in the world. It grows best where there is a moderate amount of rainfall, where the climate is warm, and where the soil is thin and underlaid with soft limestone rock. Why does henequen not grow in southern Yucatan? Figure 266 will help you answer this question. 

Find Mérida and Progreso on the map.

Mérida is in the center of the henequen country. Railroads lead out from Mérida in all directions. Progreso is the chief shipping port for henequen. 

International Harvester Company

Figure 273. Cutting henequen. The long, stiff leaves must be cut one at a time. Only a few leaves are cut from each plant. Notice the hooked knife that is used. 

Have you ever wondered what is used in making chewing gum? The chief raw material is chicle, the gum of the sapodilla tree. The gathering of chicle is the second leading industry on the peninsula of Yucatan. Practically all the chicle is shipped to the United States. 

Hereafter, then, when you chew gum, think of chicle and the peninsula of Yucatan. 

Lower California and the western lowlands. Find Lower California on the map. What state in the United States bounds it on the north? Trace the coastal plain along the Gulf of California and the Pacific Ocean. What does 

Figure 266 tell you about the rainfall in all this part of the country? 

On dry lands such as these, little can be raised except by irrigation. For this reason very few people live in Lower 

California. On the irrigated lowlands east of the Gulf of California tomatoes, peas, peppers, string beans, and other vegetables are grown. Many of these vegetables are shipped to the United States to help supply the winter market.

4. In the mining of what mineral does 

Mexico lead the world?

CENTRAL AMERICA-A LAND OF COFFEE AND BANANAS 

The smallest countries in North in 

America. Look at the map and find how many countries make up Central 

America. Notice that these countries are all very small. All of them together are not as large as the state of Texas. 

Color the countries on your outline map. 

Write the name Central America over the entire region. Find the highlands of

Central America on the map. Find the lowlands. 

Central America, like Mexico, was explored and settled by Spaniards. Spanish, then, is spoken in most of the countries. Many of the people, however, are mixed, as they are in Mexico. In the eastern lowlands there are many Negroes.

All of the countries except British Honduras, which belongs to the British Empire, are republics like the United States. 

The highlands and coffee. The highlands of Mexico extend southward into 

Central America. The climate on the highlands is cool and healthful. Figure 

266 will show you that they also have a heavy rainfall. Where, then, would you expect to find most of the people? The farmers on the highlands raise cattle and such crops as beans, potatoes, and corn.

The chief crop, however, is coffee, which is grown today in nearly all the Caribbean Lands. Coffee trees were first grown in southwestern Asia. When they were brought to the Caribbean Lands, they grew so well that the raising of coffee soon became an important industry. Today 

Guatemala, Costa Rica, and El Salvador are especially famous for their coffee.

Coffee trees must have plenty of rainfall and a well-drained soil. Then, too, they usually grow best under tall shade trees. The finest plantations are found on slopes 1,000 to 4,000 feet above the level of the sea. A coffee plantation often looks much like a beautiful forest except that the trees are usually planted in rows. 

The lowlands and bananas. The hot, wet lowlands of eastern Mexico extend southward all along the eastern shore of 

Central America. The northeast trade winds, as you can see in Figure 207, give these coastal lands a heavy rainfall. 

A large part of the lowlands was once covered with a dense jungle, but today most of it has been cleared away for the raising of bananas. The deep, rich soil, the heavy rainfall, and the hot weather are just right for growing bananas.

Indeed, this is the leading banana region in the world. Figure 264 is a picture taken on one of the large banana planta tions of Central America. 

case. 

Perhaps you have thought that bananas grow from seed, but such is not the 

Instead, they grow from pieces of roots which are planted in the ground. 

It takes twelve to fourteen months for the plants to grow and bear fruit. 

Again, you may have thought that banana plants are trees, but they are not. They are merely a kind of giant

THE PANAMA CANAL ZONE 

275 grass that grows to be ten or twelve feet tall. Each plant produces one bunch of bananas. After the fruit is removed, the plant is cut down close to the ground. 

The roots, however, live on and send up new shoots which later produce more bananas. A banana plantation always has plants of many different sizes and ages, and bananas are ready for cutting every week in the year.

It takes very careful work to raise bananas and get them to market quickly.

Plantations, therefore, must not be far from the sea and from harbors where ocean steamships can load. When a ship is coming for a load of bananas, the captain sends a radio message to the manager of a plantation. The manager then sets his men to work to cut bunches of bananas and carry them to a railroad. 

Trains come along and take the bananas to the wharves. When the ship arrives, a cargo of bananas is ready to be loaded. 

By the use of machinery, 30,000 to 60,000 bunches of bananas may be loaded in a few hours. 

Some of the banana ships go to New 

Orleans and some to other seaport cities in the United States. Here the bananas are unloaded and put into refrigerator cars. 

Then they are sent by fast freight to various parts of the country. Bananas are also shipped to many other parts of the world.

Other products. Among other products exported from Central America are cacao beans, coconuts, raw sugar, lumber, chicle, and hides. Many tropical fruits, such as pineapples, papayas, mangoes, and alligator pears, are grown for home use. 

The boy in Figure 274 is holding several large alligator pears. 

Figure 274. Alligator pears, often called avocados.

This boy is holding a popular fruit grown in tropical regions. It is used largely for making salads. 

THE PANAMA CANAL ZONE 

The southernmost country in North 

America is the Republic of Panama. 

This country is of special interest because in it the United States controls a strip of land ten miles wide known as the

Canal Zone. Lengthwise of the Canal 

Zone extends the Panama Canal, one of the greatest man-made waterways in the world. Find the Canal Zone on the map. 

Before the canal was finished in 1914, a ship going from New York to San

Francisco had to sail around the southernmost part of South America. This was a long and expensive trip. Moreover, it was a very dangerous trip. The canal cut the distance between New York and 

San Francisco from fifteen thousand miles to six thousand miles. How great a distance was saved by the new route?

Figure 275. The Pedro Miguel Locks in the Panama Canal. These locks are a very important part of the canal. 

When the ships are in the locks, they do not use their own power but are pulled by electric engines. 

It was not easy to dig the canal. To begin with, the region was very unhealthful because of mosquitoes and other insects.

The United States began, then, by filling the swampy lands where the mosquitoes and other insects made their homes.

Then, too, a part of the canal had to be built through hilly land. Some of the land rises to a height of six hundred feet above sea level. You may wonder why such a hilly region was chosen as a place for the canal. If you look at the map, however, you will find that the canal was built where the distance across the land is the shortest. By using the scale of miles you will find that the canal is about fifty miles long.

Three sets of locks were built to carry the ships over the hilly land. You have already learned how ships are raised and lowered by means of locks. It takes about seven or eight hours for a ship to travel from one end of the canal to the other. Figure 275 shows two large ships on their way through the canal. Some things to do 

1. What are the two leading crops in 

Central America? Tell the most interesting things you remember about the raising and shipping of bananas. 

2. Make a drawing to show how a lock works to raise and lower a ship. How many sets of locks are there in the Panama Canal?

THE WEST INDIES 

Look again at the map and notice the groups of islands that border the northern and eastern shores of the Caribbean Sea.

It was on these islands that Columbus first landed when he discovered America.

Three groups of islands. The West 

Indies are divided into three groups of islands: the Greater Antilles, the Lesser Antilles, and the Bahama Islands. The 

Greater Antilles consist of four large islands: Cuba, Hispaniola, Puerto Rico, and Jamaica. Find these islands again on the map. The Lesser Antilles are the smaller islands that look like stepping stones extending from the Virgin Islands to South America, along the eastern end

On your outline map color the West
