Next Next write the names of the four large islands that make up the Greater Antilles.

The people of the West Indies. Since the West Indies were first explored and settled by the Spaniards, they belonged to Spain for a great many years. Today, however, some of the islands are independent and some belong to the British Empire, France, and the Netherlands. 

Cuba, Haiti, and the Dominican Republic are independent. The people of these countries make their own laws About two-thirds of the people in Cuba and Puerto Rico are of Spanish descent; that is, their forefathers were Spanish. 

Some of the people in Jamaica, the 

Bahama Islands, and other smaller islands that belong to the British Empire are English, but most of them are Negroes. The people on the island of 

Hispaniola are Negroes, except for a few Spanish people in the eastern half. 

CUBA THE SUGAR ISLAND 

Cuba has long been known as the "Pearl of the Antilles." There are a number of reasons why you should be especially interested in Cuba. To begin with, more than half of its foreign trade is carried on with the United States. 

Then, too, among the Greater Antilles,

Cuba is nearest the United States and

45 632 Figure 277. Fruit sellers in Habana. The cover of the wagon is needed to protect the fruit from the sun. many people visit the island every year. 

Moreover, many American people have invested large sums of money in Cuba. 

Although the island is only about as large as the state of Pennsylvania, it produces much of one of the world's leading foods. The fact that it is called the sugar island tells you what the food is. 

Sugar cane raised on plantations.

Sugar cane is raised on large plantations. 

Near the center of each plantation is a mill where the cane is ground and the sweet juice is made into raw sugar and sirup, or molasses. The cane is brought to the mill on ox carts, trucks, or little cars that run on tracks as does a railroad train. The cane is then run through rollers to crush out the juice. Next the juice is boiled until it becomes a thick molasses. This is run through a special machine to separate the sugar. The molasses that is left is hauled away to market. The brown sugar must be sent to refineries before it is ready to use. 

Usually the raw sugar is refined in the countries that buy it. This explains why there are so many sugar refineries in the 

United States. 

Other products of Cuba. Besides sugar cane Cuba grows a number of other crops. 

One of the most important is tobacco. 

West of Habana is a small region about ten miles wide and ninety miles long especially good for growing tobacco. 

Find Habana on the map. Most of

Cuba's tobacco is sold to the United 

States. Cuba also raises many bananas.

Nearly three million bunches are sent to the United States each year. Grapefruit, pineapples, and many vegetables are raised. The men in Figure 277 are selling some of these fruits and vegetables.

In eastern Cuba there are several minerals. Iron, copper, and manganese ores are shipped to the United States. The leading deposits of iron are found near 

Santiago. Find this city on the map. 

Cities in Cuba. Habana is not only the largest city and the capital of Cuba, but it is also the leading port in the 

West Indies. Look at the map and notice that Habana is not far from Key 

West and Miami, Florida. Because of its delightful climate, Habana is a favorite spot for tourists in winter. Figure 

278 shows the capitol and the main part of the city. Another important city of

Cuba is Santiago, near the eastern end of the island. This city, like Habana, is a seaport and carries on an extensive trade with the United States.

HISPANIOLA-AN ISLAND WITH TWO COUNTRIES 

Hispaniola is the second largest island of the West Indies. As you will see by the map, there are two countries on the island. The country in the western part

THE WEST INDIES 

279 is known as Haiti and that in the eastern part as the 

Dominican Republic. 

How the people make a living. What do the colors on the map tell you about the interior of the island?

Much of the land here is too rough and mountainous for farming. Agriculture, how ever, is the leading occupation of the people. Coffee, corn, and tobacco are grown in both Haiti and the 

Dominican Republic. Coffee, however, is the leading crop in Haiti. Large quantities of cotton are also raised. In the lowlands of the Domini- Figure 278. An air view of Habana and its harbor. You are looking across the main business section of the city. The large building near the center is the capitol, one of the finest government buildings in the world. 

Beyond the city is the harbor. The residence sections and neighboring towns stretch along the shore on both sides for miles and miles. can Republic there are many plantations like those in Cuba, and sugar is the most valuable product. Much cacao is also grown. The mountains of Hispaniola are covered with forests containing valuable timber, such as mahogany and dyewoods. 

Lumbering is carried on only near the coast because there are no roads or railroads through the mountains. 

Although most of the people of Haiti are Negroes, they speak the French language. This is because Haiti once belonged to France. Port au Prince is the capital and chief port. The people of the Dominican Republic speak Spanish.

The capital and chief port is Ciudad 

Trujillo, formerly called Santo Domingo.

PUERTO RICO-A DEPENDENCY OF THE UNITED STATES 

Puerto Rico is about 1,400 miles southeast of New York. It was discovered by Columbus in 1493. For more than four hundred years it belonged to

Spain, but in 1898 it was turned over to the United States following the Spanish- 

American War. You will be especially interested in Puerto Rico because it is a dependency of the United States. If you were to visit the island, you would be pleased with its many fine buildings and roads. You would be pleased as well with its thrifty and energetic people. 

Most of the people are of Spanish descent and speak the Spanish language. Many of them, however, speak both Spanish and English, and both languages are taught in the schools.

Surface and climate. The interior of 

Puerto Rico is hilly and contains many beautiful valleys. The highlands extend from east to west. Much of the land along the coast is a low plain. The climate is delightful. It is never extremely hot and the winters are very mild. The rainfall, as you can see from Figure 266,

Courtesy Puerto Rican-Nicaragua Aerial Survey Farming in Puerto Rico. 

Farming is an important industry in Puerto Rico. Sugar cane is the leading crop. It is raised chiefly on large plantations just as it is in Cuba. Most of the plantations are on the coastal plains and in the river valleys. 

Nearly every plantation has its own mill such as the one shown in Figure 280. 

Tobacco is the second crop in value. It is grown chiefly in the valleys and on the steep slopes of the interior.

The finest tobacco is grown on slopes so steep that machines cannot be used in cultivation. An excellent grade of coffee is raised on some of the higher lands. Fruit, too, is an important product. Grapefruit, pineapples, coconuts, and oranges are shipped to the United States. Bananas, plantains, mangoes, alligator pears, and guavas are raised but are used mostly at home. Many vegetables are also raised. Figure 279. An airplane picture of a part of San Juan and its harbor. 

The much larger residential section of the city is not shown. 

Publishers Photo Service Figure 280. A sugar mill in Puerto Rico. The cane which you see is being carried to a series of huge crushers and rollers that press out the sweet juice. is rather heavy except on the southern coast. Most of the rainfall comes be tween May and December. Puerto Rico is in the path of the trade winds, which blow from the northeast, as shown in Figure 207. It is partly because of these winds that the island has such mild weather and such an abundant rainfall. 

Manufacturing and trade. Puerto 

Rico manufactures a number of its own products. It makes sugar, cigars, and cigarettes. Many women and girls are engaged in needlework, such as the making of clothing, embroideries, and laces. 

The island has become noted for its needlework, and needlework products rank high among its exports. 

Though Puerto Rico is only two-thirds as large as Connecticut, one of our smallest states, it carries on a larger trade with the United States than any other country in North America, excepting 

Canada, Mexico, and Cuba. Sugar,

THE WEST INDIES 

281 molasses, grapefruit, pineapples, coconuts, oranges, tobacco, coffee, clothing, embroideries, and other articles are exported. Figure 279 shows a part of the harbor of San Juan. If you were to watch ships unloading their cargoes here, you would see bags of flour and rice, cotton goods, meat products, automobiles, machinery, and hardware. San

Juan, the capital, is the second largest city in the West Indies. Another impor tant city is Ponce. 

JAMAICA-A LAND OF BANANAS AND SPICES 

Jamaica, as you have already learned, belongs to the British Empire. It is often called the banana island because it grows so many bananas. The bananas here are grown on great plantations just as they are in Central America. Jamaica is better known, however, for its spices. 

Jamaica ginger is known the world over, and the island supplies nearly all of the world's allspice. Among the other crops are sugar cane, cacao, and coconuts. In 

Figure 281 you are looking at a plantation on which coconut trees grow. In 

Jamaica thousands of swarms of bees are kept and much honey is exported. The bees feed on the blossoms of a tree known as the logwood tree. Jamaica's logwood honey is famous for its delicious flavor. 

Most of the people are Negroes, but they speak the English language. On the map find Kingston, the largest city and seaport.

THE LESSER ANTILLES 

On the map trace the long line of islands that curve southward from the

Virgin Islands. All of these islands to gether are known as the Lesser Antilles.

Figure 281. An important crop in Jamaica. The trees are coconut palms. The pickers become very skillful in climbing trees. The nuts grow in large clusters at the center of the crown of leaves.

The Virgin Islands belong to the United States. Guadeloupe and Martinique be long to France. Barbados, Grenada, Dominica, Antigua, Trinidad, and Tobago belong to the British Empire. 

Curaçao and other small islands belong to the Netherlands.

The Lesser Antilles have dry, hot lowlands and moist highlands. The soils are very fertile and a number of crops are grown. Sugar cane is the most valuable crop. Limes are gathered and made into lime juice and lime oils.

Some of the islands produce a high grade of cotton. In certain places fruits, coconuts, coffee, cacao, vanilla beans, and spices are grown.

The Virgin Islands. The Virgin 

Islands consist of St. Thomas, St. Croix, St. John, and about fifty very small islands near by. The United States

bought the islands from Denmark in 

1917. You can see from the map that even the largest islands are very small. 

In fact, all of them together are only about one-eighth as large as the state of 

Rhode Island. The chief products are sugar, bay rum, and cattle, but the islands are important not so much for their products as for their location. This is because they are situated on a steamship route from Europe to the Panama

Canal. On the map find St. Thomas, the chief port and capital.

Trinidad and its asphalt lake. The largest island of the Lesser Antilles is 

Trinidad. Find this island on the map. 

It produces sugar, molasses, and cacao and has rich oil fields, but is most famous for its asphalt lake. This lake is merely a basin covered with asphalt. 

The asphalt is cut out in large chunks. 

Although great holes are left when the chunks are taken out, they fill right up again. Most of the asphalt is used for paving streets and roads, and for making paints and varnishes. Trinidad leads the world in the production of asphalt. 

Port of Spain is the chief city. Find this city on the map.

ISLANDS OF SUNSHINE-THE BAHAMA ISLANDS 

The rainfall in the Bahama Islands, as you will see in Figure 266, is very light.

Moreover, the soils, like those of Yucatan, are thin. What plant, then, might you expect to find growing here? The henequen plant is grown successfully in the Bahamas. Some truck farming is also carried on, chiefly the raising of tomatoes. 

The most valuable crop, however, does not come from the land but from the ocean. There are many sponges in the warm, shallow waters of the Bahamas. 

Gathering sponges and preparing them for market, therefore, provide work for a number of the people. Sponges and henequen are the chief exports. 

The climate of the Bahamas is delight ful. The days are sunny and rainless, but cool breezes blow from over the ocean.

The population is small. In winter many tourists from the United States and other countries visit the Bahamas. Nassau is the capital and center of the sponge industry. Find Nassau on the map. 

A LAND OF VEGETABLES AND LILIES THE BERMUDA ISLANDS Find the Bermuda Islands on the map. 

These islands are not a part of the West 

Indies. Notice that they lie east of the 

United States in the same latitude as southern South Carolina. Their climate, however, is much milder than that of South Carolina because they lie in the path of a warm ocean current. Both the climate and the soil are favorable for growing vegetables. Some of the vegetables are shipped to the United States. and Canada in winter. Chief among these vegetables are celery and potatoes.

The Bermuda Islands, too, are noted for their lilies and lily bulbs. The warm climate of the Bermuda Islands attracts many winter visitors. 

Though the Bermuda Islands are near the United States, they belong to the

British Empire. The capital of the islands is Hamilton.

1. Making a map 

Turn to page 361 and make an outline map of North America. 

On the map draw boundaries to show the following: Alaska, Canada, Labrador, United States, Mexico, and Central America.

II. Thinking of boundaries 

You have now made an outline map of the continent of North America. Any large part of the earth, together with the islands, gulfs, and seas that are near it, is called a continent. What large bodies of water surround the continent of North America?

What seas, gulfs, and bays do you find along the eastern, southern, and western coasts? What large bay extends deep into the continent from the north?

III. Thinking of different kinds of lands 

The Rocky Mountains are much larger in every way than the Appalachians. They extend all the way from Alaska to Central 

America, and their plateaus reach far westward almost to the Pacific Ocean. In what different ways did you find people making a living in the Rockies? How did you find them using the near-by plateaus? Now think of the Appalachian Mountains. In what ways did you find people making a living there? How did you find them using the near-by plateaus? 

Where are the most fertile lowlands of North America? You have learned that in most parts of the United States the lowlands are thickly settled. In Mexico, however, you found most of the people living on highlands. How can you explain the difference?

IV. Thinking of lakes and rivers 

The Great Lakes make up one of the greatest inland waterways in the world. Find them on the map. Which lake lies wholly within the United States? What are the leading products shipped on the Great Lakes? Why are they used so much more than the lakes in Canada? 

Find the Mississippi River and notice its tributaries. A river with its tributaries, that is, the rivers flowing into it, is called a river system. All of the land that is drained by a river system is called a river valley or river basin. What are some of the leading products that are shipped on rivers in North America? What do the Mississippi and its tributaries do for farming? 

Find the Rio Grande, Colorado, and Columbia rivers. For what do you remember each?

V. Thinking of the climate and where the people live 

You have learned that in the southern part of North America plants grow throughout the year. In the northern part of the continent, however, the land is covered with snow and ice all or most of the year. How can you explain this great difference in conditions? How does it affect the growing season? Do most of the people in North America live in the northern, central, or southern part? What conditions lead them to live where they do? 285

UNIT NINE OUR ISLAND DEPENDENCIES IN THE PACIFIC INTRODUCING OUR ISLAND DEPENDENCIES IN THE PACIFIC 

You have already learned that the United States has dependencies in various parts of the world. The largest of these dependencies is Alaska, which you found in the northwestern part of North America. Southeast of the United States you found Puerto Rico and the Virgin Islands. 

In Central America you found the

Panama Canal Zone. 

The United States also has a number of dependencies in the Pacific Ocean. The most important of these dependencies are the Hawaiian Islands and the Philippine Islands. Although these lands are in another part of the world, you will find them much like the Caribbean Lands which you have just studied. Warmclimate crops are raised and an extensive trade is carried on with the United States.

OUR ISLAND DEPENDENCIES IN THE PACIFIC 

Century Photos Figure 286. Pineapples in Hawaii. Pineapples grow well on the Hawaiian Islands because of the volcanic soils and the warm climate.

THE HAWAIIAN ISLANDS 

The Hawaiian Islands are far out in the 

Pacific Ocean, about 2,100 miles southwest of San Francisco. Many ocean vessels from the United States stop at the islands on their way to various ports in Asia, Australia, and the Philippine 

Islands. Now turn to pages 346-347 and find the Hawaiian Islands on the map. 

What the islands are like. Hawaii is made up of five main islands and many smaller ones. They are really the tops of huge mountains that have been built up in the ocean. In certain places in the world hot melted rock pours out from down deep in the earth and forms mountains as it cools. The melted rock which comes from the earth is known as lava, and the openings from which it pours are known as volcanoes.

On the map find the names of two volcanoes in Hawaii, the largest island.

How high is the top of Mauna Loa above the sea? It is still active; that is, it bursts forth from time to time, pouring out vast quantities of lava, which flow down the slopes of the mountain. The lava which spreads over the ground helps to make a very fertile soil.

What lands have you studied that are about the same distance from the equator as the Hawaiian Islands? What kind of climate, then, would you expect them to have? Because they are surrounded by the Pacific Ocean, the climate here is not as hot as in other regions of the same altitude. The ocean breezes give the islands a pleasant climate the year round. 

All the Hawaiian Islands together have an area a little less than that of the state of New Jersey. Fewer people live on the islands, however, than live in New Jersey.

Only a small part of the people are 

Hawaiians. Large numbers of them are 

Chinese, Japanese, and Americans. 

How the people make a living. The rich volcanic soil in the Hawaiian Islands and the warm growing season which lasts the year round are very favorable for farming. Much of the land, however, is too mountainous to cultivate. Sugar cane is the chief crop. The stalks grow big and juicy in the fertile soil and in the frost-free climate. Although most of the plantations are owned and managed by Americans, the work is done chiefly by 

Filipinos and Japanese. The raw sugar is shipped to the United States to be refined. 

Pineapples rank second in importance among the products. They need a frostfree climate and plenty of rain. The

THE PHILIPPINE ISLANDS 

289 conditions on the islands are just right for pineapples. Most of the Hawaiian pineapples are canned and shipped to the United States.

Honolulu. The capital of the Hawaiian 

Islands is Honolulu. Find this city on the map. On which island is it situated? 

Honolulu is a beautiful city and is the chief port. Many people from the United 

States visit the city each year. 

THE PHILIPPINE ISLANDS The Philippine Islands, like the Hawai ian Islands, are far from the United States. 

They are about 7,000 miles southwest of San Francisco and on the other side of the Pacific Ocean. Find the islands on the map of the world on pages 346–347. 

About the lands and people. The 

Philippines contain about 7,000 islands. 

Most of the islands, however, are very small. The largest, and by far the most important, is Luzon, which is about as large as the state of Pennsylvania. Find

Luzon on the map on page 287. The 

Philippines are much larger than the 

Hawaiian Islands and are much more thickly settled. Altogether only sixteen. million people live on the islands. While some of the people are Spanish, some American, and some Chinese and Japanese, most of them are Filipinos. 

The Philippines have been controlled by the United States since 1898. Recently, however, a law has been passed which will make them an independent country after a period of ten years. 

How the people make a living. Like the Hawaiian Islands, the Philippine

Islands are the tops of volcanic mountains that rest on the bottom of the ocean. Therefore the soil is very fertile, Century Photos Figure 287. A coconut grove in the Philippine Islands. 

Two American sailors are climbing one of the trees. The palms are so tall that men can reach the nuts only by climbing the trees. but on some of the islands it is too rough and steep for farming. Most of the farming, then, is done in the valleys and on the coastal lowlands.

More of the land is used for growing rice than for any other purpose. The rice is not raised to sell, but the Filipinos use it as their chief food. They eat so much rice that large quantities are even imported from other countries. Sugar is the most valuable product that is raised to sell. It is grown chiefly on the island of Luzon, on a large level plain which extends between two mountain ranges northward from Manila Bay.

The abaca plant is another valuable product in the Philippines. This plant is raised for its fibers, which are called

OUR ISLAND DEPENDENCIES IN THE PACIFIC 

Manila hemp. The fibers are used for making rope. Manila hemp is the second most valuable export. Most of it is shipped to the United States and Europe. 

Many coconuts are raised on the wet coastal lowlands. They grow on palm trees like those shown in Figure 287. 

Nearly one-fourth of the coconuts in the world are produced in the Philippines.

When the coconuts are ripe, the Filipinos gather them, cut them open, and remove the white meat. Much of the white meat is dried to make a substance called copra. Large quantities of copra are shipped from the Philippine Islands to the United States and to Europe. It is used in making soaps. The Filipinos also crush some of the coconut meat to squeeze out the oil. This, too, is a valuable product. In certain places tobacco is an important crop. Most of the tobacco is used for making cigars.

The city of Manila. Figure 284 shows a view of the capital and largest city of the Philippines. Find Manila on the map. On what island is it situated?

Manila has a fine harbor and is a very busy port. Most of the sugar, hemp, coconut oil, copra, and tobacco are shipped from this city. 

OTHER ISLAND DEPENDENCIES IN THE PACIFIC 

Besides the Hawaiian Islands and the

Philippines the United States owns several other islands in the Pacific Ocean.

The most important of these islands are 

Guam and Tutuila. Guam lies on an ocean trade route between the United

States and the Philippines and serves as a stopping place for ships. Tutuila is a naval station where government ships stop for repairs and supplies. It belongs to a group of islands called the Samoan Islands, only a few of which belong to the United States. All of the other island dependencies in the Pacific are valuable only as stopping places for ships. All have a warm climate and some of them export copra and other warm-climate products.

UNIT TEN SOUTH AMERICA INTRODUCING SOUTH AMERICA 

As you studied North America you learned that the United States has friendly neighbors both to the north and to the south. These neighbors buy many of our products and sell us a number of things which we need in order to live well. You will now study about some neighbors who live on another continent. 

This continent is South America. The relief map on page 293 shows both 

North America and South America. 

The two continents together are often called the Americas or the Western Hemisphere. 

The two Americas are connected by a narrow neck of land which is known as the Isthmus of Panama.

From the United States to South 

America. If you were on board a ship traveling from New York City to the most eastern part of South America, your ship would sail east as well as south. If you were on a ship traveling from New 

York City to the west coast of South 

America, however, your ship would sail almost straight south. On this journey you would pass through the Panama 

Canal. If you were on a ship traveling from San Francisco or another city on the west coast of the United States to the western coast of South America, your ship would sail southeastward. Turn to the map on page 293 or on pages 

346-347 and trace each of these journeys. 16-5G 291

SOUTH AMERICA 

In which direction is South America from the United States? If you were to point to South America from your school, in which direction would you point? Notice that very little of South

America lies directly south of any part of the United States. Does any part of it lie directly south of New York City? 

New Orleans? San Francisco?

Why South America is a helpful neighbor. The people of the United 

States are interested in South America, not only because it is a neighboring continent, but because it supplies many things the United States needs. More over, it makes a good market for large quantities of goods that the United States produces. The trade between the United 

States and South America is therefore very great, and ships are going back and forth all the time. 

Differences in climate help to explain why the United States carries on such a great trade with South America. On the map on page 346 find the equator and trace it across South America. The northern part of the United States, as you know, is about halfway between the equator and the North Pole. What, then, is one difference you would expect to find between the climate of the United States and that of much of South America? 

You will remember that the lands near the North Pole are cold because the noonday sun, when it can be seen at all, is always low in the sky. If you were to visit these lands, you would find that your body would cast a long shadow even at noon. Along the equator the noonday sun is always high in the sky. Here, at noon, your body would cast only a small shadow about your feet. How does the position of the noonday sun help to explain why a large part of South 

America has a warmer climate than most of North America?

Tropical South America. On the map on pages 346-347 find the dotted line that stands for the Tropic of Cancer and the dotted line that stands for the Tropic of Capricorn. What line is halfway be tween these lines? The Tropic of Cancer marks the point farthest north on the earth, and the Tropic of Capricorn marks the point farthest south, upon which the noonday sun ever shines straight down.

All places between the Tropic of Cancer and the Tropic of Capricorn are said to be in the tropics. Thus a great tropical belt extends entirely around the earth. 

Somewhere in the tropics throughout the year the noonday sun is always shining straight down on the earth. You may think of the tropical belt, then, for the most part, as a region where it is always summer and usually hot. Sometimes, though, if a place is high above the level of the sea, the climate may be cool or even very cold. You may be surprised to find that some of the highest mountains in South America, even at the equator, are covered with snow and ice.

Figure 290 is a picture of one of these mountains. 

Temperate South America. Can you imagine a place where Christmas comes in summer? That is just what happens in the southern part of South America.

The climate here is much like that in your own country. The seasons change from summer to winter just as they do in the United States. The summers get shorter and the winters get longer the

40% 140° 100° 40 Figure 289. A relief map of the Americas. This map will show you several things. First, North America and South 

America are parts of one great body of land. Second, South America lies to the southeast of North America. Third, North America is a little larger than South America. Fourth, the coast line of South America is much less broken than that of North America. Fifth, South America has few near-by islands while North America has a great many. Sixth, both continents have high mountains in the western part, lower highlands in the eastern part, and central areas of lowland. 

Seventh, South America is crossed by the equator, but all of North America is north of the equator. Eighth, northern 

North America is much nearer to the North Pole than southern South America is to the South Pole. 40 ​293

SOUTH AMERICA 

Figure 290. Mount Chimborazo. Although this peak is but a short distance from the equator, it is covered with snow and ice throughout the year. farther southward you go. Strange to say, however, when the United States is having summer, southern South America is having winter. When the United 

States is having winter, southern South America is having summer. In other words, winter and summer, and spring and autumn, are just turned around from the way they are in the United States. 

What, then, are the summer months in southern South America? the winter months? the spring months? the autumn months?

Look at the map on pages 346-347 and find the dotted line near the North 

Pole marked Arctic Circle and the dotted line near the South Pole marked Antarctic Circle. All places between the Tropic of Cancer and the Arctic Circle are said to be in the north temperate belt. 

Notice that the United States lies in this belt. All places between the Tropic of Capricorn and the Antarctic Circle are said to be in the south temperate belt. In general, you may think of all places in the temperate belts as regions that have summers and winters. 

South America, then, lies in two great climatic belts. The northern and the larger part lies in the tropical belt, where for the most part it is very hot throughout the year. The southern and smaller part lies in the temperate belt, where the climate is much like that in the United States, except that the seasons are turned around.

III. Explaining the rainfall 

20 30 Figures 293 and 294 will tell you about the rainfall in South America. Notice that the rainfall is very heavy in the Amazon 

Valley and along the eastern coast of tropical South America. 

Notice, too, that the amount of rain varies greatly in tropical South America from one part of the year to another. In other words, some places have rainy seasons and dry seasons much as we have summer and winter in the United States. The changes are caused partly by the fact that the winds which bring the rain shift north and south somewhat with the sun. These winds, as you will find by looking at Figure 207, are the trade winds which come from over the Atlantic Ocean.

NATURAL REGIONS OF SOUTH AMERICA 

A continent of many regions. As you studied North America you found great differences in altitude, in the length of growing season, in the amount of rainfall, and in the fertility of the soil. Thus you found it a continent of many regions. 

South America, too, may be called a continent of many regions, for it also has great differences in altitude, length of growing season, amount of rainfall, and fertility of the soil. 

In many ways, as you have already discovered from the map, South America is much like North America. Both continents have highlands in the western part which extend north and south. 

These highlands are much higher in 60 

Figure 294. Average rainfall in South America from

November 1 to April 30. 

South America than they are in North America, but do not extend so far east and west. What is the name of the highlands in the western part of South America? 

South America, like North America, also has highlands in the eastern part. 

In fact, South America has two regions of highlands in the eastern part. The 

Brazilian Highlands, to the south of the Amazon River, form a low, broad plateau that is much wider than the Appa lachian Mountains of North America. 

The Guiana Highlands, to the north of the Amazon River, form a plateau that is somewhat narrower than the Appa lachian Mountains. 

Both continents have a great central lowland which extends from north to

FOREST TEMPERATE PAMPAS Trees 

Grass 

Figure 295. A population map of South America. 

south. In South America the central lowland extends almost the entire length of the continent. Nearly two-thirds of the continent is less than 1,000 feet above sea level. Look at the map to find whether the greater part of the central lowland is in the tropical or the temperate belt. Do you remember what a river basin is? 

What river drains the northern part of the central lowland? What great river and its tributaries drain the central part of the lowland? The southern part is drained largely by two rivers, the Rio Paraná and the Rio de la Plata, which together are often called the Paraná- 

La Plata River System. Find these two important rivers on the map. 

Figure 296. A vegetation map of South America. 

Thus the central lowland of South America consists largely of the basins of three river systems. Each of these basins has its own kinds of plants or vegetation. 

In the basin of the Orinoco River are grassland plains known as llanos. In the basin of the Amazon River and its tributaries are great tropical forests known as selvas. In the basin of the

Paraná-La Plata River System are grass lands known as pampas. Find all these regions in Figure 296. Between the selvas and pampas is a region of mixed grasslands and forests, called the Gran 

Chaco. To the northeast of the Gran

Chaco is a highland region of grasslands commonly known as the campos. 18-SG

Ewing Galloway 

Figure 297. A railroad high in the Andes. You can see how difficult it is to build railroads in the high mountains. 

The Andes Mountains. Find the 

Andes Mountains again on the map and notice that they lie close to the western coast. Notice, too, that they are very high, being much higher than most of the Rocky Mountains of North America. 

You may think of the Andes, then, as a great high wall, from 100 to 400 miles wide, that almost cuts off the rest of the continent from the Pacific Ocean. There are few railroads in the mountains, and travel in some places is very difficult. 

Look at the map and notice how few railroads there are. Figure 297 shows one of these railroads.

In the north the Andes consist of three ranges, separated by the valleys of the 

Magdalena and Cauca rivers. Find these rivers on the map. The Magdalena 

Valley serves as a gateway to the Andes from the Carribean Sea. South of the equator the Andes are much higher and wider than at any other place. Here you will find the Plateau of Bolivia.

Lake Titicaca, the highest fresh-water lake on earth, is situated on this plateau. In the southern part of the continent the mountains consist of two separate ranges, a low range along the coast and a higher range a little to the east. Between these two ranges lies the beautiful Valley of

Chile, one of the most fertile farming regions in western South America. 

If you look at Figures 293 and 294, you will find that the western slopes of the 

Andes, except in the northern and southern parts, have very little rain. This is because the northeast and southeast trade winds lose most of their moisture in the Amazon Valley and on the eastern slopes of the mountains. In southern 

South America the western slopes have a heavy rainfall, but the eastern slopes are dry. This is because southern South 

America, as you will see in Figure 207, is in the path of the westerly winds. These winds blow from over the Pacific Ocean and lose most of their moisture on the western slopes as they rise to cross over mountains. Can you explain, then, why the eastern slopes are very dry?

As you studied the United States you found the lowlands more thickly settled than the highlands. In Mexico and in many parts of South America, however, most of the people live in the highlands. 

This is because the altitude or height above the sea makes the highlands much more pleasant than the lowlands. Thus on the plateau in the widest part of the 

Andes, you will find numerous cities and towns, one of which is shown in Figure 298. Then, too, there is plenty of rain for raising crops. On some of the more level places farming is an important occupation. In other places mining is

301 

Figure 298. The city of La Paz. This picture will help you see that even the plateau in the widest part of the Andes is very rough. Although the city of La Paz is situated in a valley, it is about 12,000 feet above the level of the sea. You can readily see why most of the people in a rough country such as this live in the valleys. an important industry. In still other places the lands are too high and rough to be used for any purpose at all. 

The Brazilian Highlands. Find the 

Brazilian Highlands again on the map and notice that they extend almost to the Atlantic coast. The highlands are drained largely by two rivers, one flowing to the northeast and the other to the southwest. What are their names? 

Now look at Figures 293 and 294 to see whether the rainfall in the highlands is heavy or light. Figure 207 will show you that the highlands lie in the path of the southeast trade winds which blow from over the Atlantic Ocean. As these winds rise to cross over the land, they become cooler and lose much of their moisture as rain. Thus the eastern part of the region has an abundant rainfall. 

What does Figure 295 tell you about the population in the highlands? Notice that more people live in the eastern part of the highlands than in the western part. 

This is because the eastern part is much more pleasant and productive than the western part. All of the western part, in fact, is covered with grass and lies in the region known as the campos. The eastern part, however, is one of the most

SOUTH AMERICA 

Figure 299. A trail through the forests of the Amazon 

Valley. Trails are about the only openings there are in the forests. Indians and explorers make such openings by cutting through the dense growth with an ax or a great knife known as a machete. productive regions in South America.

Here you will find a number of industries, the most important of which are farming and mining. You will be especially interested in the farming because two of the leading crops are coffee and cacao.

The Guiana Highlands. Find the 

Guiana Highlands again on the map and notice that they cover a much smaller area than do the Brazilian Highlands. 

The Guiana Highlands, as you will see in Figure 207, are in the path of the northeast trade winds. Since these winds blow from over the Atlantic Ocean, would you expect the rainfall to be heavy or light? Look at Figures 293 and 294 to check your answer. Now look at 

Figure 295 to see whether the highlands are thickly or thinly settled. You will find that few people live in this part of South America. In fact, about the only people who try to make their homes here are a few scattered tribes of Indians. 

Much of the land is covered with a thick tropical forest and the region is very unhealthful.

Tropical forests in the Amazon Valley. 

As you look at the map you will see that the Amazon has a great many tributaries. Moreover, with its tributaries it drains a large part of the central lowland in the middle part of the continent. 

These facts tell you that it is a very large river. It is, indeed, the largest river in the world. With its tributaries it drains an area nearly as large as the United States. It is so wide that you could sail upstream for miles without seeing the bank on either side. It is longer than the distance from New York to San 

Francisco. Ocean vessels sail up the river to Iquitos, a distance of 2,300 miles.

Find Iquitos on the map. The average depth of the river from Iquitos to the sea is more than a hundred feet. Thus the mighty Amazon is long, wide, and deep. 

Figures 293 and 294 will help you see one reason why the Amazon is such a large river. The rainfall in the Amazon 

Valley is very, very heavy. Since the river lies close to the equator, all the region is very hot. If you look at Figure 207, you will find that the northern part of South America lies in the belt of the northeast and southeast trade winds. 

As these winds meet over the hot Amazon Valley, they rise and lose their moisture.

NATURAL REGIONS OF SOUTH AMERICA 

303 

Perhaps you wonder just what makes the winds rise since there are no mountains to cross. The hot weather where the winds meet makes air light, just as a hot stove or radiator makes it light.

As the hot air rises, it cools and loses some of its moisture as rain. Thus you can see that every day in the Amazon Valley the conditions are favorable for rain, and nearly every day it rains. 

If you were to visit the Amazon 

Valley, you would find that the tropical forests or selvas form a great jungle, the largest in the world. Trees and vines and tropical plants are so thick everywhere that it is almost impossible to get through. The thick vegetation shuts out the sunlight so completely that the forest is always dark and gloomy. Grass does not grow, but bushes, vines, and trees of different heights are all tangled together. You could travel for weeks along the Amazon and its tributaries without finding any open places in the jungle. It is so difficult to travel here that some places have never been explored. Even airplanes cannot be used very well for exploring because there is no place to land. If you look at the map, you will find that there are no long railroads in the Amazon Valley. Why do you think it would be hard to build railroads here? In all the region the only way to travel is on rivers or by following a few narrow trails that lead into the forest. In Figure 299 you will see a train of pack animals making its way along one of the trails. It is only where a stream flows through the forest or where the trees have been cut away for a trail or path that there is enough light for taking a picture successfully. 

Figure 300. Indians in the Amazon Valley. These two Indian girls have just returned from a garden. They are standing by the front door of their home. The house is made of materials that can be obtained easily. 

Very few people live in the Amazon basin. The climate is so hot and wet, the jungle is so dense, and the insects are so numerous that many people die of disease. Only one important city, Manaus on the Rio Negro, lies in the basin. Find this city on the map. All of the towns and trading stations are found along the rivers. Away from the rivers the only people are a few Indians, such as you see in Figure 300. These

Indians make a living largely by hunting, fishing, and raising a few vegetables. 

The llanos and campos. The llanos and campos, as you have already learned,

SOUTH AMERICA 

are great areas of grasslands. Those to the north of the Amazon Valley are the llanos and those to the south are the campos. You may wonder why these are regions of grass rather than regions of tropical forests. The reason is that they receive plenty of rainfall for the growing of grass but not enough for the growing of trees. 

You will see by looking at Figures 293 and 294 that the rainfall is heavy during a part of the year but very light during the other part. The region of heavy rainfall along the equator shifts somewhat to the north and the south with the summer seasons. It rains farthest north during the northern summer, when the noonday sun shines straight down farthest north of the equator. It rains farthest south during the southern summer, when the noonday sun shines straight down farthest south of the equator. Thus to the north of the Amazon Valley the rains are very heavy during the northern summer but very light during the northern winter, as you will see by comparing 

Figures 293 and 294. What do these figures tell you about the seasons of heavy and light rainfall south of the 

Amazon Valley? Notice that when the rainfall is heavy in the llanos it is light in the campos, and when the rainfall is heavy in the campos it is light in the llanos. During the rainy season grasses and other plants grow rapidly and provide excellent pasture. During the dry season, however, the grasses and plants dry up and die.

Now find the llanos and campos on the map. Which of the regions is higher above the level of the sea? The campos, you will find, are mostly highlands, while the llanos are lowlands. Next look at 

Figure 295 to find whether the regions are thickly or thinly settled. Few people live in either region because the climate is very unpleasant. 

Temperate lowlands. The southern parts of the great central lowland of South America may be called temperate lowlands because they lie in the south temperate belt. Find the pampas in

Figure 296. This is one of the most thickly settled regions on the continent. 

A large part of the pampas and the Gran Chaco to the north are drained by the 

Paraná-La Plata River System. Ocean vessels sail up the Paraná as far as the city of Rosario. Find this city on the map. River steamers sail up the Paraná and its tributary, the Paraguay, a distance of 2,000 miles. The pampas, as you have already learned, are grasslands.

The kinds of crops grown in various places depend largely on the amount of rainfall. Figures 293 and 294 will show you that the northeastern part has a fairly good rainfall but that the western and southern parts are very dry. Near the broad La Plata River the rainfall is about the same as it is in most of the lowlands of the United States. Farther west the rainfall is lighter, and just east of the Andes it is so light that the land must be irrigated where it is used for raising crops.

On the whole, the pampas make up a great level plain with excellent lands for raising crops and for grazing. In the eastern part the crops are about the same as they are in the Corn Belt in the 

United States. If you were to travel through this part of the pampas, you would scarcely be able to discover any

Remember that wheat ripens in southern South America during our winter. Can you suggest, then, about what time of the year the picture was taken?

Farther west you would find a great wheat region much like that in the Wheat Belt in the United States. A field in the wheat region is shown in Figure 301. 

To the west of the wheat region the lands are like the Great Plains in the 

United States. Most of the lands are covered with grass and provide excellent pasture for cattle and sheep. Figure 302 shows a herd of cattle on the pampas. 

Many of the herds are watched by cowboys, called gauchos, like the one you will see in Figure 303.

South of the pampas the plain rises gradually from the Atlantic Ocean toward the Andes Mountains and forms a low plateau called Patagonia. Find 

Find this region on the map. The rainfall here, as you will see in Figures 293 and 294, is light throughout the year. Few crops, therefore, are raised, and most of the land is used for grazing sheep.

THE PEOPLE OF SOUTH AMERICA 

You have already learned that South

America is not thickly settled. On all the continent there are only 90,111,000 people. This is just about two-thirds as many as live in the United States. Large areas, as you can see from the map, have no large cities and no railroads. Few people live in the hot lowlands because of the jungle. Moreover, the region is very unhealthful because of the mosquitoes and other insects. In general, people who must make their homes here suffer greatly from the insect pests. 

South America, like North America, was explored and settled by people from 

Europe. Most of these people came from Spain, but Brazil was settled by people from Portugal. Spanish, then, is spoken in nearly all the countries except 

Brazil, where Portuguese is spoken.

When the early settlers came, they found Indians living in South America, just as the settlers found Indians in 

North America. The Indians in certain parts of South America, especially on the highlands in the Andes, were good workers. The settlers found them raising crops much as white people do today. 

Just as in Mexico and Central America, some of the early settlers married Indian wives. In many places today, then, you will find the people a mixture between Spanish and Indian and between Portuguese and Indian. There are still many 

Indians in the northern part of South

America, and also some Negroes in the hot regions. Most of the people of the cooler regions are white people whose forefathers came from Europe. A number of settlers have also come from the United States and various other countries.

THE PEOPLE OF SOUTH AMERICA 

307 

Courtesy Federacion Nacional de Cafeteros de Colombia 

Figure 304. Some of the people of South America. This picture was taken in Colombia, a country in the northwestern part of the continent. It shows coffee pickers at work. You will see that both old and young work in the fields. In parts of South America, the schools are closed during the coffee harvest so that the children can help with the work. 

If you were to talk to the people shown here, you would probably have to speak Spanish. 

The ABC countries. Practically all of the countries of South America are republics like the United States. They are independent and make their own laws. The three leading countries are 

Argentina, Brazil, and Chile, which are frequently called the ABC countries. 

They make up nearly two-thirds of the area of South America and have about two-thirds of the population. Most of the inhabitants in these countries live in regions of moderate temperatures. You will notice from the map that Argentina and Chile lie mostly south of the Tropic of Capricorn in the temperate belt. 

Many of the people of Brazil live on the 

Brazilian Highlands, where the altitude gives the region a moderate climate. In general, you will find all over the earth that people in regions of moderate climate do more work than people in regions where it is extremely hot. 

Some questions to answer 

1. Why does Christmas come during the summer in southern South America? 

2. How does the location of natural regions in South America compare with the location of natural regions in North America? 

3. What three great river basins make up the largest part of the central lowland? 

4. What kind of vegetation is found in each of these basins? 

5. How does the population of South America compare with that of North America? 

6. What language is most commonly spoken in South America? What language is spoken in Brazil? 

7. Which of the leading countries of South

America lie mostly in the temperate belt?

BRAZIL, THE LARGEST COUNTRY 

Brazil is a very large country. It is, in fact, larger than the United States and is the fourth largest country in the world. Refer to the map and notice how much of South America is covered by this one country.

Although Brazil is a little larger than the United States, it has only about a third as many people. Figure 295 will show you where most of the people live.

Which is more thickly settled, the northern or the southern part? Do most of the people live in the highlands or the lowlands?

LIVING IN THE AMAZON VALLEY 

The "king of rivers." The Amazon

River rises high up in the Andes Mountains about sixty miles from the Pacific Ocean. For several hundred miles it is known as the Marañon River. Find the beginning of the Marañon River on the map and trace it northward through the mountains and eastward to the town of 

Iquitos. This city, as you will remember, is at the head of navigation. Now trace the Amazon eastward to its mouth on the Atlantic Ocean. Do you remember how far it is from Iquitos to the mouth of the Amazon? Using the scale of miles on the map, measure the distance. Follow the bends in the river as closely as you can.

Notice again the many large rivers that flow into the Amazon. A number of these rivers may be considered great rivers in themselves. Seventeen of the tributaries are more than a thousand miles long, and at least a hundred of them can be used by river steamers. Thus you can see why the Amazon is such a mighty river. No other river pours so much water into the ocean, and no other river carries so much mud and silt. 

Its currents sweep far out into the Atlantic Ocean. The waters for hundreds of miles from shore are made yellow by the mud which it carries. This mud has helped the Amazon to build up a large delta at its mouth. What other great river have you found carrying down mud and building a delta at its mouth? 

The selvas, or thick tropical forests. 

All the Amazon basin, as you already know, is covered with a thick jungle such as you saw in Figure 299. The trees are all matted together with vines that grow on their trunks and branches. Hot weather and heavy rainfall favor the growth of plants. You will remember that the Amazon Valley is in the path of the northeast and southeast trade winds which bring rain nearly every day.

Therefore a great variety of trees and plants which thrive in hot, wet regions cover all the land.

So far the great Amazon forests have been little used for lumber. Does Figure 

299 suggest any reasons why they have not been used? The chief reason is that it is almost impossible to get the lumber out of the forests. The trees are so bound together with vines that they will not fall even after they have been cut through at the bottom. It is very hard to build roads and railroads, for plants grow so rapidly that they soon cover all openings through the forest. About the only way, then, to haul the lumber is by boats on the rivers.

There are many animals in the jungle. 

If you were to follow a path through the forests, you would probably see a number of them. Above, you would see chattering monkeys and many beautiful birds. 

You might catch a glimpse of a sloth that spends its life hanging upside down from the limbs of trees. Slowly coiling about the limb of a tree you might also see a giant snake, the boa constrictor, watching for its prey. On the ground you might find an anteater and a tapir, which are shown in Figures 306 and 307. 

Then, too, you might find a jaguar, a very dangerous animal somewhat like a leopard. Figure 308 shows how fierce this animal looks. Perhaps you would laugh if you should see an armadillo.

This little animal has a covering of bony plates and rolls itself up in a ball when it is attacked by other animals. In the 

Amazon River and its tributaries there are many fish, alligators, eels, and turtles.

The Indians of the Amazon Valley get much of their food from the rivers. 

From Jungle Island. Courtesy W. C. Allee 

Figure 306. An anteater. This animal has a long, slender nose which it pushes down into the ant hills. 

Courtesy American Museum of Natural History 

Figure 307. A tapir. This animal would remind you very much of a hog. It lives in swampy land and feeds on roots, leaves, and other vegetation. 

Courtesy American Museum of Natural History 

Figure 308. A jaguar. Although some South Americans call the jaguar a tiger, it looks more like a leopard.

Notice that it is marked with spots rather than with stripes.

live largely by hunting and fishing, but some of them farm little patches in the forests and others make a living by gathering rubber. 

Life with a rubber gatherer. Did you know that rubber is a product that comes from trees? Here and there in the Amazon lowlands are scattered rubber trees. The Indians collect the sap from these trees and make raw rubber. They make the raw rubber in a very strange way. When a rubber gatherer finds a rubber tree, he cuts gashes in the bark and hangs little tin or clay cups on the tree to collect the sap as it oozes out. The gashes are made early in the morning and the sap is collected in the afternoon. The thick, milky sap that flows from the rubber tree is known as latex. The rubber gatherer pours a little latex over a stick and holds it over a fire to harden. He keeps on pouring and drying until he has a big ball of rubber on his stick, like those shown in Figure 310. He sells the rubber at trading centers in the more thickly settled regions. 

Century Photos 

Figure 309. A home on the Amazon. In some places it is far better to live out over the water than it is to live along the shore. Can you suggest why? Notice that everywhere the forest is very thick and gloomy. 

Few people in the Amazon Valley. 

Figure 295 shows that very few people live in the Amazon Valley. How different the population here is from the population of lowlands in the United States!

In the United States the lowlands are usually thickly settled. Although the 

Amazon Valley is one of the largest lowland regions in the world, it has only a few people. How can you explain the difference? Most of the few people live along the river and its tributaries, sometimes even in houses built over the water like the one shown in Figure 309.

The hot, moist climate of the Amazon is just as bad for people as it is good for plants. Mosquitoes and other insects make the region very unhealthful. Terrible fevers haunt the rivers and swamps.

Most of the people in the Amazon Valley, except in the few cities, are Indians. These Indians seem to be able to stand the unhealthful climate. They 

The rubber gatherer lives in a rude hut in the forest. This hut is usually open on all sides, but has a roof of palm leaves to keep out the hot sun and the rain. 

The life of a rubber gatherer is not very pleasant. The trees are widely scattered, and he must spend long hours going from tree to tree. Then, too, he often becomes ill because of his damp surroundings and poor food.

For many years most of the rubber in the world came from trees that grew wild in the Amazon Valley. Most of the rubber today, however, comes from trees that grow on large plantations in southeastern Asia.

The home of Brazil nuts. Some of the trees in the Amazon Valley produce nuts known as Brazil nuts. These nuts grow inside hard shells or pods, each pod containing several nuts. Millions of nuts are shipped to the United States each year. In fact, they make one of the most valuable products of the forests along the Amazon. Now, when you eat 

Brazil nuts, think of the great forests. 

Cities in the Amazon Valley. Two cities have grown up in the Amazon 

Valley in Brazil, Manaus, sometimes called Manáos, and Belém, sometimes called Pará. Each of these cities is a center for shipping rubber and other products from the tropical forests. Find 

Manaus again on the map. This city is in the heart of the great gloomy forest. 

It is a fine city, however, and has electric cars and many paved streets. How far is Manaus from the ocean? On what river is it?

Find Belém (Pará) on the map. This city is the gateway to the Amazon Valley. 

It ships large quantities of rubber, Brazil nuts, cacao beans, wax, gums, and furniture woods that have been brought down the Amazon and its tributaries. It is a large city, and would be larger if the climate were not so hot and moist. 

LIVING ON THE BRAZILIAN HIGHLANDS 

The map on page 295 shows that most of southeastern Brazil is a highland. It is really a plateau with ranges of mountains here and there. East of this high- 

Courtesy University of Pennsylvania 

Figure 310. Rubber gatherers in the Amazon Valley.

The picture shows balls of rubber on sticks. The sticks are removed before the rubber is shipped. Sometimes the sticks are dipped in latex and the balls formed on the end. land a narrow coastal plain borders the ocean. Look at Figure 295 to find which is more thickly populated, the plateau or the coastal plain. 

Why the highlands are important. If you look at the map, you will see that the Tropic of Capricorn passes across southern Brazil. Most of the highland region is in the tropical belt. The climate, however, is very pleasant. Can you explain why? Here, high above the heat, moisture, and fevers of tropical lowlands, the people live in comfort. A large part of the western part of the highlands is covered with grass. This is the region that is known as the campos. Find this region again on the map. 

In the coffee country. Brazil grows nearly three-fourths of the coffee that is raised in the entire world. Figure 312 will show you where the leading coffee lands are. In the heart of the coffee region is the city of São Paulo. Find this city on the map. A journey out in any direction from São Paulo would take you through great coffee plantations.

SOUTH AMERICA 

Courtesy Brazilian-American Coffee Promotion Committee 

Figure 311. A coffee plantation in southern Brazil. The coffee trees stand in rows on the hills. The large white area is a group of drying floors. In the front of the picture are several small houses where the workers live.

EQUATOR 

COFFEE 

Figure 312. Where coffee is grown in South America.

Notice that most of the coffee is grown in southeastern Brazil and in the northwestern part of the continent. In what ways is the surface alike in the two regions where most of the coffee is grown? In what ways is the climate in the two regions alike?

Coffee grows best on lands that are not too hot or too wet. The conditions on the highlands around São Paulo are exactly right. The highlands are not exceedingly hot. There is a wet growing season and a dry harvesting harvesting season.

Moreover, the soils are made up of decayed volcanic rocks which are especially good for growing coffee. 

On the coffee plantations. Figure 311 shows you part of a coffee plantation. 

Most plantations cover thousands of acres and have more than fifty thousand trees. The largest ones have more than a million trees.

If left alone, coffee trees would grow to be 15 or 18 feet high. The trees are often trimmed or pruned down to about 

8 feet, however, so that the coffee berries may be picked more easily. The trees

Figure 313. A park in São Paulo. This park is called "Garden of Light," and you can see that it is well named. 

Notice that the vegetation is a mixture of plants that grow in the tropics and plants that grow in the temperate belts. 

The same mixture of plants is common in most parts of the Brazilian Highlands. usually begin to bear when they are four years old. They bloom in October, which is a spring month in southern Brazil. 

The coffee berries grow rapidly in the summer sunshine and rain. When ripe they look somewhat like cranberries or cherries except that each berry contains two beans covered with thin husks.

The harvesting season begins late in the autumn, usually in May or June, and lasts for several months. First the berries are picked and put into a machine that takes out the beans. The beans are next spread out on floors to dry in the hot sunshine. Workmen keep turning them over and over with rakes so that they will dry evenly. Finally, the husks are removed, and the beans are polished by machinery. Then they are put into bags and stored until they are shipped. 

São Paulo is situated fifty miles from the ocean. Here on the plateau, half a mile above the sea, it has become the third largest city of South America.

Notice on the map that it is a great railroad center. It also has many mills and

This cloth is a very coarse material like burlap that is used for making coffee bags. Supplying the world with coffee, then, provides work for many people. 

Two great coffee ports. Find Santos on the map. This city is an important port largely because it is so near the great coffee market at São Paulo. Find the railroad that runs from Santos to São

Paulo. This is a busy railroad, which climbs a mountain wall so steep that the cars must be pulled up by steel cables. 

Find Rio de Janeiro, the capital of 

Brazil, on the map. This city is the second largest city of South America and is an important port. It shares the coffee trade with Santos, but exports only about one-fourth as much coffee as Santos, because it is much farther from 

São Paulo.

Figure 314 is a picture that was taken from a point overlooking Rio de Janeiro and its harbor. This harbor is one of the finest in the world. It is almost surrounded by a rim of mountains covered with dark-green forests. A fine boulevard, lined with tropical trees and brilliantly lighted at night, follows the shore for miles. High up on the slopes many fine homes are built. Rio de Janeiro is one of the cleanest and most beautiful cities in the world. It has attractive buildings, spacious gardens and parks,

and many broad boulevards and streets. Since stoves and furnaces are not needed in the homes, there is little smoke. Rio de Janeiro is a busy as well as a beautiful city. You can see from the map that railroads come in from several directions to the city. Many boats stop in the harbor. The numerous factories make cotton goods, clothing, shoes, furniture, and jute cloth.

Figure 315. A cacao tree. From the seeds, or beans as they are called, of this tree chocolate and cocoa are made. The fruit, or pods, grow on the trunk and large branches rather than on twigs as do most kinds of fruit. 

What the cacao tree furnishes. The cacao tree produces seeds from which cocoa and chocolate are made. This tree grows best (1) on lowlands where the climate is always hot, (2) in places where the ground is always moist, and (3) in places where there are other trees to shade it and protect it from the winds. 

The cacao tree grows in the eastern part of the country where the conditions are most favorable for its growth. The weather is warm the year round and the winds bring a heavy rainfall. 

The cacao seeds or beans grow inside big pods. Figure 315 shows some of the pods growing upon a tree. In harvesting, the pods are cut from the trees, after which the beans are taken out, dried, and packed for shipment. 

Find São Salvador and Recife on the map. These cities are important ports. 

São Salvador ships cacao, and Recife ships sugar and cotton, which are also raised in eastern Brazil.

Other products of Brazilian farms. 

The people of the Brazilian Highlands raise many other crops besides coffee and cacao. In a number of places a very good quality of cotton is raised. Tobacco, too, is an important crop, as are also such grains as corn, wheat, and rice. 

Even in the region of great coffee plantations mixed farming is carried on to some extent. The greatest region for mixed farming, however, is in the extreme southern part of the country. Look at the map and find the lowland region south of the plateau. This part of 

Brazil is in the temperate belt. Here large numbers of hogs and cattle are raised. The leading exports are meat and hides. Find Rio Grande do Sul, the chief port, on the map on pages 296-297. 

In Brazilian mines. The Brazilian 

Highlands have rich deposits of minerals. 

The iron deposits are the richest in the world, but little iron ore is mined.

In southern forests. Besides the great tropical forests along the Amazon, Brazil has other regions of forests. Find the

THE GUIANAS- THREE EUROPEAN COLONIES 

The Guianas are the only South American countries that are not independent. 

The region is divided into three parts and belongs to three European countries. 

The western part belongs to the British Empire, the central part belongs to the Netherlands, and the eastern part belongs to France. Find all the countries in the Guianas on the map. What country bounds the Guianas on the south? on the west?

Living in the Guiana Lowlands. What does the color on the map tell you about the coastal land of the Guianas? The trade winds, blowing from the ocean over the low coastal lands, bring drenching rains. Look at Figures 293 and 294 and find this region of heavy rainfall. 

Because of the hot, wet climate, the coastal region is an unhealthful place in which to live. 

Sugar, cacao, and rubber are products of the coastal lowlands. The work on the plantations is done mostly by Negroes and by Asiatic Indians brought over by the British government. All the people together, however, make only a scanty population. In British Guiana there are only four people per square mile. Dutch 

Guiana, also called Surinam, and French Guiana are still more thinly settled. 

Living in the Guiana Highlands. Find the Guiana Highlands on the map. Only a few people live here because of the tropical heat, the heavy rainfall, the thick jungle, and the rough land. Indians collect balata sap in the forests. This sap is used for making chewing gum and is obtained from a tree or shrub that for the most part grows wild. 

Mining also is carried on in the Guiana 

Highlands. Gold and diamonds are mined in British Guiana. In British

Guiana and Surinam bauxite is found. 

VENEZUELA Find Venezuela on the map. What great sea does it border? What country bounds it on the south? on the west? on the east? About how far is it from the equator? What kind of climate would you expect the lowlands to have?

The Orinoco River and Valley. The 

Orinoco River, which flows across Venezuela, is one of the great rivers of the world. It is nearly as long as the Mississippi. Large ocean vessels can go up the river as far as Ciudad Bolívar, more than two hundred miles from the coast. 

Ciudad Bolívar on the map. Beyond this city smaller boats use the river to travel across Venezuela into Colombia. 

Find 

Because of the great Orinoco River you might think that Venezuela is thickly settled, but it is not. Ciudad Bolívar, with only twenty thousand people, is the largest city on the Orinoco River. It is easy to see why so few people live here. 

Notice on the map that near its mouth the river divides into many branches and flows across a great delta. This delta is entirely covered with water when the river is high. Even when it is not under water, it is a wet, hot region covered with a dense jungle of trees, vines, and creepers. 

Farther upstream are the tropical grasslands known as llanos. These grasslands are low, level plains. Here and there are scattered clumps of trees. All the region is very hot because the valley of the Orinoco is less than 10° from the equator. The only change of season is 19--5G

Figure 319. Where cattle are raised in South America.

Most of the cattle are raised on the pampas. from wet to dry. The rainy season is from May to October and the dry season from

November to April. During the rainy season the Orinoco and its tributaries overflow their banks and spread over thousands of square miles of land. During the dry season, however, many of the small streams disappear and the grass withers and dies. 

Grazing cattle on the llanos. Millions of cattle are pastured on the llanos, and most of the people who live here are cattlemen.

Neither cattle nor people, however, have an easy life in the Orinoco Valley. 

In times of flood the cattle must be driven to the hills, and during the dry season they must eat poor, withered grass and go far for water. Moreover, the grass on the llanos is coarse and not good for fattening cattle. Many cattle, therefore, are raised for their hides. 

The highlands of Venezuela. Look again at the map and notice that there are highlands in both the northern and the southern parts of Venezuela. The highlands in the north are a branch of the 

Andes Mountains. These highlands form a great wall along the coast of the Caribbean Sea. More people live here than in any other part of the country. Most of the people make a living by farming. 

Corn, beans, wheat, vegetables, and fruits are raised. There are also many fine coffee and cacao plantations. The plateaus and slopes furnish excellent pasture for cattle and goats.

Most of the towns and cities are found in the Andes Highlands. Here, a mile high, you find the capital city, Caracas. 

Figure 318 shows how the city nestles in a mountain valley. The climate of the city is very pleasant because of its altitude. According to the map, about how far is Caracas from the equator? 

La Guaira is the chief seaport of 

Venezuela. This city is built on a lowland at the foot of the highlands. Although the distance between Caracas and La Guaira is only seven miles, the railroad between the two cities is twenty-three miles long. It must wind around the mountains in order to avoid the steep slopes. An excellent paved road also connects the two cities. Find Caracas and La Guaira on the map. 

The highlands in southern Venezuela are very thinly settled, as are the highlands in the southern Guianas. There are mineral deposits here, but at present only a little gold is mined.

Rich oil fields. On the map find Lake 

Maracaibo in the northwestern part of the country. This lake is in the heart of one of the world's leading oil-producing regions. Other oil-producing regions are found in eastern Venezuela and on the Caribbean coast. Pipe lines carry the crude oil from the region around Lake 

Maracaibo to the port of Maracaibo. 

Tank steamers then take it to refineries. 

Some of the oil fields in Venezuela are owned by people from the Netherlands. 

The crude oil from these fields is carried to two small islands off the coast to the north, where it is refined. Both of these islands belong to the Netherlands. From the refineries the oil products are shipped to various parts of the world. 

Venezuela is one of the leading oilproducing countries in the world. Petroleum is the chief export. Part of the petroleum is shipped to various ports in the United States. 

COLOMBIA Find Colombia on the map. What body of water bounds it on the north? 

What body of water bounds it on the west? What country of Central America also bounds it on the west? How far is the northern part of Colombia from the equator? the southern part?

Thinly settled lowlands. The southern part of Colombia is in the Amazon Valley, and is covered with thick tropical forests. 

Only a few Indians live in the valley. 

They make a living largely by gathering sap from wild rubber trees.

The eastern part of Colombia is in the llanos region of the Orinoco. All this lowland, as you have already learned, is very thinly settled. During a part of the year it is very wet and during a part of the year it is very dry. 

Colombia has other lowlands along the

Caribbean Sea and the Pacific Ocean. 

These lowlands, like the others, are thinly settled. They are hot, wet, and unhealthful. The Caribbean lowlands, however, are not so wet as the Pacific lowlands, for in the Caribbean region the season changes from wet to dry during a part of the year. Large areas of the land are covered with grass and used for pas turing cattle. The Pacific lowlands are too wet and unhealthful to be used.

This city, a mile and a half high, has a cool and pleasant climate even though it is only 5° from the equator. Moreover, it is a beautiful city with electric lights, paved streets, spacious parks, and fine public buildings. 

Many white people live in Bogotá. Their forefathers were Spaniards who settled here more than three hundred years ago.

Another land of coffee. Colombia Cartagena. From here it is shipped in tank steamers to the United States and to Europe. Find Cartagena on the map. ranks next to Brazil in the production of coffee. In fact, coffee is the most valuable product Colombia has to sell. The most important coffee lands are found in the mountain valleys and on the slopes east of the Magdalena River. Others are found on the slopes that lead down to the Cauca River. Find this river on the map. The soils of the highlands here are favorable and the climate is neither too hot nor too cold for growing coffee. 

Rich mineral deposits. Colombia, too, has many minerals. The northern Andes

Mountains are a storehouse of precious metals and stones. Gold has been taken from the rocks and gravel in the streams ever since the Spaniards first came here. 

One of the most valuable minerals is platinum. This mineral is found in only a few places in the world and is even more valuable than gold. Colombia ranks high in the world's production of platinum. Small particles of this white metal are found in the sand and gravel of certain rivers. These particles are separated from the sand and gravel by placer mining. Do you remember what placer mining is? 

The finest and most beautiful emeralds in the world are also mined in the Andes a short distance north of Bogotá. Pure emeralds are more valuable than diamonds. Most of the emerald mines are owned by the government and have been worked for hundreds of years. 

Then, too, the Andes in Colombia contain valuable deposits of copper, iron, lead, and silver, although only small quantities are mined. The valley of the 

Magdalena contains productive oil fields.

ECUADOR 

66 The word Ecuador means equator." 

Find Ecuador on the map and see whether you can tell why it was given such a name. 

You can see from the map that Ecuador, like all Andes countries, has both highlands and lowlands. There are forested lowlands along the Pacific Ocean in the western part of the country and in the Amazon Valley in the eastern part. 

These two lowlands are separated by high mountains. You would expect, therefore, to find a great variety of climate in 

Ecuador. The lowlands, of course, are very hot, but as you climb the slopes of the mountains the lands become cooler. 

Some of the peaks are covered with snow. You may think of Ecuador, then, as a country that has a very pleasant climate in the highlands even though it is on the equator. 

Living on the lowlands. The forested lowlands along the Pacific coast are the most productive part of the country. 

Here cacao trees grow in large numbers. 

Conditions are very similar to those in the places where you found cacao trees growing in Brazil. Do you remember what these conditions are? Cacao is the most valuable product Ecuador has to sell. 

Palm trees on the lowlands also furnish valuable products. One of these products is ivory nuts, which grow on trees called tagua palms. The nuts supply a material like ivory which is used in making buttons. Large quantities of the nuts are shipped to the United States.
