The best hats are woven under water because the fibers become brittle when exposed to the sun. Probably you wonder why hats from Ecuador are called Panama hats. They got their name from the fact that before the Panama Canal was built they were shipped to Panama and then on to ports in the eastern United States. 

Today, however, the hats are shipped directly from Ecuador to our eastern ports. The Pacific lowlands in Ecuador also have a number of sugar-cane plantations and cotton fields. The lowlands, then, furnish a wide variety of products. 

The Amazon lowlands, as in Brazil, are hot and rainy. About the only people here are a few Indians who make a living by hunting and fishing and by gathering sap from wild rubber trees. They live in small huts covered with palm leaves to keep out the heat and the rain. 

Quito and Guayaquil. Figure 322 is a picture taken in Quito, the capital of 

Ecuador. Find Quito on the map and notice that it is almost on the equator. 

Notice, too, that it lies in the Andes Mountains. Its altitude of more than 

9,000 feet gives it a climate the year round like that of springtime.

The largest city of Ecuador is Guaya quil on the Pacific coast. Find this city on the map. What do the colors on the map tell you about the surrounding lands? Once the lowlands around Guayaquil were unhealthful, but today all the mosquitoes and other insects that spread fevers and disease have been destroyed or driven away. Since the climate, however, is very hot, many people make their homes in the highlands. 

A railroad connects Guayaquil with 

Quito. This railroad was very hard to build because of the steep mountains. 

Since Ecuador has few good highways, the railroad is very helpful. It provides the only good outlet from the mountains.

PERU 

Peru, as you can see from the map, lies just south of Ecuador and borders on the Pacific Ocean. Like Colombia and 

Ecuador, it is largely a mountainous country. The Andes Mountains in Peru extend across the country as two ranges. 

Between these two ranges is a broad plateau, one of the highest in the world.

In certain places it is more than two and a half miles above the level of the sea. 

An old Indian country. When the 

Spaniards, under a leader by the name of 

Pizarro, first came to this plateau, they found a great country of Indians, known as the Incas. This country extended over all the central part of the Andes and had many villages and cities. The country was ruled by a king. The heart of the Incas' country was in Peru, and the capital city was Cuzco. Find Cuzco on the map. The Indians here, like the 

Aztecs of Mexico, were more advanced than most Indians. They built temples, mined metals, and had a system of irrigation. In several places ruins left by the Incas still stand. Among these ruins are old temples, forts, and high walls. 

Figure 324 is a picture of some of the ruins. Pizarro and the Spaniards soon conquered the Incas, and for a time the country belonged to Spain. Today, however, Peru, like other South American countries, is independent. 

Living on the high plateau today. The high Andean Plateau in Peru is still the home of Indians whose forefathers were 

Incas. In fact, Indians make up more than half the population. Today the 

Indian villages are almost the same as they were when the Spaniards first came.

Figure 327. An alpaca. This animal, as you can see, looks somewhat like a sheep except that it has a longer neck. It is found only in South America.

Cuzco, however, looks quite different, for the Spanish built a new city. 

The Indians on the plateau tend flocks of sheep, cattle, alpacas, and llamas. 

The alpaca and the llama are South American animals. Incas were raising them when the Spaniards first came. 

Figure 326 will show you what the llama is like. Notice that it looks somewhat like a small camel, but has no hump on its back. The llama is trained to carry light loads up the mountains. 

Figure 327 is a picture of an alpaca. 

This animal is not used to carry burdens, but is raised chiefly for its wool. 

Then, too, the Indians raise large quantities of potatoes, wheat, corn, and barley. The plateau is especially good for raising potatoes. This plateau is their home. You will remember that it was in South America that potatoes were first found. The Incas raised potatoes here long before white people came. 

Mining in the Andes. The mountains of Peru are rich in deposits of minerals. 

On the map find Cerro de Pasco in the central part of the country. This city is in the heart of the mining district. 

The railroad that runs from Callao on the coast up into the mountains to Cerro de Pasco climbs nearly 15,000 feet.

The district near Cerro de Pasco includes some of the richest copper mines in the world, as well as much gold and silver. It also contains the world's greatest supply of vanadium. This metal is mixed with iron to make a kind of steel that is very strong. Vanadium steel is used largely for making automobiles and airplanes. Nearly all of Peru's vanadium is exported to the United States. 

The jungle in eastern Peru. In eastern Peru the Andes slope down to the lowlands of the Amazon Valley. This part of Peru is known as the montaña and is hot, rainy, and thickly forested. 

The few Indians who live here gather sap from the scattered rubber trees in the forests. The rubber is brought to Iquitos and shipped down the Amazon River. 

Another product of the forests in eastern Peru is quinine, a medicine made from the bark of the cinchona tree.

The coastal lands in Peru. The lowland region between the Pacific Ocean and the Andes Mountains is a dry, barren desert. This region is quite different from the hot, wet coastal region of Ecuador farther north. The land here is dry largely because the winds blow from the east. As these winds climb the eastern slopes of the mountains, they become cooler and lose most of their moisture. As they blow down the western slopes, they become warmer and take up moisture. In other words, the coastal lands in Peru are dry because they are on the leeward side of the mountains. 

That is, they are on the side opposite to the direction from which the winds blow. 

Because the coastal region is so dry, you would expect it to be of little value. 

On the contrary, it is a very important part of the country. Farming is done by irrigation where water can be obtained from mountain streams. The soil is fertile and under the hot sun produces wonderful crops of sugar cane, cotton, and rice. Every river valley extends across the desert like a great green belt. Sugar and cotton are the leading exports. 

Near the northern end of the coastal region is a productive oil field. Many wells have been drilled and several refineries have been built. While petroleum ranks high as an export, the total quantity of oil that comes from Peru is small when compared with the quantity that comes from other parts of the world. 

Lima and Callao. Find Lima and 

Callao on the map. Lima is the capital and largest city. Although it is only a few hundred feet above the sea, it has a pleasant climate because a cold ocean current flows along the shore. Thus the people of Lima live in comfort even though they are not nearly so high as the people of Quito or Bogotá.

Callao is the chief seaport. Its nearness to Lima helps to make it important. 

It has a man-made harbor. Walls of stone have been built out from the shore as a protection for ships. 

BOLIVIA 

To the southeast of Peru find Bolivia on the map. This is one of two countries of South America that do not border the ocean. Notice that the western part of 

Bolivia is in the Andes Mountains, but that the northern and southeastern parts are lowlands.

The Andean Plateau, which extends southward across Ecuador and Peru, becomes still wider and higher in Bolivia. 

It becomes so high that the weather is very cold in winter and the nights are frosty even in summer. The plateau is dry because the high mountains on either side shut out the winds. 

As in Peru, many of the people of Bolivia are Indians. Nearly all of them live on the plateau. Some make a living by caring for cattle, sheep, llamas, and alpacas. From these animals they get both food and materials for making clothing. Where there is enough rain, the Indians raise potatoes, barley, and beans. They build their houses out of stones or sun-dried bricks, and cover them with grass, for there are no trees on the plateau.

Rich in minerals. Many of the people on the plateau make a living by working in mines, for the Bolivian Plateau is rich in minerals. There are important deposits of tin, silver, copper, lead, and zinc.

SOUTH AMERICA 

Bolivia, especially, is famous for its tin. 

It is, in fact, one of the leading tinproducing countries in the world. The 

United States mines no tin, but uses about nine-tenths of all the tin that is mined. Hereafter, then, when you see tin cans stacked high on your groceryman's shelves, think of Bolivia.

Bolivia also mines much silver. The most famous mines are found near Potosí. 

Find this city on the map. The silver mines here were opened nearly four hundred years ago and have been worked ever since.

The highest large lake in the world. 

Would you expect to find a large lake high in the mountains? Look at the map and find Lake Titicaca on the boundary between Bolivia and Peru. 

This is the highest fresh-water lake in the world, being two and a half miles above the sea. It gets its water from snows on surrounding mountains. There are steamboats on the lake, but they were made in the United States and in Europe. 

They were shipped to Peru in pieces and taken by railroad to the shores of the high lake, where they were put together. 

The eastern lowlands. The lowlands of northern Bolivia are a part of the Amazon Valley and much like the lowlands in eastern Peru. They have a hot, moist climate and are covered with tropical forests. The few Indians here make a living by gathering sap from rubber trees. The lowlands of southeastern Bolivia have much less rainfall. 

The Indians use the land for pasturing cattle. The region, however, as you will see in Figure 295, is very thinly settled. 

La Paz and Sucre. Near La Paz, the largest city in Bolivia, is Mount Illimani, which is nearly four miles high. 

Find La Paz and Mount Illimani on the map. La Paz is built in a sheltered valley nearly two miles above the level of the sea. It is an important mining and business center and an important railroad center as well. You will find a picture of La Paz in Figure 298. 

Now find Sucre on the map. This city is the capital, but nearly all of the business of the government is carried on at La Paz. This is because La Paz is a larger city and has railroads leading out in various directions.

Figure 329. A nitrate oficina. This is one of the plants to which nitrate rock is brought from the nitrate beds. In the front of the picture you will see some of the large tanks in which the nitrate is leached, or dissolved, out of the crushed rock. 

CHILE-A LONG, NARROW COUNTRY 

Chile is nearly 3,000 miles long and only 125 miles wide. Look at Chile on the map. Did you ever see a country with such a shape? Notice that it is crowded up between the ocean and the 

Andes Mountains. Colombia, Ecuador,

Peru, and Bolivia all have land on both sides of the Andes, but Chile stops at the top of the mountains. Another country extends east from the mountains. 

THE DESERT IN NORTHERN CHILE The Desert of Atacama in northern 

Chile is one of the most barren regions in the world. Rain seldom falls in all the area. In fact, some places have not had a shower for years. The desert region is a southward extension of the rainless coastal lands of Peru. The winds, as in 

Peru, come from the east and lose their moisture as they rise to blow over the 

A useful desert. From what has just been said, you would expect this desert to be no good whatever. On the contrary, it is a very productive place, and is noted for its mines. One of the leading minerals that comes from the region is nitrate. Indeed, it is the only place in the world where large quantities are mined. Nitrate is used in making a number of products, such as gunpowder and iodine. Most nitrate, however, is used for making fertilizers. You will remember that you found nitrate being unloaded at some southern ports in the 

United States. The next time you see a farmer fertilizing his fields, or hear the explosion of a gun, or put iodine on a cut, think of the Desert of Atacama.

How nitrate is mined and shipped.

Nitrate is found in rocks called caliche that lie in great beds between the Andes and a low range of mountains along the coast. These rock beds are from a few inches to several feet thick and are found near the surface of the ground. 

In order to get the nitrate, the miners bore holes down into the caliche, and gunpowder or dynamite is then exploded in the holes. The broken rocks are then taken to a factory, or oficina, as it is called. Figure 329 shows what a nitrate oficina looks like. Here the rocks are crushed and boiled in water to remove the nitrate. The mixture of water and nitrate runs out into large shallow pans. When the mixture cools, the nitrate collects in fine white crystals. The crystals are then taken from the pans, dried, and packed in bags for shipping. Most of the nitrate is shipped from two ports-Iquique and 

Antofagasta. Find both these ports on the map.

Rich copper mines. Chile also mines great quantities of copper. Indeed, it produces more copper than any other country in the world except the United States. The most important copper mines in Chile are at the foot of the Andes a short distance north of the Tropic of 

Capricorn. Just east of the nitrate country is a great mountain where the copper is found. The ore lies near the surface of the ground. People from the 

United States operate most of the mines. 

Find Chuquicamata on the map. This city is in the heart of the copper region. 

Copper is also mined in central Chile. 

Although the mines here are important, they produce much less copper than the mines in the northern part.

THE RICH CENTRAL VALLEY 

South of the dry nitrate and copper region is a rich farming country. The most fertile part of this farming country is a long, narrow valley that extends north and south between the Andes on the east and some low coast ranges on the west. This valley is more than 600 miles long, but only about 30 miles wide. On this long, narrow strip of lowland, however, live more than three-fourths of all the people of Chile. Find this lowland region on the map. 

Farming in the central valley. The central valley, as you already know, has rainy winters and dry summers. Its climate is so much like that in the southern part of California that Chile is sometimes called the "California of South America."

Much wheat is raised in the central valley. This wheat is planted in the fall and grows during the mild moist winter. 

It is harvested during the dry, sunny summer. Do you remember which are the fall months south of the equator? 

About when, then, is the wheat planted?

About when is it harvested? 

Large quantities of fruit are also raised in the central valley. Oranges, grapes, peaches, apricots, and plums are among the fruits that grow here. In what way does the growing of fruit remind you of 

California? The mild winters and the dry summers are especially good for fruit.

During the sunny summers much fruit is dried for shipment to other countries. 

Water for irrigation during the dry season is obtained from streams that flow down from the Andes Mountains.

THE COOL, WET SOUTHERN LANDS 

Refer to the map and notice how the coast line of southern Chile differs from the coast line of northern Chile. Ages and ages ago the southern part of Chile was much higher than it is today. The land, however, slowly sank and, as it did so, the water of the ocean came into the deep mountain valleys and turned them into long, narrow bays.

A land of forests. Southern Chile is as wet as northern Chile is dry. This is because southern Chile lies in the belt of the westerly winds. From which direction do these winds blow? What would you expect to find where they rise to blow over the mountains? You may think of southern Chile, then, as a land of rain-soaked mountains. 

Have you already noticed that wet lands are usually covered with forests? 

The great forests of southern Chile extend

clear down to the sea. The heavy rains make it very difficult to carry on lumbering. Lumbering, however, is being carried on to some extent, and sawmills and lumbermen are a common sight. Lumbering here would be a much greater industry if the region were nearer markets where people use lumber.

In the far south. In the southernmost part of Chile it is not only wet but very cold. You can see from the map that it is more than 50° from the equator. 

Moreover, a cold ocean current flows along the shores. Much of the land is covered with grass and is used for raising sheep.

On the map find Punta Arenas in the southern part of Chile. On what strait is it? What islands are in the Atlantic 

Ocean to the right? Punta Arenas is the farthest south of any city in the world. As you might expect, it has an extensive trade in wool, and exports large quantities of mutton.

ARGENTINA 

Argentina is mostly in the south temperate belt and is more like the United States than any other country of South America. Find Argentina on the map. What countries bound it on the north and east? What country bounds it on the west? 

What mountain range extends along the western boundary? Most of Argentina is in the belt of the westerly winds. Would you expect the rainfall in western Argentina, then, to be heavy or light? 

Why? Look at Figures 293 and 294 and notice how the rainfall increases from the mountains eastward.

MAKING A LIVING ON THE PAMPAS OR FLAT LANDS 

Eastern Argentina lies in the vast plain known as the pampas. This plain is one of the finest farming regions in the world. The soil is fertile and the land is so level that it can be cultivated easily. Moreover, most of the rain comes during the summer season, just when it is needed most. In the western part of the pampas the rainfall is too light for crops, but grass grows well and furnishes rich pastures for cattle. 

Eastern Argentina is often called the "bread land of South America" because 

Figure 332. Bags of wheat piled up on the pampas. Did you ever see so much wheat? This wheat will soon be taken to a seaport to be shipped to various parts of the world. Since it seldom rains on the pampas during the threshing season, wheat can be piled up in the open.

Figure 333. Where wheat is raised. 60 

Figure 334. Where corn is raised. it produces such enormous crops of grain. 

Moreover, it is one of the greatest cattleand sheep-raising regions in the world. 

More people live here than in any other part of the country. There are more railroads and cities here also. Among these cities is Buenos Aires, the largest city of South America. 

Raising wheat on the pampas. Much of the plain has the same conditions as are found in the winter-wheat lands of 

Kansas, Nebraska, and Oklahoma. Do you remember what these conditions are?

The wheat in Argentina is planted in the fall or early winter, from May to

July. It is harvested the following summer, usually in December or January.

Since the land is level, much of the harvesting is done with a combine. This machine, as you already know, cuts and threshes the grain all at once. Figure 332 will show you how wheat is stored.

Argentina exports more of its wheat than does the United States. One reason for this is that Argentina has only 70 60 

Figure 335. Where flax is raised. 70 60 

Figure 336. Where grapes are raised. 

12,000,000 people to feed, whereas the United States has more than ten times as many. Moreover, Argentina raises most of its wheat much nearer to seaports than does the United States. Most wheat from Argentina is shipped to Europe. 

Raising corn and flax on the pampas. 

Much of the eastern part of the plain is suitable for growing corn. Here the soil is deep and fertile, the summers are warm, and the rainfall is from 30 to 40 inches a year. These are just the conditions that corn needs. Argentina produces more corn than any other country except the United States. Only about a fourth of the corn, however, is fed to livestock. The rest of it is shipped to 

Europe. Argentina does not raise many hogs. Large numbers of cattle are raised, but they are fattened on alfalfa. 

The third important grain crop on the plain is flax, which is raised chiefly for making linseed oil. 

On the cattle ranches. Argentina raises millions of cattle and exports more beef

Courtesy Compania Swift Internacional 

Figure 337. Cattle on the pampas. 

This is only one of many herds of cattle that may be found on the pampas. A few gauchos can watch the cattle very easily, for there are no hills or trees to cut off the view. than any other country. Most of the beef is shipped to Europe.

It costs but little to raise cattle in 

Argentina, since they eat only grass and alfalfa. In fact, beef from Argentina can be sold in England at a price as low as beef produced in the United States can be sold in New York City. Most of the cattle of Argentina are raised in the wheat, corn, and flax regions. Some, however, are raised just east of the Andes where there is very little rainfall. 

Sheep on the pampas. Argentina also ranks high among the leading sheepraising countries, having about four times as many sheep as people. In the past the pampas have been the most important sheep-raising region. Today, however, fewer and fewer sheep are being raised here because farmers find it more profitable to use the land for raising wheat, corn, flax, alfalfa, and cattle. 

Irrigated fields in western Argentina.

If you were to travel west across Argentina, the land, as you already know, would become drier and drier. Finally you would reach a place where very little can be raised without irrigation. You would find, however, that several streams flow down to the parched lands from the high Andes. These streams are fed by melting snow. The water from these streams is used for irrigation, and the irrigated lands are used for growing grapes. The city of Mendoza is the center of the vineyard region. Find Mendoza on the map.

The climate here is somewhat like that of southern California. You can understand, then, why it is famous for its grapes. The grapes here ripen in autumn, which you know is the same as spring in the United States. Thus grapes from

Mendoza are shipped to the United States at a time when fresh grapes are hard to

A great capital. Find Buenos Aires on the map. On what river is it? How far is it from the equator? What large city on our Pacific coast is almost as far north of the equator as Buenos Aires is south of the equator?

With two and a half million people, Buenos Aires is one of the large cities of the world. It is not only the largest city of South America, but the third largest city of the Western Hemisphere. 

Only New York and Chicago are larger. 

The word Buenos Aires means "balmy breezes." The Spaniards gave the city this name because they found it such a pleasant place in which to live. Figure 

288 shows you part of Buenos Aires. 

You can see that it has wide streets, splendid buildings, and beautiful parks.

Truly it is a city of beauty, a great capital of a rich country. Figure 338 is a relief map of the surrounding region. 

Other cities on the pampas. Find

Rosario on the map. On what river is it? Rosario ranks second in size among

Argentine cities. It is the outlet for the northern part of the pampas and ships huge quantities of corn and wheat.

Córdoba, in the Andean foothills near the western edge of the pampas, is the third largest city. Find Córdoba on the map. This city is at the western edge of the pampas and is the center for shipment of products from farms and ranches. 

Its many factories include flour mills and tanneries.

Find Tucumán on the map. This city is in the heart of a sugar-cane country and is surrounded by large sugar plantations. Factories in Tucumán refine some of the raw sugar that is obtained from the cane juice. Large quantities are also shipped to Buenos Aires for refining. 

MAKING A LIVING IN PATAGONIA 

Look at the map and find the region. called Patagonia in southern Argentina.

On this plateau millions of sheep are raised. Patagonia, as you can see from the map, is farther from the equator than most of Argentina. The whole region is thinly settled, there being about a hundred sheep to every person. The climate is cold and dry. In most places it is so dry that the grasses do not grow to be very tall or thick. Each sheep here needs about ten times as much land on which to graze as does a sheep on the best grazing lands of the pampas. 

The sheep of Patagonia are raised largely for their wool. They are sheared in the spring, usually in November.

Much of the wool goes to Bahía Blanca to be loaded on ships. Find this port on

the map and notice that railroads come in from several directions. Wool is also shipped from a few ports farther south. 

THE GRAN CHACO 

On the map find the region called the 

Gran Chaco, or great hunting ground, north of the pampas between the mountains and the Paraná and Paraguay rivers. It includes parts of three countries: Argentina, Bolivia, and Paraguay.

Notice that the Gran Chaco is crossed by the Tropic of Capricorn. This means that it is partly in the tropical belt and partly in the temperate belt. The climate is warm, with rainy summers and dry winters. Parts of the region are covered with forests, other parts are grasslands, and still other parts are swampy during the rainy season.

The Gran Chaco is very thinly settled. 

A few Indians make a living by raising cattle. It is not a good cattle country, however, because of insects and cattle diseases. The Indians also do a little farming and raise their own food. Cotton is grown, and oranges are also now being raised to some extent.

A wood that dulls an ax. The forested parts of the Gran Chaco contain a great variety of trees, the most important of which is the quebracho. The wood of this tree is so hard that it even makes the edge of an ax blunt or dull. It is also heavy, as Figure 340 suggests. The word quebracho means "ax breaker."

The wood of the quebracho is used mostly for making an extract for tanning leather. The wood is cut into small pieces or ground into a pulp and boiled.

The tannin is pressed into cakes and shipped to all parts of the world. 

Courtesy Compania Swift International 

Figure 339. Sheep in Patagonia. You have already found that sheep thrive well in lands that are too dry for cattle. This explains why Patagonia is a sheep country. 

PARAGUAY Find Paraguay on the map. Name the countries that border it. What three rivers form part of its boundaries? Judging by the color of the map, would you say that the country is largely a highland or a lowland? What do Figures 293 and 294 tell you about the rainfall? 

Except in the larger cities, most of the people lead extremely simple lives. Although Paraguay is about as large as the state of California, it has only about oneseventh as many people. The western part of the country is in the Gran Chaco. 

This region, with its wet and dry seasons, its many swamps, and insect pests, is not a pleasant place in which to live.

Much of the eastern part is covered with forests and has a hot, wet climate that is also unhealthful. Moreover, Paraguay is an inland country and has no seaports of its own.

Yerba maté. The most valuable product of Paraguay is known as yerba maté,

SOUTH AMERICA 

Courtesy International Products Corporation 

Figure 340. Loading quebracho logs. These logs are so heavy that they cannot be floated down streams, but must be loaded on cars as near the forest as possible. Notice that it takes several men to handle one log. or Paraguay tea. It is made from the leaves of the small maté tree, which grows wild in the forests of eastern Paraguay and the near-by lands of Brazil and 

Argentina. The Indians gather the branches of the tree and dry the leaves over a fire. The dried leaves are then stripped from the branches, ground up, and put into sacks for shipment. Many people in the countries of South America drink yerba maté, and it is being used more and more by people of other continents. 

The best part of Paraguay. The most prosperous part of Paraguay is just east of the Paraguay River. Here sugar cane, tobacco, cotton, and oranges are raised. Some of the cotton, tobacco, and orange crops are exported. Only a small part of the land, however, is cultivated. 

The people still use old-fashioned methods of farming. 

Asunción, an old city. Find Asunción, the capital of Paraguay, on the map and notice that it can be reached by railroad from Buenos Aires. The railroad journey takes about two days. Asunción can also be reached from Buenos Aires by river steamer. The boat trip, however, takes four days. On what two rivers would you travel in making the trip by boat? 

Asunción is a very old city, being one of the oldest cities in America. Moreover, it is a very large city for a country that is thinly settled. Tanning extract, yerba maté, quebracho wood, meat products, hides, and cotton are shipped from the city by railroad and river steamers. 

Find the railroad on the map.

URUGUAY 

Find Uruguay on the map. How does it compare in size with most other countries of South America? What country borders it on the north and east? on the west? What river forms its western boundary? What does the color on the

map tell you about the height of the land in Uruguay? 

Figure 295 will show you that Uruguay is much more thickly settled than is 

Paraguay. Most of the people are white.

Most of them are Spanish and speak the 

Spanish language. Uruguay comes nearer having a pure white population than any other country in South America. In fact, Uruguay, Argentina, and Chile are the only three countries on the continent where nearly all of the people are white. 

A land of sheep and cattle. Except for small areas of hilly land in the northern part, Uruguay is a broad, gently rolling plain. The country is one great region of pastures where millions of cattle and sheep are raised. Most of the things that Uruguay sells to other countries come from the ranches. 

Wheat, corn, and flax are raised in certain places, but only about one acre in twenty is used for raising crops. Nearly all of Uruguay, however, could be cultivated. The soil is fertile, the land is level, and there is favorable rainfall throughout the year.

Montevideo, the capital. Figure 341 

shows a scene in Montevideo. Find this city on the map. On what river is it? 

Montevideo is the fifth largest city in

South America. It has broad paved streets, large public buildings, and many beautiful homes. Ships of all nations crowd its docks, bringing machinery, automobiles, petroleum, and cotton and woolen goods. They take on cargoes of wool, meat, hides, and flour. 

Besides flour mills and meat-packing plants, there are few factories in Montevideo. This is largely because Uruguay has no coal or waterfalls for power. Figure 341. A street in Montevideo, the capital of 

Uruguay. The building at the right is the tallest building in South America. Although Uruguay is a small country, Montevideo is considered one of the most beautiful capitals in the world.

Key to pronunciation: ã, as in āte; â, as in senâte; â, as in câre; ǎ, as in ăm; ă, as in finăl; ä, as in ärm; å, as in åsk; ȧ, as in sofa; e, as in eve; ê, as in crêate; ě, as in end; ě, as in nověl; ẽ, as in cinder; i, as in ice; Ĭ, as in ill; ō, as in ōld; ô, as in obey; ô, as in lôrd; ŏ, as in odd; o, as in connect; oo, as in food; oo, as in foot; ou, as in thou; u, as in pûre; û, as in ûnite; û, as in ûrn; ŭ, as in study; u, as in circus; p, like ng, for n before the sound of k or hard g, as in bank; N indicates the nasal tone, as in French, of the preceding vowel; g, as in go.
